P·L COMMENTARIUS IN CANTICA CANTICORUM 15

Patrologia Latina · Vol. 15 ·

COMMENTARIUS IN CANTICA CANTICORUM

PL 15, coll. 1851–1962  ·  Latin from the Migne printing  ·  First English translation  ·  column numbers follow the original plates, not the Garnier reprint

Ambrose of Milan

Ambrosius Mediolanensis

c. 339 – 4 April 397

Governor of Aemilia-Liguria who was acclaimed bishop of Milan in 374 by a crowd he had come to pacify, while still an unbaptized catechumen; he was baptized, ordained, and consecrated within a week. He learned Greek theology in office and used it, drawing on Origen, Basil, and Philo to give the Latin West an allegorical exegesis it had largely lacked, and reading his Old Testament in the Old Latin versions rather than the Vulgate that displaced them. He refused the imperial court the use of a basilica in 386, imposed public penance on Theodosius I after the massacre at Thessalonica, and baptized Augustine at Milan in 387.

PROLOGUS.

  • 1851

1851A1545 1. Osculetur me osculo oris sui (Cant. I, 1): non hic foeditatis incentiva, sed castitatis celebrantur mysteria; ubi spiritualiter agitur et affectuali quadam contemplatione de nuptiis et conjunctione Christi et Ecclesiae, increati spiritus et creati, carnis et spiritus. Circumcidamus ergo cor nostrum: nihil vile, nihil corporeum quaeramus; vile autem omne terrenum. Nihil ergo terrenum, nihil carnale, nihil saeculare, nihil corporeum, nihil leve atque mutabile in his eloquiis constituamus coelestibus: Eloquia enim Domini eloquia casta; ut in his coelestium mysteriorum immaculata et pudica sinceritas 1546 spiritali interpretatione resplendeat. Non adulterina quadam opinione misceamus terrena divinis, et illud inviolabile sacramentum propheticae visionis aut perennis oraculi, naturae nostrae aestimatione violemus: sive enim de osculis, sive de talibus, totum decet, quidquid defertur religioni; ut 1852Anullum obsequium quod proficiat ad cultum et observantiam Christi erubescamus: sicut in illa saltatione David ante arcam; non enim illa deliciarum comes fuit atque luxuriae saltatio, sed quod unusquisque corpus attollat impigrum, nec humi pigra jacere membra, vel tardis sinat torpere vestigiis. Hoc enim nos magisterium non invenimus, sed accepimus; sic instituit ordinem mystici carminis, doctrina coelestis. 1547

PROLOGUE.

  • 1851

1851A1545 1. Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth (Cant. I, 1): here it is not the incitements of foulness, but the mysteries of chastity, that are celebrated; where the matter is handled spiritually, and by a certain contemplation of the affections, concerning the marriage and the joining of Christ and the Church, of the uncreated Spirit and the created, of flesh and spirit. Let us therefore circumcise our heart: let us seek nothing base, nothing bodily; and everything earthly is base. Let us therefore set down in these heavenly utterances nothing earthly, nothing carnal, nothing worldly, nothing bodily, nothing light and changeable: for the words of the Lord are chaste words; that in them the spotless and modest sincerity of the heavenly mysteries may 1546 shine forth by a spiritual interpretation. Let us not mingle earthly things with divine by some adulterous notion, nor violate that inviolable mystery of prophetic vision or of the everlasting oracle by an estimate drawn from our own nature: for whether it be of kisses or of such things, the whole of it is seemly, whatever is offered to religion; so that we blush at 1852Ano service that avails toward the worship and the observance of Christ: as in that dance of David before the ark; for that dancing was no companion of delights and of wantonness, but that each man should lift up an unsluggish body, and not let his limbs lie sluggish on the ground, or grow numb with slow steps. For this mastery we did not find out, but received; thus has heavenly doctrine established the order of the mystic song. 1547

2.

Osculetur me ab osculis oris sui; quia bona ubera tua super vinum, et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata. Unguentum exinanitum est nomen tuum (Ibid.). Totus iste deliciarum locus ludum sonat, plausum excitat, amorem provocat. Ideo, inquit, adolescentulae dilexerunt te, et attraxerunt te; retro odorem unguentorum tuorum curramus. Induxit me rex in tabernaculum suum (Ibid., 3). Ab osculis coepit ut ad tabernaculum perveniret, atque illa impatiens dominici laboris exercitataeque virtutis, 1853Aagitur ut aperiat manu claustra, in agrum exeat, in castellis maneat; in principio tamen retro odorem currit unguenti, cum in tabernaculum venerit, unguentum mutatura castellis. Denique quo evadat, vide.

2.

Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth; for thy breasts are good above wine, and the odor of thine ointments above all spices. Thy name is an ointment emptied out (Ibid.). [Vulg. osculo oris sui, "with the kiss of his mouth," singular and without ab; meliora sunt ubera tua vino, "better than wine"; fraglantia unguentis optimis, "fragrant with the best ointments"; oleum effusum nomen tuum, "oil poured out"] This whole passage of delights rings of play, stirs up applause, provokes love. Therefore, she says, the maidens have loved thee and have drawn thee; let us run behind the odor of thine ointments. The king has brought me into his tabernacle (Ibid., 3). [Vulg. trahe me post te; curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum, "draw me after thee: we will run," an imperative; and introduxit me rex in cellaria sua, "into his storerooms"] She began from kisses that she might arrive at the tabernacle; and she, impatient of the Lord's labor and of exercised virtue, 1853Ais driven to open the bars with her hand, to go out into the field, to abide in the villages; yet at the beginning she runs behind the odor of the ointment, and when she has come into the tabernacle she will exchange the ointment for the villages. See, then, where she comes out.

3.

Si murus, inquit, est, aedificemus super eum turres argenteas (Cant. VIII, 9). Quae ludebat osculis, jam turres erigit, ut pretiosis sanctorum turrita fastigiis, non solum hostiles frustretur incursus, verum etiam bonorum propugnacula struat tuta meritorum. Etiam in his conjugiis temporalibus nubenti prius plauditur, quam imperatur; ne ante dura offendant imperia, quam blanditiis amor fotus inolescat. Bucula plausus cujusdam sonitum discit amare, cervicis ne recuset jugum; denique prius assuescitur verbo 1548 lasciviae, quam verbere disciplinae. Ast ubi colla 1853Bsubdiderit jugo, et habena constringit, et stimulus urget, et compares trahunt, et jugalis invitat. Sic etiam Virgo nostra debuit prius amore pio cudere; aurea thori coelestis fulcra mirari, et in ipso vestibulo 1854Anuptiarum et postes frondium sertis cernere coronatos, et chori strepentis interius haurire delicias; ne se prius dominico jugo timefacta subduceret, quam vocata inclinaret. Oportet enim ut gradus quosdam profectuum et ordinem noverimus, quid prius, quid consequens esse debeat: nam scire quid facias, et ordinem nescire faciendi, non est perfectae cognitionis, offendunt enim plerumque praepostera. Itaque quia quot homines, tot sententiae, si quid defecatum est in sermone nostro, omnes legant: si quid decoctum, maturiores probent: si quid modestum, pectoribus inhaereat, genas pingat: si quid florulentum, aetas florulenta non improbet. Debuimus sponsae excitare amorem; scriptum est enim: Diliges Dominum Deum tuum (Deut. VI, 5): 1854Bdebuimus in nuptiis crines sponsae calamistris quibusdam orationis ornare; scriptum est enim: Plaude manu, et percute pede (Ezech. XXI, 14): debuimus perpetuos spargere thalamos rosis.

3.

If she is a wall, he says, let us build upon her towers of silver (Cant. VIII, 9). [Vulg. propugnacula argentea, "bulwarks of silver"] She who was playing with kisses now raises up towers, so that, turreted with the precious pinnacles of the saints, she may not only frustrate the assaults of enemies, but may also build safe bulwarks of good merits. Even in these temporal marriages the bride is applauded before she is commanded, lest harsh commands give offense before love, warmed by caresses, has grown into her. The heifer learns to love the sound of a certain clapping, that she may not refuse the yoke upon her neck; and so she is first accustomed to the word 1548 of playfulness before the lash of discipline. But when she has put her neck 1853Bunder the yoke, the rein also constrains her, and the goad presses, and her yokemates draw, and the yoke-fellow invites. So too our Virgin ought first to hammer with holy love; to marvel at the golden supports of the heavenly couch, and in the very porch 1854Aof the wedding to behold the doorposts crowned with garlands of leafage, and to drink in the delights of the choir sounding within; lest, made afraid, she withdraw herself from the Lord's yoke before, being called, she bow to it. For we must know certain degrees of advancement, and their order — what ought to come first and what after: for to know what thou shouldst do, and not to know the order of doing it, is not of perfect knowledge; for things done back to front most often give offense. And so, since there are as many opinions as there are men, if anything in our discourse has been cleared, let all read it; if anything is boiled down, let the riper approve it; if anything is modest, let it cling to their breasts and paint their cheeks; if anything is flowery, let the flowering age not disapprove it. We ought to have stirred up the love of the bride; for it is written: Thou shalt love the Lord thy God (Deut. VI, 5): 1854Bwe ought to have adorned the bride's hair at the wedding with certain curling-irons of prayer; for it is written: Clap with the hand, and strike with the foot (Ezech. XXI, 14): we ought to have strewn the everlasting bridal chambers with roses.

INCIPIT TEXTUS.

HERE BEGINS THE TEXT.

CAPUT PRIMUM.

1853B1547

CHAPTER ONE.

1853B1547

1.

Ante vita quam doctrina quaerenda est; vita enim bona et sine doctrina habet gratiam, doctrina sine vita integritatem non habet, In malevolam etenim animam non cadit sapientia (Sap. I, 4): ideoque ait: Quaerent me mali, et non invenient (Prov. I, 28): quia improbitate caecatur mentis oculus, et caligante sibi iniquitate mysteria profunda invenire non potest. Primum ergo exercenda est 1853Cvitae militia, corrigendi mores. Cum haec instituerimus ad cursum debitum, ut sit offensionis correctio, puritatis gratia: tunc ad studia percipiendae cognitionis veniamus suo ordine et via. Prima igitur sunt moralia, secunda mystica: in illis vita, in his cognitio; ita ut si perfectionem requiras, nec vita sine cognitione, nec cognitio sine vita sit, utrumque astipuletur alteri. Quam institutionem secutus Salomon librum de Proverbiis scripsit, quo moralem locum uberius expressit, naturalem in Ecclesiaste, mysticum in Canticis canticorum: quamquam si diligenter discutias, et in Proverbiis mystica pleraque reperias, et in Canticis moralium suavitatem. Nam utique mysticum est: Sapientia aedificavit sibi domum, et subdidit columnas septem, interfecit hostias 1853Dsuas, etc. (Pov. IX, 1). Hoc quippe in Canticis lucet mysticum quod morale, in quo blanditiarum suavitas et affectus amantis exprimitur.

1.

Life is to be sought before doctrine; for a good life has grace even without doctrine, while doctrine without life has not wholeness. For wisdom does not fall into an ill-willing soul (Sap. I, 4): [Vulg. non introibit sapientia, "will not enter in"] and therefore he says: The wicked shall seek me, and shall not find (Prov. I, 28): [Vulg. tunc invocabunt me et non exaudiam; mane consurgent et non invenient me, "then shall they call upon me, and I will not hear"] because the eye of the mind is blinded by wickedness, and, with iniquity darkening it, it cannot find the deep mysteries. First, therefore, the soldiery of life must be exercised, 1853Cand morals corrected. When we have set these things toward their due course, so that there may be correction of offense and the grace of purity, then let us come to the pursuits of taking in knowledge, in their own order and way. First, then, come the moral matters, second the mystical: in the former is life, in the latter knowledge; so that, if thou seek perfection, there be neither life without knowledge nor knowledge without life, but each bear witness with the other. Following this arrangement, Solomon wrote the book of Proverbs, in which he set forth the moral topic more richly, the natural in Ecclesiastes, the mystical in the Song of Songs: although, if thou examine diligently, thou wouldst find very many mystical things in Proverbs too, and in the Song the sweetness of moral matters. For surely this is mystical: Wisdom has built herself a house, and has set seven columns beneath it; she has slain her victims, 1853D etc. (Pov. IX, 1). [Vulg. excidit columnas septem; immolavit victimas suas, "hewed out seven columns; sacrificed her victims"] For in the Song that shines as mystical which is moral, wherein the sweetness of endearments and the affection of a lover is expressed.

2.

(Vers. 1, 2.) Osculetur me ab osculo oris sui; quoniam optima ubera tua super vinum, et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata. Constitue ergo Virginem desponsatam multo tempore, et justo ferventem amore, quae multa praeclara opera dilecti, probabilium testium assertione cognoverit, desideriis suspensis dilatam frequenter, jam non ferentem moras, quae omnia fecisset ut sponsum 1854Bderet: aliquando votis potitam suis, ad improvisum sponsi adventum gaudio turbatam, non quaerere primordia salutationis, non verborum vices, sed statim quod desiderasset exigere. Ita ergo et sancta Ecclesia quae in primordiis mundi desponsata in paradiso, praefigurata in diluvio, annuntiata per Legem, vocata per prophetas, cum diu redemptionem hominum, Evangelii decorem, dilecti exspectasset adventum; impatiens morae, in oscula ruit dicens: Osculetur me ab osculo oris sui; et delectata osculis, 1854Cadjicit: quoniam optima ubera tua super vinum.

2.

(Vers. 1, 2.) Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; since thy breasts are best above wine, and the odor of thine ointments above all spices. [Vulg. meliora sunt ubera tua vino; the plate has printed the same lemma with bona, "good," above] Set before thee, then, a Virgin betrothed a long while, and burning with a just love, who has come to know many glorious works of her beloved by the assertion of approved witnesses; often put off, her desires held in suspense, bearing delays no longer, who would have done all things so that she might deret the bridegroom: 1854Bwho at length, having obtained her vows, troubled with joy at the unforeseen coming of the bridegroom, does not seek the first beginnings of greeting, nor the exchanges of words, but at once demands what she had desired. So then also the holy Church, betrothed in the first beginnings of the world in paradise, prefigured in the flood, announced through the Law, called through the prophets, when she had long awaited the redemption of men, the beauty of the Gospel, the coming of her beloved, impatient of delay, rushes into kisses, saying: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; and, delighted with the kisses, 1854Cshe adds: since thy breasts are best above wine.

3.

Et ut moralius dicamus, intelligo mihi carnem illam quae madefacta fuerat in Adam serpentis veneno, quae criminum marcebat fetore, quae procedebat in filiabus Sion alta cervice et nutibus oculorum, et itinere pedum trahens tunicas, et pedibus suis ludens, crinium cincinnis et compositis vultibus atque redimiculis, et omni affectata decore plus dedecens, eamdem tamen plurimis 1548 edoctam oraculis, quod venturus esset qui serpentis illecebris exclusis, Spiritus sancti infunderet gratiam; ut omnis caro videret salutare Dei, omnis caro ad Deum veniret, marcuisse desiderio: sed metuentem ne ut impatiens, ut lasciva, ut luxuriosa, ut querula, sicut ante fuerat displiceret, 1854Dquamvis longiore quam ferre jam poterat, morantis adventus Dominici exspectatione quateretur: non murmurantem tamen nec transgredientem, sed levantem in omni loco puras manus, sine ira et disceptatione, in habitu ornato cum verecundia et sobrietate, ornantem se non in tortis crinibus aut auro aut margaritis aut veste pretiosa, sed his quae castitatis et bonae conversationis decerent gratiam, orare dicentem: Osculetur me ab osculo oris sui; quoniam optima ubera tua super vinum. Jam volebat adhaerere Christo caro, jam festinabat innubere; 1855Aut esset unus spiritus, et fieret caro Christi quae erat ante meretricis. Osculetur, inquit, me: osculatur nos Dei Verbum, quando sensum nostrum spiritus cognitionis illuminat.

3.

And to speak more morally, I understand for myself that flesh which had been steeped in Adam with the serpent's venom, which was withering with the stench of crimes, which went forth among the daughters of Sion with neck held high and with beckonings of the eyes, trailing her tunics in the going of her feet and playing with her own feet, with ringlets of hair and composed countenances and headbands, and in every affected comeliness the more unseemly; and yet that same flesh, taught by very many 1548 oracles that he would come who, when the serpent's enticements had been shut out, would pour in the grace of the Holy Spirit, so that all flesh might see the salvation of God, that all flesh might come to God — that it had withered with longing: but fearing lest, as impatient, as wanton, as luxurious, as querulous, it should displease as it had been before, 1854Dalthough it was shaken by an expectation, longer than it could now bear, of the Lord's delaying coming: yet not murmuring nor transgressing, but lifting up pure hands in every place, without wrath and dispute, in adorned habit with modesty and sobriety, adorning herself not with plaited hair or gold or pearls or costly raiment, but with those things that befit the grace of chastity and of good conversation, praying and saying: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; since thy breasts are best above wine. Now the flesh wished to cleave to Christ, now she hastened to be wedded; 1855Athat she might be one spirit, and that she might become the flesh of Christ who before was a harlot's. Let him kiss me, she says: the Word of God kisses us when the spirit of knowledge illumines our understanding.

4.

Igitur vel Ecclesia est jamdiu promisso sibi per prophetas Dominico adventu per tempora multa suspensa, vel anima quae elevans se a corpore abdicatis luxuria atque deliciis, voluptatibusque carnalibus, exuta quoque sollicitudine saecularium vanitatum, jamdudum infusionem sibi divinae praesentiae et gratiam Verbi salutaris exoptet, commacerari eam quod sero veniat, et affligitur; ideo quasi vulnerata charitatis, cum moras ejus ferre non possit, conversa ad Patrem, rogare ut mittat sibi Deus Verbum, et causam qua sit ita impatiens declarare dicentem: Osculetur me ab osculis oris sui.

4.

Therefore it is either the Church, held in suspense through many seasons by the Lord's coming long since promised to her through the prophets, or the soul which, raising herself from the body, luxury and delights and carnal pleasures being renounced, stripped also of the anxiety of worldly vanities, has this long while yearned for the inpouring of the divine presence into her and for the grace of the saving Word: that she is worn away because he comes late, and is afflicted; and therefore, as one wounded with charity, since she cannot bear his delays, turning to the Father, that she asks God to send her the Word, and declares the cause why she is so impatient, saying: Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth.

5.

Non unum osculum quaerit, sed plura oscula, ut desiderium suum possit explere; quae enim diligit, 1855Bnon est unius osculi parcitate contenta, sed plurima exigit, plura vindicat, et ita se amplius dilecto commendare consuevit. Denique illa in Evangelio sic probata est, Quia non cessavit, inquit Dominus, osculari pedes meos, et ideo remissa sunt ei peccata multa, quia dilexit multum (Luc. VII, 46, 47). Ergo et haec anima oscula Verbi multa desiderat, ut illuminetur divinae cognitionis lumine; hoc est enim osculum Verbi, lumen scilicet cognitionis sacrae. Osculatur enim nos Deus Verbum, quando cor nostrum et ipsum principale hominis Spiritus divinae cognitionis illuminat; quo anima donata charitatis pignore nuptiali, laeta atque ovans dicit: Os meum aperui et eduxi spiritum (Psal. CXVIII, 131). Osculum est enim quo invicem amantes 1855Csibi adhaerent, et velut gratiae interioris suavitate potiuntur. Per hoc osculum adhaeret anima Dei Verbo, quod sibi spiritus transfunditur osculantis: sicut etiam hi qui se osculantur, non sunt labiorum praelibatione contenti, sed spiritum suum sibi invicem videntur infundere. 1549

5.

She does not seek one kiss, but many kisses, that she may be able to fulfil her desire; for she who loves is 1855Bnot content with the scantness of a single kiss, but demands very many, claims more, and has been wont thus to commend herself the more to her beloved. And so she in the Gospel was thus approved: Because she has not ceased, says the Lord, to kiss my feet; and therefore many sins are forgiven her, because she has loved much (Luc. VII, 46, 47). [Vulg. remittuntur ei peccata multa quoniam dilexit multum, a present "are forgiven"] So this soul also desires many kisses of the Word, that she may be enlightened with the light of divine knowledge; for this is the kiss of the Word, namely the light of sacred knowledge. For God the Word kisses us when the Spirit of divine knowledge illumines our heart and the very governing part of man; and the soul, endowed with this pledge of nuptial charity, glad and exulting, says: I opened my mouth and drew forth the spirit (Psal. CXVIII, 131). [Vulg. adtraxi spiritum, "drew in the spirit"] For a kiss is that whereby lovers cleave to one another 1855Cand, as it were, take possession of the sweetness of an inward grace. Through this kiss the soul cleaves to the Word of God, in that the spirit of the one kissing is poured over into her: just as they also who kiss one another are not content with a foretaste of the lips, but seem to pour their own spirit into one another. 1549

6.

Osculetur me ab osculis oris sui. Vis ad Christum aptare? Nihil gratius. Vis ad animam tuam? Nihil jucundius. Osculetur me, videt te mundum esse ab omni peccato, quia delicta de terra sunt; ideo te sacramentis coelestibus dignum judicat, et ideo invitat ad coeleste convivium; ut Osculetur te ab osculis oris sui. Tamen propter sequentia, anima tua, vel humana conditio, vel Ecclesia vidit se ab omnibus mundatam esse peccatis, dignam quae ad altare Dei 1855Dpossit accedere. Quid est enim altare, nisi forma corporis Christi? Vidit sacramenta mirabilia, et ait: Osculetur me ab osculis oris sui; hoc est, osculum mihi Christus infigat. Quare? quia:

6.

Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth. Wilt thou fit this to Christ? Nothing is more welcome. Wilt thou fit it to thy own soul? Nothing is more delightful. Let him kiss me: he sees thee to be clean from every sin, since offenses are of the earth; therefore he judges thee worthy of the heavenly sacraments, and therefore invites thee to the heavenly banquet, that he may kiss thee with the kisses of his mouth. Yet, because of what follows, thy soul, or the human condition, or the Church, has seen herself cleansed from all sins, worthy to be able to approach 1855Dthe altar of God. For what is the altar but the form of the body of Christ? She has seen the wondrous sacraments, and says: Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth; that is, let Christ fix a kiss upon me. Why? Because:

7.

Meliora ubera tua super vinum, hoc est, meliores sensus, meliora tua sacramenta super vinum, super illud vinum quod etsi suavitatem habet, laetitiam 1856Ahabet, gratiam habet: tamen in illo laetitia saecularis, in te autem jucunditas spiritalis est. Jam tunc ergo Salomon inducebat nuptias vel Christi et Ecclesiae, vel spiritus et carnis et animae. Et tamquam despiciens omnes jucunditates et delectationes suas, coelestibus cupieos inhaerere mandatis, ait: quoniam optima praecepta testamentorum tuorum super omnem appetentiam carnis, et saeculi voluptatem; meminerat enim se in Eva ante sic lapsam, dum voluptatem corporis praefert mandatis coelestibus. Ostendens itaque non solam speciem Verbi, et vultum quemdam, sed omnia interiora ejus diligere, adjungit ad osculorum gratiam: Quia bona, inquit, ubera tua super vinum, et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata. Illa osculum poposcit, Deus Verbum se ei totus infudit, et nudavit ei ubera sua, hoc est, dogmata sua et interioris sapientiae disciplinas, et unguentorum suorum dulci odore fragravit: 1856Bquibus capta dicit uberiorem esse jucunditatem divinae cognitionis, quam laetitiam omnis corporeae voluptatis.

7.

Thy breasts are better above wine, [Vulg. meliora sunt ubera tua vino, "better than wine"; the plate has printed the same lemma with bona and with optima above] that is, thy senses are better, thy sacraments are better above wine — above that wine which, though it has sweetness, 1856Athough it has gladness, though it has grace, yet in it the gladness is worldly, while in thee the delight is spiritual. Even then, therefore, Solomon was introducing the marriage either of Christ and the Church, or of spirit and flesh and soul. And as though despising all her own delights and pleasures, desiring cupieos to cleave to the heavenly commandments, she says: since the precepts of thy testaments are best above every appetite of the flesh and every pleasure of the world; for she remembered that she had thus fallen before, in Eve, while she set the pleasure of the body before the heavenly commandments. And so, showing that she loves not only the appearance of the Word and a certain countenance, but all his inward parts, she adds to the grace of the kisses: Because thy breasts are good, she says, above wine, and the odor of thine ointments above all spices. She asked for a kiss; God the Word poured himself wholly into her, and bared his breasts to her, that is, his doctrines and the disciplines of his inward wisdom, and was fragrant with the sweet odor of his ointments: 1856Band, taken captive by these, she says that the delight of divine knowledge is more abundant than the gladness of every bodily pleasure.

8.

Unguentum exinanitum vel oleum effusum nomen tuum; hoc est, totus immundis impuritatibus diversorum facinorum fetebat hic mundus, nunc spirat ubique suavitas pudicitiae, unguentum fidei, flos integritatis. Aspirat etiam in Verbo odor gratiae et remissio peccatorum, quae in totum diffusa mundum, omnia tamquam exinanito replevit unguento, quia per universos gravis illuvies detersa vitiorum est. Hoc unguentum exinanitum est supra Judaeos, et collectum est agentibus: exinanitum in Judaea, et redoluit in omnibus terris. Hoc unguento uncta est Maria, et Virgo concepit, Virgo peperit bonum odorem Dei Filium: hoc unguentum effusum est super 1856Caquas, et sanctificavit aquas: hoc unguento uncti sunt tres pueri, et humorem his flamma roravit; hoc unguento Daniel unctus est, et leonum ora mollivit, feritatemque permulsit. Fluit hoc unguentum quotidie, et numquam deficit. Accipe vas tuum, virgo, et accede, ut possis hoc unguento repleri: accipe unguentum aestimatum trecentis denariis, sed gratis datum, non venditum, ut omnes haberent gratis. Virgo, ungere; noli contristari sicut Judas, quia hoc unguentum effunditur, sed consepeli in te Christum: claude sane vas tuum, ne unguentum effluat: claude integritatis clave, loquendi verecundia, abstinentia gloriandi; hoc quae habet unguentum, Christum recipit: Unguentum exinanitum nomen tuum. 1856D

8.

Thy name is an ointment emptied out, or oil poured forth; that is: this world was wholly stinking with the unclean impurities of divers crimes; now everywhere the sweetness of modesty breathes, the ointment of faith, the flower of integrity. There breathes also in the Word the odor of grace and the remission of sins, which, being poured out over the whole world, has filled all things as with an ointment emptied out, because through all men the heavy filth of vices has been wiped away. This ointment was emptied out above the Jews, and was gathered up for those acting: emptied out in Judaea, and it gave forth its scent in all lands. With this ointment Mary was anointed, and the Virgin conceived, the Virgin bore the good odor, the Son of God: this ointment was poured out upon 1856Cthe waters, and sanctified the waters: with this ointment the three young men were anointed, and the flame bedewed them with moisture; with this ointment Daniel was anointed, and he softened the mouths of the lions and stroked down their wildness. This ointment flows daily, and never fails. Take thy vessel, O virgin, and draw near, that thou mayest be able to be filled with this ointment: take the ointment valued at three hundred pence, but given freely, not sold, that all might have it freely. O virgin, be anointed; be not made sorrowful as Judas was, because this ointment is poured out, but bury Christ together with thee: shut thy vessel indeed, lest the ointment flow away: shut it with the key of integrity, with modesty in speaking, with abstinence from boasting; she who has this ointment receives Christ: Thy name is an ointment emptied out. 1856D

9.

(Vers. 2.) Propterea adolescentulae dilexerunt te. Quae sunt istae adolescentulae, nisi animae singulorum, quae deposuerunt istius corporis senectutem, renovatae per Spiritum sanctum? Unguentum effusum nomen tuum, cujus virtute sermonis nihil potest esse praestantius. Nam sicut inclusum in vase aliquo unguentum cohibet odorem suum, qui odor quamdiu vasis illius angustiis coercetur, etsi ad plures non 1857Apotest pervenire, tamen vim suam servat: cum vero de vase illo quo claudebatur unguentum fuerit effusum, longe lateque diffusum dicitur: ita et Christi nomen ante ejus adventum in Israel populo, quasi in vase aliquo Judaeorum mentibus claudebatur: Notus enim in Judaea Deus, in Israel magnum nomen ejus. (Psal. LXXV, I). Hoc itaque nomen quod vasa Judaeorum angustiis suis coercitum continebant. Magnum quidem et tunc nomen, cum infirmorum atque paucorum haereret angustiis, sed magnitudinem sui nondum per corda gentium et in fines totius orbis effuderat: 1550 postquam vero per omnem mundum suo illuxit adventu, per omnem utique creaturam divinum illud suum nomen extendit, non repletum accessione aliqua, plenitudo enim nescit augmentum; sed replens vacua, ut esset admirabile nomen ejus in 1857Buniversa terra. Hujus igitur effusio nominis abundantem quamdam exuberantiam gratiarum, bonorumque coelestium significat ubertatem, ex abundanti enim superfluit quidquid effunditur. Ideo adolescentulae dilexerunt te. Bona, inquit, prudentia, sed dulcis misericordia; illam enim pauci assequuntur, haec ad omnes pervenit: propter hanc, inquit, indulgentiam tuam diligunt te animae renovatae spiritu.

9.

(Vers. 2.) Therefore the maidens have loved thee. Who are these maidens, but the souls of individual men, who have laid aside the old age of this body, being renewed through the Holy Spirit? Thy name is an ointment poured forth, than the power of which utterance nothing can be more excellent. For just as ointment shut up in some vessel holds in its own odor — which odor, so long as it is confined within the narrows of that vessel, keeps its force, even though it 1857Acannot reach many — but when the ointment has been poured out of the vessel in which it was shut, it is said to be spread far and wide: so also the name of Christ, before his coming, was shut up in the people of Israel, as it were in some vessel of the minds of the Jews: for God is known in Judaea, in Israel his name is great. (Psal. LXXV, I). This name, therefore, which the vessels of the Jews, confined within their own narrows, contained. A great name indeed even then, when it clung within the narrows of the weak and the few, but it had not yet poured out its greatness through the hearts of the nations and unto the ends of the whole world: 1550 but after he shone forth by his coming through the whole world, he stretched out that divine name of his through the whole creation, not filled up by any addition — for fulness knows no increase — but filling the empty places, that his name might be wonderful in 1857Ball the earth. The pouring forth of this name, therefore, signifies a certain abounding overflow of graces and a plenty of heavenly goods; for whatever is poured out overflows out of abundance. Therefore the maidens have loved thee. Good, she says, is prudence, but sweet is mercy; for the first few attain, while the second reaches to all: for the sake of this thy indulgence, she says, the souls renewed in spirit love thee.

10.

(Vers. 3.) Attrahe nos post te, curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum. Osculata jam anima sancta Verbum Dei, modum non capit, nec expletur dicens: Suavis es, Domine, et in jucunditate tua doce me justificationes tuas (Psal. CXVIII, 12). Osculata Dei Verbum concupiscit super omnem decorem, diligit super omnem laetitiam, delectatur super omnia 1857Caromata, cupit frequenter videre, saepe intendere, cupit attrahi ut sequi possit: Unguentum, inquit, exinanitum nomen tuum, propterea te diligimus adolescentulae; propterea certamus, sed comprehendere non possumus: attrahe nos, ut possimus currere; ut odore unguentorum tuorum accipiamus virtutem sequendi. Festinat etiam interna mysteria videre, ipsam requiem Verbi, ipsam boni illius summi habitationem, et lucem ejus et claritatem in illo sinu ac recessu patrio; festinat audire sermones ejus, et cum audierit super omnem suavitatem accipit.

10.

(Vers. 3.) Draw us after thee; we will run in the odor of thine ointments. [Vulg. trahe me post te, "draw me," singular; the exposition that follows turns on the plural "us"] The holy soul, having now kissed the Word of God, takes no measure and is not satisfied, saying: Sweet art thou, O Lord, and in thy delightfulness teach me thy justifications (Psal. CXVIII, 12). Having kissed the Word of God, she desires him above all beauty, loves him above all gladness, delights in him above all 1857Cspices, longs to see him often, to gaze on him frequently, longs to be drawn that she may be able to follow: Thy name, she says, is an ointment emptied out; therefore we maidens love thee; therefore we strive, but we cannot lay hold: draw us, that we may be able to run; that from the odor of thine ointments we may receive strength to follow. She hastens also to see the inward mysteries, the very rest of the Word, the very dwelling-place of that highest good, and his light and brightness in that fatherly bosom and retreat; she hastens to hear his discourses, and when she has heard she receives him above all sweetness.

11.

Doceat te propheta qui gustavit, et ait: Quam dulcia faucibus meis verba tua, super mel et favum ori meo (Psal. CXVIII, 103)! Quid enim aliud desiderat 1857Danima, quae semel Verbi suavitatem gustaverit, quae semel claritatem ejus viderit? Moyses in monte positus quadraginta diebus Legem accipiens, cibum corporis non requirebat: Elias ad illam festinans requiem, rogabat ut acciperetur anima sua a se: Petrus aspiciens et ipse in monte Dominicae resurrectionis gloriam, nolebat descendere, dicens: Domine, bonum est nos hic esse (Matth. XVII, 4). Quanta igitur illa divinae substantiae gloria, quanta Verbi dona, in quae concupiscunt et angeli prospicere!

11.

Let the prophet teach thee who has tasted, and says: How sweet are thy words to my throat, above honey and the honeycomb to my mouth (Psal. CXVIII, 103)! [Vulg. quam dulcia faucibus meis eloquia tua, super mel ori meo, without "and the honeycomb"] For what else does the soul desire 1857Dthat has once tasted the sweetness of the Word, that has once seen his brightness? Moses, set on the mountain for forty days receiving the Law, sought no food for the body: Elijah, hastening to that rest, prayed that his soul might be taken from him: Peter, himself also beholding on the mountain the glory of the Lord's resurrection, was unwilling to come down, saying: Lord, it is good for us to be here (Matth. XVII, 4). How great, then, is that glory of the divine substance, how great the gifts of the Word, upon which the angels also desire to look!

12.

Anima igitur quae illud videt, corpus hoc non requirit, minimamque familiaritatem cum eo esse 1858Adebere intelligit, renuntiat saeculo, abducit se a vinculis carnis, et exuit omnibus voluptatum istarum nexibus. Denique Stephanus Jesum videbat, et lapidari non formidabat; immo cum lapidaretur, non pro se, sed pro illis a quibus perimebatur, rogabat. Paulus quoque raptus usque ad tertium coelum, cum corpore esset vel sine corpore nesciebat, raptus, inquam, in paradisum, usum proprii jam non sentiebat corporis, et audiens verba Dei, quomodo ad corporis infirmitates descenderet, erubescebat. Itaque sciens quid vidisset in paradiso, vel quid audisset, clamabat dicens: Quid adhuc velut viventes de hoc mundo decernitis? Ne tetigeritis, ne attaminaveritis, ne gustaveritis, quae sunt omnia ad corruptelam ipso usu (Coloss. II, 20 et 21). Volebat enim nos in figura esse istius mundi, non possessione atque usu, 1858But ita utamur hoc mundo tamquam non utamur, tamquam praetereuntes, non tamquam residentes; ambulantes tamquam in imagine saeculi, non in cupiditate, ut velocissima disputatione ipsam imaginem hujus mundi transeamus. Denique ipse fide ambulans, non specie, peregrinabatur a corpore, aderat Deo: et cum esset in terris, non in terrenis, sed in coelestibus conversabatur. Ergo anima nostra quae Deo vult appropinquare, elevet se a corpore, semper illi summo bono adhaereat, illi bono quod est divinum, quod est semper, et quod erat ab initio, et quod erat apud Deum (I Joan. I), hoc est, Dei Verbum: ipsum est illud divinum, In quo vivimus, et sumus, et movemur (Act. XVII, 28).

12.

The soul, therefore, that sees that, does not seek this body, and understands that there ought to be 1858Athe least possible familiarity with it; she renounces the world, leads herself away from the bonds of the flesh, and strips herself of all the entanglements of these pleasures. And so Stephen was seeing Jesus, and did not dread to be stoned; nay, when he was being stoned, he prayed not for himself but for those by whom he was being destroyed. Paul too, caught up as far as the third heaven, did not know whether he was with the body or without the body; caught up, I say, into paradise, he no longer felt the use of his own body, and, hearing the words of God, blushed at how he should come down to the infirmities of the body. And so, knowing what he had seen in paradise, or what he had heard, he cried out, saying: Why do you still, as though living out of this world, make decrees? Touch not, handle not, taste not, which things are all unto corruption by the very use (Coloss. II, 20 et 21). [Vulg. ne tetigeritis neque gustaveritis neque contrectaveritis, quae sunt omnia in interitu ipso usu; the three prohibitions stand in a different order, and "unto destruction"] For he wished us to be in the figure of this world, not in possession and use, 1858Bso that we might so use this world as though we used it not, as those passing by, not as those who settle; walking as in the image of the age, not in covetousness, that by a most swift reckoning we may pass beyond the very image of this world. And so he himself, walking by faith, not by sight, was a pilgrim away from the body and present with God: and when he was upon the earth, he had his conversation not in earthly but in heavenly things. Therefore let our soul, which wishes to draw near to God, lift herself up from the body, let her always cleave to that highest good, to that good which is divine, which is always, and which was from the beginning, and which was with God (I Joan. I), that is, the Word of God: this is that divine thing, in which we live, and are, and are moved (Act. XVII, 28). [Vulg. in ipso enim vivimus et movemur et sumus, "live and are moved and are," the last two members in the other order]

13.

Curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum. Hunc 1858Codorem fragrat anima quae Christo aperire incipit, ut accipiat primum odorem Dominicae sepulturae, et credat, quia caro ejus non vidit corruptionem, nec odore aliquo mortis emarcuit: sed aeterni illius floris et semper virentis odore condita surrexit. Quomodo enim poterat vel in carne marcescere, cujus nomen Unguentum exinanitum 1551 est? Exinanivit se ut spiraret tibi; semper hoc unguentum erat, sed erat apud Patrem, erat in Patre, olebat tantum angelis et archangelis, tamquam intra vas coeli. Aperuit os Pater dicens: Ecce posui te in testamentum generis mei, in lucem gentium; ut sis in salutem usque ad extremum terrae (Esai. XLIX, 6). Descendit Filius, repleta sunt omnia novo odore Verbi, eructavit cor Patris Verbum bonum, fragravit Filius, Spiritus 1858Dsanctus exhalavit, atque per omnium corda diffudit: Diffusa est enim charitas Dei in cordibus nostris per Spiritum sanctum (Rom. V, 5). Ipse Dei Filius in corpore tamquam in vase odorem primo cohibebat suum, opperiens tempus sicut ait: Dominus dat mihi linguam eruditionis, ut sciam quando oporteat dicere sermonem. Venit hora, aperuit os, exivit unguentum, quando virtus exibat de eo. Et ideo ista festinat ad Verbum, et rogat ut attrahatur, ne forte derelinquatur; quia currit Dei Verbum, et non est alligatum. Denique Exsultat tamquam gigas ad currendum 1859Aviam (Psal. XVIII, 6). Et quia Egressus ejus summo coelo, et occursus ejus usque ad summum ejus (Ibid., 7), videns se imparem tantae velocitati, dicit: Attrahe nos.

13.

We will run in the odor of thine ointments. This 1858Codor is breathed by the soul that begins to open to Christ, that she may first receive the odor of the Lord's burial, and may believe that his flesh saw no corruption, nor withered with any odor of death, but rose again, embalmed with the odor of that eternal and ever-flourishing flower. For how could he wither even in the flesh, whose name is 1551 an ointment emptied out? He emptied himself that he might breathe upon thee; this ointment always was, but it was with the Father, it was in the Father, and gave forth its scent only to angels and archangels, as within the vessel of heaven. The Father opened his mouth, saying: Behold, I have set thee for a covenant of my race, for a light of the nations, that thou mayest be for salvation unto the utmost part of the earth (Esai. XLIX, 6). [Vulg. dedi te in lucem gentium, ut sis salus mea usque ad extremum terrae, without the covenant clause and with "my salvation"] The Son came down, all things were filled with the new odor of the Word, the heart of the Father uttered forth a good Word, the Son was fragrant, the Holy 1858DSpirit breathed out, and poured himself abroad through the hearts of all: for the charity of God has been poured abroad in our hearts by the Holy Spirit (Rom. V, 5). The Son of God himself at first held in his own odor within the body as within a vessel, awaiting the time, as he says: The Lord gives me the tongue of learning, that I may know when it behoves me to speak a word. [Vulg. Isa. 50:4 Dominus dedit mihi linguam eruditam, ut sciam sustentare eum qui lassus est verbo, "to sustain him that is weary with a word"] The hour came, he opened his mouth, the ointment went forth, when virtue was going out from him. And therefore she hastens to the Word, and asks to be drawn, lest perchance she be left behind; because the Word of God runs, and is not bound. And so he exults as a giant to run 1859Athe way (Psal. XVIII, 6). [Vulg. exultavit ut gigans ad currendam viam, a past "he exulted"] And because his going forth is from the highest heaven, and his meeting even unto the highest thereof (Ibid., 7), seeing herself unequal to so great a swiftness, she says: Draw us.

14.

Bona anima quae non pro se sola, sed pro omnibus rogat: Attrahe, inquam, nos; habemus enim cupiditatem sequendi, quam unguentorum tuorum inspirat gratia: sed quia cursus tuos aequare non possumus, Attrahe nos; ut auxilio tuo fultae, vestigiis tuis possimus insistere. Si enim tu attraxeris, curremus et nos, et spiritalia velocitatis flabra capiemus. Deponitur enim sarcina quibus manus tua fulcro est: et oleum tuum infunditur, quo curatus est ille qui a latronibus vulneratus est.

14.

Good is the soul that asks not for herself alone, but for all: Draw, I say, us; for we have the desire of following, which the grace of thine ointments breathes into us: but because we cannot match thy courses, Draw us; that, propped by thy help, we may be able to press upon thy footsteps. For if thou shalt draw, we too shall run, and shall catch the spiritual breezes of swiftness. For the burden is laid down for those to whom thy hand is a prop: and thine oil is poured in, by which he was healed who had been wounded by robbers.

15.

At ne impudens tibi videatur quod ait: Attrahe nos, audi dicentem: Venite ad me, omnes qui 1859Blaboratis, et onerati estis, et ego vos reficiam (Matth. XI, 28). Vides quod libenter nos attrahit, ne remaneamus sequentes: sed qui vult attrahi, currat ut comprehendat, et currat posteriora obliviscens, et ea quae sunt priora appetens, sic enim poterit Christum comprehendere; unde et Apostolus ait: Sic currite ut comprehendatis. Vult et ista ad bravium pervenire, quae desiderat comprehendere. Prudenter ergo rogat ut attrabatur, quia non omnes sequi possunt. Denique Petro dicenti: Quo vadis? respondit Verbum Dei: Non potes me sequi modo, sequeris autem postea (Joan. XIII, 36; Matth. XIV, 30). Claves ei commiserat regni coelorum, et in sequendo se imparem judicavit. Hanc tamen animam non distulit, quia non praesumebat, sed rogabat. Denique, 1859C

15.

But lest what she says seem to thee shameless — Draw us — hear him who says: Come to me, all you that 1859Blabor and are burdened, and I will refresh you (Matth. XI, 28). Thou seest that he draws us willingly, lest we be left behind as we follow: but let him who wishes to be drawn run that he may lay hold, and let him run forgetting the things behind and reaching after the things that are before; for thus will he be able to lay hold of Christ; whence the Apostle also says: So run that you may lay hold. She too wishes to come to the prize, who desires to lay hold. Prudently, therefore, she asks that she may be drawn attrabatur, because not all are able to follow. And so, when Peter said: Whither goest thou? the Word of God answered: Thou canst not follow me now, but thou shalt follow hereafter (Joan. XIII, 36; Matth. XIV, 30). He had committed to him the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and yet judged him unequal to himself in following. This soul, however, he did not put off, because she was not presuming, but asking. And so, 1859C

16.

(Vers. 3.) Introduxit me in cubiculum suum. Beata anima quae Verbi ingreditur penetralia, namque ea insurgens de corpore, ab omnibus fit remotior; atque illud quod est supra semetipsum, divinum illud si quomodo assequi possit, scrutatur et quaerit: quod cum potuerit comprehendere, ea quae sunt intelligibilia supergressa, in illo confirmatur, atque eo pascitur. Talis erat Paulus qui sciebat se raptum in paradisum, sed sive extra corpus sive in corpore nesciebat. Assurrexerat enim anima ejus de corpore, et se a visceribus et vinculis carnis abduxerat atque levaverat, factusque a semetipso alienus, intra semetipsum tenuit verba ineffabilia quae audivit et vulgare non potuit, quae advertit ea homini loqui non licere. Anima ergo bona contemnit visibilia 1859Det sensibilia, nec consistit in eis, nec in respiciendis his immoratur et residet: sed ascendit ad illa aeterna et invisibilia et plena miraculis, puro sensu se piae mentis attollens. Etenim perfectioni studens, solum illud bonum divinitatis intendit, nec aliud quidquam requirendum putat, quia tenet quod summum est.

16.

(Vers. 3.) He has brought me into his chamber. [Vulg. introduxit me rex in cellaria sua, "into his storerooms," plural] Blessed is the soul that enters the inner chambers of the Word; for she, rising up out of the body, becomes more remote from all things, and searches and seeks after that which is above her very self, that divine thing, whether by any means she may attain it: and when she has been able to grasp it, having passed beyond the things that are intelligible, she is confirmed in it and is fed upon it. Such was Paul, who knew that he had been caught up into paradise, but did not know whether outside the body or in the body. For his soul had risen up out of the body, and had led and lifted itself away from the inward parts and the bonds of the flesh; and, made a stranger to himself, he held within himself the unspeakable words which he heard and could not publish abroad, having marked that it is not lawful for a man to utter them. The good soul, therefore, despises the visible 1859Dand the sensible, and does not settle in them, nor tarry and sit down in the beholding of them: but she ascends to those things that are eternal and invisible and full of wonders, raising herself with the pure perception of a devout mind. For indeed, being zealous for perfection, she reaches toward that good of the Godhead alone, and thinks that nothing else is to be sought, because she holds what is highest.

17.

Introduxit me rex in cubiculum suum. Simplex est osculum, sed negotiosum cubiculi secretum; hoc enim loco mysteria plenius cognoscere se velle testatur, ut ingrediatur penetralia mysteriorum coelestium, 1860Aet aperiantur sibi thesauri sapientiae et scientiae in Christo absconditi: quod ut possit, fortasse illud ait quod supra: Osculetur me ab osculis oris sui. Significat Spiritus sancti supervenientis gratiam, sicut Angelus ad Mariam dixit: Spiritus sanctus superveniet in te, et virtus Altissimi obumbrabit tibi (Luc. I, 35). Quando autem introduxit eam rex in cubiculum suum, passionis tempus, lateris compunctio declarat, sanguinis effusio, sepulturae unguentum, resurrectionis 1552 mysterium; ut osculum quasi sponsa acceperit: in cubiculum autem Christi sit introducta Ecclesia, non jam quasi tantummodo desponsata, sed etiam quasi nupta: nec solum thalamum sit ingressa, sed etiam legitimae claves copulae consecuta sit. Ideoque quasi in thalamo sita ait: Colligatio guttae frater meus mihi, inter 1860Bmedia ubera mea requiescit (Vers. 12).

17.

The king has brought me into his chamber. The kiss is simple, but the secret of the chamber is full of business; for in this place she testifies that she wishes to know the mysteries more fully, that she may enter the inner shrines of the heavenly mysteries, 1860Aand that the treasures of wisdom and knowledge hidden in Christ may be opened to her: and that she may be able to do this, perhaps she says that which was said above: Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth. This signifies the grace of the Holy Spirit coming upon her, as the Angel said to Mary: The Holy Spirit shall come upon thee, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee (Luc. I, 35). But when the king brought her into his chamber, the time of the passion declares it, the piercing of the side, the shedding of blood, the ointment of the burial, the mystery 1552 of the resurrection; so that she received the kiss as a betrothed, but into the chamber of Christ the Church was brought in, no longer as merely betrothed, but also as wedded: nor did she only enter the bridal chamber, but also obtained the keys of the lawful union. And therefore, set as it were in the bridal chamber, she says: A binding of the drop is my brother to me; between 1860Bthe midst of my breasts he rests (Vers. 12). [Vulg. fasciculus murrae dilectus meus mihi, inter ubera mea commorabitur, "a bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me… he shall abide"]

18.

Quod si cubiculum quaerimus, ipse nos edocet qui ait: tu autem cum orabis, intra in cubiculum tuum, et clauso ostio, ora Patrem tuum in abscondito (Matth. VI, 6). Cubiculum Ecclesiae corpus est Christi. Introduxit eam rex in omnia interiora mysteria, dedit ei claves ut aperiret sibi thesauros scientiae, sacramentorum clausas ante fores panderet, cognosceret quiescentis gratiam, defuncti somnum, virtutem resurgentis. In illo cubiculo justitias Domini Jesu nupta reperit. Quae sunt illae justitiae? Utique sacramenta baptismatis, sicut legimus, quia cum venienti ad baptismum diceret Joannes: Ego a te debeo baptizari, et tu venis ad me; respondit Jesus: Sine modo, sic enim decet nos implere omnem 1860Cjustitiam (Matth. III, 15). In illo cubiculo justificationes Domini discit, consilium Dei cognovit, quo nihil potest esse sublimius; hoc est enim divinum, in quo fit remissio peccatorum.

18.

But if we seek the chamber, he himself teaches us who says: but thou, when thou shalt pray, enter into thy chamber, and, the door being shut, pray to thy Father in secret (Matth. VI, 6). The chamber of the Church is the body of Christ. The king brought her into all the inward mysteries, gave her the keys that she might open for herself the treasures of knowledge, might spread open the doors of the sacraments that were closed before, might know the grace of him who rested, the sleep of him who died, the power of him who rose again. In that chamber the bride finds the justices of the Lord Jesus. What are those justices? Surely the sacraments of baptism, as we read: for when John said to him as he came to baptism, I ought to be baptized by thee, and comest thou to me? Jesus answered: Suffer it now, for so it becomes us to fulfil all 1860Cjustice (Matth. III, 15). In that chamber she learns the justifications of the Lord, she has come to know the counsel of God, than which nothing can be more sublime; for this is the divine thing, in which remission of sins is made.

19.

Haec a Domino quaerit discere, quae homines docere non poterant, nisi antea Deus docuisset: quae posteaquam a Christo didicerunt apostoli, Ecclesia dicit: Introduxit me rex in cubiculum suum, hoc est, in illud secretum, in quo sunt omnes thesauri scientiae et cognitionis suae. Ideoque tibi dicitur: Cum oras, intra in cubiculum tuum (Matth. VI, 6). quod significat mentis animaeque secretum. In hoc cubiculo justitiarum sponsa se poposcit induci, cum post odorem unguenti excurrerit, quod semper fluit, et numquam deficit. 1860D

19.

She seeks to learn from the Lord those things which men could not teach unless God had taught them first: and after the apostles had learned them from Christ, the Church says: The king has brought me into his chamber, that is, into that secret place in which are all the treasures of his knowledge and understanding. And therefore it is said to thee: When thou prayest, enter into thy chamber (Matth. VI, 6). [Vulg. cum orabis, a future "when thou shalt pray"] which signifies the secret place of mind and soul. Into this chamber of justices the bride asked to be led, when she had run out after the odor of the ointment, which flows always and never fails. 1860D

20.

Introduxit me rex in cubiculum suum, Graecus in promptuarium suum et in cellarium suum habet: ubi bona libamina, ubi boni odores, mella suavia, ubi fructus diversi, ubi epulae variae sunt, ut plurimis epulis tuum prandium condiatur. Denique in illud divini illius sponsi introducta secretum:

20.

The king has brought me into his chamber. The Greek has "into his storehouse" and "into his cellar": where are the good libations, where are the good odors, sweet honeys, where are the divers fruits, where are the varied dishes, that thy meal may be seasoned with very many dishes. And so, having been brought into that secret place of the divine bridegroom:

21.

Exsultemus, inquit, et laetemur in te; diligamus ubera tua super vinum; non enim in divitiis et auri argentique thesauris, non in possessionum fructibus, non in potestatibus, non in conviviis, sed in solo Deo exsultat, dicens ad eum: Diligamus 1861Aubera tua super vinum. Sic tamen bibebat, ut non absorberetur a vino, sed gratia ejus laetitiam cordis hauriret, non corporis ebrietate titubaret. Denique bibens hoc vinum, aperuit oculos suos, et vidit viam rectam, reliquit tortuosas vias dicens: Aequitas dilexit te, hoc est, non flexuosa itinera te sequuntur: sed solus ad te potest justitiae trames pervenire, qui enim diligit justitiam non se avertit a Christo. Quomodo enim arbitrum justitiae, remuneratoremque meritorum innocens metuat conscientia? Eadem tamen anima cognoscens corporis societate se fuscatam, dicit ad alias animas, vel ad alias coelestes et appositas sacro mysterio potestates: Nolite aspicere me, quoniam obfuscata sum, quia non est intuitus me sol.

21.

Let us exult, she says, and be glad in thee; let us love thy breasts above wine; [Vulg. exultabimus et laetabimur in te, memores uberum tuorum super vinum, a future "we will exult" and "mindful of thy breasts"] for she exults not in riches and in treasures of gold and silver, not in the fruits of possessions, not in powers, not in banquets, but in God alone, saying to him: Let us love 1861Athy breasts above wine. Yet she so drank that she was not swallowed up by the wine, but drew the gladness of the heart from his grace, and did not stagger with the drunkenness of the body. And so, drinking this wine, she opened her eyes and saw the straight way, and left the crooked ways, saying: Equity has loved thee, [Vulg. recti diligunt te, "the upright love thee"] that is: winding journeys do not follow thee, but the path of justice alone can come to thee; for he who loves justice does not turn himself away from Christ. For how should an innocent conscience fear the judge of justice and the rewarder of merits? Yet the same soul, knowing herself darkened by the fellowship of the body, says to other souls, or to other heavenly powers set beside the sacred mystery: Do not look upon me, since I have been darkened, because the sun has not looked upon me.

22.

(Vers. 4.) Fusca sum et decora, filiae Hierusalem. 1861BConstitue Dominum Jesum recumbentem in convivio, reclinantem se Joannem supra pectus ejus, mirantes alios quod servus supra Dominum reclinaret, quod caro illa peccatrix supra templum Verbi recumberet, quod anima illa carnis vinculis innexa aulam divinae plenitudinis scrutaretur. Haec ergo mirantibus aliis, respondit anima Joannis: Fusca sum et decora, filiae Jerusalem; fusca per culpam, decora per gratiam. Dicit et caro, fusca sum et decora; fusca pulvere saeculari, quem certando collegit, decora oculo spiritali, quo mundi hujus pulverem squaloremque detersit: fusca per vitium, sed decora jam per lavacrum, quod abluit omne delictum: fusca sum quia peccavi, decora quia jam me diligit Christus; quam relegaverat enim in Eva, recipit in 1861Cvirgine, suscipit ex Maria.

22.

(Vers. 4.) I am dusky and comely, O daughters of Jerusalem. 1861B[Vulg. nigra sum sed formosa, "I am black but beautiful," and decoloravit me sol, "the sun has discolored me," where the plate prints the negative "the sun has not looked upon me"] Set before thee the Lord Jesus reclining at the banquet, and John leaning himself upon his breast, and the others marvelling that a servant should lean upon his Lord, that that sinful flesh should recline upon the temple of the Word, that that soul, bound in by the chains of the flesh, should search out the court of the divine fulness. To these others, therefore, as they marvelled, the soul of John answered: I am dusky and comely, O daughters of Jerusalem; dusky through fault, comely through grace. The flesh says it too, I am dusky and comely; dusky with the dust of the world, which she gathered in her striving, comely with the spiritual eye, wherewith she wiped away the dust and squalor of this world: dusky through vice, but comely now through the washing, which cleanses away every offense: I am dusky because I have sinned, comely because Christ now loves me; for her whom he had banished in Eve, he takes back in 1861Cthe virgin, he receives from Mary.

23.

Dicit etiam Synagoga, cujus mysteria hoc loco plerisque videntur exprimi; quae cum se rejici videret propter totius plebis impietatem; solabatur se tamen, 1553 quia ipsum Christum de populo suo non tantum, sed et Petrum, Joannem, Jacobum Christo adhaerentes videbat, quia habebat eloquia sibi credita. Et ideo dicebat: Fusca sum et decora, filiae Hierusalem: fusca per incredulitatem, decora per legem: fusca per lapsum, decora quia dilexit me sol, et prior facta sum congregatio Dei. Nolite me refugere quia fusca sum; ideo fusca sum quia sol me reliquit justitiae, qui ante illuminare consueverat. Amisi colorem vultus mei, obtusa facta est acies oculorum meorum quibus ante solem videbam: 1861Din tenebris ambulo, quae diem Christi nescio. Nolite tamen despicere me, quoniam qui reliquit, potest rursum aspicere. Misereri iterum solet, congregare dispersos, desertos requirere, colligere destitutos. Omnes tribus Israel, convenite, aspicite me decoram ante delicta; nolite aspicere me, quoniam obfuscata sum; hoc est, non hoc solum aspiciatis quia obfuscata sum; non est intuitus me sol, et ideo obfuscata sum. Sed lucet sol super justos et injustos, super justos pro gratia, super injustos pro 1862Amisericordia; illis mercedem meritorum tribuens, istis peccata dimittens. Et gentibus non lucebat ante, nunc lucet, nunc illis oritur qui oriebatur mihi; qui illis remisit, remittet et mihi. Nolite arbitrari quoniam obfuscata sum, quod penitus reliquerit me sol, et jam non intueatur, non requirat aegram. Dissimulavit a me, quia non servavi mandata ejus: reconciliabitur cum viderit meorum poenitentiam delictorum. Non vidit me sol, quia non recepi advenientem, non aperui fenestras, ut vitae lumen intraret: cum aperuero, illuminabit oculos meos, qui venit ut totum mundum illuminaret, et videant etiam non videntes.

23.

The Synagogue says it too, whose mysteries very many think to be expressed in this place; who, when she saw herself cast off on account of the impiety of the whole people, yet consoled herself, 1553 because she saw Christ himself out of her own people, and not him only, but Peter, John, and James cleaving to Christ, and because she had the oracles entrusted to her. And therefore she said: I am dusky and comely, O daughters of Jerusalem: dusky through unbelief, comely through the law: dusky through the fall, comely because the sun has loved me, and I was made the first congregation of God. Do not flee from me because I am dusky; I am dusky for this reason, that the sun of justice has forsaken me, who before was wont to give me light. I have lost the color of my countenance, the keenness of my eyes has been dulled, wherewith before I looked upon the sun: 1861DI walk in darkness, I who know not the day of Christ. Yet do not despise me, since he who forsook me can look upon me again. He is wont to have mercy again, to gather the scattered, to seek out the forsaken, to collect the destitute. All ye tribes of Israel, come together, look upon me as I was comely before my offenses; do not look upon me, since I have been darkened; that is, do not look at this alone, that I have been darkened: the sun has not looked upon me, and therefore I have been darkened. But the sun shines upon the just and the unjust, upon the just for grace, upon the unjust for 1862Amercy; giving to the one the reward of their merits, forgiving the others their sins. And upon the nations he did not shine before; now he shines, now he rises upon them who once rose upon me; he who has forgiven them will forgive me also. Do not judge, because I have been darkened, that the sun has utterly forsaken me and no longer looks upon me, nor seeks me out in my sickness. He has hidden himself from me because I have not kept his commandments: he will be reconciled when he shall have seen the repentance of my offenses. The sun has not seen me, because I did not receive him when he came, I did not open the windows that the light of life might come in: when I shall have opened them, he will enlighten my eyes, who came that he might enlighten the whole world, and that even those who do not see may see.

24.

Vel dicit Ecclesia de gentibus: Nolite aspicere me quoniam fusca sum, quia non respexit me sol; eo quod velut pruinis hiemalibus geluque constricta 1862Bgentilis erroris, congregationem gentium sol justitiae diu indignam aestimaverit, quam serena vultus sui luce lustraret. Nonne tibi videbatur hiberni rigor temporis, quando notus erat in Judaea tantummodo Deus? Nunc autem plenitudo lucis fulget aestivae, quando omnia et in omnibus Christus. Nonne terra Domini et plenitudo ejus (Psalm. XXIII, 1)? Et vere orbis terrarum in Ecclesia, quia non Judaeus tantummodo aut Graecus, non Barbarus aut Scytha, non servus aut liber: sed omnes in Christo unum sumus (Coloss. III, 11). Sol omnibus fulget, dies omnibus lucet. Nigra sum, et decora. Praemisit nigram, ut augeret decorem: non dixit: Nigra sum decora, ne quod nigrum est decorum aestimaretur: sed ait: Nigra sum superiore peccato, sed decora 1862Cconfessione peccati, et correctionis studio, atque amore virtutis. Quamvis ergo Et syllaba ut grammatici appellant conjunctiva sit, habet tamen distinctionem, qua confusio distinguitur ac separatur; ut si dicas Ambrosium Bassum intus esse, unus putatur: sin autem asseras Ambrosium et Bassum intus esse, duo utique intelliguntur.

24.

Or the Church of the nations says: Do not look upon me, since I am dusky, because the sun has not regarded me; in that the sun of justice long judged the congregation of the nations, bound as it were with the winter's frosts and the ice 1862Bof gentile error, unworthy to be surveyed by the serene light of his countenance. Did not the harshness of a wintry season seem to thee to be there, when God was known in Judaea only? But now the fulness of the summer light shines out, when Christ is all things and in all. Is not the earth the Lord's, and the fulness thereof (Psalm. XXIII, 1)? And truly the whole round world is in the Church, because there is not Jew only or Greek, not Barbarian or Scythian, not slave or free: but we are all one in Christ (Coloss. III, 11). [Vulg. sed omnia et in omnibus Christus, "but Christ is all and in all"] The sun shines upon all, the day gives light to all. I am black, and comely. She has put "black" first, that she might increase the comeliness: she did not say, I am black comely, lest what is black should be thought comely: but she says, I am black by the former sin, but comely 1862Cby the confession of sin, and by zeal for amendment, and by love of virtue. Although therefore And is a syllable which, as the grammarians call it, is conjunctive, yet it has a distinguishing force, whereby confusion is distinguished and separated; as, if thou shouldst say that Ambrose Bassus is within, one man is thought of; but if thou assert that Ambrose and Bassus are within, two are certainly understood.

25.

Sicut tabernacula Cedar, sicut pelles Salomonis; tentoria enim de exuviis fiunt mortuorum animalium. Igitur quae hoc dicebat, mortua erat peccato, et vivebat Deo: et nos igitur peccato moriamur; ut Deo vivamus. Repleti spiritus, jucunditate et suavitate laetitiae, erimus exuviae spirituales, carentes infirmitate corporea, et integro mentis sinu infusam nobis divinorum mysteriorum gratiam 1862Dreservantes. Tabernacula autem Cedar, pellesve Salomonis corpus hoc dici liquet ex pluribus locis: tamen etiam inde intellige, quod Adam et Eva ubi imaginem deposuere coelestis quam ante portabant, imaginem terreni hominis induentes, tunicas dicti sunt vestiti esse pelliceas; corporales enim eos fecerat de spiritualibus culpa commissa.

25.

As the tabernacles of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon; for tents are made from the hides of dead animals. Therefore she who said this was dead to sin and living to God: and let us too, then, die to sin, that we may live to God. Filled with the spirit, with the delight and sweetness of gladness, we shall be spiritual hides, lacking bodily infirmity, and keeping in the whole bosom of the mind the grace of the divine mysteries poured into us. 1862DBut that the tabernacles of Cedar, or the skins of Solomon, mean this body is clear from many passages: yet understand it also from this, that when Adam and Eve laid aside the image of the heavenly man which they bore before, and put on the image of the earthly man, they are said to have been clothed with tunics of skin; for the fault they had committed had made them bodily out of spiritual.

26.

Nec illud absurdum ad intelligendum, quod ita se dicit sponsa factam sicut pelles solis adustas ardoribus; eo quod in asperitate licet tribulationum 1863Aposita, mortificaverit tamen corpus suum, ne sentire possit illam asperitatem: sicut enim ardorem solis non sentit pellis tabernaculi, eo quod pellis sit animalis mortui; ita haec mortua peccato, ardorem peccati sentire non poterat: vel quia non frangebatur necessitatibus, vel quia corpus 1554 afflictionis sumus, et mortem Domini Jesu semper in corpore nostro circumferre debemus.

26.

Nor is that absurd to understand, that the bride says she has been made like skins scorched by the burnings of the sun; in that, though she was set in the harshness of tribulations, 1863Ashe yet mortified her body, that it might not be able to feel that harshness: for as the skin of a tabernacle does not feel the burning of the sun, in that it is the skin of a dead animal, so she, being dead to sin, could not feel the burning of sin: either because she was not broken by necessities, or because we are a body 1554 of affliction, and ought always to carry about the death of the Lord Jesus in our body.

27.

(Vers. 5.) Filii matris meae pugnaverunt adversum me; hoc est, impugnaverunt me corporis passiones, carnis illecebrae decoloraverunt: ideo sol justitiae mihi non refulsit, quia viduata praesidio devotionem meam et observantiam plenam servare non potui, hoc est enim, vineam meam non custodivi, quia spinas et non uvam attuli, id est faciens peccata pro fructibus. Et cum de Verbo loquitur, 1863Birradiante sibi verbi splendore, conversa ad ipsum dicit:

27.

(Vers. 5.) The sons of my mother have fought against me; that is, the passions of the body have assailed me, the enticements of the flesh have discolored me: therefore the sun of justice has not shone again upon me, because, widowed of my defense, I could not keep my devotion and my full observance; for this is what is meant by I have not kept my own vineyard, because I brought forth thorns and not the grape, that is, doing sins instead of fruits. And when she speaks of the Word, 1863Bthe splendor of the Word shining upon her, she turns to him and says:

28.

(Vers. 6.) Ubi pascis, ubi manes in meridiano? Recte dicit, ubi pascis, quia regale est Dei Verbum: ubi manes, quia morale: in meridiano, quia mysticum: siquidem meridie Joseph cum fratribus suis in convivio constitutus futurorum mysteria temporum revelabat. Sed etiam David dicit: Revela ad Dominum vitam tuam et spera in eum, et ipse faciet, et educet sicut lumen justitiam tuam, et judicium tuum sicut meridiem (Psalm. XXXVI, 5). Et ipse Paulus circumfulsisse sibi lumen meridie asseruit, quando a persecutione est conversus ad gratiam.

28.

(Vers. 6.) Where dost thou feed, where dost thou abide at midday? [Vulg. ubi pascas, ubi cubes in meridie, subjunctive and "where thou liest down"] Rightly she says, where dost thou feed, because the Word of God is royal: where dost thou abide, because he is moral: at midday, because he is mystical: since indeed at midday Joseph, set at the banquet with his brothers, was revealing the mysteries of the times to come. But David also says: Reveal thy life to the Lord and hope in him, and he himself will do it, and will lead forth thy justice as the light, and thy judgment as the noonday (Psalm. XXXVI, 5). [Vulg. revela Domino viam tuam, "reveal thy way to the Lord"] And Paul himself asserted that a light shone about him at midday, when he was turned from persecution to grace.

29.

Queritur ergo quod derelicta sit, quod destituta sit pauper ex divite; abundabat enim munere 1863Cgratiarum: sed egere coepit, ubi divinae praesentiae sibi copia denegata est. Et ideo vel quasi mercenaria haberi postulat, quae ante sibi pretiosioris copulae gratiam vindicabat.

29.

She complains, therefore, that she has been forsaken, that she has been left destitute, poor out of rich; for she abounded in the gift 1863Cof graces, but she began to be in want when the abundance of the divine presence was denied her. And therefore she asks even to be held as a hireling, she who before claimed for herself the grace of a more precious union.

30.

Posuerunt me custodem in vineis, vineam meam non custodivi (Vers. 5). Putant aliqui hoc de illis dici qui Synagogam ad legis praecepta constringunt, ut legem custodiat, vineam suam tueatur, quam custodire non potuit. Denique custodiebat ut faceret uvas, fecit autem spinas. Quae sit igitur vinea, venit David testificare nobis dicens: Vineam de Aegypto transtulisti, et ejecisti gentes, et plantasti eam (Psalm. LXXIX, 9). Possunt et apostoli esse filii matris ejus, qui vere impugnaverunt Synagogam dicentes: Ecce convertimur ad gentes: et nationibus verbum 1863DDei seminare coeperunt. Possunt et prophetae intelligi qui monendo denuntiandoque ut vineam suam custodiret Synagoga, nihil profecerunt; fecit enim spinas improbitatis, quae virtutis uberes fructus deferre debuerat: et ideo confitetur quod populum suum custodire non potuit meritoque sero quaerit tenere, quem cum teneret, amisit: multo dissimilior ejus quae dicit: Inveni quem diligit anima mea, tenui eum, et non relinquam eum (Cant. III, 4). Denique illa invitat in hortum suum sponsum; haec ubi pascat requirens, dicit ad ipsum: 1864A

30.

They have set me as a keeper in the vineyards; my own vineyard I have not kept (Vers. 5). Some think that this is said of those who bind the Synagogue to the precepts of the law, that she may keep the law and guard her own vineyard, which she could not keep. And so she was keeping it that she might make grapes, but she made thorns. What, then, the vineyard is, David comes to testify to us, saying: Thou hast brought a vineyard out of Egypt, and hast cast out the nations, and hast planted it (Psalm. LXXIX, 9). The apostles too may be the sons of her mother, who truly assailed the Synagogue, saying: Behold, we turn to the nations: and they began to sow the word 1863Dof God among the peoples. The prophets too may be understood, who, by warning and denouncing that the Synagogue should keep her own vineyard, accomplished nothing; for she made the thorns of wickedness, who ought to have brought forth the rich fruits of virtue: and therefore she confesses that she could not keep her own people, and deservedly seeks too late to hold him whom, while she held him, she lost: far unlike her who says: I have found him whom my soul loves; I held him, and I will not let him go (Cant. III, 4). [Vulg. tenui eum, nec dimittam, "I held him, nor will I let him go"] And so that one invites the bridegroom into her garden; this one, asking where he feeds, says to him: 1864A

31.

Annuntia mihi quem dilexit anima mea. Cur quem dilexit anima mea, dicis, et non quem diligit? Cur dimisisti quem tenebas? Dilexisti patrum fide, amisisti incredulitate: tenebas charitatis vinculis, amisisti longo fune perfidiae. Ideo nescis ubi pascat, ubi maneat; nam si scires, non quaereres. Dicis enim: Ubi pascis, ubi manes in meridie? Meridiem nosti esse Ecclesiam quae teneat Christum, et tu eum in noctibus quaeris. Dic, inquit, mihi, Christe, responde vel propter id quia ante carus fuisti atque dilectus mihi; etsi ergo tantae praerogativam charitatis amisi, ut tamen quasi pius, responde mihi, Ubi pascis, ubi manes? Reliquisti me, abiisti ad gentes, recessisti longe a me, et illis propior factus es a quibus longe eras. Sed factus es prope, quia sanguine tuo crediderunt. A me recessisti, quia ego crucem non suscepi 1864But mundi redemptionem, sed ut noxii damnanationem. Qui autem receperunt sicut salutis auctorem, in meridie sunt; illis luces, illis refulges, illis calet gratia tua sicut meridies. Mihi matutinus eras, cum adhuc crederem, sed non credebam plene, quia non sum inventa in meridie, sicut Joseph cum fratribus suis qui tenebant meridiem: illis meridies factus es, qui pascuntur in divitiis tuis, et in te sperant. Ideo ut dixit David: Deduces eorum justitiam tamquam lumen, et judicium eorum tamquam meridiem (Psalm. XXXVI, 6).

31.

Tell me, whom my soul has loved. [Vulg. indica mihi quem diligit anima mea, "show me, whom my soul loves," in the present; the gloss that follows turns on the past tense] Why dost thou say whom my soul has loved, and not "whom she loves"? Why hast thou let go him whom thou didst hold? Thou didst love him by the faith of the fathers, thou hast lost him by unbelief: thou didst hold him by the bonds of charity, thou hast lost him by the long rope of faithlessness. Therefore thou knowest not where he feeds, where he abides; for if thou didst know, thou wouldst not seek. For thou sayest: Where dost thou feed, where dost thou abide at midday? Thou knowest that the midday is the Church which holds Christ, and thou seekest him in the nights. Say to me, she says, O Christ, answer me at least for this cause, that thou wast dear to me before and beloved; therefore, though I have lost the privilege of so great a charity, yet, as one who art merciful, answer me, Where dost thou feed, where dost thou abide? Thou hast forsaken me, thou hast gone away to the nations, thou hast withdrawn far from me, and hast been made nearer to those from whom thou wast far. But thou hast been made near, because they have believed in thy blood. Thou hast withdrawn from me, because I did not receive the cross 1864Bas the redemption of the world, but as the condemnation damnanationem of a guilty man. But they who received it as the author of salvation are at midday; for them thou shinest, for them thou blazest out, for them thy grace is warm as the noonday. To me thou wast the morning, when as yet I believed, but I did not believe fully, because I have not been found at midday, like Joseph with his brothers, who held the midday: for them thou hast been made noonday, who are fed in thy riches and hope in thee. Therefore, as David said: Thou shalt lead forth their justice as the light, and their judgment as the noonday (Psalm. XXXVI, 6). [Vulg. educet quasi lumen iustitiam tuam, et iudicium tuum tamquam meridiem, "he will lead forth thy justice… and thy judgment"]

32.

Quaeris ergo tamquam aliena quae proxima eras, tamquam pauper quae dives fuisti: vis sequi quae praecedebas, atque utinam vel sequaris quos praecedere debuisti! Mercenaria esse desideras quae 1864Cante mercenarios colligebas; mercenariae itaque vox ista dicentis est: Ne 1555 forte fiam circumamicta super greges sodalium tuorum. Quae proselytos ex gentibus ante suscipiebas, nunc ipsa in nationibus vis pro proselytis suscipi, et tamquam advena congregari. Respondit Verbum Dei animae desideranti:

32.

Thou seekest, then, as a stranger, thou who wast a neighbor; as a poor woman, thou who wast rich: thou wishest to follow, thou who didst go before; and would that thou mightest at least follow those whom thou oughtest to have gone before! Thou desirest to be a hireling, thou who 1864Cbefore didst gather hirelings; and so this is the voice of a hireling, saying: Lest 1555 perchance I become one wrapped about, beside the flocks of thy companions. [Vulg. ne vagari incipiam post greges sodalium tuorum, "lest I begin to wander after the flocks"] Thou who before didst receive proselytes out of the nations, now thou thyself among the peoples wishest to be received in the place of proselytes, and to be gathered as a stranger. The Word of God answered the longing soul:

33.
