(Vers. 7.) Nisi cognoscas te decora inter mulieres. Nosce teipsum, homo, tuae animae dicitur, nisi cognoveris te formosam in mulieribus. Cognosce te, anima, quia non de terra, non de luto es, quia insufflavit in te Deus, et fecit in animam viventem. Opus es magnificum, Dei inspiratione formatum: Attende tibi, ut Lex dicit, id est animae tuae: saecularia te et mundana non teneant, terrestria non 1864Dmorentur. Ad illum tota intentione festina, ex cujus inspiratione consistis: Grande, inquit, homo, et pretiosum vir misericors: virum fidelem opus est invenire (Prov. XX, 6). Disce, homo, ubi grandis, atque pretiosus sis. Vilem te terra demonstrat, sed gloriosum virtus facit, fides rarum, imago pretiosum. Ubi namque quidquam tam pretiosum, quam imago est Dei? quae tibi primo fidem debet infundere, ut in corde tuo refulgeat quaedam auctoris effigies; ne qui mentem tuam interrogat, non agnoscat auctorem. An quidquam tam pretiosum, quam humilitas; 1865Aut naturam corporis animaeque perspiciens, altero te subjicias, altero recognoscas. Prona in vitium carnis illecebra misericordiam suadet; quia quod alteri contuleris, tibi solvis: quidquid a te procedit, in te recurrit, et quidquid feceris, tibi solvis: et quidquid profueris, tibi proficit. Vivacis animae vigor, sensus rationis et capax intellectus atque judicii, ut digna domus tanto habitatore videatur, non amittat suae praerogativam naturae. Nisi cognoscas te, decora inter mulieres. Quae quereris quod derelicta sis, nisi te cognoscas, nisi poeniteat te lapsus tui, nisi intentionem devotionis approbes, nisi fides tua et sinceritas augeatur, querela nihil proderit. Aut sic, nisi cognoscas te quia decora es, nisi pulchritudinem naturae tuae serves, et corporis te illecebrae non demergant, 1865Bnec impedimenta detineant, nihil tibi creaturae melioris nobilitas suffragabitur.

33.

(Vers. 7.) Unless thou know thyself, O comely one among women. [Vulg. si ignoras te, o pulcherrima inter mulieres, egredere, "if thou know not thyself, O fairest among women, go forth"] Know thyself, O man: it is said to thy soul, unless thou hast known thyself comely among women. Know thyself, O soul, that thou art not of earth, not of clay, because God breathed into thee and made thee into a living soul. Thou art a magnificent work, formed by the inbreathing of God: Attend to thyself, as the Law says, that is, to thy soul: let not the things of the world and of the age hold thee, let not earthly things 1864Ddelay thee. Hasten with thy whole intention to him by whose inbreathing thou standest: A great thing, he says, is man, and precious is a merciful man: but to find a faithful man is a labor (Prov. XX, 6). [Vulg. multi homines misericordes vocantur; virum autem fidelem quis inveniet?, "many men are called merciful; but who shall find a faithful man?"] Learn, O man, wherein thou art great and precious. The earth shows thee to be vile, but virtue makes thee glorious, faith makes thee rare, the image makes thee precious. For where is anything so precious as the image of God? which ought first to pour faith into thee, so that some likeness of its author may shine again in thy heart; lest he who questions thy mind should not recognize the author. Or is anything so precious as humility; 1865Athat, looking upon the nature of body and of soul, by the one thou shouldst subject thyself, by the other recognize thyself. The enticement of the flesh, prone to vice, urges mercy; because what thou hast bestowed upon another, thou payest to thyself: whatever proceeds from thee runs back into thee, and whatever thou shalt do, thou payest to thyself: and whatever profit thou shalt bring, it profits thee. Let the vigor of the living soul, the perception of reason and the mind capable of understanding and of judgment, so that it may be seen a worthy house for so great a dweller, not lose the privilege of its own nature. Unless thou know thyself, O comely one among women. Thou who complainest that thou hast been forsaken: unless thou know thyself, unless thou repent of thy fall, unless thou approve the intention of thy devotion, unless thy faith and sincerity be increased, thy complaint will profit nothing. Or thus: unless thou know thyself, that thou art comely; unless thou keep the beauty of thy nature, and the enticements of the body do not sink thee down, 1865Bnor hindrances hold thee back, the nobility of a better creature will avail thee nothing.

34.

Cognosce igitur te et naturae tuae decorem, et exi quasi exuta vinculis pedum, et nudo exerta vestigio, ut carnalia integumenta non sentias: vestigium mentis tuae corporalia vincla non implicent, ut pes tuus speciosus appareat. Tales enim sunt, qui ad regnum coelorum annuntiandum eliguntur a Domino, de quibus dictum est: Quam speciosi pedes evangelizantium pacem (Esai. LII, 7). Talis Moyses cui dicitur: Solve calceamentum pedum tuorum (Exod. III, 5); ut qui vocaturus populum ad Dei regnum procederet, prius carnis abjiciens exuvias, nudo spiritu vestigioque mentis incederet; hoc est ergo quod ait: Exi tu in calceamentis gregum, et pasce 1865Chaedos tuos in tabernaculis pastorum.

34.

Know thyself, therefore, and the comeliness of thy nature; and go forth as one stripped of the bonds of the feet, and thrust out with bare footprint, that thou mayest not feel the coverings of the flesh: let not bodily chains entangle the footstep of thy mind, that thy foot may appear shapely. For such are they who are chosen by the Lord to announce the kingdom of heaven, of whom it is said: How shapely are the feet of them that preach the gospel of peace (Esai. LII, 7). Such was Moses, to whom it is said: Loose the shoe of thy feet (Exod. III, 5); [Vulg. Exod. 3:5 solve calciamentum de pedibus tuis, "from thy feet"] so that he who was to go forth to call the people to the kingdom of God should first cast off the hides of the flesh and walk with bare spirit and with the footstep of the mind; this, then, is what he says: Go thou forth in the sandals of the flocks, and feed 1865Cthy kids in the tabernacles of the shepherds. [Vulg. egredere et abi post vestigia gregum, et pasce hedos tuos iuxta tabernacula pastorum, "go after the footsteps of the flocks… beside the tabernacles"]

35.

Per greges regnum intelligimus, eo quod potestatis sit gregibus praesidere. Praesidet autem unusquisque sibi quadam potestate regali, si coerceat in se corporis luxus, et servituti redigat carnem suam; ideoque dictum est: Regnum Dei intra vos est. Unde pulchre ait ad animam: Exi, id est, exi a servitio, exi a carnis imperio atque dominatu, et exi non in carne sed in spiritu, exi ad regimen potestatis. Ideoque addidit: Pasce haedos tuos, hoc est, rege ea quae in sinistra tua sunt; nam si non regantur, facile labuntur. Coerce petulantiam, lasciviam tui corporis et luxuriam irrationabilem, edoma leves motus: pasce eos non in corporeis tabernaculis, sed in tabernaculis pastorum qui regere gregem norunt; sunt enim amabilia tabernacula Israel, sicut nemora obumbrantia super flumen, in quibus anima tamquam 1865Din procinctu bellico sita bonam exercet militiam, incursus explorat hostiles.

35.

By the flocks we understand a kingdom, in that it belongs to power to preside over flocks. And every man presides over himself by a certain royal power, if he restrains in himself the excesses of the body and reduces his flesh to servitude; and therefore it is said: The kingdom of God is within you. Whence beautifully he says to the soul: Go forth, that is, go forth from servitude, go forth from the command and dominion of the flesh, and go forth not in the flesh but in the spirit, go forth to the governance of power. And therefore he added: Feed thy kids, that is, rule those things that are on thy left hand; for if they are not ruled, they easily slip away. Restrain the wantonness, the playfulness of thy body and its unreasoning excess, tame its light motions: feed them not in bodily tabernacles, but in the tabernacles of the shepherds who know how to rule a flock; for the tabernacles of Israel are lovely, like groves overshadowing a river, in which the soul, set as it were 1865Din battle array, exercises a good soldiery and searches out the assaults of the enemy.

36.

Nisi noscas te, decora inter mulieres. Quid est se noscere, nisi ut sciat unusquisque hominem se ad imaginem et similitudinem Dei factum, rationis capacem, qui terram suam excolere tamquam bonus agricola debeat aratro quodam et falce sapientiae; ut vel dura findantur, vel luxuriantia recidantur, qui interiorem sui portionem animi imperio debeat gubernare. 1866AUnde etiam in Lege scriptum est: Attende tibi, ne fiat verbum absconditum in corde tuo (Deut. XV, 9): tibi, inquit, attende, non utique 1556 dicit pecuniae tuae, non possessionibus tuis, non viribus corporis, sed animo tuo ac menti tuae, unde omnia consilia, facta, cogitationesque manant. Tibi ergo attende ibi, ubi potiorem esse te nosti. Nosce teipsum, quod Apollini Pythio assignant gentiles viri, quasi ipse auctor fuerit hujus sententiae; cum de nostro usurpatum ad sua transferant, et longe anterior Moyses fuerit qui scripsit librum Deuteronomii, quam philosophi qui ista finxerunt.

36.

Unless thou know thyself, O comely one among women. What is it to know oneself, but that each man should know that he was made to the image and likeness of God, capable of reason, who ought to till his own earth like a good husbandman with a certain plough and sickle of wisdom, so that either hard things may be split or luxuriant things cut back, and who ought to govern the inward portion of himself by the command of the mind? 1866AWhence also it is written in the Law: Attend to thyself, lest a hidden word be made in thy heart (Deut. XV, 9). [Vulg. Deut. 15:9 cave ne forte subrepat tibi impia cogitatio, "beware lest perchance an impious thought creep upon thee"] Attend to thyself, he says: he certainly does not say 1556 to thy money, not to thy possessions, not to the strength of the body, but to thy spirit and to thy mind, whence all counsels, deeds, and thoughts flow. Attend to thyself, therefore, there, where thou knowest thyself to be the stronger. Know thyself: which the men of the nations assign to Pythian Apollo, as though he were the author of this maxim; whereas they carry over to their own what has been usurped from ours, and Moses, who wrote the book of Deuteronomy, was far earlier than the philosophers who invented these things.

37.

Unde et Salomon oraculum divinum secutus, scripsit in Canticis canticorum; Nisi scias te, decora inter mulieres; hoc est, nisi cognoscas te mortalem 1866Brationalem, et cito tua peccata fatearis, cito dicas iniquitates tuas ut justificeris, nisi convertaris, et prior accuses delicta tua, venit dies mortis et jam nullum conversionis remedium est; praeveniris dum cogitas. Accende facem tuam, priusquam sponsi tibi janua claudatur, qui exspectare diu non solet negligentes. Nisi scias te, inquit, decora inter mulieres; et dicas: Fusca sum et decora: fusca sum, quia peccavi, decora, quia diligor, quia genus sum Abrahae, electum genus, dilectum Deo; nihil tibi proderit patrum gratia: Potens enim Deus est de lapidibus istis suscitare filios Abrahae (Matth. III, 9). Et alibi in Evangelio legisti, quia Abrahae filiam ligavit diabolus nequitiae suae vinculis, quam Dominus solvit in die sabbati. Nisi ergo scias te, nihil 1866Ctibi proderit. Etsi dicas, Abrahae sum filia quae non credis, quae errorem non corrigis; Abraham quidem salvatur, sed te nobilitas generis non juvabit, nisi fides salvaverit. Non te decipiat data patribus repromissio, non suscipio personam hominis, non suscipio praerogativam generis; nisi videro congruentem generi morum nobilitatem, ut fiat justi generis electio.

37.

Whence Solomon also, following the divine oracle, wrote in the Song of Songs: Unless thou know thyself, thou comely one among women [Vulg. Si ignoras te, o pulcherrima inter mulieres, "If thou know not thyself, O fairest among women"]; that is, unless thou know thyself to be mortal 1866Band rational, and quickly confess thy sins, quickly tell thy iniquities that thou mayest be justified — unless thou be converted, and be the first to accuse thy own faults, the day of death comes and there is now no remedy of conversion; thou art forestalled while thou art thinking of it. Kindle thy torch before the bridegroom's door be shut against thee, for he is not wont to wait long for the negligent. Unless thou know thyself, he says, thou comely one among women; and say thou: I am swarthy and comely [Vulg. Cant. 1:4 Nigra sum, sed formosa, "I am black, but beautiful"]: I am swarthy, because I have sinned, comely, because I am loved, because I am of the stock of Abraham, a chosen stock, beloved of God. The grace of the fathers will profit thee nothing: For God is able of these stones to raise up sons to Abraham (Matth. III, 9). And elsewhere in the Gospel thou hast read that the devil bound a daughter of Abraham with the chains of his own wickedness, whom the Lord loosed on the sabbath day. Unless therefore thou know thyself, it will profit thee nothing 1866C. Even if thou say, I am a daughter of Abraham — thou who believest not, who correctest not thine error: Abraham indeed is saved, but nobility of stock will not help thee, unless faith have saved thee. Let not the promise given to the fathers deceive thee; I accept not the person of a man, I accept not the prerogative of stock, unless I have seen a nobility of manners answering to the stock, so that there be an election of a just stock.

38.

Quod si scias te, et cognoscas quia peccato obnoxia es, exeundum in calcaneis gregum est tibi. Exi ergo nuda pede, et pasce haedos tuos in tabernaculis pastorum. Qui sine peccato est, agnos pascit, oves pascit: quae autem peccato obnoxia est, pascat haedos qui a sinistris sunt; non enim potest esse in dextera pastoris boni. Sequatur magis dicentem Petro: Veni retro me, Satana (Marc. VIII, 33). Sequendo Petrus meruit in dextera collocari; ideoque 1866Dei dicitur: Pasce agniculos meos (Joan. XXI, 16). Sed quo exeat audi: Ad tabernacula, inquit, pastorum, hoc est, ad gentes, ad dispersionem. Ideoque dixit: Percutiam pastorem, et dispergentur oves gregis (Zach. XIII, 7); ut repleatur totus orbis terrarum grege Christi, significans tunc fore salvam Synagogam, cum se huic dispersioni et ipsa sociaverit, quae replevit hunc mundum; tabernacula enim pastorum sunt regna terrae. 1867A

38.

But if thou know thyself, and know that thou art liable to sin, thou must go out among the heels of the flocks. Go out therefore with bare foot, and feed thy kids in the tents of the shepherds. He who is without sin feeds lambs, feeds sheep; but she who is liable to sin, let her feed the kids which are on the left; for she cannot be at the right hand of the good shepherd. Let her rather follow him who says to Peter: Come behind me, Satan (Marc. VIII, 33) [Vulg. Vade retro me, Satana, "Go behind me"]. By following, Peter deserved to be set at the right hand; and therefore 1866Dit is said to him: Feed my little lambs (Joan. XXI, 16) [Vulg. agnos, "lambs" — without the diminutive]. But hear whither she is to go out: To the tents, he says, of the shepherds, that is, to the nations, to the dispersion. And therefore he said: I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock shall be scattered (Zach. XIII, 7) [Vulg. Zach. Percute pastorem, et dispergentur oves, "Strike thou the shepherd, and the sheep shall be scattered"]; that the whole round of the earth might be filled with the flock of Christ — signifying that the Synagogue would then be safe, when she too shall have joined herself to this dispersion which has filled this world; for the tents of the shepherds are the kingdoms of the earth. 1867A

39.

(Vers. 8.) Equae meae in curribus Pharaonis assimilavi te, proxima mea. In quo dum velocium equorum cursui similitudo Ecclesiae comparari videtur, aestimatur dives ejus gratia. Non igitur unam equam divitis regis intelligas, sed absoluto pro equitatu, ut saepe diximus, qualis ovis illius est, pro grege ovium. Pharao igitur ut potentissimus rex et praedives habebat equos potentes; unde et Scriptura dicit: Currus Pharaonis (Exod. XIV, 9), quasi potentissimum genus ad bellorum usum, et subsidium praeliandi: denique his curribus Pharao fugientes Hebraeos facile comprehendit. Sicut ergo equi juncti facile trahunt currum, et patienter suscipiunt jugum, et portant cum gratia, et jugi illius susceptione mansuescunt: sic et congregatio nationum 1867Bgentilibus indomita moribus se ante jactabat. Ubi vero suscepit jugum dicentis: Tollite jugum meum super vos, quia leve est; et onus meum, quia suave est (Matth. XI, 29 et 30); et coepit sponsa Christi concordia et mansuetudine populorum esse sublimis, et toto circumferri orbe, tamquam currus equis velocibus supra mundum rapta ascendit ad sponsum.

39.

(Vers. 8.) To my mare among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee, my neighbor [Vulg. Equitatui meo in curribus Pharaonis assimilavi te, amica mea, "To my cavalry… my friend"]. In that the likeness of the Church seems here to be compared to the running of swift horses, the richness of her grace is reckoned. Do not therefore understand one mare of the rich king, but a word put absolutely for the cavalry, as we have often said — of what sort that sheep of his is, put for the flock of sheep. Pharaoh therefore, as a most powerful and most wealthy king, had powerful horses; whence Scripture also says: The chariots of Pharaoh (Exod. XIV, 9), as being a most powerful kind for the use of wars and a support in fighting: and indeed with these chariots Pharaoh easily overtook the fleeing Hebrews. As therefore horses yoked together easily draw the chariot, and patiently take up the yoke, and bear it with grace, and by the taking up of that yoke grow gentle: so also the congregation of the nations, 1867Buntamed in its heathen manners, once vaunted itself. But when it took up the yoke of him who says: Take my yoke upon you, for it is light; and my burden, for it is sweet (Matth. XI, 29 et 30) [Vulg. jugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve, "my yoke is sweet and my burden light" — the two epithets exchanged]; and the bride of Christ began to be exalted by the concord and gentleness of the peoples, and to be carried about the whole world, then, like a chariot caught up above the world by swift horses, she went up to the bridegroom.

40.

Habet enim equos suos Christus, de quibus dicit propheta: Immisisti in mare equos tuos, turbantes aquas multas (Habac. III); eo quod gentium populos qui moventur ut aquae multae, et excitantur in fluctus aquarum, evangelizando commoverint apostoli, ut assurgentes a terrenis idolorum ceremoniis, in Christum crederent. Et supra ait: Ascendisti 1867Cin equos tuos, equitatus tuus sanitas. O bonorum equorum duo decajugum mirabile, quibus frena pacis, 1557 habenae sunt charitatis: constricti inter se concordiae vinculis, et jugo fidei subjecti, quatuor rotis Evangelii mysterium totius orbis finibus invehentes, bonum aurigam portantes Dei Verbum: cujus flagello fugatae sunt illecebrae saeculares, exterminatus mundi istius princeps, justorum cursus impletus est. O rationabilium equorum grande certamen! o mirandum mysterium! Rota intra rotam currebat, et non impediebatur, novum Testamentum in veteri Testamento, intra illud currebat per quod annuntiabatur. In quatuor partes ibant, rotae, et non revertebantur retrorsum; quia spiritus vitae erat in his, qui currebat in quatuor partes 1867Dtotius mundi: et sine offensione currebat, quia bona equorum vita quadrabat. Currebant igitur equi, quia non dormiebat qui ascenderat equos.

40.

For Christ has his own horses, of whom the prophet says: Thou hast sent thy horses into the sea, troubling many waters (Habac. III) [Vulg. Hab. 3:15 Viam fecisti in mari equis tuis, in luto aquarum multarum, "Thou madest a way in the sea for thy horses"]; in that the apostles, by preaching the Gospel, stirred up the peoples of the nations, who are moved like many waters and are roused into billows of waters, so that, rising up from the earthly ceremonies of idols, they might believe in Christ. And above he says: Thou hast mounted 1867Cupon thy horses, thy cavalry is health [Vulg. Hab. 3:8 quadrigae tuae salvatio, "thy chariots are salvation"]. O marvellous duo decajugum of good horses, for whom the bits are of peace, 1557 the reins are of charity: bound fast among themselves by the bonds of concord, and subjected to the yoke of faith, carrying in upon four wheels the mystery of the Gospel to the bounds of the whole world, bearing the good charioteer, the Word of God: by whose whip the enticements of the world were put to flight, the prince of this world driven out, the course of the just fulfilled. O great contest of rational horses! O mystery to be wondered at! A wheel ran within a wheel, and was not hindered: the New Testament within the Old Testament, it ran within that through which it was announced. Into four parts the wheels went, and they turned not backward; because the spirit of life was in them, which ran into the four parts 1867Dof the whole world: and it ran without stumbling, because the good life of the horses squared with it. The horses therefore ran, because he who had mounted the horses was not sleeping.

41.

Animarum igitur nostrarum auriga Jesus; qui nos quoque vult ascendere equos nostros, id est, corpora nostra; et vigilare semper ne dicatur nobis: Dormitaverunt qui ascenderunt equos (Psalm. LXXV, 7). Transeundum est hoc mare impigre, vix transitur a vigilantibus. Si quis autem dormierit, transire non poterit, sed demergitur, ut Aegyptius cujus et anima et corpus interiit. Equum enim et ascensorem projecit 1868Ain mare (Exod. XV, 1), qui non sequebantur legem, sed per sequebantur.

41.

The charioteer of our souls, therefore, is Jesus; who wills that we too should mount our own horses, that is, our bodies, and always be watchful, lest it be said of us: They have slumbered who mounted horses (Psalm. LXXV, 7). This sea must be crossed briskly; it is scarcely crossed even by the watchful. But if any man shall have slept, he will not be able to cross, but is drowned, like the Egyptian, whose soul and body alike perished. For the horse and the rider he cast 1868Ainto the sea (Exod. XV, 1) [Vulg. dejecit, "cast down"], who did not follow the law, but per sequebantur.

42.

Equae meae in curribus Pharaonis assimilavi te. Virtutis labor quaerit victoriam, ut non possit equae illi quae Salomonis est comparari, velox ad currendum, habilis ad partum; quoniam fecunditas animae desideratur et quaeritur. Haec ergo equa pretiosa est, et currus Pharaonis veloces. Sed hoc loco de anima dicendum suscepimus, hujus equae quae similis aestimatur huic animae, hoc est, propheticae vel apostolicae virtutis: quod in eorum annumeretur grege, qui fecunditate praedicationis suae totius orbis terrarum spatia repleverunt. Et quamvis in corpore constituti, nulla tamen cursus spiritalis sensere dispendia. Ideoque laudatur quod coelesti sibi illuminante 1868Bpraecepto, jam speciosa, jam pulchra sit quae vultu praeferat castitatis decorem, et redimicula cervicis tollat, in qua sunt patientiae et humilitatis insignia. Atque idcirco sponsam suam collaudans dicebat:

42.

To my mare among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee. The labour of virtue seeks victory, so that she cannot be compared to that mare which is Solomon's, swift for running, apt for bearing young; since the fruitfulness of the soul is desired and sought. This mare therefore is precious, and the chariots of Pharaoh are swift. But in this place we have undertaken to speak of the soul, of this mare who is reckoned like to this soul, that is, of prophetic or apostolic virtue: that she be numbered in the flock of those who by the fruitfulness of their preaching have filled the spaces of the whole round of the earth. And although set in the body, yet they felt no losses of their spiritual course. And therefore she is praised, that, the heavenly precept enlightening her, 1868Bshe is now shapely, now beautiful, who bears in her countenance the grace of chastity, and lifts up the necklaces of her neck, in which are the marks of patience and humility. And on this account, praising his bride, he said:

43.

(Vers. 9.) Quam speciosae factae sunt genae tuae sicut turturis, cervix tua sicut redimicula. Vultus liberior est, ubi est charitatis conscientia; et portare jugum Christi suave est, si ornamenta putes cervicis tuae esse, non onera. Attolle ergo oculos semper ad Dominum tuum, et quaere Deum, ut invenias. Erige cervicem, redimicula non vincula geris: mutaque animalia redimiculis gaudent, et phalerari sibi magis, quam nodari videntur. Genae sicut turturis, perferant insignia verecundiae: Redimicula cervicis libertatis attollant fiduciam; leve est enim jugum 1868CChristi, et ideo cervix eo non premitur, sed levatur.

43.

(Vers. 9.) How shapely are thy cheeks become, as a turtle-dove's, thy neck as necklaces [Vulg. Pulchrae sunt genae tuae sicut turturis; collum tuum sicut monilia, "Beautiful are thy cheeks… thy neck as jewels"]. The countenance is freer where there is the consciousness of charity; and to bear the yoke of Christ is sweet, if thou reckon them to be the ornaments of thy neck, not its burdens. Lift up therefore thine eyes always to thy Lord, and seek God, that thou mayest find him. Raise up thy neck: thou wearest necklaces, not chains: even dumb beasts rejoice in trappings, and seem to themselves rather to be adorned than to be tied. Cheeks as a turtle-dove's: let them bear the marks of modesty; the necklaces of the neck: let them lift up the confidence of freedom; for the yoke of Christ is light, 1868Cand therefore the neck is not pressed down by it, but lifted up.

44.

(Vers. 10, 11.) Similitudines, inquit, auri faciemus tibi, ex distinctionibus argenti, quoad usque rex erit in declinatione sua. Ex iis enim qui ex Lege sunt et prophetis, mediocriter ante crediderant gloriam Domini Jesu, et haereditas ejus diffusa per populos, quo frequentius examinata, hoc amplius est probata. Crebrae enim persecutiones Ecclesiae justorum nobis titulos, martyrii victorias ediderunt. Itaque sicut aurum bonum, ira Ecclesia cum utitur, detrimenta non sentit, sed magis fulgor ejus augetur, donec Christus veniat in regnum suum, et reclinet caput suum in Ecclesiae fide. Qui cum venisset ad oves perditas domus Israel, non habebat ubi caput suum reclinaret: nunc autem jam redolet fides, ideoque 1868Ddicit Ecclesia: Nardus mea dedit odorem suum; et dicit cum praesumptione exspectans retributionem. Fragrat unguentum gratiae, in quo virgo generavit, et Dominus Jesus sacramentum incarnationis assumpsit.

44.

(Vers. 10, 11.) Likenesses, he says, of gold will we make for thee, out of distinctions of silver, until the king shall be in his reclining [Vulg. Murenulas aureas faciemus tibi, vermiculatas argento. Dum esset rex in accubitu suo, "Golden chains will we make thee, inlaid with silver. While the king was at his couch"]. For out of those who are of the Law and of the prophets, they had before believed but moderately in the glory of the Lord Jesus; and his inheritance, spread abroad through the peoples, the more often it was tried, so much the more was it approved. For the frequent persecutions of the Church have brought forth for us the titles of the just, the victories of martyrdom. And so, like good gold: when the Church makes use of wrath, it feels no losses, but rather its brightness is increased, until Christ come into his kingdom, and lay back his head upon the faith of the Church. He, when he had come to the lost sheep of the house of Israel, had not where to lay back his head: but now faith is already fragrant, and therefore 1868Dthe Church says: My spikenard has given its odour; and she says it, awaiting her recompense with confidence. The ointment of grace breathes forth, in which the Virgin brought forth, and the Lord Jesus took up the sacrament of the incarnation.

45.

(Vers. 12, 13.) Colligatio guttae frater meus mihi, in medio uberum meorum manebit, sive commorabitur. Prius quidem dixerat, quod nardus sua dedisset ei odorem sponsi; et quia per unguentum quo unxerat, odoris ejus fragrantiam recepisset: nunc autem ait: Frater meus mihi guttam redolet, et hanc non diffusam neque ut libet dispersam, sed 1869Acolligatam et constrictam, quo scilicet odor ipsius suavitatis densior reddatur et vehementior. Et hic, inquit, 1558 cum talis sit, in medio uberum meorum commoratur et commanet, et requiem ac mansionem suam facit in loco pectoris mei. Colligatio, inquit, guttae consobrinus meus, botrus cypri frater meus mihi, inter media ubera mea requiescet. Corpus enim suscipiens Dominus Jesus, charitatis se vinculis illigavit, et non solum se membris nostris et passionibus naturalibus, sed etiam cruci junxit; ideoque sicut botrus in Ecclesiae fide, et morali requiescit gratia.

45.

(Vers. 12, 13.) A binding-together of the drop is my brother to me; in the midst of my breasts he shall remain, or shall dwell [Vulg. Fasciculus myrrhae dilectus meus mihi, inter ubera mea commorabitur, "A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall abide between my breasts"]. She had indeed said before that her spikenard had given her the odour of the bridegroom, and that through the ointment with which she had anointed him she had received the fragrance of his odour: but now she says: My brother smells to me of the drop, and this not poured abroad nor scattered as it may chance, but 1869Abound together and drawn tight, so namely that the odour of its own sweetness may be rendered denser and stronger. And he, she says, 1558 being such as he is, dwells and abides in the midst of my breasts, and makes his rest and his mansion in the place of my bosom. A binding-together of the drop is my cousin, a cluster of cypress is my brother to me; between the midst of my breasts he shall rest. For the Lord Jesus, taking a body, bound himself with the chains of charity, and joined himself not only to our members and to natural sufferings, but even to the cross; and therefore, like a cluster, he rests in the faith of the Church and in her moral grace.

46.

Nardus cypri consobrinus meus in vineis Engaddi. Si locum quaerimus regionis cujusdam quae in Judaea est, locus sic dicitur in quo opobalsamum gignitur: si interpretationem, tentatio Latine significatur. 1869BIn illis vero vineis lignum est, quod si quis compungat, unguentum emittit, hic fructus est ligni. Si non incidatur lignum, non ita fragrat et redolet, cum autem compunctum fuerit artificis manu, tunc lacrymam distillat. Sicut et Christus in illo tentationis ligno crucifixus, illacrymabat populum, ut peccata nostra dilueret, et de visceribus misericordiae suae fundebat unguentum dicens: Pater, dimitte illis, quia nesciunt quid faciunt (Luc. XXIII, 34). Tunc ergo in ligno compunctus lancea, exivit de eo sanguis et aqua, omni unguento suavior, accepta Deo hostia, per totum mundum odorem sanctificationis effundens. Et quasi balsamum ex arbore, sic virtus exibat e corpore, unde ait: Sentio virtutem de me exiisse 1869C(Luc. VIII, 46). Inde opobalsamum expressius dicitur, eo quod compunctione ligni balsamum per cavernam punctionis erumpit.

46.

Spikenard of cypress is my cousin in the vineyards of Engaddi [Vulg. Botrus cypri dilectus meus mihi, in vineis Engaddi, "A cluster of cypress is my beloved to me"]. If we seek the place of a certain region which is in Judaea, that place is so called in which the balsam-sap is produced: if we seek the interpretation, in Latin it signifies temptation. 1869BBut in those vineyards there is a wood which, if one prick it, sends forth ointment; this is the fruit of the wood. If the wood be not cut, it does not so give fragrance and odour; but when it has been pricked by the craftsman's hand, then it distils a tear. So also Christ, crucified upon that wood of temptation, wept over the people, that he might wash away our sins, and out of the bowels of his mercy poured forth ointment, saying: Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do (Luc. XXIII, 34) [Vulg. non enim sciunt quid faciunt, "for they know not"]. Then therefore, pricked upon the wood with the lance, there went out from him blood and water, sweeter than any ointment, a victim acceptable to God, pouring out through the whole world the odour of sanctification. And as balsam out of the tree, so virtue went forth out of his body, whence he says: I perceive that virtue has gone out from me 1869C(Luc. VIII, 46) [Vulg. nam ego novi virtutem de me exiisse, "for I know that virtue has gone out from me"]. Thence it is called more expressly opobalsamum, in that by the pricking of the wood the balsam bursts out through the hollow of the puncture.

47.

Compunctus igitur Jesus, odorem remissionis peccatorum et redemptionis effudit. Nam et constrictus erat, cum Verbum esset homo factus: et pauper factus est, cum dives esset; ut nos illius inopia ditaremur: potens erat, et despiciendum se praebuit, ita ut Herodes sperneret eum et illuderet ei: terram movebat, et haerebat in ligno: coelum obducebat tenebris, et mundum crucifigebat, et crucifixus erat: inclinabat caput, et exibat Verbum: exinanitus erat, et replebat omnia: descendit Deus, ascendit homo: Verbum caro factum est, ut caro sibi Verbi solium in Dei dextera vindicaret: vulnus erat et 1869Dfluebat unguentum: scarabaeus audiebatur, et Deus agnoscebatur. Respondit et Christus.

47.

Jesus therefore, being pricked, poured forth the odour of the remission of sins and of redemption. For he was also drawn tight, when the Word was made man: and he was made poor, when he was rich, that we might be enriched by his want: he was powerful, and offered himself to be despised, so that Herod scorned him and mocked him: he moved the earth, and clung to the wood: he covered heaven with darkness, and crucified the world, and was crucified: he bowed his head, and the Word went forth: he was emptied out, and filled all things: God descended, man ascended: the Word was made flesh, that the flesh might claim for itself the throne of the Word at the right hand of God: he was a wound, and 1869Dointment flowed: he was heard as a beetle, and was acknowledged as God. And Christ answered.

48.

(Vers. 14.) Ecce bona es, proxima mea, ecce es bona: oculi tui columbae. Quoniam cognoverat Ecclesia mysterium, et pro totius mundi redemptione crucifixum Dominum Jesum praedicabat, meretur audire: Ecce es bona, quae me bonum dicis; Ecce es bona, quae vidisti gloriae meae decorem, et ipsa speciosa es et decora. Quid est autem dicere Christum: Bona es, vel decora es, nisi illud Evangelium: Constans esto, filia, remissa sunt tibi peccata tua (Matth. IX, 22)? Ergo cui Christus peccata donavit, 1870Arecte dicit: Retribue servo tuo, ut vivam, et custodiam mandata tua (Psal. CXVIII, 146). Non habet in retributione quid desperet; quia Dominus Jesus venit mundum salvare, non perdere. Ideo immemor est injuriae, memor gratiae, sicut in prophetico libro testificatur ipse dicens: Ego sum, ego sum qui deleo iniquitates tuas, et memor non ero: tu autem memor esto et judicemur, dic tu prius iniquitates tuas ut justificeris (Esai. XLIII, 25 et 26). Quicumque ergo dicit iniquitates suas Deo, justificatur: et quicumque justificatur, retributionem non timet, sed exposcit: qui retributionem non timet, vivit.

48.

(Vers. 14.) Behold thou art good, my neighbor, behold thou art good: thine eyes are a dove's [Vulg. Ecce tu pulchra es, amica mea, ecce tu pulchra es; oculi tui columbarum, "Behold thou art beautiful, my friend… thine eyes are doves' eyes"]. Because the Church had known the mystery, and was preaching the Lord Jesus crucified for the redemption of the whole world, she deserves to hear: Behold thou art good, thou who callest me good; Behold thou art good, thou who hast seen the comeliness of my glory, and art thyself shapely and comely. But what is it for Christ to say: Thou art good, or Thou art comely, if not that word of the Gospel: Be steadfast, daughter, thy sins are forgiven thee (Matth. IX, 22) [Vulg. Matt. 9:22 Confide, filia, fides tua te salvam fecit, "Have confidence, daughter, thy faith hath made thee whole"; the clause on forgiveness stands at Matt. 9:2]? Therefore he to whom Christ has forgiven sins 1870Arightly says: Reward thy servant, that I may live and keep thy commandments (Psal. CXVIII, 146) [Vulg. Ps. 118:17 Retribue servo tuo, vivifica me, et custodiam sermones tuos, "quicken me, and I will keep thy words"]. He has nothing in the recompense whereof to despair; because the Lord Jesus came to save the world, not to destroy it. Therefore he is unmindful of injury, mindful of grace, as he himself bears witness in the prophetic book, saying: I am, I am he who blot out thine iniquities, and I will not be mindful of them: but be thou mindful, and let us be judged; tell thou first thine iniquities, that thou mayest be justified (Esai. XLIII, 25 et 26) [Vulg. Ego sum, ego sum ipse qui deleo iniquitates tuas propter me, et peccatorum tuorum non recordabor. Reduc me in memoriam, et judicemur simul: narra si quid habes ut justificeris]. Whosoever therefore tells his iniquities to God is justified: and whosoever is justified fears not the recompense, but demands it: he who fears not the recompense, lives.

49.

(Vers. 15, 16.) Ecce, inquit, formosus consobrinus meus, et quidem pulcher. Acclinatio nostra opaca, trabes domorum nostrarum cedri, lacunaria nostra 1870Bcupressi. Laudat sponsi decorem Ecclesia, quem plus unusquisque tacito laudat affectu, et fidus mysteriorum interpres tacendo plus praedicat. Nam quae arcana divulgat, imminuit Christi decorem; ideo nemo mittat margaritas suas porcis, ne conculcari faciat ornamenta pretiosa. Non ergo in tabernaculo loquacis et garruli; quia multiloquio peccatum admittitur: sed in viri serii, qui sit parcus alloquii, nec sermonis intemperans, et verborum sobrietate temulentiam loquacitatis evitans, caput suum Christus reclinat.

49.

(Vers. 15, 16.) Behold, she says, my cousin is well-formed, and indeed beautiful. Our reclining-place is shady, the beams of our houses are cedars, our panelled ceilings 1870Bcypresses [Vulg. Ecce tu pulcher es, dilecte mi, et decorus. Lectulus noster floridus; tigna domorum nostrarum cedrina, laquearia nostra cypressina, "Behold thou art beautiful, my beloved, and comely. Our little bed is flowery"]. The Church praises the comeliness of the bridegroom, whom each one praises the more with silent affection; and the faithful interpreter of the mysteries preaches the more by keeping silence. For she who divulges secret things diminishes the comeliness of Christ; therefore let no one cast his pearls before swine, lest he cause precious ornaments to be trodden down. Not then in the tent of the talkative and the chattering — because by much speaking sin is admitted — but in that of a serious man, who is sparing of address, and not intemperate of speech, and by the sobriety of his words avoids the drunkenness of talkativeness, does Christ lay back his head.

50.

Et merito opacam significat reclinationem; quia in Ecclesia constitutos virtus obumbrat Altissimi. Hac umbra David protegi postulabat, ne eum per diem sol ureret, vel luna per noctem (Psalm. 1870CCXX, 6). Hanc umbram spiritualis ministrat 1559 gratia, torrida saeculi hujus et mundi aestiva fugientibus. Opaca igitur acclinatio Christi et Ecclesiae, quibus Dei Patris aeterna illa requies aspirat. In hac ergo requiescamus umbra, peccatorum nostrorum aestibus fatigati si quos adussit libido, hos Domini crux refrigeret, in qua se reclinavit, ut nostra debita susciperet. Si quos culpa lassavit, hos Jesus gremio excipiat suo, et molli foveat amplexu; unde audeo dicere, quod caro Christi acclinatio est Ecclesiae.

50.

And with reason does she signify a shady reclining-place; because the virtue of the Most High overshadows those who are set in the Church. In this shadow David asked to be protected, lest the sun burn him by day, or the moon by night (Psalm. 1870CCXX, 6). This shadow spiritual grace 1559 ministers to those who flee the parched and summer heats of this age and of the world. Shady therefore is the reclining-place of Christ and of the Church, upon whom that eternal rest of God the Father breathes. In this shadow therefore let us rest, wearied by the heats of our sins: whomsoever lust has scorched, let the cross of the Lord refresh them, upon which he laid himself back, that he might take up our debts. Whomsoever guilt has wearied, let Jesus receive them in his bosom, and cherish them with a soft embrace; whence I dare to say that the flesh of Christ is the reclining-place of the Church.

51.

Trabes domorum nostrarum cedri. Cedri specie, majorum qui justi fuerunt, gloria designatur. Justus enim ut palma florebit, et sicut cedrus quae in Libano est, multiplicabitur (Psalm. XCI, 13); sicut enim cedrus 1870Dnon putrescit, ita nec majorum gloria ulla vetustate corrumpitur.

51.

The beams of our houses are cedars. Under the figure of the cedar the glory of those elders who were just is designated. For the just shall flourish like the palm tree, and like the cedar which is in Lebanon he shall be multiplied (Psalm. XCI, 13) [Vulg. sicut cedrus Libani multiplicabitur, "like the cedar of Lebanon"]; for as the cedar 1870Ddoes not rot, so neither is the glory of the elders corrupted by any old age.

52.

Lacunaria nostra cupressi. Genus hoc arboris numquam amittit viriditatem suam, hieme juxta atque aestate comam pascit, nec diverso colore mutatur. Solam hanc arborem ventus numquam suo honore despoliat, sola numquam veteri exuitur amictu, aut novo flore vestitur. Sic apostolica quoque gratia nescit defectum, sed vetustate sui floret. Anima ergo corrumpi nescit, quae florentibus membris viget, semper justitiae caeterarumque virtutum culmina patienti 1871Amagnanimitate sustentat; et ideo non defluit, neque deficit; quia nihil in ea rimosum est ac remissum, nihil mobile, nihil lubricum, nihil quod vitio sermonis ex ea possit effundi. Sequitur Ecclesia vel fidelis anima, animam dico, ideo quia nihil tam est homo, quam anima.

52.

Our panelled ceilings cypresses. This kind of tree never loses its greenness; in winter as in summer it feeds its foliage, nor is it changed with a different colour. This tree alone the wind never despoils of its honour; it alone is never stripped of its old cloak, nor clothed with a new blossom. So too apostolic grace knows no failing, but flourishes by its own age. The soul therefore knows not how to be corrupted, which is vigorous with flourishing members, and always upholds with patient greatness of mind the summits of justice and of the other virtues; 1871Aand therefore it neither flows away nor fails, because there is nothing in it chinked or slack, nothing shifting, nothing slippery, nothing which could be poured out of it by a fault of speech. There follows the Church, or the faithful soul — the soul, I say, because man is nothing so much as soul.

CAPUT SECUNDUM.

CHAPTER THE SECOND.

1.

(Vers. 1, 2.) Ego flos campi, et lilium convallium, sicut lilium in medio spinarum; in totam enim terram fides populi credentis exivit, et in spatioso posuit pedes suos Christus; et ideo pulchre ait florem se esse campi. Flos erat etiam Paulus qui dicebat: Bonus odor Christi sumus Deo (II Cor. II, 15). Et vere flos, qui poterat nova et vetera de sui cordis proferre thesauro. Ego flos campi. Et bene flos est 1871BEcclesia quae fructum annuntiat, hoc est, Dominum Jesum Christum, de quo dictum est Mariae: Benedicta tu inter mulieres, et benedictus fructus ventris tui (Luc. I, 42).

1.

(Vers. 1, 2.) I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys, as a lily in the midst of thorns [Vulg. Sicut lilium inter spinas, "As a lily among thorns"]; for into the whole earth the faith of the believing people has gone out, and Christ has set his feet in a spacious place; and therefore he beautifully says that he is the flower of the field. A flower too was Paul, who said: We are the good odour of Christ unto God (II Cor. II, 15). And truly a flower, who could bring forth new things and old out of the treasure of his own heart. I am the flower of the field. And well is 1871Bthe Church a flower, who announces the fruit, that is, the Lord Jesus Christ, of whom it was said to Mary: Blessed art thou among women, and blessed is the fruit of thy womb (Luc. I, 42).

2.

Alio quoque sensu florem tibi demonstro carpendum, illum utique qui dixit: Ego flos campi, et lilium convallium. Plerique comparationem hanc secundum naturam floris, et usum putant propositi germinis aptiorem; eo quod lilia nullius annui usus cultum requirant, nec sicut erga proventum fructuum caeterorum, ita etiam erga generationem istius floris, sollicitis in orbem redeat labor actus agricolis; quibuslibet enim siccitatibus agri, omne illud quod alitur in florem quadam virtute genitali ex sese, atque in se succi semper virentis animatur; ita ut cum 1871Cfoliorum puberum caulem videas aruisse, floris tamen natura virescat; absconditur enim viriditas, non amittitur. Ast ubi vernis fuerit provocata blanditiis, indumentum germinis, comam floris, liliive resumit ornatus.

2.

In another sense also I show thee a flower to be plucked, that one surely who said: I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys. Very many think this comparison more apt according to the nature of the flower and the use of the shoot set before us; in that lilies require no tending of any yearly use, nor, as in the case of the yield of the other fruits, does the labour of the year come round again in its circuit for anxious husbandmen with regard to the generation of this flower; for whatever the droughts of the field, all that which is nourished into flower is quickened out of itself by a certain generative power, and in itself by a sap always green; so that when 1871Cthou seest the stalk of its grown leaves to have withered, yet the nature of the flower is green; for the greenness is hidden, not lost. But when it has been called forth by the caresses of spring, it takes up again the clothing of the shoot, the foliage of the flower, or the adornments of the lily.

3.

Illud etiam delectat advertere, quia non in asperitatibus montium incultisque silvarum lilia, sed in hortorum amoenitate generantur. Sunt enim horti quidam diversarum pomiferi virtutum, juxta quod scriptum est: Hortus conclusus, etc. (Cant. IV, 12), eo quod ubi integritas, ubi castitas, ubi religio, ubi fida silentia secretorum; ibi claritas angelorum est, ibi confessorum violae, lilia virginum, rosae martyrum sunt. Bene lilium Christus est, quia ubi martyrum sanguis, ibi Christus qui est flos sublimis, 1871Dimmaculatus, innoxius, in quo non spinarum offendat asperitas, sed gratia circumfusa clarebat. Sunt enim spinae rosarum, quae sunt tormenta martyrum; non habet spinas inoffensa divinitas, quae tormenta non sensit.

3.

It delights one also to notice this, that lilies are not begotten in the roughnesses of mountains and the uncultivated places of woods, but in the pleasantness of gardens. For there are certain gardens fruitful with divers virtues, according to that which is written: A garden enclosed, etc. (Cant. IV, 12), in that where there is integrity, where chastity, where religion, where the faithful silences of secret things, there is the brightness of angels, there are the violets of confessors, the lilies of virgins, the roses of martyrs. Well is Christ a lily, because where the blood of the martyrs is, there is Christ, who is the flower on high, 1871Dunspotted, harmless, in whom the roughness of thorns gives no offence, but the grace poured about him was shining. For the thorns of the roses are the torments of the martyrs; the unoffended Godhead, which felt no torments, has no thorns.

4.

Tamquam lilium in medio spinarum. Quod est evidentis indicii spiritalium nequitiarum sentibus virtutem obsideri, unde fructum nemo referat, nisi qui cautus accedat. Sume igitur alas virgo, sed spiritus: ut supervoles vitia, si contingere cupis Christum, 1872AQui in altis habitat, et humilia respicit (Psalm. CXII, 5). Et species ejus sicut cedrus Libani, quae comam nubibus, radicem terris inserit; principium enim ejus de coelo, posteriora ejus in terris, 1560 fructus coelo proximos ediderunt. Scrutare diligentius tam bonum florem, necubi eum in pectoris tui convalle reperias; humilibus enim frequenter inhalatur. Amat generari in hortis, in quibus cum Susanna dum deambularet, invenit: mori potius quam violari parata.

4.

As a lily in the midst of thorns. Which is of plain evidence, that virtue is beset by the brambles of spiritual wickednesses, whence no man may carry off fruit save he who comes warily. Take wings therefore, O virgin, but wings of the spirit, that thou mayest fly above the vices, if thou desirest to touch Christ, 1872AWho dwelleth on high, and beholdeth lowly things (Psalm. CXII, 5). And his form is like the cedar of Lebanon, which sets its foliage in the clouds, its root in the earth; for his beginning is from heaven, his latter parts on earth, 1560 and they have brought forth fruits nearest to heaven. Search out more diligently so good a flower, to see whether thou mayest find him anywhere in the valley of thy breast; for he is often breathed upon lowly things. He loves to be begotten in gardens, in which Susanna, while she walked, found him: ready rather to die than to be violated.

5.

Aliter, Ego flos campi, et lilium convallium, tamquam lilium in medio spinarum. Ecce alium locum in quo solet Dominus conversari, immo non in uno, sed in pluribus: Ego, inquit, flos campi; quia patentem simplicitatem purae mentis frequentat. 1872BEt lilium convallium; flos enim humilitatis est Christus, non luxuriae, non voluptatum atque lasciviae, sed flos simplicitatis, flos humilitatis. Flos odorem suum et succisus reservat, et contritus accumulat, nec avulsus amittit; ita et Dominus Jesus in illo patibulo crucis nec contritus emarcuit, nec avulsus evanuit, et illa lanceae punctione succisus, sacro speciosior fusi cruoris colore vernavit, mori ipse nescius, et mortuis aeternae vitae munus exhalans.

5.

Otherwise: I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys, as a lily in the midst of thorns. Behold another place in which the Lord is wont to abide — nay, not in one, but in many: I, he says, am the flower of the field; because he frequents the open simplicity of a pure mind. 1872BAnd the lily of the valleys; for Christ is the flower of humility, not of luxury, not of pleasures and wantonness, but the flower of simplicity, the flower of humility. A flower keeps its odour even when cut down, and heaps it up when bruised, nor does it lose it when plucked away; so also the Lord Jesus, upon that gibbet of the cross, neither withered when bruised, nor vanished when plucked away, and, cut by that pricking of the lance, he blossomed the more shapely with the sacred colour of the blood poured out, himself not knowing how to die, and breathing out upon the dead the gift of eternal life.

6.

(Vers. 3.) Tamquam lilium in medio spinarum, nonne inter asperitates laborum contritionesque animorum boni flos odoris assurgit; quia contrito corde Deus placatur. Hoc est desertum, filiae, quod ducit ad regnum, hoc est etiam desertum, quod 1872Cfloret ut lilium, juxta illud quod scriptum est: Laetare, sterilis, et exsulta, desertum, et floreat ut lilium (Esai. XXXV, 1). In hoc deserto, filiae, arbor illa bona fructifera quae bonos fructus facit, incipit brachia factorum suorum diffundere, verticem divinitatis attollere, juxta quam nostrae ligna silvae frutescant; quia sicut arbor mali inter ligna silvae, ita frater meus in medio filiorum; ut videns hoc Ecclesia, et fidei nostrae jam laeta successu, dicat: In umbra ejus concupivi et sedi, et fructus ejus dulcis in faucibus meis.

6.

(Vers. 3.) As a lily in the midst of thorns — does not the flower of good odour rise up amid the roughnesses of labours and the contritions of minds? For by a contrite heart God is appeased. This is the desert, O daughters, which leads to the kingdom; this is also the desert which 1872Cflowers as a lily, according to that which is written: Rejoice, thou barren one, and exult, thou desert, and let it flower as a lily (Esai. XXXV, 1) [Vulg. Laetabitur deserta et invia, et exsultabit solitudo, et florebit quasi lilium, "The desert and the pathless land shall be glad… and shall flower like a lily" — indicative, not imperative]. In this desert, O daughters, that good fruitful tree, which makes good fruits, begins to spread abroad the arms of its own deeds, to lift up the crown of its divinity, beside which the trees of our wood may put forth their shoots; because, as the apple tree among the trees of the wood, so is my brother in the midst of the sons; so that the Church, seeing this, and already glad at the success of our faith, may say: In his shadow I have desired and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws [Vulg. Sicut malus inter ligna silvarum, sic dilectus meus inter filios. Sub umbra illius quem desideraveram sedi, et fructus ejus dulcis gutturi meo, "Under the shadow of him whom I had desired I sat, and his fruit was sweet to my throat"].

7.

(Vers. 3, 4.) Nota quod saepe in hoc Canticorum libro Salomon triplicem illam sapientiam evidenter expressit, moralem scilicet, naturalem, et mysticam, sicut in Proverbiis dixerat; ut tripliciter 1872Dsibi scriberet, qui sapientiam ejus vellet audire. Ait ergo in Canticis sponsa de sponso: Ecce pulcher es, consobrinus meus, ecce pulcher es et decorus; acclinatio nostra opaca, trabes domorum nostrarum cedri, lacunaria nostra cupressi (Cant. I, 15 et 16). Possumus hoc de moralibus accipere; ubi enim requiescit Christus et Ecclesia, nisi in operibus suae plebis? Denique ubi impudicitia, ubi superbia, ubi iniquitas erat, ibi ait Dominus Jesus: Filius autem hominis non habet ubi caput suum reclinet (Matth. VIII, 20). De naturalibus 1873Aautem quid accipiamus? In umbra, inquit, ejus concupivi et sedi, et fructus ejus dulcis in faucibus meis; qui enim terrena supergreditur, et cui mundana moriuntur (quoniam crucifigitur ei mundus, et ipse mundo) omnia quae sunt sub sole, refugit et contemnit. De mysticis quoque ait: Introducite me in domum vini, constituite in me charitatem. Etenim sicut vineam suam vitis, ita Dominus Jesus populum suum quasi vitis aeterna quibusdam brachiis charitatis amplectitur.

7.

(Vers. 3, 4.) Note that often in this book of Songs Solomon has plainly expressed that threefold wisdom, namely the moral, the natural, and the mystical, as he had said in the Proverbs; that he might write in threefold fashion 1872Dfor him who should wish to hear his wisdom. He says therefore in the Songs, the bride concerning the bridegroom: Behold thou art beautiful, my cousin, behold thou art beautiful and comely; our reclining-place is shady, the beams of our houses are cedars, our panelled ceilings cypresses (Cant. I, 15 et 16). We can take this of moral things; for where does Christ rest, and the Church, if not in the works of his own people? And indeed where there was unchastity, where pride, where iniquity, there the Lord Jesus says: But the Son of man has not where to lay back his head (Matth. VIII, 20). But what shall we take 1873Aof natural things? In his shadow, she says, I have desired and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws; for he who steps over earthly things, and to whom worldly things die (since the world is crucified to him, and he to the world), flees and despises all things which are under the sun. Of mystical things also she says: Bring me into the house of wine, set charity in me. For as the vine embraces its own vineyard, so the Lord Jesus, as an eternal vine, embraces his own people with certain arms of charity [Vulg. Cant. 2:4 Introduxit me in cellam vinariam; ordinavit in me caritatem, "He brought me into the wine cellar; he set charity in order in me" — indicative, not imperative].

8.

Considera singula; in moralibus flos est inter spinas lilium, sicut ipse ait: Ego flos campi, et lilium convallium (Sup., v. 1). In moralibus ergo flos est, sicut in naturalibus sol justitiae, qui oriens et resurgens illuminat, occidens obumbrat. Cave ne tibi occidat, quia scriptum est: Sol non occidat super 1873Biracundiam vestram (Ephes. IV, 26). In mysticis charitas est, quia plenitudo legis est Christus. Et ideo Ecclesia quae diligit Christum, vulnerata est charitate.

8.

Consider them one by one: in moral things the flower among thorns is the lily, as he himself says: I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys (Sup., v. 1). In moral things therefore he is a flower, as in natural things the sun of justice, who, rising and rising again, gives light, and setting, overshadows. Beware lest he set upon thee, for it is written: Let not the sun go down upon 1873Byour wrath (Ephes. IV, 26). In mystical things he is charity, because Christ is the fullness of the law. And therefore the Church, which loves Christ, is wounded with charity.

9.

Pulchre etiam lilium dicitur Ecclesia; sicut enim lilium fulget, ita etiam splendent opera sanctorum. Pulcherrime etiam dictum est: Lilium convallium; quia in humilibus magis elucet gratia. Sed hoc lilium in medio spinarum, hoc est, inter Judaeos et haereticos, hoc est inter sollicitudines hujus saeculi, quae mentem hominis animumque compungunt. Possumus etiam aliter accipere, quia sicut lilium inter spinas eminet, ita super omnes conventus Ecclesia Dei refulget.

9.

Beautifully also is the Church called a lily; for as the lily shines, so also do the works of the saints shine forth. Most beautifully also was it said: The lily of the valleys; because in lowly things grace shines out the more. But this lily is in the midst of thorns, that is, among the Jews and the heretics, that is, among the anxieties of this age, which prick the mind and the spirit of man. We can take it also otherwise: that as the lily stands out among thorns, so the Church of God shines above all assemblies.

10.

Refert etiam considerare, quod hoc lilium 1873Csplendore circumfusum sit, intus autem quod habet, rubeum sit, boni et ipsum odoris; eo quod caro Christi velut morali divinitatis claritudine circumsepta, coelestis habuerit protectionem gratiae. Denique in posterioribus ait: Fraternus meus candidus, et rubeus (Cant. V, 10): candidus claritate 1561 divina, rubeus specie coloris humani, quem sacramento incarnationis assumpsit. Meritoque et ipsum quod est rubrum, bene olet, quia caro Christi sine peccato est, quam perfidi contrectantes, manus suas inquinaverunt: sancti venerantes, pietatis odore fragrarunt.

10.

It is worth considering also that this lily 1873Cis compassed about with splendour, but that what it has within is red, and this too of good odour; in that the flesh of Christ, hedged about as it were with the moral brightness of the Godhead, had the protection of heavenly grace. And indeed in the latter parts he says: My kinsman is white and red (Cant. V, 10) [Vulg. Dilectus meus candidus et rubicundus, "My beloved is white and ruddy"]: white with the brightness 1561 divine, red with the appearance of human colour, which he took up in the sacrament of the incarnation. And deservedly that also which is red smells well, because the flesh of Christ is without sin — which the faithless, in handling, defiled their own hands; the saints, in venerating, were fragrant with the odour of piety.

11.

Accepit hunc odorem Dominus Jesus fragrantis Ecclesiae suae, et ait: Ecce proxima mea bona. Et Ecclesia dicit ad Christum: Ecce es bonus fraternus meus, ecce es bonus, tamquam malum in lignis silvae. 1873DPulcherrima collatio! Quid enim pulchrius aptiusque dici queat, quam ut sponsum arbori mali comparet? Hujusmodi enim pomum odorem gratum habet, ut caeterorum pomorum fragrantiam vincat. Et Christus affixus ad lignum, sicut malum pendens in arbore, bonum odorem mundanae fundebat redemptionis, quae peccati gravem detersit fetorem, et unguentum potus vitalis effudit.

11.

The Lord Jesus received this odour of his fragrant Church, and says: Behold my neighbor is good. And the Church says to Christ: Behold thou art good, my kinsman, behold thou art good, as an apple among the trees of the wood. 1873DA most beautiful comparison! For what could be said more beautifully or more aptly than that she should compare the bridegroom to an apple tree? For a fruit of this kind has a pleasing odour, so that it surpasses the fragrance of the other fruits. And Christ, fastened to the wood, like an apple hanging on the tree, poured out the good odour of the world's redemption, which wiped away the heavy stench of sin, and poured forth the ointment of a life-giving draught.

12.

Tamquam malum, inquit, in lignis silvae, ita consobrinus meus in medio filiorum; eo quod super 1874Aprophetas et apostolos, intima corda hominum, verborum suavitate mulcebat. Sed non solum odor, verum etiam cibus suavis in malo est: ergo cibus suavis est Christus.

12.

As an apple, he says, among the trees of the wood, so is my cousin in the midst of the sons; in that above 1874Athe prophets and apostles he soothed the inmost hearts of men with the sweetness of his words. But not only odour, but also sweet food is in the apple: therefore Christ is sweet food.

13.

In umbra ejus concupivi, et sedi; legem enim acceperat, hanc sequebatur, hanc currebat viam: in lege itaque requiescens, in umbra Christi requiescebat. Bona umbra, quae nos ab iniquitatis sole defendit! Cui autem dubium quod lex Dei umbra sit Christi? Quid est lex diei festi, neomeniae, sabbatorum, nisi umbra futurorum? Qui dies secundum legem Moysi: Ut videam sex dies, et iterum septimum diem, et sic perpetua saecula? Quis mensis secundum legem Moysi, ut sciens mensem, sciam primam mensis neomeniam, et vere sancta in neomeniis offerenda? Quis secundum legem Moysi annus, 1874Bsex illis diebus constans de quibus ait: Sex annis serviet Hebraeus, et septimo anno liberabitur (Deut. XV, 12); ut inveniantur sex anni quibus aliquis operatur terram, septimo autem anno remittit illam proselytis, et pauperibus, et bestiis terrae? Quis septimus annus, quo concedantur omnia debita Hebraeis?

13.

In his shadow I have desired, and sat down; for she had received the law, this she followed, this way she ran: resting therefore in the law, she was resting in the shadow of Christ. Good is the shadow which defends us from the sun of iniquity! And to whom is it a doubt that the law of God is the shadow of Christ? What is the law of the feast day, of the new moon, of the sabbaths, if not a shadow of things to come? What days are they according to the law of Moses: That I may see six days, and again the seventh day, and so perpetual ages? What month is there according to the law of Moses, that, knowing the month, I may know the first new moon of the month, and the things truly holy which are to be offered on the new moons? What year is there according to the law of Moses, 1874Bconsisting of those six days of which he says: Six years shall the Hebrew serve, and in the seventh year he shall be set free (Deut. XV, 12) [Vulg. serviet tibi sex annis: in anno septimo dimittes eum liberum, "he shall serve thee six years: in the seventh year thou shalt let him go free"]; so that there be found six years in which a man works the land, but in the seventh year he releases it to the strangers, and to the poor, and to the beasts of the earth? What is the seventh year, in which all debts are remitted to the Hebrews?

14.

In hac umbra requiescebat Patrum fides, prophetarum sancta devotio. Dic ergo congregatio religiosa vel sancta anima: In umbra ejus concupivi, et sedi. Umbra est jubilaeus annus: quis enim ejus mystica videt, ut facie ad faciem cognoscat quae anno quingentesimo complenda Lege praescripta sunt? Umbra sunt dies festi secundum Legem a primo mense decima die mensis, usque ad quartam decimam, 1874Cet inde usque ad vigesimam primam mensis. Quae umbra est celebritas illa inter primum et septimum mensem in tempore illo anonymo? Umbra est sanctificata libertas in sabbato. Umbra est dies festus septimi mensis neomeniae, et memoriale tubarum, septimi mensis dies decimus, dies propitiationis. Vides quia monas et decas, duo festi dies. Item quintus decimus dies, dies Tabernaculorum per octo dies: in umbra haec omnia. In umbra prima dies vocata, et octava dies vocata in umbra, quae scenopaegiis praescripta secundum Legem.

14.

In this shadow rested the faith of the Fathers, the holy devotion of the prophets. Say therefore, O religious congregation or holy soul: In his shadow I have desired, and sat down. A shadow is the jubilee year: for who sees its mystical things, so as to know face to face those things which were prescribed by the Law to be fulfilled in the five hundredth year? Shadows are the feast days according to the Law, from the first month, from the tenth day of the month unto the fourteenth, 1874Cand thence unto the twenty-first of the month. What shadow is that celebration between the first and the seventh month, at that nameless season? A shadow is the sanctified liberty on the sabbath. A shadow is the feast day of the new moon of the seventh month, and the memorial of the trumpets, the tenth day of the seventh month, the day of propitiation. Thou seest that the monad and the decad are two feast days. Likewise the fifteenth day, the day of the Tabernacles through eight days: in shadow are all these things. In shadow is the first day so called, and the eighth day so called is in shadow, which things are prescribed for the feasts of tabernacles according to the Law.

15.

Constitue mihi aliquos ex circumcisione credentes, in Lege doctos, observantiae sollicitae viros, et nunc Evangelii agnitione illuminatos, spiritali saginatos gratia, dicit in his Ecclesia quae videt Christum, 1874Dquae sponsum recipit, quae cibo ejus pascitur: In umbra ejus concupivi, et sedi, et fructus ejus dulcis in faucibus meis. Quis fructus ejus dulcis, nisi praedicatio Dominicae passionis? ut ipse ait: Ecce haereditas Domini, filii merces fructus ventris (Psal. CXXVI, 3). Quis enim dulcior fructus potest esse in faucibus nostris, quam remissio peccatorum?

15.

Set before me some of those who believe out of the circumcision, learned in the Law, men of careful observance, and now enlightened by the acknowledgment of the Gospel, fattened with spiritual grace: in these the Church speaks, which sees Christ, 1874Dwhich receives the bridegroom, which is fed with his food: In his shadow I have desired, and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws. What is his sweet fruit, if not the preaching of the Lord's passion? as he himself says: Behold the inheritance of the Lord, sons; the reward, the fruit of the womb (Psal. CXXVI, 3). For what fruit can be sweeter in our jaws than the remission of sins?

16.

Et fructus ejus dulcis gutluri meo. Est spirituale manna, hoc est, pluvia spiritalis sapientiae, quae ingeniosis et quaerentibus de coelo infunditur, 1875Aet irrorat mentes piorum, et obdulcat fauces eorum: qui igitur intellexerit infusionem divinae sapientiae, delectatur nec alium cibum quaerit, nec in solo pane vivit, sed in omni verbo Dei. Qui curiosior fuerit, quaerit quid sit illud quod melle dulcius sit: respondet illi minister Dei: Hic est panis quem dedit vobis Dominus manducare. Quid sit iste panis, audi: Verbum, inquit, quod ordinavit Deus. 1562 Haec ergo ordinatio Dei, haec alimonia alit animam sapientis, et illuminat atque obdulcat, resplendens veritatis corusco, et mulcens tamquam favo quodam, ita diversarum virtutum suavitate, et sermone sapientiae. Favi enim mellis sermones sunt boni, sicut scriptum est in Proverbiis. Ergo ubi gustavit fructum suavitatis, impatiens ad perfectiora festinat, dicens: 1875B

16.

And his fruit is sweet to my gutluri. There is a spiritual manna, that is, a rain of spiritual wisdom, which is poured from heaven upon the ingenious and upon those who seek, 1875Aand bedews the minds of the pious, and sweetens their jaws: he therefore who has understood the infusion of divine wisdom is delighted, and seeks no other food, nor lives on bread alone, but on every word of God. He who is more curious asks what that is which is sweeter than honey: the minister of God answers him: This is the bread which the Lord has given you to eat. What this bread is, hear: The Word, he says, which God ordained. 1562 This ordinance of God therefore, this nourishment, feeds the soul of the wise man, and enlightens and sweetens it, shining with the flash of truth, and soothing as with a certain honeycomb, so by the sweetness of divers virtues and by the word of wisdom. For the honeycombs of honey are good words, as it is written in the Proverbs [Vulg. Prov. 16:24 Favus mellis composita verba, "Well-ordered words are a honeycomb"]. Therefore, when it has tasted the fruit of sweetness, impatient, it hastens on to more perfect things, saying: 1875B

17.

(Vers. 4-7.) Introducite me in domum vini, constituite in me dilectionem, confirmate me in unguentis, stipate me in malis; quia vulneratae dilectionis ego sum. Laeva ejus sub capite meo, et dextera ejus complectetur me. Adjuravi vos, filiae Hierusalem, in virtutibus et fortitudinibus agri, si suscitaveritis et resuscitaveritis dilectionem, usquequo voluerit. Merito quaerit eum, merito desiderat; quia bene omnia in hoc cursu atque itinere disposuit imperator noster. Primum omnium fidei fundamento tabernaculum hoc confirmandum putavit. Deinde si qua est nobis aspera atque arida et praerupta mansio, Imperator tamen iste turbata discernit, arida irrigat, deserta fecundat. Si quid amaritudinis, si quid tentationis, si quid infirmitatis est, ductor noster et amara temperat, 1875Cet sollicita mitigat; dura dissolvit et in valida confirmat.

17.

(Vers. 4-7.) Bring me into the house of wine, set love in me, strengthen me with ointments, hedge me about with apples; for I am of wounded love. His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me. I have adjured you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the powers and strengths of the field, if ye shall rouse and rouse up again love, until she will [Vulg. Cant. 2:4–7 Introduxit me in cellam vinariam, ordinavit in me caritatem. Fulcite me floribus, stipate me malis, quia amore langueo… Adjuro vos, filiae Jerusalem, per capreas cervosque camporum, ne suscitetis, neque evigilare faciatis dilectam, quoadusque ipsa velit, "He brought me into the wine cellar… Prop me up with flowers… because I languish with love… I adjure you… by the roes and harts of the fields, that ye stir not up, nor awake the beloved, till she please"]. Rightly does she seek him, rightly does she desire him; because our commander has well disposed all things in this course and journey. First of all he judged that this tent must be strengthened upon the foundation of faith. Then, if any lodging of ours is rough and dry and steep, yet that Commander of ours parts asunder the troubled things, waters the dry, makes fruitful the deserts. If there is anything of bitterness, anything of temptation, anything of weakness, our leader both tempers the bitter things, 1875Cand mitigates the anxious; he loosens hard things and strengthens them into strong.

18.

Sicubi vult requiescere rex terrae exercitum suum, non ignobilem vicum non commeatibus indigentem, non arenosa et nuda gignentium: sed urbem aedificiis nobilem, refectam et uberem copiarum, aut agrum amoenum, aut virentem pascuis, aut nemorosa et campestria stativis opportuna decernit. Ergo si reges terrae norunt commoda providere sequentibus se, quanto magis Deus qui bonus est, novit quemadmodum profutura disponat diligentibus se? Ac primum si incognitum carpendum est iter, duces eliguntur viarum, qui praecurrant agmini, sed hoc imperatores isti injuriosum sibi arbitrantur: Deus autem praeibat, cum Hebraei facerent iter, denique in columna nubis loquebatur ad eos. Et ut scias 1875Dquia praeibat, Deus, inquit, praeibat illos: interdiu quidem in columna nubis ostendens illis viam; noctu autem in columna ignis, et non deficiebat columna nubis interdiu (Exod. XIII, 21 et 22). Hanc praecedentem nobis columnam sequens Ecclesia, non deficientem ne ipsa deficeret, umbra ejus refrigerabatur; et ideo dicit: In umbra ejus concupivi, et sedi, et dulcis ejus fructus in faucibus meis (Sup., v. 3); quia a Domino pascebatur, et deducta in locum pascuorum et aquam refectionis. 1876A

18.

Wherever the king of the earth wills his army to rest, he appoints no ignoble village, none lacking in supplies, no sandy and bare places of growth: but a city noble in buildings, restored and rich in stores, or a pleasant field, or one green with pastures, or wooded and level places convenient for a standing camp. If therefore the kings of the earth know how to provide advantages for those who follow them, how much more does God, who is good, know how to dispose things profitable for those who love him? And first, if an unknown road must be taken, guides of the ways are chosen, who run before the column; but these commanders reckon this an injury to themselves: God, however, went before, when the Hebrews made their journey; and indeed he spoke to them in the pillar of cloud. And that thou mayest know 1875Dthat he went before, God, he says, went before them: by day indeed in a pillar of cloud, showing them the way; but by night in a pillar of fire, and the pillar of cloud did not fail by day (Exod. XIII, 21 et 22) [Vulg. Exod. 13:21–22 Dominus autem praecedebat eos ad ostendendam viam… Numquam defuit columna nubis per diem, "And the Lord went before them… Never did the pillar of cloud fail by day"]. Following this pillar going before us, which does not fail lest she herself should fail, the Church was refreshed by its shadow; and therefore she says: In his shadow I have desired, and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws (Sup., v. 3); because she was fed by the Lord, and led into the place of pastures and the water of refreshment. 1876A

19.

Introducite me in domum vini. De copiis praeparatis, quaerit progredi ad aliam mansionem in qua mysteriorum gratiam et laetitiae capiat suavitatem. Hinc quoque promovens verum iter, ait: Ordinate in me dilectionem. Bona stativa, ubi plenitudo est charitatis. Charitas non potest esse sine fide; tres enim velut vades sunt Ecclesiae, spes, fides, charitas, cum spes praecesserit, fides fundata fuerit, ordinatur charitas, Ecclesia copulatur.

19.

Bring me into the house of wine. Out of the supplies made ready, she seeks to go forward to another lodging, in which she may take the grace of the mysteries and the sweetness of gladness. Hence too, advancing on the true way, she says: Order love in me. Good standing camps, where there is the fullness of charity. Charity cannot be without faith; for there are as it were three sureties of the Church: hope, faith, charity; when hope has gone before, when faith has been founded, charity is set in order, the Church is joined.

20.

Confirmate me, inquit, in unguentis, stipate me in malis. Habes alias mansiones, ad quas Ecclesia cum delectatione succedit. Istae mansiones sunt crucis Christi et sepulturae, in quibus vulnerata est Ecclesia, sed vulnere charitatis; vulnus enim est quod Christus excepit, sed unguentum est quod effudit. 1876BPomum est quod pependit, hoc pomum gustavit Ecclesia, et ait: Et fructus ejus dulcis in faucibus meis. Et ut scias quia pomum est Dominus, legisti supra: Tamquam malum in lignis silvae, ita consobrinus meus. Nos quoque vulnus fatemur, cum praedicamus Christum crucifixum, sed bonus odor sumus Deo; quia crux Christi Judaeis scandalum, Graecis stultitia, nobis autem virtus Dei atque sapientia est. Hoc vulnere Ecclesia vulneratur, cum Salvatoris sui praedicat mortem, sed hoc vulnus est charitatis. Denique qui non credit, negat: qui diligit, confitetur. Manichaeus negat, Christianus fatetur; et ideo scriptum est: Utiliora vulnera amici, quam voluntaria oscula inimici (Prov. XXVII, 6). Pulchre ergo dicit Ecclesia: Quia vulnerata charitate 1876Cego sum.

20.

Strengthen me, she says, with ointments, hedge me about with apples. Thou hast other lodgings, to which the Church succeeds with delight. These lodgings are of the cross of Christ and of the burial, in which the Church is wounded, but with the wound of charity; for it is a wound that Christ received, but it is ointment that he poured out. 1876BIt is an apple that hung; this apple the Church has tasted, and says: And his fruit is sweet in my jaws. And that thou mayest know that the Lord is an apple, thou hast read above: As an apple among the trees of the wood, so is my cousin. We too confess a wound, when we preach Christ crucified, but we are a good odour to God; because the cross of Christ is to the Jews a scandal, to the Greeks foolishness, but to us the power of God and wisdom. With this wound the Church is wounded, when she preaches the death of her Saviour, but this wound is of charity. And indeed he who believes not, denies: he who loves, confesses. The Manichean denies, the Christian confesses; and therefore it is written: More useful are the wounds of a friend than the willing kisses of an enemy (Prov. XXVII, 6) [Vulg. Meliora sunt vulnera diligentis quam fraudulenta oscula odientis, "Better are the wounds of one who loves than the deceitful kisses of one who hates"]. Beautifully therefore the Church says: For wounded with charity 1876Cam I.

21.

Nudemus membra nostra bono vulneri, nudemus sagittae electae: sagitta haec Christus est qui dicit: Posuit me sicut sagittam electam (Esai. XLIX, 2); bonum est ergo hac vulnerari sagitta, non mediocris mansionis iste processus, non omnes possunt dicere, quia vulnerati sunt dilectione. 1563 Dicebant apostoli, cum pro Christo lapidarentur et Christum praedicarent: dicebat Paulus cum ter virgis caederetur, et die ac nocte Christum adorandum gentibus disputaret: dicunt hoc martyres, qui vulnerantur pro Christo; et quia vulnerari pro ejus meruerunt nomine, plus diligunt. Ad hanc ergo veniens mansionem Ecclesia, ad hunc processum, ut pro Christo filios suos offerat, ut excipiat vulnera 1876Dcharitatis, quasi bona fidei alimenta reperiens, pietatis fructus gustavit ipsa, et hortari caeteros coepit, dicens: Gustate et videte quoniam suavis est Dominus. Et addidit Ecclesia dicere:

21.

Let us bare our members to the good wound, let us bare them to the chosen arrow: this arrow is Christ, who says: He has set me as a chosen arrow (Esai. XLIX, 2); it is good therefore to be wounded with this arrow. This advance is not of a middling lodging; not all can say that they are wounded with love. 1563 The apostles said it, when they were stoned for Christ and preached Christ: Paul said it, when he was beaten thrice with rods, and disputed day and night that Christ was to be adored by the nations: the martyrs say this, who are wounded for Christ; and because they have deserved to be wounded for his name, they love the more. Coming therefore to this lodging, to this advance, that she may offer her sons for Christ, that she may take upon her the wounds 1876Dof charity, the Church, finding as it were the good nourishments of faith, has herself tasted the fruits of piety, and has begun to exhort the rest, saying: Taste and see that the Lord is sweet. And the Church added, saying:

22.

Laeva ejus sub capite meo, et dextera ejus complectetur me. Et istae mansiones sunt via regali incedentis Ecclesia; Multae enim sunt mansiones apud Patrem meum (Joan. XIV, 2). Testificatur Dominus Jesus: Et ecce venio, et accerso vos (Ibid.). Bona mansio sapientiae, et sive in laeva ejus, sive in dextera, bona mansio; quia via regalis est.
