Ideoque 1877Asapientes nuntii dicunt: Via regali ibimus, non divertemus in dextera, neque in sinistra, quoadusque transeamus terminos tuos (Num. XX, 17). Hoc dixerunt missi a Moyse nuntii ad regem Edom, hoc est terrenum; quia terrena omnia sive in dextera, sive in sinistra, mala sunt. Mala mansio insipientia, mala mansio intemperantia; et ideo pertransit has mansiones Hebraeus: non divertit ad eas, sed pertransit; ut perveniat ad sinistram sapientiae et dexteram, et in ipsis maneat, ubi divitiae simplicitatis, ubi gloria, ubi longitudo vitae est. Longitudo enim vitae in dextera ejus, ut ait Salomon: In sinistra autem ejus divitiae et gloria (Prov. III, 16): in dextera vita est, in sinistra requies. Utinam super sinistram ejus requiescam, nec quaeram cervicalia! Vae enim his qui 1877Bassuunt cervicalia, Ezechiel dicit (Ezech. XIII, 18). Has mansiones praeparat Ecclesiae suae bonus ductor, et per vias sapientiae dirigit iter ejus. Denique laudat sapiens vias ejus: Viae ejus viae bonae, et omnes semitae ejus in pace (Prov. III, 17).

22.

His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me. And these lodgings belong to the Church as she walks upon the royal way; For there are many mansions with my Father (Joan. XIV, 2). The Lord Jesus bears witness: And behold I come, and summon you (Ibid.). Good is the lodging of wisdom, and whether in her left hand or in her right, it is a good lodging; because it is the royal way. And therefore 1877Athe wise messengers say: By the royal way will we go; we will not turn aside to the right hand, nor to the left, until we pass thy borders (Num. XX, 17). This the messengers sent by Moses said to the king of Edom, that is, to the earthly king; because all earthly things, whether on the right or on the left, are evil. An evil lodging is folly, an evil lodging is intemperance; and therefore the Hebrew passes through these lodgings: he does not turn aside to them, but passes through, that he may come to the left hand of wisdom and to her right, and abide in these, where are the riches of simplicity, where glory, where length of life is. For length of life is in her right hand, as Solomon says: and in her left hand riches and glory (Prov. III, 16) [Vulg. Longitudo dierum in dextera ejus, "Length of days is in her right hand"]: in the right hand is life, in the left rest. Would that I might rest upon her left hand, and not seek pillows! For woe to them who 1877Bsew pillows, Ezekiel says (Ezech. XIII, 18) [Vulg. Vae quae consuunt pulvillos sub omni cubito manus, "Woe to them that sew cushions under every elbow"]. These lodgings the good leader prepares for his Church, and by the ways of wisdom he directs her journey. And indeed the wise man praises her ways: Her ways are good ways, and all her paths are in peace (Prov. III, 17) [Vulg. Viae ejus viae pulchrae, et omnes semitae illius pacificae, "Her ways are beautiful ways, and all her paths peaceable"].

23.

Laeva ejus sub caput meum, et dextera illius complectetur me. Haec loquitur sponsa de Christo, anima de Verbo Dei: Christus autem idem est Verbum Dei atque sapientia. Beata ergo anima quam complectitur sapientia. Magna est sapientiae manus, magna dextera, totam complectitur animam; tota enim munita est, quae desponsata Verbo Dei est. Plenitudo enim sapientiae, timere Deum: quae ergo Deum timet anima, pleno se munit ipsa praesidio. Mittit sapientia laevam suam sub collum ejus, 1877Cdexteram autem in complexum ejus. Utrumque quidem brachium ejus ad utilia extenditur; habent tamen propria sua singulae manus sapientiae: In dextera longitudo vitae est, in sinistra autem divitiae et gloria. Bonis utique manus utroque dotata est dotibus; in eo tamen habent varietatem sui muneris, quod et praesentia et futura tempora comprehendunt; ut laeva praesentium remuneratrix, dextera futurorum sit.

23.

His left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me. These things the bride speaks concerning Christ, the soul concerning the Word of God: but Christ is the same as the Word of God and wisdom. Blessed therefore is the soul whom wisdom embraces. Great is the hand of wisdom, great her right hand; she embraces the whole soul; for she is wholly fortified who is betrothed to the Word of God. For the fullness of wisdom is to fear God: the soul therefore which fears God fortifies itself with a full defence. Wisdom sends her left hand under her neck, 1877Cand her right hand into her embrace. Both her arms indeed are stretched out unto useful things; yet the several hands of wisdom have their own proper gifts: In the right hand is length of life, in the left hand riches and glory. With good gifts assuredly is her hand endowed on both sides; yet they have herein a variety of their own office, that they comprehend both present and future times; so that the left hand is the rewarder of present things, the right of things to come.

24.

Possumus hoc etiam de prophetia sancti patriarchae Israel cognoscere, nam cum dexteram suam posuisset super Ephraem, sinistram autem super Manassen, et convertere eas Joseph aetatis consideratione voluisset; ut dexteram patris super caput Manasse senioris filii collocaret, noluit, et dixit: Scio, fili, et hic erit in populum, et hic exaltabitur, 1877Dsed frater junior major erit illo (Genes. XLVIII, 19). In quo major? in eo utique quia benedictione praelatus est, dicentibus posteris Ephraem: Faciat vos Deus sicut fecit Ephraem et Manassen (Ibid., 20): vel quia semen ejus multitudo gentium, quae in hoc saeculo laborem elegit, credens in Dominum Jesum, ut consolationem habeat futurorum. Manasse autem semen est populus oblivionis, qui proprium oblitus auctorem, exaltatus ad tempus in hoc saeculo est, daturus in reliquum poenas graves; quia Deum suum et Dominum denegavit. 1878A

24.

We can know this also from the prophecy of the holy patriarch Israel; for when he had set his right hand upon Ephraim, and his left upon Manasseh, and Joseph had wished to change them out of consideration of their age, so that he might place the father's right hand upon the head of Manasseh the elder son, he would not, and said: I know, son, and this one also shall be for a people, and this one shall be exalted, 1877Dbut the younger brother shall be greater than he (Genes. XLVIII, 19) [Vulg. Scio, fili mi, scio: et iste quidem erit in populos, et multiplicabitur; sed frater ejus minor major erit illo, "I know, my son, I know: and this one indeed shall be for peoples, and shall be multiplied"]. In what greater? In this surely, that he was preferred in the blessing, since those who came after said of Ephraim: God make you as he made Ephraim and Manasseh (Ibid., 20) [Vulg. Faciat tibi Deus sicut Ephraim et sicut Manasse, "God make thee as Ephraim and as Manasses" — singular]: or because his seed is a multitude of nations, which in this world chose labour, believing in the Lord Jesus, that it might have the consolation of things to come. But the seed of Manasseh is the people of forgetfulness, which, having forgotten its own author, has been exalted for a time in this world, and shall pay heavy penalties for the rest; because it denied its own God and Lord. 1878A

25.

Hoc eo diximus, ut probaremus ea esse potiora quae futura sunt. Denique laeva sapientiae sub sponsae capite est, dextera autem superior, quae totam complectitur sponsam. Itaque illa quasi fulcrum est quietis praesentis, quo anima innixa requiescit. Habet ubi reclinet caput suum, quia laeva sapientiae divitiae sunt et gloria. Per id tempus haec mulcent, et ex parte solatio sunt, et ideo; Filius hominis non habebat ubi caput 1564 suum reclinaret (Matth. VIII, 20); quia cum dives esset, pauper factus est (II Cor. VIII, 9). Nec gloriam aliquam istius saeculi requirebat; quia venerat non ut in portione, sed ut universo humano generi subveniret, dicens: Mihi indignamini quia totum hominem sanum feci in sabbato (Joan. VII, 23); hoc est totum, non ut facultatum 1878Bcollatione ditaret, non ut honorum insignibus, non ut gloriae saecularis cumularet augmento; haec enim non habent beatitudinis et gratiae plenitudinem: sed totum, hoc est, quod longitudinem vitae complecteretur aeternae. Neque enim communis vitae similis vita in dextera est sapientiae, sed longitudo vitae est, ut qui accipit vitam a sapientia, non exiguitatem vitae, sed perpetuitatem et aeternitatis longitudinem adipiscatur.

25.

We have said this in order to prove that those things are preferable which are to come. And indeed the left hand of wisdom is under the head of the bride, but the right hand is higher, which embraces the whole bride. And so that hand is as it were the prop of present quiet, upon which the soul, leaning, rests. She has where to lay back her head, because the left hand of wisdom is riches and glory. For that season these things soothe, and are in part a solace; and therefore: The Son of man had not where to lay back his 1564 head (Matth. VIII, 20); because, when he was rich, he was made poor (II Cor. VIII, 9) [Vulg. quoniam propter vos egenus factus est, cum esset dives, "because for your sakes he became needy, whereas he was rich"]. Nor did he seek any glory of this world; because he had come, not to succour a portion, but the whole human race, saying: Ye are angry with me because I made a whole man sound on the sabbath (Joan. VII, 23). that is, wholly — not that he might enrich him 1878Bby the bestowal of substance, not with the marks of honours, not that he might heap him up with an increase of worldly glory; for these things have not the fullness of blessedness and of grace: but wholly, that is, so as to embrace the length of eternal life. For the life which is in the right hand of wisdom is not like the common life, but is length of life, so that he who receives life from wisdom may obtain not scantiness of life, but perpetuity and the length of eternity.

26.

Bonus amictus aeternitas vitae. Itaque sponsa malorum granatorum, hoc est, diversorum et innumerabilium fructuum, et praecipue fidei odoribus, gratiae, sapientiae et gloriae, vitaeque aeternae subnixa divitiis quae sunt circa sinistram et dexteram sponsi, per animas sanctas laudantium congregationum plausibus 1878Cexcitat Christum, dicens:

26.

A good garment is the eternity of life. And so the bride, upheld by the riches of pomegranates, that is, of divers and innumerable fruits, and chiefly by the odours of faith, of grace, of wisdom and of glory and of eternal life, which are about the left hand and the right hand of the bridegroom, rouses Christ through holy souls by the applause of the congregations that praise him, 1878Csaying:

27.

Adjuravi vos, filiae Hierusalem, in virtutibus e fortitudinibus agri, si suscitaveritis, et resuscitaveritis dilectionem usquequo voluerit. Ager ille quem benedixit Dominus, non iste terrenus aut horridus silvis, aut confragosus torrentibus, aut inutilis vitibus, aut infecunda glarea saxosus, aut hiulcus, et aridus siccitate, aut sanguine madidus, aut sentibus incultus et spinis, sed ille ager de quo Dominus ait: Et species agri mecum est (Psalm. XLIX, 19). In hoc agro uva illa reperitur quae expressa sanguinem fudit, et mundum diluit: in hoc agro est ficus illa sub qua sancti requiescent, spiritalis gratiae suavitate recreati: in hoc agro est oliva illa fructifera, dominicae fluens pacis unguentum: in hoc agro florent 1878Dmalogranata, quae plurimos fructus uno fidei munimine tegunt, et quodam fovent charitatis amplexu.

27.

I have adjured you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the powers from the strengths of the field, if ye shall rouse and rouse up again love, until she will. That field which the Lord blessed is not this earthly one, either bristling with woods, or broken with torrents, or useless for vines, or stony with barren gravel, or gaping and dry with drought, or wet with blood, or uncultivated with brambles and thorns; but that field of which the Lord says: And the beauty of the field is with me (Psalm. XLIX, 19). In this field is found that grape which, being pressed, poured out blood and washed the world: in this field is that fig tree under which the saints shall rest, refreshed by the sweetness of spiritual grace: in this field is that fruitful olive tree, flowing with the ointment of the Lord's peace: in this field flower 1878Dthe pomegranates, which cover very many fruits under one defence of faith, and cherish them with a certain embrace of charity.

28.

Quia igitur sapientia et honesta claritas ita perfecta sunt, si habeant charitatem (plenitudo enim legis (Rom. XIII, 10) est charitas) suscitari et resuscitari vult charitatem, suscitari in veteri Testamento, resuscitari in novo. Charitas Deus est, ut legimus, charitas Christus est (I Joan. IV, 8). Suscitatur tamquam leo, et catulus leonis; ut Judae ascendat ex germine (Gen. XLIX, 9): resuscitatur ut 1879Adormiens, ut recumbens; quia non humana, sed sua et Patris majestate suscitatus est a mortuis. Unde ait Scriptura: Quis suscitabit illum? Non enim poterat illum aut angelus aut Potestas suscitare aliena, cum ipse alios suscitaret. Ergo cum hic dicit: Si suscitaveritis et resuscitaveritis dilectionem, usquequo voluerit; de iis dicit, qui resurrectionem ejus congrue possunt praedicare, ut audientibus ardorem fidei et devotionis accendant: vel suscitatur Christus in his qui primum accedunt, resuscitatur in his qui postquam accesserint, dormierunt. Dormit ergo Christus negligentibus, suscitatur in sanctis.

28.

Since therefore wisdom and honourable brightness are so far perfect, if they have charity (for the fullness of the law (Rom. XIII, 10) is charity), she wills charity to be roused and roused up again: to be roused in the Old Testament, to be roused up again in the New. Charity is God, as we read; charity is Christ (I Joan. IV, 8) [Vulg. Deus caritas est, "God is charity"]. He is roused as a lion, and a lion's whelp; that he may go up out of the seed of Judah (Gen. XLIX, 9) [Vulg. Catulus leonis Juda: ad praedam, fili mi, ascendisti, "Juda is a lion's whelp: to the prey, my son, thou art gone up"]: he is roused up again as 1879Aone sleeping, as one reclining; because he was raised from the dead not by a human majesty, but by his own and his Father's. Whence Scripture says: Who shall rouse him up? For neither an angel nor an alien Power could rouse him, since he himself was rousing others. Therefore when he here says: If ye shall rouse and rouse up again love, until she will; he speaks of those who can fittingly preach his resurrection, so as to kindle in their hearers the ardour of faith and of devotion: or Christ is roused in those who first come to him, and roused up again in those who, after they have come, have slept. Christ therefore sleeps for the negligent, and is roused in the saints.

29.

Hanc itaque charitatem suscitat et resuscitat, donec vocem ejus accipiat, et praesentiam ejus accersat: quia quaesitus non solum venit, sed etiam 1879Bsaliens venit. Sed jam sponsus aderat, ut probaret illud quod adhuc loquentibus nobis dicit: Adsum. Adest, quia suscitatus a filiabus Judae, quasi resuscitatus a filiabus Hierusalem. Audit Ecclesia sonum vocis ejus et dicit:

29.

This charity therefore she rouses and rouses up again, until she receive his voice and summon his presence: because, being sought, he not only comes, but even 1879Bcomes leaping. But by now the bridegroom was at hand, so as to prove that which, while we are yet speaking, he says: I am here. He is present, because he is roused by the daughters of Judah, as it were roused up again by the daughters of Jerusalem. The Church hears the sound of his voice, and says:

30.

(Vers. 8.) Vox consobrini mei, ecce hic advenit saliens super montes, transiliens super colles. Manipularis est hic liber multas habens personas nobiles, velut manipulus triumphalis, multosque actus significans, quos intelligere magis quam expressos tenere possumus. Nam quasi advenientem sponsum audierit cum aliquibus pariter viantibus collonquentem, dicit sponsa: Vox consobrini mei. Dum loquitur cum filiabus Hierusalem, et rogat ut excitent, et resuscitent sponsum; subito tamquam de longinquo 1879Cmissae vocis sonum sentiens, dicit: Vox consobrini mei, annuntians quem nuntiari sibi ante quaerebat. Quem cupiebat ab aliis suscitari, sua prece resuscitatum, a Patre venire jam credens, laeta dicit: Vox consobrini mei. Bene addidit, consobrini mei; ut non aliae, sed consobrina sola sibi ejus vindicaret adventum. 1565

30.

(Vers. 8.) The voice of my cousin: behold, here he comes, leaping over the mountains, bounding over the hills [Vulg. Vox dilecti mei; ecce iste venit saliens in montibus, transiliens colles, "The voice of my beloved; behold he cometh leaping upon the mountains"]. This book is like a maniple, having many noble persons, as it were a triumphal maniple, and signifying many acts, which we can rather understand than hold expressed. For, as though she had heard the bridegroom coming and collonquentem with certain who were journeying along with him, the bride says: The voice of my cousin. While she speaks with the daughters of Jerusalem, and asks that they stir up and rouse up again the bridegroom, suddenly, perceiving as it were the sound of a voice sent 1879Cfrom afar, she says: The voice of my cousin, announcing him whom before she was seeking to have announced to her. Him whom she desired to be roused by others, believing him now, roused up again by her own prayer, to be coming from the Father, she gladly says: The voice of my cousin. Well did she add, my cousin; that no other women, but she alone, his cousin, might claim his coming for herself. 1565

31.

Ecce, inquit, iste advenit, adhuc ego eum quaero, et ille jam venit: adhuc ego suffragia capto ut veniat, et ille jam proximus est: ego suscitari mihi charitatem cupio, ego me vulneratam charitatis puto, et ad me plus ipsa charitas festinat: ego dixi, veni; salit ille et transilit: ego rogo eum venire cum gratia, iste gratiarum operatur augmenta; et dum venit, incrementa gratiae secum vehit, 1879Det veniendo acquirit, quia studet etiam ipse suae placere dilectae. Salit super excelsa, ut ascendat ad sponsam; sponsae enim thalamus tribunal est Christi. Salit super Adam, transilit super Synagogam; salit super Gentes, transilit super Judaeos.

31.

Behold, she says, he is come [Vulg. Cant 2:8 ecce iste venit, "behold, he cometh"]: still am I seeking him, and he has already come; still am I catching at intercessions that he may come, and he is already near at hand; I long for charity to be roused up in me, I deem myself wounded of charity, and charity herself hastens to me the more. I said, Come; he leaps and leaps over. I ask him to come with grace; he works increases of graces; and while he comes, he carries increases of grace with him, 1879Dand by coming he acquires, because he too is eager to please his own beloved. He leaps upon the high places, that he may ascend to the bride; for the bride's marriage-chamber is the tribunal of Christ. He leaps upon Adam, he leaps over the Synagogue; he leaps upon the Gentiles, he leaps over the Jews.

32.

Videamus salientem Salit de coelo in Virginem, de utero in praesepe, de Jordane in crucem, de cruce in tumulum, in coelum de sepulcro. Proba mihi David salientem, proba currentem; tu enim dixisti: Exsultavit ut gigas ad currendam viam, a summo coelo egressio ejus; et occursus ejus usque ad summum ejus, nec est qui se abscondat a calore ejus (Psal. XVIII, 6 et 7). Et nunc salit, et nunc currit, de 1880Acorde Patris super sanctos suos, de Oriente super Occidentem, de Septentrione super Meridiem. Iste est qui ascendit super Occasum, ipse super coelos coelorum ad Orientem, ipse ascendit super montes, ipse super colles.

32.

Let us see him leaping He leaps from heaven into the Virgin, from the womb into the manger, from the Jordan into the cross, from the cross into the tomb, into heaven from the sepulchre. Prove to me, David, that he leaps; prove that he runs. For thou hast said: He hath rejoiced as a giant to run the way. His going forth is from the height of heaven, and his meeting even to the height thereof; and there is none that can hide himself from his heat (Psal. XVIII, 6 et 7). And now he leaps, and now he runs, from 1880Athe heart of the Father upon his saints, from the East upon the West, from the North upon the South. This is he who ascends above the sunset, he himself above the heavens of heavens toward the East, he himself ascends above the mountains, he himself above the hills.

33.

Utinam ego miser dicam, dicat anima mea: Ecce iste advenit, et advenit non super terrena, non super valles, sed advenit: Saliens super montes; Deus enim Deus montium est, non vallium: ubi ergo salit, super montes salit. Si sis mons, salit super te, salit super Isaiam, salit super Hieremiam, salit super Petrum, Joannem, Jacobum. Montes in circuitu ejus (Psalm. CXXIV, 2): si non potest esse mons et non praevales, esto vel collis, ut super te Christus ascendat: et si transilit, ut te transitus ejus custodiat. 1880B

33.

Would that I, wretched man, might say it, and that my soul might say: Behold, he is come, and he is come not upon earthly things, not upon the valleys, but he is come Leaping upon the mountains [Vulg. Cant 2:8 saliens in montibus, "leaping in the mountains"]; for God is the God of mountains, not of valleys: where therefore he leaps, he leaps upon mountains. If thou be a mountain, he leaps upon thee; he leaps upon Isaiah, he leaps upon Jeremiah, he leaps upon Peter, John, James. The mountains are round about him (Psalm. CXXIV, 2): if one cannot be a mountain and thou dost not prevail, be at least a hill, that Christ may ascend upon thee; and if he leaps over, that his passing may guard thee. 1880B

34.

Diximus de Christo et Ecclesia, dicamus de anima et Verbo. Anima justi sponsa est Verbi, haec si desideret, si cupiat, si oret et oret assidue, et oret sine ulla disceptatione, et tota intendat in Verbum: subito vocem sibi videtur ejus audire quem non videt, et in timore sensus odorem divinitatis ejus agnoscit, quod patiuntur plerumque qui bene credunt. Replentur subito nares animae spiritali gratia, et sentit sibi praesentiae ejus flatum aspirare quem quaerit, et dicit: Ecce iste ipse est quem requiro, ipse quem desidero.

34.

We have spoken of Christ and the Church; let us speak of the soul and the Word. The soul of the just man is the bride of the Word. If she should desire, if she should long, if she should pray and pray unceasingly, and pray without any disputing, and stretch herself wholly toward the Word: suddenly she seems to hear the voice of him whom she does not see, and in the fear of her senses she recognizes the odor of his divinity, which those who believe well for the most part experience. Suddenly the nostrils of the soul are filled with spiritual grace, and she feels the breath of his presence breathe upon her, of him whom she seeks, and she says: Behold, this is the very one whom I seek, the very one whom I desire.

35.

Nonne cum aliquid de Scripturis cogitamus, et explanationem ejus invenire non possumus; dum dubitamus, dum quaerimus, subito nobis quasi super montes, id est, altissima dogmata videtur, ascendere. 1880CDeinde quasi super colles apparens nobis, illuminat mentem, ut infundat sensibus, quod invenire posse difficile videbatur: ergo quasi ex absente fit praesens Verbum in cordibus nostris. Et rursus cum aliquid nobis subobscurum est, tamquam subducitur Verbum, et tamquam absentis adventum desideramus; et iterum apparet, ostendit se nobis tamquam praesens sit nobis in iis quae requirimus cognoscendis.

35.

Is it not so, that when we ponder something out of the Scriptures and cannot find the explanation of it, while we doubt, while we seek, suddenly he seems to us to ascend, as it were, upon the mountains, that is, upon the loftiest doctrines. 1880CThen, appearing to us as it were upon the hills, he enlightens the mind, that he may pour into our senses what seemed difficult to be found: and so the Word becomes, as it were from absent, present in our hearts. And again, when something is somewhat obscure to us, the Word is as it were withdrawn, and we long for the coming of him as though he were absent; and again he appears, he shows himself to us as though he were present with us in the knowing of those things which we seek.

36.

Salit ergo in uniuscujusque corde frequenter, transilit et exit et revertitur: si sequaris, si requiras, si tibi fidelium grata disputatione doctorum Verbum quod exierat atque transierat, postules resuscitari. Sicut illa quae quaesivit et invenit, quae dixit: Frater meus transivit, anima mea exivit in verbo ejus (Cant. V, 6). Ergo etsi super montes salit, sequere: etsi 1880Dsuper colles, sequere. Reperiuntur enim in montibus et in collibus venatores Domini, qui investigant eos qui capiuntur ad vitam; sic enim dixit per Hieremiam Deus: Ecce mitto multos piscatores et multos venatores, et venabuntur eos super omnem montem et super omnem collem (Jerem. XVI, 16). Ibi ergo populus Dei quaeratur et inveniatur in Petri et in Pauli doctrina, gratia, disciplina; ut non in convalle sint, ubi fletus, sed in montibus a quibus unumquemque Christus illuminat, et cum Petrum legimus, Christus illuminat: et cum Paulum legimus, Christus illuminat. Curavit Paulus, Christus illuminavit; quoniam invocato Domini Jesu nomine, resurrexit, cujus sanatus 1881Aest munere. Suscitavit mortuam Petrus, Christus illuminavit; et ideo ait: Illuminans tu mirabiliter a montibus aeternis (Psal. LXXV, 5).

36.

He leaps, then, often in the heart of each one; he leaps over and goes out and returns: if thou shouldst follow, if thou shouldst seek, if by the welcome disputation of faithful teachers thou shouldst beg that the Word which had gone out and had passed over be raised up again for thee. Like her who sought and found, who said: My brother has passed by, my soul went out at his word (Cant. V, 6) [Vulg. at ille declinaverat atque transierat; anima mea liquefacta est, ut locutus est, "but he had turned aside and passed on; my soul melted when he spoke"]. Therefore even if he leaps upon the mountains, follow; even if 1880Dupon the hills, follow. For there are found on the mountains and on the hills the hunters of the Lord, who track out those who are taken for life; for thus God said through Jeremiah: Behold, I send many fishers and many hunters, and they shall hunt them upon every mountain and upon every hill (Jerem. XVI, 16) [Vulg. de omni monte et de omni colle, "from every mountain and from every hill"]. There, then, let the people of God be sought and found in the doctrine, the grace, the discipline of Peter and of Paul; that they be not in the deep valley, where there is weeping, but on the mountains from which Christ enlightens each man. And when we read Peter, Christ enlightens; and when we read Paul, Christ enlightens. Paul healed, Christ enlightened; for when the name of the Lord Jesus had been invoked, he rose again, by whose gift he was made 1881Awhole. Peter raised up a dead woman, Christ enlightened; and therefore he says: Thou dost enlighten wonderfully from the everlasting mountains (Psal. LXXV, 5).

37.

Nos igitur qui montes esse non possumus, stemus 1566 in montibus vel in collibus; ut cum miserit piscatores suos Dominus et venatores, ut venentur eos qui supra omnem montem sunt, vel super omnem collem, id est, in legis et prophetarum praeceptis; et novi et veteris Testamenti cognitione omnem habentes conversationem, inveniant nos paratos, et velut bonas colligant spicas, opportuno missi messores tempore; quia si quis extra montem aut collem fuerit repertus, colligi velut bona spica non poterit ab his, qui ut comparationis faciamus alterius mentionem, triticum a paleis 1881Bseparare mittentur. Plurimarum igitur operationum solertes ministros Dominus habet, iidem piscatores, iidem etiam venatores sunt, atque messores. Si in tempore messis quo diriguntur isti metere, maturus exspectes, salientem in montibus poteris intueri, et videbis Dominum Jesum similem capreolo aut hinnulo cervorum super montes Bethel. Salit enim super Ecclesiam quae est domus panis, eo quod fidelium corda confirmet; unde et sponsa de eo dicit:

37.

We therefore who cannot be mountains, let us stand 1566 on the mountains or on the hills; so that when the Lord shall have sent his fishers and hunters to hunt those who are upon every mountain, or upon every hill, that is, in the precepts of the law and of the prophets, and who have all their conversation in the knowledge of the New and the Old Testament, they may find us ready and may gather us like good ears of corn, reapers sent at the opportune season; because if anyone shall have been found outside the mountain or the hill, he will not be able to be gathered as a good ear by those who, that we may make mention of another comparison, shall be sent to separate the wheat 1881Bfrom the chaff. The Lord therefore has skilled ministers of very many operations: the same are fishers, the same also are hunters, and reapers. If in the time of harvest, at which these are directed to reap, thou shouldst wait ripe, thou wilt be able to behold him leaping upon the mountains, and thou wilt see the Lord Jesus like a roe or a young hart upon the mountains of Bethel. For he leaps upon the Church, which is the house of bread, in that he strengthens the hearts of the faithful; whence the bride also says of him:

38.

(Vers. 9.) Similis est consobrinus meus capreae aut hinnulo cervorum, super montes Bethel; salit enim super Ecclesiam, quae est domus Dei, eo quod fidelium corda confirmet. Merito sicut capreola, quia capra in altis pascitur. Dorcas dicitur a videndo, 1881Cdorcadion enim visus acutioris est. Quid hoc aptius Christo, qui Patrem vidit quem nemo vidit: aut si quis vidit in Christo, ipse filius revelavit? Merito sicut hinnulus cervorum, hinnulus quasi filius, cui paternae inoleverit vis naturae; ut eum occulta non lateant, serpentes fugiant, venena non laedant. Denique eductus de latibulis suis serpens qui educebatur ex homine, dicebat: Quid venisti ante tempus torquere nos (Matth. VIII, 29). Spectemus ergo salientem hunc hinniculum, ut non possimus timere serpentem.

38.

(Vers. 9.) My cousin is like a roe or a young hart, upon the mountains of Bethel; [Vulg. Cant 2:9 similis est dilectus meus capreae hinnuloque cervorum, "my beloved is like a roe and a young hart," with no mountains of Bethel] for he leaps upon the Church, which is the house of God, in that he strengthens the hearts of the faithful. Rightly, like a little roe, because the goat is pastured in high places. She is called dorcas from seeing, 1881Cfor the dorcadion is of keener sight. What is fitter to Christ than this, who saw the Father whom no one has seen, or, if anyone has seen him in Christ, the Son himself revealed him? Rightly like a young hart: the fawn is as it were a son, in whom the force of the paternal nature has grown, so that hidden things do not lie concealed from him, serpents flee, poisons do not harm. Accordingly the serpent, led forth out of his lurking-places, who was being led forth out of the man, said: Why hast thou come before the time to torment us (Matth. VIII, 29). Let us therefore gaze upon this leaping fawn, that we may not be able to fear the serpent.

39.

En ipse stat post parietem nostrum, respiciens per fenestras, prospiciens per retia. Venit ergo et primo post parietem est, ut inimicitias animae et corporis solvat, sublato pariete qui impedimentum 1881Dconcordiae videbatur afferre. Deinde prospicit per fenestras: quae sint fenestrae, audi Prophetam dicentem: Fenestrae apertae sunt e coelo (Esai. XXIV, 18). Prophetas itaque significat, per quos Dominus genus respexit humanum, priusquam in terras ipse descenderet. Et hodie si qua eum anima multum requirat, multum merebitur misericordiae; quoniam qui multum requirit, multum debetur ei. Si qua ergo anima eum studiosius quaerat, longe audit vocem ejus: et quamvis ab aliis requirat, prae ipsis a quibus quaerit, audit vocem ejus. Videt eum salientem ad se venire, id est, properantem atque currentem 1882Aet transilientem eos, qui infirmo corde virtutem ejus capere non possunt: deinde prospicientem per aenigmata prophetarum, legendo eos, et tenendo eorum sermones; videt enim prospicientem, sed quasi per fenestram, non quasi adhuc praesentem. Videt eminentem super retia. Quid est hoc? Nisi forte quia illa retia nobis sunt, non illi retia sunt, eo quod illa anima adhuc intra sensibilia et mundana sit, quae mentem hominis capere et quodam sinu proprio consueverunt involvere.

39.

Behold, he himself stands behind our wall, looking through the windows, peering through the nets. [Vulg. Cant 2:9 prospiciens per cancellos, "looking through the lattices"] He comes, then, and first is behind the wall, that he may loose the enmities of soul and body, the wall being taken away which seemed to bring a hindrance 1881Dto concord. Then he peers through the windows: what the windows are, hear the Prophet saying: The windows are opened from heaven (Esai. XXIV, 18) [Vulg. Isa 24:18 cataractae de excelsis apertae sunt, "the floodgates from on high are opened"]. He signifies therefore the prophets, through whom the Lord looked upon the human race, before he himself came down to the earth. And today too, if any soul seeks him much, she will merit much of mercy; since to him who seeks much, much is owed. If therefore any soul should seek him more earnestly, she hears his voice from afar: and although she seeks him from others, she hears his voice before those very ones from whom she seeks. She sees him coming to her with a leap, that is, hastening and running 1882Aand leaping over those who with feeble heart cannot take in his power; then peering through the riddles of the prophets, by reading them and holding fast their words; for she sees him peering, but as it were through a window, not as though he were yet present. She sees him raised above the nets. What is this? Unless perhaps because those nets are nets to us, they are not nets to him, in that that soul is still within things sensible and worldly, which are wont to catch the mind of man and to wrap it in a certain fold of their own.

40.

En ipse stat post parietem nostrum, respiciens per fenestras, prospiciens per retia. Viderat enim Ecclesia vel anima justi salientem sicut hinniculum super montes, et repente primum aspiciens post parietem domus suae, prospicientem per fenestras, 1882Beminentem per retia, exsultat et gaudet, quia etiam ipsa amatur a Sponso. Qui tamquam et ipse vulneratur charitatis decore, dilectae cum primum abesset, ad oscula rogatus adfuerit (rogatur enim cum dicitur (Cant. I, 1): Osculetur me ab osculis oris sui ), deinde preces blanditiasque sponsae non spreverit, dilecta sibi ubera praebentis; atque in interiora domus eam benignus induxerit: deinde tamquam lascivienti ludens amore, quia vellet pertentare sensus amantis, saepe egressus ut quaereretur a Sponsa, saepe regressus ut invitaretur ad oscula, astiterit post parietem, prospexerit per fenestras, eminens super retia; ut non totus abesset, nec quasi totus intraret, et ipse ad se sponsam vocaret; ut veniendi ad se invicem fierent gratiora commercia, amoremque 1882Cmutuis adolerent sermonibus. 1567

40.

Behold, he himself stands behind our wall, looking through the windows, peering through the nets. [Vulg. Cant 2:9 prospiciens per cancellos, "looking through the lattices"] For the Church, or the soul of the just man, had seen him leaping like a fawn upon the mountains, and, first catching sight of him suddenly behind the wall of her house, peering through the windows, 1882Braised above the nets, she exults and rejoices, because she too is loved by the Bridegroom. And he, as though he himself also were wounded by the beauty of charity, when at first he was away from his beloved, was present at her asking for kisses (for he is asked when it is said (Cant. I, 1): Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth ); then did not spurn the prayers and blandishments of the bride as she offered him her breasts; and kindly led her into the inner parts of the house; then, playing as with a wanton love, because he wished to try the senses of her who loved him, going out often that he might be sought by the Bride, returning often that he might be invited to kisses, stood behind the wall, peered through the windows, raised above the nets; that he might neither be wholly absent, nor as it were wholly enter in, and that he himself might call the bride to himself; that the exchanges of coming to one another might become the more welcome, and that they might kindle love 1882Cwith mutual words. 1567

41.

(Vers. 10.) Exsurge, inquit, veni, proxima mea, formosa mea, columba mea; et tu si habeas fundatum parietem, non illum medium qui domus unius separet membra, sed aedificatum supra fundamentum apostolorum et prophetarum, ut compaginata ejus structura crescat in templum, nec disterminet ejus interna, sed muniat. Si habeas ergo in te aedificationem Dei, et ad Orientem pateant semper fenestrae tuae, venit Verbum, Stat post parietem tuum (Cant. II, 9); oculi enim Domini super justos (Psal. XXXIII, 16); prospicit per fenestras tuas (Cant. II, 9).

41.

(Vers. 10.) Rise up, he says, come, my neighbor, my well-formed one, my dove; [Vulg. Cant 2:10 surge, propera, amica mea, columba mea, formosa mea, et veni, "arise, make haste, my love, my dove, my beautiful one, and come"] and thou too, if thou hast a wall well founded, not that middle wall which divides the members of one house, but one built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets, so that its framed structure may grow into a temple, and may not sever its inner parts but fortify them. If therefore thou hast in thee the building of God, and thy windows stand ever open toward the East, the Word comes, He stands behind thy wall (Cant. II, 9); for the eyes of the Lord are upon the just (Psal. XXXIII, 16); he peers through thy windows (Cant. II, 9).

42.

Quae sunt fenestrae istae? Legimus fenestras de quibus dicit Hieremias: Intravit mors per fenestras 1882D(Jerem. IX, 21), per quas intravit avaritia, intravit libido. Oculus tuus fenestra est. Si videris mulierem ad concupiscendum eam, intravit mors per oculum tuum: si videris possessionem viduae, vel thesauros minoris, et tuas excitaveris cupiditates, Intravit mors per fenestram. Sicut ergo per haec intrat mors, intrat et vita: si decorem puellae aspiciens sacrae, venereris Dominum Jesum, quod in teneros annos venerit aetas senectutis, vita immaculata, et ipse filiam tuam offeras; ut pio consecretur velamine: si possessionem minoris non tamquam sollicitus invasor aspicias, sed quasi parens sedulus religioso tuearis 1883Aaffectu: per has fenestras prospicit Christus, ut vocet sponsam.

42.

What are these windows? We read of the windows of which Jeremiah says: Death has entered through the windows 1882D(Jerem. IX, 21) [Vulg. quia ascendit mors per fenestras nostras, "for death is come up through our windows"], through which avarice entered, lust entered. Thine eye is a window. If thou hast looked upon a woman to lust after her, death has entered through thine eye: if thou hast looked upon a widow's estate, or the treasures of a minor, and hast stirred up thy covetings, Death has entered through the window. As therefore death enters through these, life enters too: if, looking upon the comeliness of a consecrated girl, thou reverencest the Lord Jesus, in that the age of old age has come into her tender years, a life unspotted, and thou thyself offerest thy daughter, that she may be consecrated with the holy veil; if thou lookest upon a minor's estate not like an anxious invader, but guardest it like a diligent parent with religious 1883Aaffection: through these windows Christ peers, that he may call the bride.

43.

Eminens super retia: bene eminens; quia solus est quem retia non involverint peccatorum. Omnes intra retia eramus, immo adhuc intra retia sumus; quia nemo sine peccato nisi solus Jesus, quem non cognoscentem peccatum, peccatum pro nobis fecit Pater, etenim tradidit eum laqueis, tradidit eum retibus: mittens eum non in peccato in quo erant omnes homines, sed in similitudinem carnis peccati, ut de peccato damnaret peccatum in carne. Peccatum erat caro, secundum illud quia haereditario erat damnata maledicto, peccatum erat illecebra et ministra peccati. Venit Dominus Jesus, et in carne peccato obnoxia militiam virtutis exercuit: facta sunt membra 1883Bnostra jam non arma libidinis, sed arma virtutis; namque ubi erant incentiva libidinis, ibi nunc sunt domicilia castitatis. Hic ergo venit ad laqueos, sed voluntarius: venit ad retia, sed securus. Plena erant omnia retibus, referta laqueis, audi dicentem: In via hac quam ambulabam, absconderunt superbi laqueum mihi (Psal. CXLI, 4). Et in libro sapientiae Sirach moneris ut cognoscas quia in medio laqueorum ambulas (Eccli. IX, 20). Quot vitia, tot retia: quot peccata, tot laquei.

43.

Raised above the nets: rightly raised; because he alone is the one whom the nets of sins have not enwrapped. We were all within the nets, nay rather we are still within the nets; because no one is without sin save Jesus alone, whom, knowing no sin, the Father made sin for us; for indeed he delivered him to the snares, delivered him to the nets: sending him not in the sin in which all men were, but in the likeness of sinful flesh, that of sin he might condemn sin in the flesh. The flesh was sin, according to that saying, because it had been condemned by a hereditary curse; sin was the enticement and the handmaid of sin. The Lord Jesus came, and in flesh liable to sin he practiced the warfare of virtue: our members 1883Bhave been made now no longer weapons of lust, but weapons of virtue; for where the incitements of lust were, there now are the dwellings of chastity. Here therefore he came to the snares, but willingly: he came to the nets, but secure. All things were full of nets, crammed with snares; hear him who says: In this way wherein I walked, the proud have hidden a snare for me (Psal. CXLI, 4) [Vulg. Ps 141:4 in via hac qua ambulabam absconderunt laqueum mihi, without superbi, "the proud"]. And in the book of the wisdom of Sirach thou art admonished to know that thou walkest in the midst of snares (Eccli. IX, 20). As many vices, so many nets: as many sins, so many snares.

44.

Haereditarii jam te nexus tenebant, venit ad laqueos Jesus, ut Adam solveret: venit liberare quod perierat. Omnes retibus tenebamur, nullus alium eruere poterat, cum se ipsum non posset eruere. Talis ergo necessarius fuit, quem vincula generationis 1883Chumanae delictis obnoxia non tenerent, non cepisset avaritia, non ligasset dolus. Is solus erat Jesus, qui cum hujus carnis se circumdedisset vinculis, captus non erat, non erat alligatus: sed dirumpens ea atque dissolvens, ad se magis vocavit Ecclesiam, Prospiciens per laqueos, eminens super retia; ut et ipsa disceret vinculis non teneri. Denique eo usque non longe a vinculis fuit, ut pro nobis subiret et mortem, sed tamen non servus mortis effectus, sed liber inter mortuos; liber enim erat qui solvendae mortis potestatem habebat. Denique ipse te doceat qui ait: Solvite templum hoc, et in triduo resuscitabo illud (Joan. II, 19). Audiamus itaque quid dicat:

44.

Already the hereditary bonds held thee; Jesus came to the snares, that he might loose Adam: he came to set free what had perished. We were all held by the nets, no one could rescue another, since he could not rescue himself. Such a one therefore was needful, whom the chains of human generation, liable to offenses, 1883Cwould not hold, whom avarice had not taken, whom guile had not bound. That one alone was Jesus, who, though he had surrounded himself with the bonds of this flesh, was not captured, was not fettered: but bursting them and dissolving them, he called the Church the more to himself, Peering through the snares, raised above the nets; that she too might learn not to be held by chains. In fact he was so far from being far from the chains, that for us he underwent even death, yet not made a slave of death, but free among the dead; for he was free who had the power of loosing death. In fine, let him himself teach thee who says: Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up again (Joan. II, 19) [Vulg. John 2:19 et in tribus diebus excitabo illud, "and in three days I will raise it up"]. Let us hear, then, what he says:

45.

(Vers. 11 - 13). Exsurge, veni proxima mea; hoc est, surge a mortuis, exsurge a vinculis, quibus 1883Dcircumdata tenebaris. Exsurge, quia ego resurrexi tibi: solve vincula iniquitatis, quia ego jam solvi tibi: veni, quia jam retia soluta sunt, Virgo peperit, puer natus ex virgine est, nihil debet muliebri haereditati, quasi filius mulieris non tenetur: cerne medium parietem maceriae jam solutum, qui interiorum affectuum concordiam dividebat, et in diversum excitabat dissensiones corporalium passionum. Veni ergo secura, cupio videre faciem tuam, et audire vocem tuam: veni, ut jam me per retia non videas, sed facie ad faciem vultibus amatoris dilecta potiaris.

45.

(Vers. 11 - 13). Rise up, come, my neighbor; that is, rise from the dead, rise up from the chains by which thou wast held 1883Dencompassed. Rise up, for I have risen again for thee: loose the bonds of iniquity, for I have already loosed them for thee: come, for already the nets are loosed, the Virgin has borne, a child is born of a virgin, he owes nothing to the inheritance of woman, as the son of a woman he is not held: behold the middle wall of the enclosure now loosed, which divided the concord of the inward affections, and stirred up in diverse directions the dissensions of bodily passions. Come therefore secure; I long to see thy face and to hear thy voice: come, that thou mayest no longer see me through nets, but, beloved, mayest enjoy face to face the countenance of thy lover.

46.

Et hic ista ethice decursa sint. Mystica autem 1884Ailla si possimus, vel linea intueamur extrema. Sedebat intra domum Sponsa devota Domino, intra parietem Legis et 1568 prophetarum, spiritalium lapidum exaedificatione fundatum, qui claudebat atque vallabat domum regiam, plenam jucunditatis atque laetitiae. Mirabatur thesauros regios, et intuebatur sollicite, cupiens sapientiam quae has sibi divitias demonstraret, adsciscere. In secreto erat, sed secreti ipsius interpretem requirebat. Venit Dominus Jesus saliens super montes. Nobis sero videbatur venire, sed ille properabat. Saliebat denique et transiliebat, ut Judaeorum corporalia et saxosa dogmata transiliret. Astitit post parietem domus quae erat in veteri Testamento, prospiciens per Legis fenestram, et prophetarum cavernas. 1884B

46.

And let these things be run through thus far in the moral sense. But those 1884Amystical things, if we can, let us behold at least in their outermost line. The Bride was sitting within the house, devoted to the Lord, within the wall of the Law and 1568 of the prophets, founded upon a building of spiritual stones, which shut in and walled about the royal house, full of delight and gladness. She marveled at the royal treasures, and gazed upon them anxiously, longing to take to herself the wisdom which might show her these riches. She was in secret, but she was seeking the interpreter of that very secret. The Lord Jesus came, leaping upon the mountains. To us he seemed to come late, but he was hastening. He was leaping, in fine, and leaping over, that he might leap over the bodily and stony doctrines of the Jews. He stood behind the wall of the house which was in the Old Testament, peering through the window of the Law and the caverns of the prophets. 1884B

47.

Nondum erant domus illius aperta vestibula, nondum claves sapientiae portarum claustra patefecerant, quibus Legis claudebantur interna; de superiore tamen, hoc est, spiritali parte prospectans Ecclesiam vocat, ut per Legem et prophetas, Evangelii vertice sublimis assurgens, retia Judaicae interpretationis, et nodos intrepido calcet vestigio. Ideoque Proxima vocatur, ut adhaereat Christo, mundana non quaerat: ideo Formosa, ut speciosos evangelizantium pedes perferat: ideo Columba, ut spiritalia petat, terrestria derelinquat.

47.

Not yet were the vestibules of that house open, not yet had the keys of wisdom laid open the barriers of the gates by which the inner parts of the Law were shut; yet from the higher part, that is, the spiritual part, looking forth he calls the Church, that she, rising sublime through the Law and the prophets to the summit of the Gospel, may tread with fearless foot upon the nets and the knots of Jewish interpretation. And therefore she is called Neighbor, that she may cleave to Christ and not seek worldly things: therefore Well-formed, that she may bear the shapely feet of those who preach the Gospel: therefore Dove, that she may seek spiritual things and leave earthly things behind.

48.

Ecce, inquit, hiems proeteriit, imber abiit, discessit sibi, flores visi sunt in terra, tempus secandi advenit. Ante adventum Christi hiems erat: venit Christus, fecit aestatem. Tunc omnia erant florum 1884Cindiga, nuda virtutum: passus est Christus, et omnia coeperunt novae gratiae fecundari germinibus. Imber abiit luxuriae profluentis, et nubila tetris orta flagitiis aestiva jam purae conscientiae serenitate laxantur. Ideo non evadunt quorum fuga in hieme fit; quia non sequuntur Domini passionem, non tollunt crucem suam, et Christum sequuntur.

48.

Behold, he says, the winter is past, the rain is gone, it has departed to itself, flowers have been seen in the earth, the time of cutting is come. [Vulg. Cant 2:11-12 jam enim hiems transiit; imber abiit, et recessit. Flores apparuerunt in terra nostra; tempus putationis advenit, "for now the winter is past; the rain is gone and departed. The flowers have appeared in our land; the time of pruning is come"] Before the coming of Christ it was winter: Christ came, he made summer. Then all things were destitute of flowers, 1884Cbare of virtues: Christ suffered, and all things began to be made fruitful with the buds of a new grace. The rain of overflowing wantonness is gone, and the clouds risen from foul disgraces are now dispersed in the summer serenity of a pure conscience. Therefore they do not escape whose flight is made in winter; because they do not follow the passion of the Lord, they do not take up their cross, and they follow Christ.

49.

Imber impedit flores: at nunc flores videntur in terra. Boni flores apostoli, qui diversorum scriptorum suorum atque operum fuderunt odorem. Tempus secandi advenit, quando matura in horreis frumenta conduntur; et qui metit, mercedem accipit. Vox turturis audita est, quia invenit sibi nidum; Ecclesia enim domus est castitatis. Ficus quae propter infecunditatem jubebatur excidi, fructus 1884Dferre jam coepit. Vinea translata ex Aegypto, jam non maceriis destructa depositis, incursatur a bestiis; nec informis sentibus, sed odora jam floribus.

49.

Rain hinders flowers: but now flowers are seen in the earth. Good flowers are the apostles, who poured out the fragrance of their diverse writings and works. The time of cutting is come, when ripe grain is stored in the barns; and he who reaps receives a reward. The voice of the turtledove has been heard, because she has found a nest for herself; for the Church is the house of chastity. The fig tree, which for its unfruitfulness was ordered to be cut down, has now begun to bear fruit. 1884DThe vine carried over out of Egypt, destroyed by the casting down of its walls, is now not assailed by beasts; nor is it unshapely with brambles, but now fragrant with flowers.

50.

Exsurge, inquit, veni, proxima mea, formosa mea, columba mea, perfecta mea; proxima utique fidei desiderio, formosa decore virtutis, columba gratia spirituali; pennae enim columbae deargentatae aeternam illam significant potestatem, et columbae volatus Spiritus sancti praesentiam declaravit. Vocat igitur ad se eam Christus ut veniat, quoniam spiritualibus jam pretiis dotata veniebat. Quia ecce, inquit, hiems proeteriit, imber abiit, discesit 1885Asibi, flores visi in terra. Videte quemadmodum sancta invitetur Ecclesia. Hiems, inquit, discessit sibi, ne hiemem nuda formidet, hiemem non temporis, sed infirmitatis, quae agrum animae fecundum omni flore dispoliat. Est enim hiems non terreni solis, est hiems mentis, quando animo frigus illabitur, quando vapor animi vanescit, quando solvitur vigor sensus, quando nimius humor exundat, et mentem gravat, quando interior caligat aspectus. Et ideo ait Dominus: Videte ne fiat fuga vestra hieme vel sabbato (Matth. XXIV, 20); bonum enim ut tunc dies judicii vel mortis adveniat, cum viget animi blanda temperies, cum coeleste mysterium serena luce resplendet, cum cor nostrum ardet in nobis. Tunc enim Christus est praesens, sicut testatur 1885Bin Evangelio Ammaon et Cleophas dicentes: Nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis in via, cum aperuisset in nobis Scripturas (Luc. XXIV, 32). Viget autem animus, quando etiam flos videtur in terris. Quis est iste flos boni odoris, nisi ille qui dixit: Ego flos campi et lilium convallium (Supra, vers. 1): de quo et in Esaia scriptum est: Exiet virga de radice Jesse, et virga ex radice ascendet, et flos ex virga exiet (Esai. XI, 1). Radix utique familia Judaeorum est, virga Maria, flos Mariae Christus: qui quando nostra resplendet in terra, et in agro animae redolet, vel in Ecclesia sua vernat, nec frigus possumus timere, nec pluviam vereri, sed exspectare judicii diem. Et ideo Ecclesia ut hunc florem videret, omni studio festinabat, sicut ipsa in posterioribus 1569 1885Ctestatur dicens: In cubili noctibus quaesivi quem diligit anima mea (Cant. III, 1).

50.

Rise up, he says, come, my neighbor, my well-formed one, my dove, my perfect one; neighbor indeed by the desire of faith, well-formed by the comeliness of virtue, dove by spiritual grace; for the wings of the dove covered with silver signify that eternal power, and the flight of the dove declared the presence of the Holy Spirit. Christ therefore calls her to himself that she may come, since she was already coming endowed with spiritual dowries. For behold, he says, the winter is past, the rain is gone, it has departed 1885Ato itself, flowers seen in the earth. See in what manner the holy Church is invited. The winter, he says, has departed to itself, lest, being naked, she dread the winter, a winter not of the season but of infirmity, which strips the fruitful field of the soul of every flower. For there is a winter not of the earthly sun, there is a winter of the mind, when cold glides into the soul, when the warmth of the spirit fades away, when the vigor of sense is loosened, when excessive humour overflows and weighs down the mind, when the inward sight grows dark. And therefore the Lord says: See that your flight be not in winter or on the sabbath (Matth. XXIV, 20) [Vulg. Matt 24:20 orate autem ut non fiat fuga vestra in hieme vel sabbato, "but pray that your flight be not in winter or on the sabbath"]; for it is good that the day of judgment or of death should come then, when the kindly temperateness of the soul is in vigor, when the heavenly mystery shines back with serene light, when our heart burns within us. For then Christ is present, as Ammaon and Cleophas testify 1885Bin the Gospel, saying: Was not our heart burning within us in the way, when he had opened the Scriptures in us (Luc. XXIV, 32). And the soul is in vigor when the flower too is seen in the earth. Who is this flower of good fragrance, save he who said: I am the flower of the field and the lily of the valleys (Supra, vers. 1): of whom also it is written in Isaiah: A rod shall go forth from the root of Jesse, and a rod shall ascend from the root, and a flower shall go forth from the rod (Esai. XI, 1) [Vulg. Isa 11:1 et egredietur virga de radice Jesse, et flos de radice ejus ascendet, "and there shall come forth a rod out of the root of Jesse, and a flower shall rise up out of his root," with no middle member]. The root indeed is the family of the Jews, the rod is Mary, the flower of Mary is Christ: and when he shines forth in our earth, and is fragrant in the field of the soul, or blooms in his own Church, we can neither fear cold nor dread rain, but may await the day of judgment. And therefore the Church, that she might see this flower, was hastening with all zeal, as she herself testifies 1569 1885Cin what follows, saying: In the bed by nights I sought him whom my soul loves (Cant. III, 1) [Vulg. Cant 3:1 in lectulo meo per noctes quaesivi quem diligit anima mea, "in my little bed by night I sought him whom my soul loveth"].

50.

Ficus protulit grossos suos. Apta Synagogae arboris istius comparatio est, quia sicut arbor ista foliis redundans fluentibus, spem possessoris sui cassa speratorum proventuum exspectatione destituit: ita etiam in Synagoga, dum doctores ejus operibus infecundi, verbis tantum velut foliis redundantibus gloriantur, inanis umbra Legis exsuperat; spes autem falsi exspectata proventus, populi vota credentis illudit.

50.

The fig tree has put forth its green figs. The comparison of that tree is apt for the Synagogue, because just as that tree, overflowing with flowing leaves, disappoints the hope of its owner by an empty expectation of the yields that were hoped for: so also in the Synagogue, while its teachers, unfruitful in works, glory only in words as in overflowing leaves, the empty shade of the Law prevails; and the hope of a false yield, once awaited, mocks the prayers of the believing people.

51.

Est etiam in natura arboris quo magis credas Synagogae speciem comparatione hujus expressam. Etenim si diligenter inquiras, discretam ab usu arborum caeterarum consuetudinem naturae hujus invenies; 1885Dnamque aliae florem ferunt antequam fructum, pomaque ventura praenuntio sui flore designant: haec sola ab initio poma germinat pro floribus: in aliis flos decutitur et poma nascuntur; in hac poma decidunt, ut poma succedant. Ergo priora illa cassa fructuum, vice floris emergunt: ita praecoci quodam nascendi usu, quae ordinem naturae nesciunt, beneficium naturae servare non possunt. Etenim qua se medio trudere de cortice gemma consuevit, ea minutissima quaeque hujus generis poma prorumpunt: de quibus legimus in Canticis 1886Acanticorum: Ficus protulit grossos suos. Itaque caeteris albentibus primo vere virgultis, sola ficus proprio nescit flore canescere; eo forte quod nullus sit istis maturior usus in pomis. Namque succedentibus aliis quae quasi degenerantia despuuntur, et arenti infirma radice, renovatis quibus sit succus utilior, exuuntur. Manent tamen aliqua perrara, nec decidunt, quibus hic proventus arriserit, ut de medio duarum virgarum claviculo brevi erumpente promergant, quo geminis tecta praesidiis, tamquam naturae parentis gremio, succi fotu plenioris inolescant. Ea clementioris aurae provocata temperie: et prolixioribus adulta temporibus, ubi silvestrem animum succi prioris exuerint, specie caeteris et maturitatis gratia praeferuntur. 1886B

51.

There is also in the nature of the tree that whereby thou mayest the more believe that the likeness of the Synagogue is expressed by comparison with it. For indeed, if thou inquire diligently, thou wilt find the habit of this tree's nature distinct from the practice of the rest; 1885Dfor others bear the flower before the fruit, and mark out the apples that are to come by the flower that heralds them: this one alone from the beginning buds forth apples in place of flowers; in the others the flower is shaken off and the apples are born, in this one the apples fall off, that apples may succeed them. Therefore those earlier ones, void of fruit, come forth in the place of the flower: thus, by a certain premature habit of being born, they that know not the order of nature cannot keep the benefit of nature. For indeed where the bud is wont to thrust itself out from the middle of the bark, there each of the tiniest apples of this kind breaks forth: concerning which we read in the Song 1886Aof Songs: The fig tree has put forth its green figs. And so, while the other shoots whiten in the first spring, the fig alone knows not how to grow white with a flower of its own; perhaps because there is for these no riper use in their apples. For when others succeed, which are spat out as though degenerate, and the weak root being dry, they are stripped away, those being renewed whose sap is more serviceable. Yet some very few remain, and do not fall, upon which this yield has smiled, so that they push forth from the middle of two twigs by a short tendril breaking out, whereby, covered by twin defenses, as though in the lap of nature their parent, they grow up by the fostering of a fuller sap. These, called forth by the mildness of a gentler air, and grown to maturity through longer seasons, when they have put off the wild temper of their former sap, are preferred to the rest in appearance and in the grace of ripeness. 1886B

52.

Specta nunc cultus et animos Judaeorum, qui quasi primi fructus male feracis Synagogae, quasi labentis similitudine corruerunt, ut mansura supra aevum nostri generis poma succederent. Etenim primus Synagogae populus, quasi radice operum infirmus arenti, ubertatem sapientiae naturalis haurire non potuit. Et ideo velut inutilis decidit fructus, ut quasi ex iisdem claviculis arboris fructuosae, de pinguedine religionis antiquae novus Ecclesiae populus emergeret. Ergo ille qui erat, esse desivit, ut iste inciperet qui non erat: primi tamen ex Israel, quos naturae validioris ramus extulerat, sub umbra Legis et crucis, in utriusque sinu succo gemino colorati, grossi maturescentis exemplo, pulcherrimorum gratia fructuum caeteris praestiterunt, quibus 1886Cdicitur: Sedebitis super duodecim thronos, judicantes duodecim tribus Israel (Matth. XIX, 28).

52.

Look now upon the observances and the minds of the Jews, who, as it were the first fruits of the ill-bearing Synagogue, fell after the likeness of what is slipping away, that apples of our kind, destined to abide beyond the age, might succeed them. For indeed the first people of the Synagogue, weak as it were with a dry root of works, could not draw in the richness of natural wisdom. And therefore it fell like useless fruit, that as it were from the same tendrils of a fruitful tree, out of the richness of the ancient religion, the new people of the Church might come forth. Therefore he who was ceased to be, that this one might begin who was not: yet the first out of Israel, whom the branch of a stronger nature had lifted up, under the shadow of the Law and of the cross, colored in the bosom of each by a twin sap, after the pattern of the ripening green fig, surpassed the rest in the grace of most beautiful fruits, to whom 1886Cit is said: You shall sit upon twelve thrones, judging the twelve tribes of Israel (Matth. XIX, 28) [Vulg. Matt 19:28 sedebitis et vos super sedes duodecim, "you also shall sit upon twelve seats"].

53.

Vineae florentes dederunt odorem suum (Sup., v. 5). Bene per vineam Ecclesia designatur; ut enim vitis maritatur arboribus, ita corpus animae, anima quoque corpori conjungitur: ut vinea dum ligatur, erigitur, et recisa non minuitur, sed augetur; ita plebs sancta dum ligatur, exuitur: dum humiliatur, attollitur: dum reciditur, coronatur. Quin etiam ut tener surculus, veteri abscissus ab arbore, in alterius fetu radicis inseritur: ita populus hic sanctus surculi veteris cicatricibus enodatis, in novo illo crucis signo tamquam gremio piae parentis fotus inolescit: Spiritusque sanctus in hoc carcereum corpus infusus, velut in altas demissus 1886Dterrarum scrobes, aquae salutaris irriguo diluit quidquid est fetidum, membrorumque nostrorum habitus in coelestem erigit disciplinam.

53.

The vines in flower have given their fragrance (Sup., v. 5). Well is the Church designated by the vine; for as the vine is wedded to trees, so the body is joined to the soul, and the soul also to the body: as the vine, while it is bound, is raised up, and when cut back is not diminished but increased; so the holy people, while it is bound, is stripped: while it is humbled, it is lifted up: while it is cut back, it is crowned. Nay more, as a tender shoot, cut off from the old tree, is grafted into the offspring of another root: so this holy people, the scars of the old shoot being smoothed away, grows up cherished in that new sign of the cross as in the lap of a devoted parent; and the Holy Spirit, poured into this prison-like body, as though let down into deep 1886Dtrenches of the earth, washes away by the watering of the water of salvation whatever is foul, and raises the bearing of our members into heavenly discipline.

54.

Hanc vineam diligens agricola fodere, rigare, contendere consuevit, eruderatisque terrenae molis aggeribus, opera corporis nostri nunc sole torrere, nunc imbre perfundere, stipare agrum solitus, ne sentibus gemma laedatur, ne luxuriet umbra foliorum, verborumque infructuosa jactantia virtutibus obumbrando, maturitatem naturalis indolis impediat. 1887A

54.

This vine the diligent husbandman is wont to dig, to water, to strive over; and, the mounds of earthy mass being cleared away, he is accustomed now to scorch the works of our body with the sun, now to drench them with rain, and to hem the field close, lest the bud be hurt by brambles, lest the shade of the leaves run riot, and lest the unfruitful boasting of words, by overshadowing the virtues, hinder the ripeness of the natural disposition. 1887A

55.

(Vers. 14.) Et veni, inquit, columba mea, in tegimento petrae, juxta praemunitionem; hoc est, veni juxta Evangelium: propugnacula fidei tuae, gesta sunt Christi: muri tui subsidia, 1570 verba sunt Domini: passio Dominici corporis, tua virtus est.

55.

(Vers. 14.) And come, he says, my dove, in the covering of the rock, beside the outwork; [Vulg. Cant 2:14 columba mea in foraminibus petrae, in caverna maceriae, "my dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the wall"] that is, come beside the Gospel: the bulwarks of thy faith are the deeds of Christ; the supports of thy wall 1570 are the words of the Lord; the passion of the Lord's body is thy strength.

56.

Ostende mihi faciem tuam, et insinua vocem tuam; quia vox tua suavis est, et facies tua decora. Suavis est vox tua; quia ore fit confessio ad salutem: et decora facies; quia quae non erubescit auctorem, non confunditur redemptore. Ostendit ergo faciem suam, signaculum crucis praeferens: et insinuat vocem suam, auctoritatem praedicationis assumens. Integimento enim corporis Christi, quo redemptus est a peccato, munimentum gratiae spiritalis invenit; ut salutaria sibi et sentiat et loquatur. Suavis 1887Bergo vox, quae in divinis testimoniis loquebatur: Decora facies, quae in conspectu regum non confundebatur.

56.

Show me thy face, and make thy voice known; for thy voice is sweet, and thy face comely. [Vulg. Cant 2:14 sonet vox tua in auribus meis: vox enim tua dulcis, "let thy voice sound in mine ears: for thy voice is sweet"] Sweet is thy voice; for with the mouth confession is made unto salvation: and comely the face; because she who is not ashamed of her author is not confounded at her Redeemer. She shows therefore her face, bearing before her the seal of the cross; and she makes her voice known, taking up the authority of preaching. For by the covering of the body of Christ, whereby he was redeemed from sin, he finds the defense of spiritual grace, so that he may both perceive and speak the things that are for his salvation. Sweet 1887Btherefore is the voice, which spoke in the divine testimonies: Comely the face, which was not confounded in the sight of kings.

57.

Exsurge, inquit, veni, proxima mea; id est, exsurge a delectationibus mundi, exsurge a terrenis, et veni ad me, quae adhuc laboras, et onerata es, quae sollicita es quae sunt mundi: veni supra mundum, veni ad me, quia ego vici mundum: veni prope me, jam pulchra aeternae vitae decore, jam columba, id est, mitis atque mansueta, jam tota plena gratiae spiritalis. Jure ergo jam retia timere non debet, cum is vocet ad se animam, qui tentamentis et retibus mundi non potuit capi. Nam cum homines in medio laqueorum ambulemus, per appetentiam escae retibus simul et laqueis obnoxii 1887Csumus. Ille in corpore positus retia non timebat, sed eminebat super retia, id est, supra tentamenta mundi et corporis passiones; immo et alios eminere faciebat.

57.

Rise up, he says, come, my neighbor; that is, rise up from the delights of the world, rise up from earthly things, and come to me, thou who still labourest and art laden, thou who art anxious for the things that are of the world: come above the world, come to me, for I have overcome the world: come near to me, now beautiful with the comeliness of eternal life, now a dove, that is, gentle and meek, now wholly full of spiritual grace. Rightly therefore she ought no longer to fear the nets, since he calls the soul to himself who could not be caught by the temptations and the nets of the world. For since we men walk in the midst of snares, through the appetite for food we are liable at once to nets and to snares. 1887CHe, set in a body, did not fear the nets, but was raised above the nets, that is, above the temptations of the world and the passions of the body; nay, he made others also to be raised above them.

58.

Exsurge, veni, proxima mea, noli timere retia, jam praeteriit hiems. Venit pascha, venit indulgentia, venit remissio peccatorum, cessavit tentatio, imber abiit, procella abiit et quassatio. Ante adventum Christi hiems erat, post adventum Christi flores sunt, unde ait: Flores visi sunt in terra. Ubi ante spinae, ibi nunc flores; Tempus, inquit, secandi advenit. Ubi ante desertum, ibi messis est. Vox turturis audita est in terra nostra: bene addidit propheta nostra, quasi admirans, quod ubi ante impudicitia, ibi castitas. Ficus protulit grossos suos. 1887DQuae quasi infructuosa jubebatur abscindi, haec fructus afferre jam coepit. Sed quid dubitas, quia grossos dixit? Priores discutit, ut posteriores meliores afferat: sicut Synagogae fructus abjicitur, renovatur autem Ecclesiae.

58.

Rise up, come, my neighbor, fear not the nets, already the winter is past. The passover is come, indulgence is come, the remission of sins is come, temptation has ceased, the rain is gone, the storm and the buffeting are gone. Before the coming of Christ it was winter; after the coming of Christ there are flowers, whence he says: Flowers have been seen in the earth. Where before there were thorns, there now are flowers. The time, he says, of cutting is come. Where before there was desert, there is harvest. The voice of the turtledove has been heard in our land: well did the prophet add our, as though marveling, that where before there was shamelessness, there is chastity. The fig tree has put forth its green figs. 1887DShe who as unfruitful was ordered to be cut down has now begun to bring forth fruit. But why dost thou doubt, because he said green figs? He shakes off the earlier ones, that he may bring forth later and better ones: as the fruit of the Synagogue is cast away, but that of the Church is renewed.

59.

Et quamvis plena tranquillitas sit, et adoleverint mysteria, iterum tamen dicit: Exsurge secura in tegimento petrae, id est, tuta praesidio passionis meae et fidei munimento. Suxerunt enim mel de petra et oleum de firma petra. Hoc integimento animae piorum indutae, jam nudae non sunt, et haec illis est 1888Apraemunitio. Ideoque et huic animae ait: Et veni, tu columba mea, in tegimento petrae juxta praemunitionem: ostende mihi faciem tuam, et insinua vocem tuam. Hortatur ad confidentiam, ut non erubescat crucem Christi, nec ejus signaculum. Hortatur ad confessionem, vult omnes insidias dimoveri; ut bonus fidei odor spiret, ut dies fulgeat, ut adversae noctis umbra non noceat; quoniam qui juxta Christum est, dicit: Nox praecessit, dies autem appropinquavit (Rom. XXIII, 12). Est et umbra saecularium quae praeterit, et dies coelestium Christus, qui sanctis suis lucet.

59.

And although there is full tranquillity, and the mysteries have grown to their strength, yet again he says: Rise up secure in the covering of the rock, that is, safe by the protection of my passion and by the defense of faith. For they sucked honey out of the rock and oil out of the firm rock. Clothed with this covering, the souls of the pious are no longer naked, and this is their 1888Aoutwork. And therefore to this soul also he says: And come thou, my dove, in the covering of the rock beside the outwork: show me thy face, and make thy voice known. He exhorts her to confidence, that she be not ashamed of the cross of Christ, nor of its seal. He exhorts her to confession; he wills that all snares be removed, that the good fragrance of faith may breathe, that the day may shine, that the shadow of the hostile night may not harm; since he who is beside Christ says: The night is passed, and the day has drawn near (Rom. XXIII, 12). There is also a shadow of worldly things which passes away, and the day of heavenly things is Christ, who shines for his saints.

60.

(Vers. 15.) Prendite nobis vulpes pusillas exterminantes vineas, ut vineae nostrae floreant. Scriptura eum hominem dicit, qui est ad imaginem et similitudinem 1888BDei: peccantem autem non hominem, sed aut serpentem, aut equum adhinnientem feminis, aut vulpeculam, aut jumentum vocare consuevit. De quorum uno ait Dominus: Ite, dicite vulpi illi (Luc. XIII, 32), hoc est, de Herode. Et alibi cum se fraudulenter interrogari adverteret, dixit: Vulpes foveas habent, et volucres coeli nidos, ubi requiescant (Matth. VIII, 20); eo quod fraudulenter in terrestrium passionum quibusdam latibulis demorentur.

60.

(Vers. 15.) Take for us the little foxes that destroy the vines, that our vines may flower. [Vulg. Cant 2:15 capite nobis vulpes parvulas quae demoliuntur vineas: nam vinea nostra floruit, "catch us the little foxes that destroy the vines: for our vineyard hath flourished"] Scripture calls him a man who is after the image and likeness 1888Bof God; but the sinner it is wont to call not a man, but either a serpent, or a horse neighing after females, or a little fox, or a beast of burden. Of one of these the Lord says: Go, tell that fox (Luc. XIII, 32), that is, of Herod. And elsewhere, when he perceived that he was being questioned deceitfully, he said: The foxes have dens, and the birds of heaven nests, where they may rest (Matth. VIII, 20) [Vulg. Matt 8:20 vulpes foveas habent, et volucres caeli nidos; Filius autem hominis non habet ubi caput reclinet, "the foxes have holes, and the birds of the air nests; but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head"]; in that they linger deceitfully in certain lurking-places of earthly passions.

61.

Unde etiam Samson binas vulpes sibi nexuit, ad quarum caudas alligavit faces, et dimisit eas per messes Allophylorum (Judic. XV): per hanc figuram significans quod improbi ac fraudulenti homines, et maxime haeretici, liberam linguam habeant ad latrandum: sed exitus impeditos, aut litigiosa principia, 1888Cfinem vero fraudis suae incendio deputatum. Ideoque et trecentas vulpes dimisit, eo quod perfidi crucis quidem praedicatione se commendare desiderant: sed mysterium ejus tenere non possunt, qui hac 1571 praedicatione composita, et falsa atque simulata, urere magis fructus conantur alienos: cum utique vera crux Domini non exurat aliena merita, sed fecundet.

61.

Whence Samson too bound foxes to himself two by two, to whose tails he tied torches, and sent them out through the harvests of the Allophyli (Judic. XV): signifying by this figure that wicked and deceitful men, and most of all heretics, have a tongue free for barking, but that their outcomes are hindered, or their beginnings quarrelsome, 1888Cwhile the end of their fraud is assigned to burning. And therefore he sent out three hundred foxes also, in that the faithless do indeed desire to commend themselves by the preaching of the cross, but cannot hold its mystery: they who, by this 1571 preaching made up, and false and feigned, attempt rather to burn up the fruits of others, whereas assuredly the true cross of the Lord does not burn up the merits of others, but makes them fruitful.

62.

Meritoque scriptum est: Prendite nobis vulpes pusillas, exterminantes vineas, ut vineae nostrae floreant. Quo ostenditur quod vel Dominus Jesus vel Ecclesia fraudulentorum dolos vineis suis exterminandos esse praecipiat; ne pusillulis vineis noceant, quia adultis jam vitibus nocere non possunt. Haereticus enim imperfectum tentare potest, non potest supplantare 1888Dperfectum.

62.

And deservedly is it written: Take for us the little foxes that destroy the vines, that our vines may flower. By which it is shown that either the Lord Jesus or the Church commands that the wiles of the deceitful be driven out from his vines, lest they harm the tiny vines, since they cannot harm vines already grown up. For a heretic can tempt the imperfect; he cannot supplant 1888Dthe perfect.

63.

(Vers. 16.) Frater meus mihi, et ego illi. Vox ista est virginis; nam immaculatum corpus a virili permixtione servare, et palmam castimoniae, limoso in corpore usque ad angelorum conversationem, custodia integritatis evehere, utilitas privata, laus est publica. Quam rarus in terris, qui hoc potest dicere, quam alienus a vitiis, quam segregatus ab omni labe peccati, qui nihil habeat cum saeculo commune, dicens: Dilectus meus mihi et ego illi, in medio uberum meorum commorabitur. Scilicet nihil 1889Amundi hujus sibi vindicet, cui non sit corporalium possessio cupiditatum, quem non inflammet libido, non stimulet avaritia, non lascivia effeminet, non luxuria decoloret, non sternat ambitio, non maceret invidia, non aliqua negotiorum saecularium cura sollicitet: verus minister altaris, Deo natus, non sibi; Levi enim, ut habet interpretatio, significat: Ipse mihi assumptus; significat: Et ipse meus; significat et tantum: Assumptus; significat: Et assumptus mihi.

63.

(Vers. 16.) My brother is mine, and I am his. [Vulg. Cant 2:16 dilectus meus mihi, et ego illi, "my beloved to me, and I to him"] This is the voice of a virgin; for to keep the body unspotted from mingling with a man, and, in a body of clay, to bear up the palm of chastity by the guardianship of integrity even to the converse of angels, is a private benefit and a public praise. How rare on earth is he who can say this, how estranged from vices, how set apart from every stain of sin, who has nothing in common with the world, saying: My beloved is mine and I am his, he shall abide in the midst of my breasts. [Vulg. Cant 1:12 inter ubera mea commorabitur, "he shall abide between my breasts" — the second member of the printed lemma follows this verse rather than 2:16] That is to say, let him claim nothing 1889Aof this world for himself, one who has no possession of bodily desires, whom lust does not inflame, avarice does not goad, wantonness does not unman, excess does not discolor, ambition does not lay low, envy does not waste, no care of secular business makes anxious: a true minister of the altar, born for God, not for himself; for Levi, as the interpretation has it, signifies: He himself taken up for me; it signifies: And he himself is mine; it signifies also merely: Taken up; it signifies: And taken up for me.

64.

Ipse idem, et mihi Levi et Deo est. Quomodo idem et mihi sacerdos et Deo est. Et mihi advocatus et Domini deprecator est: pro me offerens sacrificium, et idem se offerens Domino.
