Denique et alibi Levi interpretatione dicitur, Pro me; etenim si a me nominetur Levi, Levi pro me est: si pro me offerat, 1889Bpro me est: si pro me interveniat, pro me est: sin autem vocetur a Domino, ipse mihi dicitur: hoc est, non alii tributarius, non decimarius, non de possessione munificus. Ipse mihi, hoc est, tamquam abundans pro suis omnibus. Non ab hoc decimas quaero, non fructus, non dona, non munera; ipse mihi pro munere est, ipse est pro tributo. Non in possessione sua munificus mihi, sed ipse mihi possessio, ipse meus fructus, ipse meus sensus. Assumptus a me, vel assumptus mihi, non potest hoc sine divina esse gratia. Sicut enim possessio non potest mea esse, nisi emero eam: sic non potest esse Levi, nisi fuerit assumptus a Domino, cum enim assumptus fuerit, recte dicitur, ipse meus. Denique cum Moysi dictum esset, ut populo Judaeorum per tribus singulas divideret 1889Cincolatus, et distribueret singulis tribubus portionem: tribum Leviticam excepit Deus dicens filiis Levi: Non erit ei portio, neque sors in medio fratrum suorum; quia Dominus Deus pars eorum est (Deut. X, 9): et alibi: Ego Dominus portio eorum (Num. XVIII, 20).

64.

The very same man is Levi both for me and for God. How the same is a priest both for me and for God. And he is both my advocate and the Lord's intercessor: offering sacrifice for me, and the same man offering himself to the Lord. In fine, elsewhere too by the interpretation of Levi it is said, For me; for indeed if Levi be named by me, Levi is for me: if he offer for me, 1889Bhe is for me: if he intercede for me, he is for me: but if he be called by the Lord, he is said to be mine: that is, tributary to no other, tithe-paying to no other, bountiful to no other out of a possession. He himself is mine, that is, as one abounding in place of all his own goods. From him I seek no tithes, no fruits, no gifts, no offerings; he himself is to me in place of an offering, he himself in place of tribute. Not bountiful to me in his own possession, but he himself is my possession, he himself my fruit, he himself my understanding. Taken up by me, or taken up for me: this cannot be without divine grace. For as a possession cannot be mine unless I have bought it, so he cannot be Levi unless he has been taken up by the Lord; for when he has been taken up, rightly is it said, he himself is mine. In fine, when it had been said to Moses that he should divide the dwelling-places among the people of the Jews tribe by tribe, 1889Cand should distribute a portion to each several tribe, God excepted the Levitical tribe, saying to the sons of Levi: He shall have no portion, nor lot in the midst of his brethren; because the Lord God is their part (Deut. X, 9) [Vulg. Deut 10:9 non habuit Levi partem neque possessionem cum fratribus suis, quia ipse Dominus possessio ejus est]: and elsewhere: I the Lord am their portion (Num. XVIII, 20) [Vulg. Num 18:20 ego pars et hereditas tua in medio filiorum Israel, "I am thy part and inheritance in the midst of the children of Israel"].

65.

Negatur his terrena divisio, ut ipsi dum saecularem sibi non vindicant portionem, fiant coelestis possessio: vel hoc solum noverint possidere, hoc est, fidei et devotionis obsequium, multo ditiores quam qui possessionum suarum spatia ampla diffundunt. Quantumlibet enim fines suos extendant, et terra deficit, et mare includit insanas cupiditates, et pendunt tributa majora quam fructus sunt. At vero iste nihil possidens, nulli nisi Deo militat, supra terram 1889Dejus est portio, non cum terra deficit, non mari clauditur. Cujus portio Deus est, totius possessor est naturae. Pro agris igitur ipse sibi satis est, habens bonum fructum, qui numquam possit perire. Pro domibus satis est sibi, ut sit habitatio Domini, et templum Dei, quo nihil potest esse pretiosius. Quid enim pretiosius Deo? Aut quid deest huic viro qui potest dicere: Non contingat mihi gloriari nisi in cruce Domini nostri Jesu Christi, per quem mihi mundus crucifixus est, et ego mundo (Galat. VI, 4) ?

65.

An earthly division is denied to these, that they themselves, while they claim no secular portion for themselves, may become the possession of the heavenly one; or that they may know how to possess this only, that is, the obedience of faith and of devotion, being far richer than those who spread out the ample expanses of their possessions. For however far they extend their boundaries, both the land fails and the sea shuts in their mad desires, and they pay tributes greater than their fruits are. But this man, possessing nothing, serves as a soldier to none but God; his portion is above the earth, 1889Dit does not fail with the earth, it is not shut in by the sea. He whose portion is God is possessor of the whole of nature. In place of fields, therefore, he is enough for himself, having a good fruit which can never perish. In place of houses it is enough for him to be the dwelling of the Lord and the temple of God, than which nothing can be more precious. For what is more precious than God? Or what is lacking to this man who can say: Let it not befall me to glory save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through whom the world is crucified to me, and I to the world (Galat. VI, 4) [Vulg. Gal 6:14 mihi autem absit gloriari nisi in cruce, "but God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross"] ?

66.

Non potest in hoc princeps mundi hujus sibi 1890Aconsortium vindicare, in quo nihil suum invenit. Ideoque Dominus qui venit docere, ut fieret nobis Deus portio, ait: Venit enim hujus mundi princeps, et in me invenit nihil (Joan. XIV, 30). Meritoque imitatores sui esse volens, ait: Nolite possidere aurum, neque argentum, neque pecuniam (Matth. X, 9). Unde Petrus ostendens in Deo portionem sibi esse, non in saeculo, ait: Argentum et aurum non habeo, sed quod habeo, 1572 hoc tibi do: in nomine Jesu Christi Nazareni surge, et ambula (Act. III, 6); hoc est, haec est portio mea, portio mea Christus est: In nomine igitur Christi Jesu surge, et ambula (Ibid). Hoc est, in portione mea dives sum, in portione mea potens sum. Merito tales praesumo fructus istius portionis, ut salus et vita donetur; quia hoc est istius patrimonium portionis quam elegi mihi: Dilectus meus 1890Bmihi, et ego illi.

66.

The prince of this world cannot claim 1890Afellowship for himself in this man, in whom he finds nothing of his own. And therefore the Lord, who came to teach that God should become our portion, says: For the prince of this world comes, and in me he finds nothing (Joan. XIV, 30) [Vulg. John 14:30 et in me non habet quidquam, "and in me he hath not any thing"]. And deservedly, willing them to be imitators of himself, he says: Do not possess gold, nor silver, nor money (Matth. X, 9). Whence Peter, showing that his portion was in God, not in the world, says: Silver and gold I have not, but what I have, 1572 this I give thee: in the name of Jesus Christ of Nazareth rise up and walk (Act. III, 6) [Vulg. Acts 3:6 argentum et aurum non est mihi, "silver and gold I have none"]; that is, this is my portion, my portion is Christ: In the name therefore of Christ Jesus rise up and walk (Ibid). That is, in my portion I am rich, in my portion I am powerful. Deservedly do I look for such fruits of that portion, that health and life may be given; because this is the patrimony of that portion which I have chosen for myself: My beloved is mine, 1890Band I am his.

67.

Qui pascitur inter lilia. Lilium castitatem significat. Scit itaque bonus pastor, ubi pascat gregem suum, quae pabula ovibus suis expediant. Quae sunt pascua nostra, hoc est, fidelium, nisi Christus? In cujus pascuis se locatum Propheta laetatus est, dicens: In loco pascuae, ibi me collocavit (Psal. XXII, 2); ibi enim nos pascit et reficit. Bona pascua, sacramenta divina sunt. Carpis illic novum florem, qui bonum odorem dedit resurrectionis: carpis lilium, in quo sit splendor aeternitatis: carpis rosam, hoc est, Dominici corporis sanguinem. Bona etiam pascua, libri sunt Scripturarum coelestium, in quibus quotidiana lectione pascimur, in quibus recreamur ac reficimur; cum ea quae scripta sunt degustamus, 1890Cvel summo ore libata frequentius ruminamus. His pascuis grex Domini saginatur. Bona etiam Christi pascua montes convallium; nam et in his pascit Christus sicut capreolus aut hinnulus cervorum, hoc est, in splendoribus sanctorum.

67.

Who is pastured among the lilies. The lily signifies chastity. The good shepherd therefore knows where he may pasture his flock, what fodder is fit for his sheep. What are our pastures, that is, the pastures of the faithful, save Christ? In whose pastures the Prophet rejoiced that he was set, saying: In a place of pasture, there he has set me (Psal. XXII, 2); for there he pastures and refreshes us. Good pastures are the divine sacraments. There thou pluckest the new flower, which has given the good fragrance of the resurrection: thou pluckest the lily, in which is the splendor of eternity: thou pluckest the rose, that is, the blood of the Lord's body. Good pastures too are the books of the heavenly Scriptures, in which we are fed by daily reading, in which we are restored and refreshed; when we taste the things that are written, 1890Cor more often chew over what we have sipped with the edge of the lips. By these pastures the flock of the Lord is fattened. Good pastures of Christ too are the mountains of the valleys; for in these also Christ pastures like a roe or a young hart, that is, in the splendors of the saints.

68.

(Vers. 17.) Donec aspiret dies, et inclinentur umbrae. Zachaeus in arborem sycomori ascendit, ut Christum videret: nobis quoque expandit manus suas Jesus, ut totum mundum obumbraret. Quomodo non sumus in umbra, qui crucis ejus protegimur velamento? Quomodo non sumus in umbra, quos crucifixus a malignitate saeculi et corporis ardore defendit? An nescimus, quoniam Verbum Dei veniens in hunc mundum, non sicut Verbum advenit. Quale erat in principio, quale erat apud Deum: sed semetipsum 1890Dexinanivit, formam servi accipiens (Philip. II, 7). Venit in nube levi, et cum esset virtus Altissimi, obumbravit Mariae; ut transfiguraret corpus humilitatis nostrae, conforme fieri corpori gloriae suae. Sicut ille ergo formam mutavit, cum ex Virgine nasceretur: ita et nobis sermones Dei transfigurari videntur, cum leguntur in Evangelio, cum species eorum videtur in Scripturis quasi per speculum; quia tota veritas hic non potest comprehendi, cum autem venerit quod perfectum est, non jam per discussionem, non per speciem ut transfigurati, sed integra atque expressa veritate resplendent. 1891A

68.

(Vers. 17.) Until the day breathe, and the shadows decline. Zacchaeus climbed up into a sycamore tree that he might see Christ: for us too Jesus spread out his hands, that he might overshadow the whole world. How are we not in the shadow, we who are protected by the veil of his cross? How are we not in the shadow, whom the crucified defends from the malice of the world and from the burning of the body? Or do we not know that the Word of God, coming into this world, did not come as the Word. Such as it was in the beginning, such as it was with God: but he emptied 1890Dhimself, taking the form of a servant (Philip. II, 7). He came in a light cloud, and since he was the power of the Most High, he overshadowed Mary; that he might transfigure the body of our lowliness, to be made conformable to the body of his glory. As he therefore changed his form when he was born of the Virgin, so to us also the words of God seem to be transfigured when they are read in the Gospel, when their appearance is seen in the Scriptures as through a mirror; because the whole truth cannot be grasped here, but when that which is perfect shall have come, they shine forth no longer through examination, not through appearance as transfigured, but in whole and expressed truth. 1891A

69.

Flectat ergo animam jugo Verbi, et habenis suis regat, ut voluntate Dei abducatur a vitiis, et vitae odorem capiat aeternae; non enim haec vita perfecta est, sed haec vita in umbra. Ideo ut homo Christus Jesus ex Virgine nasceretur, obumbravit virtus Altissimi matrem futuram; quia in umbra descendit, ab umbra incipiens operari salutem hominis, et consummaturus claritate solis aeterni. Umbra est ergo vita haec. Festina ad solem, ut te defendat ab umbrae hujus frigore, et calorem tibi perfundat aestivum. Et ideo suadet orandum, ne fiat fuga nostra hieme aut sabbato, non tempus diemque significans, sed frigere nos meritis prohibens, et bonorum operum esse jejunos.

69.

Let him therefore bend the soul with the yoke of the Word, and rule it with his reins, that by the will of God it may be led away from vices and take in the fragrance of eternal life; for this life is not the perfect life, but this life is in shadow. Therefore, that Christ Jesus might be born a man of the Virgin, the power of the Most High overshadowed her who was to be his mother; because he came down in shadow, beginning from shadow to work the salvation of man, and about to complete it in the brightness of the eternal sun. This life, then, is a shadow. Hasten to the sun, that he may defend thee from the cold of this shadow, and pour over thee the warmth of summer. And therefore he urges that prayer must be made that our flight be not in winter or on the sabbath, not signifying a season and a day, but forbidding us to be cold in merits and to be fasting from good works.

CAPUT TERTIUM.

CAPUT TERTIUM.

1.

1891B(Vers. 1.) In cubili meo, in nocte quaesivi quem dilexit anima mea: quaesivi eum et non inveni. Exsurgam itaque, et quaeram in civitate, in foro, in plateis, quem dilexit anima mea. Acceperat jam haec anima bona pignora charitatis, sed quia semper solliciti esse debemus, semper intenti, et quia Verbum Dei sicut capreola exsilit, aut sicut hinnulus cervorum; semper vigilet anima, et praetendat eum quem quaerit, et quem tenere desiderat. Ideo quasi sublapsum sibi deplorans, In cubili, inquit, meo, in noctibus quaesivi quem dilexit anima mea. Qui bene quaerit, in cubili quaerat, in noctibus quaerat: nec dies feriatus, neque noctes sint: nullum tempus vacet a pietatis officio; etsi non invenerit primo, perseveret in requirendo. Ideoque dicit:

  • 1891C

1.

1891B(Vers. 1.) In my bed, in the night I sought him whom my soul loved: I sought him and I found him not. I will rise up therefore, and will seek in the city, in the forum, in the streets, him whom my soul loved. [Vulg. Cant 3:1-2 in lectulo meo per noctes quaesivi quem diligit anima mea… surgam, et circuibo civitatem: per vicos et plateas quaeram, "in my bed by night I sought him whom my soul loveth… I will rise, and will go about the city: in the streets and the broad ways I will seek," with no forum] This soul had already received the good pledges of charity; but because we ought always to be anxious, always intent, and because the Word of God leaps forth like a little roe, or like a young hart, let the soul be always watchful, and hold out before her him whom she seeks and whom she longs to hold. Therefore, as though lamenting that he had slipped away from her, In my bed, she says, by nights I sought him whom my soul loved. Let him who seeks well seek in the bed, seek by night: let there be neither a day of rest nor nights of it; let no time be empty of the duty of piety; and even if he has not found at the first, let him persevere in seeking. And therefore she says:

  • 1891C

2.

(Vers. 2.) Exsurgam itaque, et quaeram in civitate, in foro, in plateis. Et fortasse ideo non invenit adhuc, quia in foro quaesivit, ubi lites sunt: in plateis, ubi nundinae rerum 1573 venalium; non enim Christus pecunia comparatur. Nullum tempus praedicandi vacuum sinat sancta devotio praeterire. Si studentes doctrinis saecularibus perparum somno indulgent; quanto magis qui Deum cupiunt cognoscere, non debent somno corporis impediri, nisi quantum naturae satis est? David per singulas nocctes lectum suum lacrymis diluebat, surgebat etiam noctis medio, ut Domino confiteretur: et tu totam noctem sopori existimas deputandam? Tunc magis tibi orandus est Dominus, tunc praesidia postulanda, culpa cavenda, quando videtur habere secretum. 1891DTunc maxime quando tenebrae in circuitu meo, et parietes me operiunt, considerandum quod Deus omnia intuetur abscondita. Ne dixeris ergo circumfusus tenebris, quis me videt? Et quem vereor septus parietibus et inclusus? Quia Vultus Domini super facientes mala (Psalm. XXXIII, 17). Denique si arbitrum non vides, te ipsum non vides? Tuae conscientiae testimonium non vereris? Nescis illam caliginem noctis non opertorium, sed incentivum esse peccati? Cum somno et cibo fervent corpora, tunc etiam mentis vigor sopore laxatur, somno resolvitur. Tunc 1892Airrepit impuri libido concubitus, tunc perturbatur cor squalore immunditiae, non videtur castitas, puritas non consideratur, pudicitiae gloria non recensetur.

2.

(Vers. 2.) I will rise up therefore, and will seek in the city, in the forum, in the streets. And perhaps she has not yet found him for this reason, that she sought in the forum, where lawsuits are; in the streets, where the markets of things 1573 for sale are; for Christ is not purchased with money. Let holy devotion suffer no time of preaching to pass by empty. If those who study secular teachings indulge very little in sleep, how much more ought those who long to know God not to be hindered by the sleep of the body, save so far as is enough for nature? David washed his bed with tears through the several nocctes, and rose also in the middle of the night that he might confess to the Lord: and dost thou think that the whole night is to be assigned to slumber? Then the more must the Lord be prayed to by thee, then must protection be sought and fault be guarded against, when it seems to have secrecy. 1891DThen most of all, when darkness is round about me and walls cover me, must it be considered that God beholds all hidden things. Say not therefore, wrapped about with darkness: Who sees me? And whom do I fear, hedged in and shut up by walls? Because The face of the Lord is upon them that do evil (Psalm. XXXIII, 17). In fine, if thou seest no witness, dost thou not see thyself? Dost thou not fear the testimony of thine own conscience? Dost thou not know that that gloom of night is not a covering but an incitement of sin? When bodies grow hot with sleep and food, then too the vigor of the mind is loosened by slumber, is dissolved by sleep. Then 1892Athe lust of impure embrace creeps in, then the heart is troubled with the squalor of uncleanness, chastity is not seen, purity is not considered, the glory of modesty is not reckoned.

3.

Nox erat, quando Judas prodidit, quando Petrus negavit. Illo igitur praecipue tempore justificationes Dei animo retexendae sunt, relegenda mandata hortatoria: illa de castitate praecepta non absint, ut his occupata mens, flagrantiam libidinis, carnis restinguat ardorem. Tene illud animo: Lavabo per singulas noctes lectum meum (Psal. VI, 7). Quis enim stupris deditus, flagitiis involutus, lavat per singulas noctes lectum suum? Nescit flere, qui flenda committit; et cum sit ipse lacrymabilis, non habet poenae lacrymas suae. At ille qui castigat corpus suum, et sui sollicitus gubernator est, atque ingemiscens et 1892Bdolens lapsus superioris offensam, quaerit quomodo abluat eam fletibus poenitentiae, hic lavat per singulas noctes lectum suum. Non dormiamus igitur totis noctibus, sed maximam partem earum lectioni et orationibus deputemus. Audi Ecclesiae vocem quaerentis etiam in noctibus Christum: In cubili, inquit, meo in noctibus quaesivi quem dilexit anima mea.

3.

It was night when Judas betrayed, when Peter denied. At that time therefore especially must the justifications of God be woven over again in the mind, and the exhorting commandments be read again: let those precepts concerning chastity not be absent, so that the mind, occupied with these, may quench the blaze of lust, the burning of the flesh. Hold that in mind: I will wash my bed every night (Psal. VI, 7). For who, given over to debauchery, wrapped up in disgraces, washes his bed every night? He knows not how to weep who commits what must be wept for; and though he himself is a thing to be wept over, he has no tears for his own punishment. But he who chastises his body, and is an anxious governor of himself, and, groaning and 1892Bgrieving over the offense of a former fall, seeks how he may wash it away with the weeping of penitence, this man washes his bed every night. Let us not therefore sleep whole nights through, but let us assign the greatest part of them to reading and to prayers. Hear the voice of the Church seeking Christ even by night: In my bed, she says, by nights I sought him whom my soul loved.

4.

Accipe secundum litteram, quia quaesivit in noctibus orando, deprecando, etiam lacrymis deplorando. Quaesivit in noctibus; quia Posuit tenebras latibulum suum (Psal. XVII, 12), ut quaerere eum sollicitius disceremus. Quaesivit igitur in prophetis Ecclesia ex gentibus congregata, et ideo credidit. Denique adventus Dominici testimonia de prophetis, Evangelistae Paulusque posuerunt, unde scriptum 1892Cest: Quia nox nocti indicat scientiam (Psal. XVIII, 3). Quaesivit in noctibus, in persecutionibus et adversis, in tribulationibus et doloribus duris. Nox est omnibus, quibus non est perfecta securitas; unde ait Dominus: Veniet nox quando nemo potest operari. Cum in hoc mundo sum, lux hujus mundi sum (Joan. IX, 4). Non ergo operemur in tenebris; si enim lucent opera nostra, non operamur in tenebris, sed in lumine.

4.

Take it according to the letter, that she sought by night by praying, by beseeching, and also by lamenting with tears. She sought by night; because He made darkness his hiding-place (Psal. XVII, 12), that we might learn to seek him more anxiously. The Church gathered out of the nations therefore sought him in the prophets, and therefore believed. In fine, the Evangelists and Paul set down testimonies of the Lord's coming out of the prophets, whence it is written 1892C: For night unto night showeth knowledge (Psal. XVIII, 3). She sought by night, in persecutions and adversities, in tribulations and hard sorrows. It is night for all for whom there is no perfect security; whence the Lord says: The night will come when no man can work. While I am in this world, I am the light of this world (Joan. IX, 4) [Vulg. John 9:4-5 venit nox, quando nemo potest operari. Quamdiu in mundo sum, lux sum mundi, "the night cometh, when no man can work. As long as I am in the world, I am the light of the world"]. Let us not therefore work in darkness; for if our works shine, we do not work in darkness, but in light.

5.

Dies est quibus adest Christus, nox est quibus se denegat. Non magnum tibi si tunc Domino gratias agas, quando in prosperis et secundis es: sed si tunc adhaereas Christo, quando te persecutor exagitat, quando aliqua procella perturbat. Amisisti filium? In illo dolore, in illa nocte, in illa destitutione, memento Domini Dei tui; ne quasi non exauditus, 1892Dingratus sis, et in tua praevariceris aerumna. Pulsus es in exsilium? Memento Domini Dei tui, ne patriae interdictae charitatem Deo praeferas. Alicujus divitis oppressus potentia, amisisti proprias facultates, indiges sumptu? Memento Domini Dei tui, ne nox te egestatis abducat a devotionis affectu. Hoc est enim mandatum Legis, ut in nocte magis quaeras, quando magis exaudiaris a Domino, et possis dicere: In tribulatione invocavi Dominum, et exaudivit me in latitudine Dominus (Psal. CXVII, 5).

5.

It is day for those to whom Christ is present, it is night for those to whom he denies himself. It is no great thing for thee if thou give thanks to the Lord then, when thou art in prosperity and in favorable things: but if thou cleave to Christ then, when a persecutor harries thee, when some storm troubles thee. Hast thou lost a son? In that grief, in that night, in that destitution, remember the Lord thy God; lest, as one not heard, 1892Dthou be ungrateful and transgress in thine affliction. Hast thou been driven into exile? Remember the Lord thy God, lest thou prefer the love of a fatherland forbidden thee before God. Crushed by the power of some rich man, hast thou lost thine own means, dost thou lack the wherewithal to live? Remember the Lord thy God, lest the night of want lead thee away from the affection of devotion. For this is the commandment of the Law, that thou seek the more by night, when thou art the more heard by the Lord, and mayest be able to say: In tribulation I called upon the Lord, and the Lord heard me in a broad place (Psal. CXVII, 5).

6.

Sed nec quaerere perfunctorie abundat ad gratiam, 1893Ased ut insistas et intendas muneri. Denique ista vel Ecclesia vel anima, quae requisivit in cubili, quaesivit in noctibus, primo non invenit; quia fortasse in cubili quaesivit. 1574 Sed postea quam surrexit, et abiit in civitatem (vide ne in illam civitatem in qua Dominus celebraturus pascha, discipulos destinavit dicens (Matth. XXVI, 18): Ite in civitatem ad quemdam ) postea quam in foro, ut dixi, quaesivit, ubi oleum venditur, quod exspectantes Sponsum emere consuerunt; ubi jus, ubi leges. Si enim lex spiritualis, et forum utique spirituale, ubi disputant periti legis aeternae. Quod forum non litibus tumultuosum est, sed Christi tribunalibus gloriosum. Ubi quaesivit, inquam? In foro, et post quaesivit in plateis, de quibus hi qui ad patrisfamilias Evangelici prandium 1893Bconvenirent, colligebantur: qui vocati excusandum se ab hujusmodi epulis non putarunt. Postea quam in foro, inquam, et plateis quaesivit, incidit in eos qui circumeunt civitatem, et tunc demum potuit invenire quod quaereret; fortasse quod in tribulatione et metu posita magis inveniat gratiam. Denique posterius ait:

6.

But neither does it suffice for grace to seek perfunctorily, 1893Abut that thou press on and bend thyself to the task. In fine, this Church or soul, who sought in the bed, sought by night, did not find at the first; because perhaps she sought in the bed. 1574 But after she rose and went away into the city (see whether it be into that city in which the Lord, about to celebrate the passover, sent his disciples, saying (Matth. XXVI, 18): Go into the city to a certain man ), after she sought, as I have said, in the forum, where oil is sold, which those awaiting the Bridegroom are wont to buy; where right is, where the laws are. For if the law is spiritual, the forum too is assuredly spiritual, where those skilled in the eternal law dispute. Which forum is not tumultuous with lawsuits, but glorious with the tribunals of Christ. Where did she seek, I say? In the forum, and afterward she sought in the streets, out of which were gathered those who were to come together to the banquet of the householder of 1893Bthe Gospel: who, being called, did not think that they should excuse themselves from such a feast. After she sought, I say, in the forum and in the streets, she fell in with those who go about the city, and then at last she was able to find what she was seeking; perhaps because, set in tribulation and fear, she the more finds grace. In fine, she says afterward:

7.

(Vers. 3.) Invenerunt me qui circumeunt civitatem, tulerunt mihi pallium. Habuit ergo certamen: sed qua ratione hic ei non tulerint pallium, haereo reperire; nisi forte quia ibi interrogasse se dixit: Num quem dilexit anima mea, vidistis? Quae Christum loquebatur, nex exuta est pallio, et quem quaerebat, invenit. Disce quemadmodum quaeratur Christus: ab his utique qui non perfunctorie quaerunt, sed violenter 1893Cretinent, sicut ista quae ait: Tenui eum nec dimittam, cum fide invenit, meditatione constrinxit.

7.

(Vers. 3.) They found me who go about the city; they took away my cloak. [Vulg. Cant. III, 3 Invenerunt me vigiles qui custodiunt civitatem; the taking of the cloak stands at Cant. V, 7] She had a struggle, then: but by what reasoning they did not take away her cloak here, I am at a loss to find out; unless perhaps because there she said that she had asked: Have you seen him whom my soul has loved? She who was speaking Christ, nex was not stripped of her cloak, and she found him whom she was seeking. Learn in what manner Christ is sought: by those, surely, who do not seek him perfunctorily, but hold him fast 1893Cby force, as she does who says: I held him and I will not let him go, who found him with faith and bound him fast with meditation.

8.

Si bonos custodes accipimus, utique angeli sunt. Qui vero angelos transivit. Verbum invenit; ideoque non multum intervalli fuit, cum transivit ab his, et invenit Jesum. Quomodo ergo infra tulerunt pallium, nisi forte quia processu offensae fidei, Ecclesia dum nudatur, a Domino plus amatur, exspolians se veterem hominem ut novum induat, quem non operiunt peccata, sed mentis secreta clarificant, aut quia amictum sapientiae saecularis ad Christum ventura deposuit? et non exutus est pallio, postea quam coepit esse perfectior.

8.

If we take them for good guards, they are certainly angels. But he who passed beyond the angels. Found the Word; and therefore there was not much of an interval, when he passed beyond these and found Jesus. How then did they take away her cloak below, unless perhaps because, by the advance of an offense against faith, the Church, while she is stripped bare, is the more loved by the Lord, putting off from herself the old man that she may put on the new, whom sins do not cover but the hidden things of the mind make bright; or because, being about to come to Christ, she laid aside the mantle of worldly wisdom? And he was not stripped of his cloak, after he had begun to be more perfect.

9.

Simus ergo memores justificationum coelestium; ut dum eas secreta mentis voce cantamus, memores 1893Dsimus in nocte nominis Domini, et dicamus ut scriptum est: Haec facta est mihi, quia justificationes tuas exquisivi (Psal. CXVIII, 56); hoc est, haec memoria facta est mihi, ut memor essem et in nocte nominis tui, non ebrietate sopitus, non epulis resolutus in somnum, non curis saecularibus occupatus, ne oblivia mihi tuae venerationis irreperent: sed quotidiana meditatione membra castigans, et intentionem mentis exercens; ut assiduitate fiat nobis cursus iste solemnis, ut Dominum Jesum etiam in noctibus toto veneremur affectu.

9.

Let us therefore be mindful of the heavenly ordinances of justification; that while we sing them with the hidden voice of the mind, we may be mindful 1893Din the night of the name of the Lord, and may say as it is written: This was made mine, because I sought out thy justifications (Psal. CXVIII, 56); that is, this remembrance was made mine, that I might be mindful even in the night of thy name, not lulled asleep by drunkenness, not loosed into slumber by feasting, not taken up with worldly cares, lest forgetfulness of the reverence owed to thee should creep upon me: but chastening my members by daily meditation, and exercising the intention of the mind; so that by constancy this course may become a solemn one for us, so that we may reverence the Lord Jesus with our whole affection even in the nights.

10.

In cubiculo meo, in noctibus quaesivi quem 1894Adilexit anima mea. Possumus et sic intelligere. In cubili quaerit Christum, qui quaerit eum cum tranquillitate, cum pace. In noctibus quaerit, quoniam per parabolas loquebatur; Posuit enim tenebras latibulum suum, et nox nocti indicat scientiam (Psal. XVII, 12). Deinde, quoniam quae dicimus in corde nostro, ea nos compungere debent in cubilibus.

10.

In my chamber, in the nights I sought him whom 1894Amy soul has loved. [Vulg. In lectulo meo per noctes quaesivi quem diligit anima mea] We can understand it in this way as well. He seeks Christ in his bed who seeks him with tranquillity, with peace. He seeks in the nights, since he was speaking in parables; for He hath set indeed darkness for his covert, and night sheweth knowledge unto night (Psal. XVII, 12). Then, because the things which we say in our heart ought to prick us in our beds.

11.

Sed tamen nec sic invenit, et ideo dicit: Exsurgam, id est, erigam et attollam intentionem meam, ut quaeram impigre, quaeram sollicite: introibo in civitatem. Est et anima quae dicit: Ego civitas munita, ego civitas obsessa (Esai. XXVII, 3). Est civitas munita per Christum, est civitas illa Hierusalem in coelo, in qua abundant divinae legis interpretes et disciplinarum periti, per quos Verbum Dei quaeritur. 1894BQuaeram, inquit, in foro illius civitatis, in illo foro ubi tractant jura consulti, ubi oleum venditur, quod Evangelicae virgines emunt, ut semper faces suae luceant, nec eas fumus iniquitatis exstinguat. Quaeram, inquit, in plateis in quibus superfluunt aquae de illis fontibus prorumpentes, de quibus Salomon dicit esse potandum.

11.

But even so she did not find him, and therefore she says: I will rise up, that is, I will raise and lift up my intention, that I may seek untiringly, may seek anxiously: I will go into the city. There is also a soul which says: I am a fortified city, I am a besieged city (Esai. XXVII, 3). [Vulg. Isa. XXVII, 3 Ego Dominus qui servo eam] There is a city fortified through Christ; there is that city Jerusalem in heaven, in which the interpreters of the divine law abound, and men skilled in the disciplines, through whom the Word of God is sought. 1894BI will seek, she says, in the forum of that city, in that forum where those learned in the law handle rights, where the oil is sold which the Gospel virgins buy, that their torches may always shine, and that the smoke of iniquity may not put them out. I will seek, she says, in the streets in which the waters overflow, bursting out from those fountains of which Solomon says that one must drink.

12.

Invenerunt me, inquit, custodes qui circumeunt civitatem. Dum quaerit igitur Christum; invenit custodes qui in ministerio sunt; ab his requirit: Num quem diligit anima mea vidistis? Sed anima quae Deum quaerit, etiam custodes transit. Sunt enim mysteria quae etiam angeli concupiscunt videre, unde Petrus ait: Nuntiata sunt, inquit, vobis per eos quibus evangelizaverunt, Spiritu 1575 sancto misso de coelo, 1894Cin quem concupiscunt angeli prospicere (I Pet. I, 12).

12.

They found me, she says, the guards who go about the city. While therefore she seeks Christ, she finds the guards who are in ministry; from these she asks: Have you seen him whom my soul loves? But the soul which seeks God passes even the guards by. For there are mysteries which even the angels long to see, whence Peter says: They were announced, he says, to you by those to whom they preached the gospel, the Holy 1575 Spirit having been sent from heaven, 1894Cupon whom the angels long to look (I Pet. I, 12). [Vulg. per eos qui evangelizaverunt vobis … in quem desiderant angeli prospicere]

13.

(Vers. 4.) Quam modicum cum transivi ab eis, inveni eum, tenui eum, et non dimisi eum. Ergo qui transierit custodes, Verbum invenit. Transivit Joannes, qui Verbum apud Patrem invenit. Multi quoque alii sunt qui in otio quaerunt Christum, et non inveniunt: et quaerunt eum in persecutionibus, et inveniunt. Et ideo quasi post tentationes, quia in periculis fidelium suorum adest: Quam modicum, inquit, cum transivi ab eis, inveni eum, et tenui eum, et non dimisi eum. Omnis enim qui quaerit, invenit; et qui invenerit, adhaerere debet, ne possit amittere.

13.

(Vers. 4.) How little a while after I had passed beyond them, I found him, I held him, and I did not let him go. [Vulg. Cant. III, 4 Paululum cum pertransissem eos, inveni quem diligit anima mea] Therefore he who has passed beyond the guards has found the Word. John passed beyond them, who found the Word with the Father. There are also many others who seek Christ in leisure and do not find him; and they seek him in persecutions, and they find him. And therefore, as if after temptations — because he is present in the perils of his faithful ones — How little a while, he says, after I had passed beyond them, I found him, and I held him, and I did not let him go. For everyone who seeks, finds; and he who has found ought to cleave to him, lest he be able to lose him.

14.

Et quoniam per Evangelium in terris videmus coelestia mysteria figurata, veniamus ad Magdalenam, 1894Dveniamus ad alteram Mariam. Consideremus quemadmodum Christum in cubili corporis sui, in quo defunctus jacebat, in noctibus quaesiverint, quando dixit illis angelus: Jesum qui crucifixus est quaeritis: non est hic, surrexit enim: quid igitur quaeritis viventem cum mortuis (Matth. XXVIII, 5 et seq.)? Quid quaeritis in sepulcro eum, qui jam in coelo sit? Quid quaeritis in vinculis sepulturae, universorum vincula solventem? Non sepulcrum huic sedes, sed coelum est; ideo dixit una ex his: Quaesivi eum, et non inveni (Cant. III, 1). Tamen dum vadunt apostolis renuntiare, misertus quaerentes, occurrit eis Jesus dicens: 1895AAvete. Illae autem accesserunt, et tenuerunt pedes ejus, et adoraverunt eum.

14.

And since through the Gospel we see the heavenly mysteries figured upon earth, let us come to Magdalene, 1894Dlet us come to the other Mary. Let us consider in what manner they sought Christ in the nights, in the chamber of his body in which he lay dead, when the angel said to them: You seek Jesus who was crucified: he is not here, for he is risen: why then do you seek the living with the dead (Matth. XXVIII, 5 et seq.)? [Vulg. Matth. XXVIII, 6 ends surrexit enim, sicut dixit; Quid quaeritis viventem cum mortuis stands at Luc. XXIV, 5] Why do you seek in the sepulchre him who is already in heaven? Why do you seek in the bonds of burial him who looses the bonds of all men? Not the sepulchre but heaven is his seat; therefore one of them said: I sought him, and I did not find him (Cant. III, 1). Yet while they go to bring word to the apostles, Jesus, having pity on those who sought him, met them, saying: 1895AHail. But they came near, and held his feet, and adored him.

15.

Tenetur ergo Jesus, et delectatur sic teneri, quia fide tenetur. Denique delectavit eum et illa mulier, quae tetigit eum, et curata est a sanguinis fluxu, de qua dixit: Tetigit me aliquis; nam sentio virtutem de me exisse (Luc. VIII, 46). Tange ergo, et fide tene eum, et constringe fideliter pedes ejus; ut virtus de eo exeat, ut sanet animam tuam. Et si dicat: Noli me tangere; tu tene. Nondum enim ascendi ad Patrem meum, dixit (Joan. XX, 17). Semel dixit: Noli me tangere, quando resurrexit: aut forte illi dixit, quae putabat furto esse sublatum, et non virtute propria resuscitatum. Denique in alio libro habes, quia tenentibus pedes et adorantibus dixit: Nolite 1895Btimere (Matth. XXVIII, 10).

15.

Jesus therefore is held, and is delighted to be held so, because he is held by faith. In short, that woman also delighted him who touched him and was healed of the flow of blood, of whom he said: Someone has touched me; for I feel that power has gone out from me (Luc. VIII, 46). [Vulg. nam ego novi virtutem de me exiisse, "for I know"] Touch him therefore, and hold him by faith, and clasp his feet faithfully; that power may go out from him to heal thy soul. And if he say: Do not touch me; do thou hold him. For I have not yet ascended to my Father, he said (Joan. XX, 17). Once he said: Do not touch me, when he had risen: or perhaps he said it to her who thought that he had been carried off by theft and not raised up again by his own power. In short, in another book thou hast it that to those holding his feet and adoring him he said: Do not 1895Bbe afraid (Matth. XXVIII, 10).

16.

Tene ergo et tu, anima, sicut tenebat et Maria, et dic: Tenui eum, et non dimittam: cui dicebant ambae, tenemus te. Vade ad patrem, sed non derelinquas me, ne iterum labar; et cum Maria non errante, sed arborem vitae tenente, tecum rape tuis pedibus inhaerentem. Tecum ascendam, noli me dimittere, ne iterum serpens venena sua fundat, ne iterum queat femineum mordere vestigium, ut supplantet Adam. Dicat ergo anima tua: Teneo te, et inducam te in domum matris meae, et in secretum ejus quae concepit me (Cant. III, 4); ut cognoscam mysteria tua, ut hauriam sacramenta tua.

16.

Hold him therefore, thou also, O soul, as Mary too was holding him, and say: I held him, and I will not let him go: to whom both of them were saying, we hold thee. Go to the Father, but do not forsake me, lest I slip again; and with Mary, who does not stray but holds the tree of life, snatch up with thee her who clings to thy feet. I will ascend with thee, do not let me go, lest again the serpent pour out his venom, lest again he be able to bite the woman's heel, that he may supplant Adam. Let thy soul therefore say: I hold thee, and I will bring thee into the house of my mother, and into the secret place of her who conceived me (Cant. III, 4); that I may know thy mysteries, that I may drink in thy sacraments.

17.

Tenui eum, nec relinquam eum, donec introducam eum in domum matris meae, et in cubiculum ejus 1895Cquae me concepit. Quae est domus matris meae et cubiculum ejus, nisi internum tuae secretumque naturae? Hanc domum serva, hujus domus mundato penetralia, ut cum immaculata domus fuerit, nec ullius adulterinae conscientiae sordibus inquinata, spiritalis domus in sacerdotium sanctum lapide angulari coagmentata consurgat, et Spiritus sanctus habitet in ea. Quae sic Christum requirit, quae sic Christum obsecrat, non relinquitur ab eo: immo etiam frequenter revisitur.

17.

I held him, nor will I leave him, until I bring him into the house of my mother, and into the chamber of her 1895Cwho conceived me. [Vulg. Cant. III, 4 in cubiculum genitricis meae, "into the chamber of her that bore me"] What is the house of my mother and her chamber, but the inward and hidden part of thy nature? Guard this house, cleanse the inner rooms of this house, so that when the house has been unstained, and defiled by the filth of no adulterous conscience, a spiritual house may rise up, framed together upon the corner stone into a holy priesthood, and the Holy Spirit may dwell in it. She who seeks Christ thus, who thus entreats Christ, is not left by him: nay rather, she is even visited again and again.

18.

Vel domus matris, fortasse domus est, in qua praefulget moralium disciplina: secretum autem illud est, in quo sunt altiora mysteria, in quibus divinae gratiae mella redolent. An non omni melle dulcior peccatorum remissio? Nonne omni floregratior resurrectio mortuorum? Suscipe igitur Evam, non 1895Djam ficulneae foliis coopertam, sed sancto amictam Spiritu, et nova gratia gloriosam; quia jam non tamquam nudata absconditur, sed tamquam circumdata vestimenti splendore fulgentis occurrit, quia vestit eam gratia: sed nec Adam primo nudus erat, quando eum innocentia vestiebat. Videntes itaque eam filiae Hierusalem Christo inhaerentem, et adhuc ascendentem cum eo (dignatur enim quaerentibus frequenter occurrere, et condescendere, ut eos elevet) dicunt:

18.

Or the house of the mother is perhaps the house in which the discipline of morals shines out: while that secret place is the one in which are the higher mysteries, in which the honey of divine grace gives out its fragrance. Or is not the remission of sins sweeter than all honey? Is not the resurrection of the dead floregratior than every one? Receive then Eve, no 1895Dlonger covered with the leaves of the fig tree, but clothed with the Holy Spirit, and glorious with new grace; because now she is not hidden away as one stripped bare, but comes to meet us as one surrounded with the splendour of a shining garment, because grace clothes her: yet neither was Adam naked at first, when innocence clothed him. And so the daughters of Jerusalem, seeing her clinging to Christ and still ascending with him (for he deigns often to meet those who seek him, and to come down, that he may lift them up), say:

19.

(Vers. 6.) Quae est haec quae ascendit a deserto? 1896ASis sicut illa anima, quae Christi in se charitatem excitat, quam mirantur ascendentem virtutes coelorum; quod sine offensione 1576 ascendat, et ex hoc mundo ascendat cum laetitia et jucunditate, sicut vitis propago, et sicut fumus se ad superna subrigat, fragrans odorem resurrectionis piae, et suavitatem fidei, sicut habes scriptum: Quae est haec quae ascendit a deserto, sicut vitis propago, fumo incensi odorificata, myrrha et thure ab omnibus pulveribus unguenti. Pulchre subtilitatem ejus expressit, pulveris comparatione, et unguenti commemoratione; quia in Exodo subtile legimus, et ex multis compositum thymiama, illud esse incensum propheticum, quod est sanctorum oratio, ut dirigatur in conspectu Domini, sicut et David dicit: Dirigatur oratio mea, sicut incensum in 1896Bconspectu tuo (Psalm. CXL, 2).

19.

(Vers. 6.) Who is this that ascends from the desert? 1896ABe thou like that soul which stirs up in itself the charity of Christ, at whose ascent the powers of the heavens marvel; that she ascends without stumbling 1576 and ascends out of this world with gladness and delight, like the shoot of a vine, and lifts herself like smoke to the things above, fragrant with the odour of a holy resurrection and with the sweetness of faith, as thou hast it written: Who is this that ascends from the desert, like the shoot of a vine, made odorous with the smoke of incense, with myrrh and frankincense, out of all the powders of the ointment. [Vulg. sicut virgula fumi ex aromatibus myrrhae et thuris et universi pulveris pigmentarii] Beautifully has he expressed her fineness by the comparison of powder and by the mention of ointment; because in Exodus we read of a fine incense compounded of many things, that this is the prophetic incense which is the prayer of the saints, that it may be directed in the sight of the Lord, as David also says: Let my prayer be directed as incense in 1896Bthy sight (Psalm. CXL, 2).

20.

Quae est ista quae ascendit a deserto? Desertus hic terrae locus et incultus videtur, sentibus et spinis nostrorum obsitus delictorum. Mirantur videlicet quomodo anima quae ante in inferno relinquebatur, inhaereat Dei Verbo, et ascenderit sicut vitis propago, in superiora subrigens se, velut fumus natus ex igne, atque alta petens, tum praeterea bonis odoribus fragret. Odor autem ille orationis piae redolet suavitatem, quae dirigitur sicut incensum in conspectu Domini (Psal. CXL, 2). Et in Apocalypsi legimus, quod Ascendit fumus incensorum de orationibus sanctorum (Apoc. VIII, 4). quae incensa referuntur per angelorum suorum orationes, scilicet super altare illud aureum quod est ante sedem Dei. Et tamquam suave 1896Cpiae precationis fragrat unguentum; quia de aeternorum et invisibilium, non de corporalium petitione compositum est: praecipue tamen myrrham redolet et thus, eo quod peccato mortuus sit, et Deo vivat. Unde et in posterioribus: Ibo, inquit, mihi ad montem myrrhae, et ad collem thuris. Sequitur:

20.

Who is she that ascends from the desert? This desert appears as a place of earth and untilled, overgrown with the briars and thorns of our offenses. They marvel, plainly, how the soul which before was being left in hell should cleave to the Word of God, and should have ascended like the shoot of a vine, raising herself into the higher regions, like smoke born of fire and seeking the heights, and should moreover be fragrant with good odours. And that odour of holy prayer breathes out a sweetness which is directed as incense in the sight of the Lord (Psal. CXL, 2). And in the Apocalypse we read that The smoke of the incense went up from the prayers of the saints (Apoc. VIII, 4). which are offered as incense through the prayers of his angels, namely upon that golden altar which is before the seat of God. And it is fragrant like the sweet 1896Cointment of holy entreaty; because it is compounded out of the asking for eternal and invisible things, not for bodily ones: yet chiefly it smells of myrrh and frankincense, in that she is dead to sin and lives to God. Whence also in what follows: I will go, he says, for myself to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense. There follows:

21.

(Vers. 7.) En sexaginta potentes in circuitu propaginis ejus, strictis armati ensibus, et eruditi praeliaribus disciplinis. Qui sunt isti fortes et eruditi praeliaribus disciplinis, adverte. Fortis et ecclesiastici est viri non dissimulare cum aliquid immineat, sed praetendere et tamquam explorare de specula quadam mentis, et obviare cogitatione provida rebus profuturis; ne forte dicat postea: Ideo in ista incidi, quia non arbitrabar posse evenire. Denique nisi explorentur 1896Dadversa, cito occupant. Ut in bello improvisus hostis vix sustinetur, et si imparatos inveniat, facile opprimit: ita animum mala inexplorata plus frangunt. In his ergo duobus illa est animi excellentia, ut primum animus tuus bonis exercitatus cogitationibus, mundo corde quod verum et honestum est, videat: Beati enim mundo corde, quia ipsi etiam Deum videbunt: atque id quod honestum est, solum bonum judicet. Deinde nullis perturbetur occupationibus, nullis cupiditatibus fluctuet. 1897A

21.

(Vers. 7.) Behold, sixty mighty men round about her shoot, armed with drawn swords, and trained in the disciplines of war. [Vulg. Cant. III, 7–8 En lectulum Salomonis sexaginta fortes ambiunt ex fortissimis Israel, "sixty valiant men surround Solomon's bed"] Consider who these strong men are, and trained in the disciplines of war. It belongs to a strong and churchly man not to dissemble when something threatens, but to look out ahead and, as it were, to scout from some watchtower of the mind, and to meet with provident thought the things that are to come; lest afterwards he say: I fell into this because I did not think it could happen. In short, unless adverse things are scouted out, 1896Dthey quickly overtake us. As in war an unforeseen enemy is scarcely withstood, and if he finds men unprepared he easily overwhelms them: so evils unexplored break the mind the more. In these two things, then, lies that excellence of mind: first, that thy mind, exercised in good thoughts, may see with a clean heart what is true and honourable: for Blessed are the clean of heart, because they also shall see God: and that it may judge that alone to be good which is honourable. Then, that it be disturbed by no occupations, that it waver with no covetous desires. 1897A

22.

Neque vero id quisquam facile facit: quid enim tam difficile quam dispicere tamquam ex arce aliqua sapientiae, opes aliaque omnia, quae plerisque videntur magna et praecelsa? Deinde ut judicium tuum stabili ratione confirmes, et quae judicaveris levia, tamquam nihil profutura contemnas? Deinde ut si quid acciderit adversi, idque grave et acerbum putetur, ita feras, ut nihil praeter naturam accidisse putes, cum legeris: Nudus sum natus, nudus exibo: quae Dominus dedit, Dominus et abstulit (Job. I, 21); et utique et filios amiserat et facultates: servesque in omnibus personam sapientis et justi, sicut ille servavit, qui ait: Sicut Domino placuit, ita factum est, sit nomen Domini benedictum (Ibid.). Et infra, Sicut una insipientium mulierum locuta es: si bona 1897Bsuscepimus de manu Domini, quae mala sunt non sustinebimus (Ibid., 2) ?

22.

Nor indeed does anyone do this easily: for what is so difficult as to look down, as from some citadel of wisdom, upon riches and all the other things which seem to most men great and lofty? Then, that thou shouldst confirm thy judgment by settled reason, and despise the things thou hast judged trifling as things that will profit nothing? Then, that if anything adverse should befall, and be thought grievous and bitter, thou shouldst bear it in such a way as to think that nothing has happened contrary to nature, since thou hast read: Naked was I born, naked shall I go out: the things which the Lord gave, the Lord has also taken away (Job. I, 21); [Vulg. Job I, 21 Nudus egressus sum de utero matris meae, et nudus revertar illuc] and he had indeed lost both his sons and his goods: and that in all things thou shouldst keep the character of a wise and just man, as he kept it who said: As it pleased the Lord, so it has been done, blessed be the name of the Lord (Ibid.). And below, Thou hast spoken as one of the foolish women: if we have received good things 1897Bfrom the hand of the Lord, shall we not bear the things that are evil (Ibid., 2) ? [Vulg. Job II, 10 si bona suscepimus de manu Dei, mala quare non suscipiamus?]

23.

Non est igitur mediocris nec discreta a caeteris animi fortitudo, quae bellum cum virtutibus gerat; sed quae sola defendat ornamenta virtutum omnium, et judicia custodiat, et quae inexpiabili praelio adversus omnia vitia decernat, invicta ad labores, fortis ad pericula, rigidior adversus voluptates, dura adversus illecebras, quibus aurem deferre nesciat, nec (ut dicitur) ave dicat, pecuniam negligat, avaritiam fugiat tamquam labem quamdam, quae virtutem effeminet; nihil enim tam contrarium fortitudini, quam lucro vinci. Frequenter pulsis hostibus, inclinata in fugam adversariorum acie, dum exuviis caesorum capitur praeliator, inter ipsos quos 1897Cstravit, miserandus occubuit: et triumphis suis dejectae legiones dum 1577 spoliis occupantur, hostem in se revocarunt, qui fugerat.

23.

The fortitude of the mind, therefore, is no middling thing, nor one set apart from the rest, that it should wage war with the virtues; but it is that which alone defends the ornaments of all the virtues, and guards their judgments, and which contends in inexpiable battle against all vices, unconquered in labours, brave in dangers, more rigid against pleasures, hard against enticements, to which it knows not how to lend an ear, nor (as the saying is) to bid them hail; which neglects money, flees avarice as a kind of stain that unmans virtue; for nothing is so contrary to fortitude as to be overcome by gain. Often, when the enemy has been driven back and the line of the adversaries turned to flight, while the fighter is taken up with the spoils of the slain, he has fallen, a man to be pitied, among the very men 1897Cwhom he laid low: and legions cast down by their own triumphs, while they are busied with the 1577 spoils, have called back upon themselves the enemy who had fled.

24.

Fortitudo igitur tam immanem pestem repellat et proterat, nec tentetur cupiditatibus, nec frangatur metu; quia virtus sibi constat, ut fortiter omnia persequatur vitia, tamquam virtutis venena. Iracundiam velut quibusdam propulset armis, quae tollit consilium, et tamquam aegritudinem vitet. Gloriae quoque caveat appetentiam, quae frequenter nocuit immoderatius appetita, semper autem usurpata. Emolliri enim adulatione non solum fortitudinis non esse, sed etiam ignaviae videtur. Horum fortium castra Jacob videns in spiritu, Castra, inquit, Dei sunt haec (Gen. XXXII, 2). 1897D

24.

Let fortitude therefore drive back and trample down so monstrous a pest, and let it not be tempted by covetous desires, nor broken by fear; because virtue is consistent with itself, so that it pursues all vices bravely, as the poisons of virtue. Let it drive off anger as with certain weapons, since anger takes away counsel, and let it shun it as a sickness. Let it beware also of the appetite for glory, which has often done harm when sought too immoderately, but always when usurped. For to be softened by flattery seems not only not to belong to fortitude, but even to belong to cowardice. Seeing in the spirit the camp of these strong men, Jacob said: These are the camps of God (Gen. XXXII, 2). 1897D

25.

Quid autem sunt sexaginta? Scimus hebdomadam veteris, ogdoaden autem novi esse Testamenti, scilicet quando Christus resurrexit, et dies omnibus novae salutis illuxit: quo die se fulgor plenae atque perfectae circumcisionis humanis pectoribus infudit. Septem autem et octo, quindecim faciunt. Isti ergo sunt septem et octo, id est, quindecim, quibus conduxit Osee meretricem, quam conducendam Dominus ei praeceperat. Hebdomada itaque et octava significant duorum Testamentorum consummationem, 1898Aet fidei plenitudinem: quindenarius vero numerus quater ductus, qui sunt sexaginta, Evangelii perfectionem.

25.

But what are the sixty? We know that the hebdomad belongs to the Old Testament, and the ogdoad to the New — namely when Christ rose again, and the day of the new salvation shone upon all: on which day the brightness of the full and perfect circumcision poured itself into human breasts. Now seven and eight make fifteen. These, then, are the seven and the eight, that is, the fifteen, for which Hosea hired the harlot whom the Lord had commanded him to hire. And so the hebdomad and the eighth signify the consummation of the two Testaments 1898Aand the fullness of faith: while the number fifteen taken four times, which is sixty, signifies the perfection of the Gospel.

26.

Quae est autem propago in cujus circuitu potentes isti sunt? Nephthalim, inquit, vitis remissa, porrigens in germine decorem. Sarmentum vitis aliud amputatur quod videtur inutile, ne cassa vitis sarmentorum exsultatione luxuriet, et aliud paulisper reciditur et remittitur, ut fructum afferat, cujus decus in generatione porrigitur; quia dum ad superna se subrigit, vitem amplectitur, et ad verticem ascendens, jugi quaedam colla vitium pretiosi palmitis vestit monili. Est et ille decorus in generatione, quia plenis palmitibus multos fructus exundat. Decorum hoc, sed multo illud pulchrius, quod haerentem 1898Bpalmitem viti significat spiritali, cujus nos sarmentum sumus, et fructum afferre possumus, si maneamus in vite; sin autem, praecidimur. Sanctus patriarcha Nephthalim erat palmes abundans, unde et Moyses dicit: Nephthalim satietas accipientium, replebitur benedictione a Domino: mare et Africum possidebit (Deut. XXXIII, 23); exponens hoc quod Jacob dixerat, quid sit vitis remissa, hoc est, per fidei gratiam vinculis mortis exutus: in quo significatur populus Dei ad libertatem fidei et ubertatem vocatus gratiae, toto orbe diffusus, qui jugum Christi bono fructu vestiat, et ligna verae illius vitis, hoc est, mysteria Dominicae crucis ambiat, nec timeat confessionis ejus periculum: sed magis etiam in persecutionibus positus, Christi nomine glorietur. 1898C

26.

But what is the shoot round about which these mighty men are? Nephthalim, he says, a vine let loose, putting forth beauty in its bud. [Vulg. Gen. XLIX, 21 Nephthali, cervus emissus, "a hind let loose"] One branch of the vine is cut away which seems useless, lest the vine run to waste in an empty exultation of branches, and another is pruned back a little and let loose, that it may bear fruit, whose beauty is put forth in its bearing; because while it lifts itself to the things above it embraces the vine, and climbing to the top it clothes the necks of the vines' yoke-poles with a certain necklace of precious shoot. That one too is comely in its bearing, because with full shoots it pours out many fruits. This is comely, but that other is far more beautiful, which signifies the shoot 1898Bclinging to the spiritual vine, of which we are the branch, and we can bear fruit if we remain in the vine; but if not, we are cut off. The holy patriarch Nephthalim was an abounding shoot, whence Moses also says: Nephthalim, the satisfying of those who receive, shall be filled with blessing from the Lord: he shall possess the sea and the south-west wind (Deut. XXXIII, 23); [Vulg. Deut. XXXIII, 23 mare et meridiem possidebit, "shall possess the sea and the south"] explaining what Jacob had said, what a vine let loose is, that is, one stripped of the bonds of death through the grace of faith: in which is signified the people of God, called to the liberty of faith and the richness of grace, spread through the whole world, which clothes the yoke of Christ with good fruit, and encircles the wood of that true vine, that is, the mysteries of the Lord's cross, and does not fear the peril of confessing him: but rather, even when set in persecutions, glories in the name of Christ. 1898C

27.

Tenent autem fortes isti gladios in manibus, quos pro Christi nomine non refutent, vel quibus corporalia a spiritualibus, umbram a veritate distinguant. Vivus est enim Dei sermo et efficax, et penetrabilior omni gladio ancipiti, et pertingens usque ad divisionem animae ac spiritus; compagum quoque ac medullarum, et discretor cogitationum, et intentionum cordis: et non est ulla creatura invisibilis in conspectu ejus, sed omnia nuda et aperta sunt oculis ejus (Hebr. IV, 12 et 13). Est enim verbum Dei gladius bis acutus. Sunt et alii duo gladii in Ecclesia de quibus dicitur: Domine, ecce duo gladii hic, ecclesiasticae scilicet potestatis et mundanae, quibus falsa repugnat misericordia.

27.

Now these strong men hold swords in their hands, which they do not refuse for the name of Christ, or by which they distinguish bodily things from spiritual, the shadow from the truth. For the word of God is living and effectual, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, and reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit, of the joints also and of the marrow, and a discerner of the thoughts and intentions of the heart: and there is no creature invisible in his sight, but all things are naked and open to his eyes (Hebr. IV, 12 et 13). For the word of God is a sword twice sharpened. There are also two other swords in the Church, of which it is said: Lord, behold, here are two swords, namely of the churchly power and of the worldly, against which a false mercy fights.

28.

Est justa misericordia: sed est etiam injusta 1898Dmisericordia. Denique in Lege scriptum est de quodam: Non misereberis illius (Deut. XIX, 13). Et in libris Regnorum legis quia Saul propterea contraxit offensam, quia miseratus est Agag hostium regem, quem prohibuerat sententia divina servari (I Reg. XV, 9). Ut si quis latronem filiis deprecantibus motus, et lacrymis conjugis ejus inflexus, absolvendum putet, cui adhuc latrocinandi aspiret affectus: nonne innocentes tradet exitio, qui liberat multorum exitia cogitantem? Certe si gladium reprimit, vincula dissolvit, 1899Alaxat exsilium; car latrocinandi qua potest clementiore via non eripit facultatem, qui voluntatem extorquere non potuit? Deinde inter duos, hoc est, accusatorem et reum pari periculo de capite decernentes, alterum si non probasset, alterum si non esset ab accusatore convictus, non id 1578 quod justitiae est, judex sequatur; sed dum miseretur rei, damnet probantem: aut dum accusatori favet qui probare non possit, abdicat innoxium. Non potest igitur haec dici justa misericordia.

28.

There is a just mercy: but there is also an unjust 1898Dmercy. In short, it is written in the Law concerning a certain man: Thou shalt not have mercy upon him (Deut. XIX, 13). And in the books of Kingdoms thou readest that Saul incurred offense for this reason, that he had mercy on Agag the king of the enemy, whom the divine sentence had forbidden to be kept alive (I Reg. XV, 9). So that if anyone, moved by the pleading of a robber's children and bent by the tears of his wife, should think that he ought to be acquitted, while the desire of robbing still pants within him: will he not hand over the innocent to destruction, who sets free a man who is plotting the destruction of many? Assuredly, if he holds back the sword, looses the chains, 1899Arelaxes the exile; car why does he not take away, by the gentler road that lies open to him, the power of robbing, he who could not wrest away the will? Then, between two men, that is, an accuser and a defendant contending at equal peril of life — the one if he did not prove his case, the other if he were not convicted by the accuser — let not the judge follow that 1578 which belongs to justice; but while he has mercy on the defendant, let him condemn the man who proves the charge: or while he favours the accuser who cannot prove it, let him cast off the guiltless. This therefore cannot be called a just mercy.

29.

In ipsa Ecclesia ubi maxime misereri decet, teneri quam maxime debet forma justitiae, ne quis a consortio communionis abstentus, brevi lacrymula, atque ad tempus parata, vel etiam uberioribus fletibus communionem quam plurimis debet postulare 1899Btemporibus, facilitate sacerdotis extorqueat. Nonne cum uni indulget indigno, plurimos facit ad prolapsionis contagium provocari? Facilitas enim veniae, incentivum tribuit delinquendi. Hoc eo dictum est, ut sciamus secundum verbum Dei, secundum rationem dispensandam esse misericordiam debitoribus: alias autem gladius quod suum est, exsequatur.

29.

In the Church itself, where it is most fitting to show mercy, the form of justice ought to be kept as strictly as possible, lest anyone who has been kept back from the fellowship of communion should, by a brief little tear made ready for the moment, or even by more abundant weeping, wring out through the easiness of the priest 1899Bthe communion which he ought to ask for over a very long time. Does he not, when he indulges one unworthy man, cause very many to be drawn on to the contagion of falling? For easiness of pardon supplies an incentive to offending. This has been said for this reason, that we may know that mercy is to be dispensed to debtors according to the word of God, according to reason: but otherwise let the sword carry out what belongs to it.

30.

(Vers. 9, 10.) Lectum fecit sibi Salomon rex ex lignis Libani, columnas ejus fecit argenteas, acclinatorium aureum, ascensum ejus purpureum, dorsum ejus gemmis stratum. Quis est iste lectus, nisi corporis nostri species? Namque in gemmis aer specie fulgoris ostenditur, in auro ignis, aqua in argento, terra per lignum; ex quibus corpus humanum quatuor constat elementis, in quo nostra recubat anima, 1899Csi non aspero montium, non humi arido expers quietis exsistat; sed sublimis a vitiis ligno fulta requiescat. Unde et David dicit: Dominus opem ferat illi super lectum doloris ejus (Psal. XL, 4); nam lectus doloris esse qui potest, cum dolere non possit, qui non habet sensum? Corpus autem doloris, sicut corpus est mortis: Infelix ego homo! quis me liberabit de corpore mortis ejus (Rom. VII, 24)?

30.

(Vers. 9, 10.) King Solomon made himself a bed out of the woods of Lebanon; he made its pillars of silver, its headrest of gold, its ascent of purple, its back strewn with jewels. [Vulg. Cant. III, 9–10 Ferculum fecit sibi rex Salomon, "a litter"; and Vulg. has nothing answering to dorsum ejus gemmis stratum] What is this bed, but the appearance of our body? For in jewels the air is displayed under the appearance of brightness, in gold fire, water in silver, earth by wood; of which four elements the human body consists, in which our soul reclines, 1899Cif it be not without rest upon the roughness of the mountains or upon the dry ground; but, raised on high above the vices, may rest propped upon the wood. Whence David also says: May the Lord bring help to him upon the bed of his sorrow (Psal. XL, 4); for how can there be a bed of sorrow, when that which has no feeling cannot feel sorrow? Now a body of sorrow is like a body of death: Unhappy man that I am! who will free me from the body of that death (Rom. VII, 24)? [Vulg. Rom. VII, 24 de corpore mortis hujus, "of this death"]

31.

Et quoniam versiculum induximus, in quo Dominici corporis fecimus mentionem, ne quem forte perturbet legentem, quod Dominus corpus suscepit doloris, recordetur quia Lazari mortem et doluit et flevit, et in passione est vulneratus, atque ex vulnere sanguis et aqua exivit, spiritumque exhalavit (Joan. XI, 35). Aqua ad lavacrum, sanguis ad 1899Dpotum, spiritus ad resurrectionem. Unus enim est Christus nobis spes, fides, charitas: spes in resurrectione, fides in lavacro, charitas in sacramento. Tamen ut corpus suscepit doloris, ita etiam stratum in infirmitate versavit; quia convertit in commodum carnis humanae: nam passione infirmitas est soluta, mors resurrectione. Et tamen moerere pro saeculo, gaudere debetis in Domino: tristes ad poenitentiam, alacres ad gratiam, licet et flere cum flentibus, et gaudere cum gaudentibus oportere gentium Doctor salutari praeceptione praescripserit (Rom. XII, 15). 1900A

31.

And since we have brought in a verse in which we have made mention of the Lord's body, lest perhaps it disturb any reader that the Lord took upon himself a body of sorrow, let him remember that he both sorrowed over the death of Lazarus and wept, and in the passion was wounded, and from the wound blood and water came out, and he breathed out his spirit (Joan. XI, 35). Water for the washing, blood for 1899Ddrink, spirit for the resurrection. For Christ is to us one hope, faith, charity: hope in the resurrection, faith in the washing, charity in the sacrament. Yet as he took upon himself a body of sorrow, so also he turned the couch in infirmity; because he turned it to the advantage of human flesh: for by the passion infirmity was loosed, death by the resurrection. And nevertheless you ought to mourn for the world and to rejoice in the Lord: sorrowful unto penitence, eager unto grace — although the Doctor of the Gentiles has laid it down by a saving precept that we must both weep with them that weep and rejoice with them that rejoice (Rom. XII, 15). 1900A

32.

Media charitate constravit, propter filias Hierusalem. Quae sunt istae filiae? Non debet esse occultum, quod superiores videantur qui ante generali sunt, infirmiores qui postea. Comparantur enim utero mulieris partus saeculi hujus; quoniam fortiores sunt qui in virtute juventutis nati sunt, infirmiores qui tempore senectutis. Defecit enim multitudine generationis hoc saeculum sicut vulva generantis, et tamquam senescens creatura robur juventutis suae, velut marcenti jam virium suarum vigore deponit. Istae ergo sunt filiae Hierusalem, id est, teneriores et infirmiores animae: istis se exhibet ut luceat imago ejus in confessione earum, luceat in dilectione, luceat in operibus; et si fieri potest, tota ejus species exprimatur in ipsis.

32.

He strewed the midst with charity, for the daughters of Jerusalem. Who are these daughters? It ought not to be hidden that those seem superior who are generali beforehand, and weaker those who come afterwards. For the birth-throes of this world are compared to the womb of a woman; since those are stronger who were born in the strength of youth, weaker those born in the time of old age. For this world has failed in the multitude of its begetting, like the womb of one giving birth, and like an aging creature it lays down the strength of its youth, as though the vigour of its powers were now withering. These, then, are the daughters of Jerusalem, that is, the more tender and weaker souls: to these he shows himself, that his image may shine in their confession, may shine in their love, may shine in their works; and, if it can be done, that his whole form may be expressed in them.

33.

1900BHae igitur filiae Hierusalem sponsam ascendentem, et non resistentem videntes, et meritorum ejus odoribus delectatae, agnoscentes quoque Salomonis illius esse pacifici sponsam, etiam comitatu sedulo persequuntur usque ad lectum Salomonis: eo quod ei vera in Christo requies debeatur. Lectus enim sanctorum Christus est, in quo universorum fessa saecularibus praeliis corda requiescunt. In hoc lecto requievit Isaac, et benedixit filium juniorem, dicens: Major serviet minori: in hoc lecto recubans Jacob, benedixit duodecim patriarchas: in hoc lecto recubans archisynagogi filia, surrexit a morte: in hoc lecto jacens unicus viduae defunctus filius, mortis vincula Christi voce vocatus dissolvit. Perducta igitur sponsa usque ad sponsi requiem, nuptiale 1900Ccanunt carmen, dicentes filiabus Hierusalem:

33.

1900BThese daughters of Jerusalem, therefore, seeing the bride ascending and not resisting, and delighted by the odours of her merits, recognizing also that she is the bride of that peaceable Solomon, follow after her with sedulous escort as far as the bed of Solomon: because true rest in Christ is due to her. For the bed of the saints is Christ, in which the hearts of all men, wearied by the battles of the world, find rest. In this bed Isaac rested, and blessed his younger son, saying: The greater shall serve the lesser: reclining in this bed Jacob blessed the twelve patriarchs: reclining in this bed the daughter of the ruler of the synagogue rose from death: lying in this bed the widow's only son, who had died, loosed the bonds of death when called by the voice of Christ. The bride, therefore, having been brought as far as the rest of the bridegroom, they sing the marriage 1900Csong, saying to the daughters of Jerusalem:

34.

(Vers. 11.) Egredimini et videte in rege Salomone coronam, qua coronavit eum mater ejus, in die sponsalium ejus. Canunt epithalamium, et vocant caeteras potestates coelorum vel 1579 animas, ut videant charitatem, quam circa filias Hierusalem habet Christus. Unde et meruit coronari a matre quasi filius charitatis, sicut Paulus ostendit dicens: Quia Deus eripuit nos de potestate tenebrarum, et transtulit in regnum filii charitatis suae (Coloss. I, 13). Charitatis itaque filius et ipse charitas est: non ex accidentibus habens charitatem, sed habens eam substantia sua, sicut regnum de quo dicit: Ego in hoc natus sum (Joan. XVIII, 17).

34.

(Vers. 11.) Go forth and see upon king Solomon the crown with which his mother crowned him, in the day of his espousals. [Vulg. Cant. III, 11 in diademate quo coronavit illum mater sua in die desponsationis illius] They sing the epithalamium, and call the other powers of the heavens, or 1579 the souls, to see the charity which Christ has toward the daughters of Jerusalem. Whence he also deserved to be crowned by his mother as the son of charity, as Paul shows, saying: Because God has rescued us from the power of darkness, and has carried us over into the kingdom of the son of his charity (Coloss. I, 13). [Vulg. Col. I, 13 in regnum filii dilectionis suae, "of the son of his love"] And so the son of charity is himself also charity: not having charity from accidents, but having it by his own substance, like the kingdom of which he says: For this was I born (Joan. XVIII, 17).

35.

Ideoque dicit, Egredimini, id est, exite de 1900Dsollicitudinibus et cogitationibus saeculi: exite de angustiis corporalibus: exite de vanitatibus mundi, et videte quam rex pacificus in die sponsalium suorum habeat charitatem: quam gloriosus sit, quia corporibus resurrectionem dedit, et animas sibi junxit. Haec magni est corona certaminis, hoc praeclarum munus sponsalium Christi, sanguis ejus et passio. Quis enim plus potuit dare, qui sibi etiam non pepercit, et pro nobis in mortem animam suam posuit?

35.

And therefore he says, Go forth, that is, go out from the 1900Danxieties and thoughts of the world: go out from bodily straits: go out from the vanities of the world, and see what charity the peaceable king has in the day of his espousals: how glorious he is, because he gave resurrection to bodies, and joined souls to himself. This is the crown of a great contest, this the illustrious gift of Christ's espousals, his blood and his passion. For who could give more, he who did not even spare himself, and laid down his soul unto death for us?

36.

Quae autem corona gloriae nisi Ecclesia quae 1901Acaput suum Christum coronat? Quae jucunditas universae terrae, nisi domus populi Christiani, aula sanctorum, de quibus scriptum est: In omnem terram exivit sonus eorum (Psal. XVIII, 5)? Ergo quia corona gloriae est Ecclesia, ideo dicitur: Egredimini, filiae Sion, et videte regem Salomonem, in corona qua coronavit eum mater ejus, in die sponsalium ejus, et in die jucunditatis cordis ejus.

36.

But what is the crown of glory, but the Church which 1901Acrowns Christ her head? What is the delight of the whole earth, but the house of the Christian people, the court of the saints, of whom it is written: Their sound has gone out into all the earth (Psal. XVIII, 5)? Therefore, because the Church is the crown of glory, it is said: Go forth, daughters of Sion, and see king Solomon, in the crown with which his mother crowned him, in the day of his espousals, and in the day of the gladness of his heart.

37.

Ad animas dicitur ut e gurgustiis et claustris exeant corporalibus, extra corporis cogitationes exeant, egrediantur a cupiditatibus, et a curis, et a caeteris carnis istius et affectus lubrici passionibus: supra mundum ascendant, de hoc mundo exeant, Christo occurrant, paratae adsint facibus ardentibus relucentes, quasi angeli Christi haec loquuntur: Non 1901Bpotestis videre claritatem ejus, gloriam ejus, nisi egrediamini humanae fragilitatis curas, filiae Hierusalem. Quasi dicant: Quid viventem inter mortuos quaeritis (Luc. XXIV, 5)? Non intra hunc mundum quaeritur Christus, qui supra mundum suos esse voluit discipulos.

37.

It is said to souls that they should go out from the hovels and prisons of the body, that they should go out beyond the thoughts of the body, that they should depart from covetous desires, and from cares, and from the other passions of this flesh and of slippery affection: that they should climb above the world, go out from this world, meet Christ, be present and ready, shining with burning torches. As though the angels of Christ were speaking, they say these things: You 1901Bcannot see his brightness, his glory, unless you go out from the cares of human frailty, O daughters of Jerusalem. As if they said: Why do you seek the living among the dead (Luc. XXIV, 5)? [Vulg. Luc. XXIV, 5 Quid quaeritis viventem cum mortuis?] Christ is not sought within this world, who willed his disciples to be above the world.

38.

Quae est corona qua coronatur Christus, nisi corona gloriae? Joseph coronam habuit castitatis, Paulus justitiae, Petrus fidei. Singularum virtutum coronae sunt. Solus Christus habet coronam gloriae, qua eum Ecclesia coronavit. In hac corona omnes coronae sunt; quia gloria non portio unius coronae, sed praemium omnium coronarum est.

38.

What is the crown with which Christ is crowned, but the crown of glory? Joseph had the crown of chastity, Paul of justice, Peter of faith. There are crowns of the several virtues. Christ alone has the crown of glory, with which the Church crowned him. In this crown are all the crowns; because glory is not the portion of one crown, but the reward of all the crowns.

CAPUT QUARTUM.

CHAPTER FOUR.

1.

1901C(Vers. 1.) Jam vero conversus Sponsus in laudem sponsae dicit: Quam pulchra es, amica mea, quam pulchra es! oculi tui sicut columbae, extra taciturnitatem. Pulchra in praedicatione, pulchra in conversatione. Oculi quoque ejus, inquit, sicut columbae. Oculi sunt viri, videlicet spiritalibus ornati sensibus qui ad videnda mysteria sint acuti, et parati ad penetranda Scripturae secreta divinae, rationabili lacte fulgentes, in quibus non sit aliqua doli maculosa confusio, sed simplicis affectus pura et immaculata simplicitas. Ideo in aquarum abundantia lotas has columbas in lacte postea memoravit.

1.

1901C(Vers. 1.) But now the Bridegroom, turned to the praise of the bride, says: How beautiful thou art, my love, how beautiful thou art! thine eyes are as doves, apart from silence. [Vulg. Cant. IV, 1 Oculi tui columbarum, absque eo quod intrinsecus latet] Beautiful in preaching, beautiful in her manner of life. Her eyes also, he says, are as doves. They are the eyes of a man, plainly adorned with spiritual senses, which are keen for seeing the mysteries, and ready for penetrating the secret things of divine Scripture, shining with reasonable milk, in which there is no spotted confusion of guile, but the pure and unstained simplicity of a single-hearted affection. Therefore he afterwards mentioned these doves as washed in milk, in an abundance of waters.

2.
