Jam caetera dentes et genas, et sicut coccineum resticulum labia sponsae intelligimus animae esse virtutes doctorumque diversitates: qui vel spiritalem 1901Dmenti alimoniam dispensatione sedula subministrent, vel praedicatione Dominicae crucis, sicut Verbi quadam linea, alligent audientem: vel modestia verecundi, et juventutis flore gratissimi. Etsi a tactu pudore revocentur, redoleat tamen in his odor Christi: et sicut in genas, quasi a capite sacerdotali descendat unguentum, sic et in his eluceat pulchritudo doctrinae.

2.

Now as for the rest — the teeth and the cheeks, and the lips of the bride like a scarlet cord — we understand them to be the virtues of the soul and the diversities of teachers: who either supply spiritual 1901Dnourishment to the mind by sedulous dispensation, or, by the preaching of the Lord's cross, bind the hearer as with a certain cord of the Word: or who are modest in their bashfulness and most pleasing in the flower of youth. And though they be called back from the touch by shame, yet let the odour of Christ be fragrant in them: and just as the ointment runs down upon the cheeks, as from the priestly head, so also let the beauty of doctrine shine out in them.

3.

Oculi tui sicut columbae extra taciturnitatem tuam; quod et spiritualiter videat, et noverit tempus tacendi esse et tempus loquendi; ut in tempore sermonem 1902Asuum proferat, ne importunitate loquendi peccatum possit incurrere.

3.

Thine eyes are as doves apart from thy silence; which both sees spiritually, and knows that there is a time for keeping silence and a time for speaking; so that it may bring forth its word 1902Ain season, lest by unseasonableness of speaking it be able to incur sin.

4.

Oculi tui sicut columbae. Hic columbae, id est, Ecclesiae simplicitas revelatur et gratia. Vide itaque simplicitatem oculi, qui enim justum videt et gaudet etiam ipse vult justus esse: pulchrum est enim ut eo in aliis delectetur, 1580 quod vult ipse in se servare, si possit: est enim insitum bonis, ut castum pudicus, prudentem sapiens affectu pio diligat, misericors liberalem, virtutes suas in aliis amet. Plerisque etiam justi aspectus admonitio correctionis est, perfectioribus vero laetitia. Nam si tanta vis in naturalibus est, ut animal visum prosit ictericis, ita ut mortui quoque corpus ejus animantis prodesse dicatur, si fuerit demonstratum iis, qui hujusmodi 1902Binciderint passionem, dubitare possumus quod justi sanet aspectus, cujus sunt oculi sicut columbae, simplices et pudici? Ergo vile animal irrationabile tantam virtutem habet, ut sanare hominem possit momento exiguo, quo videtur: homo justus, si tamen cum fide ab eo conspiciatur, qui utilitatem ab eo percipere desiderat, nihil conferet? Quam pulchrum igitur ut videaris et prosis!

4.

Thine eyes are as doves. Here the simplicity and grace of the dove, that is, of the Church, is revealed. See then the simplicity of the eye: for he who sees a just man and rejoices, himself also wishes to be just: for it is a beautiful thing that a man should be delighted in others by that 1580 which he wishes to keep in himself, if he can: for it is inborn in good men that the modest man should love the chaste, the wise man the prudent, with holy affection, the merciful man the generous, and that they should love their own virtues in others. For very many, indeed, the sight of a just man is an admonition to correction, but for the more perfect it is a joy. For if there is such power in natural things that a living creature, when seen, profits the jaundiced — so that even the body of that creature when dead is said to profit, if it be shown to those who have fallen into 1902Bthis kind of ailment — can we doubt that the sight of a just man heals, whose eyes are as doves, simple and modest? Therefore a cheap and irrational animal has such power that it can heal a man in the tiny moment in which it is seen: will a just man, if only he be looked upon with faith by one who desires to receive benefit from him, confer nothing? How beautiful a thing it is, then, that thou shouldst be seen and do good!

5.

Bonum ergo vir justus. Propterea denique apostolus Paulus ascendit Hierosolymam, ut justos videret: et cum Petro mansit diebus quindecim, ut ex ejus aliquid cohabitatione proficeret (Gal. I, 18). Propterea ipse Paulus et Barnabas cum Hierosolymam ingrederentur, magnifice excipiebantur ab 1902CEcclesia, et apostolis, et majoribus natu: cum autem abire vellent, ne discederent rogabantur, et ut postea de Paulo legimus, quod cum lacrymis deducebatur. Nonne vel ipsi oculorum radii virtutem quamdam videntur infundere, iis qui fideliter eum videre desiderant?

5.

A just man, then, is a good thing. This, in short, is why the apostle Paul went up to Jerusalem, that he might see just men: and he stayed with Peter fifteen days, that he might gain something from living with him (Gal. I, 18). For this reason Paul himself and Barnabas, when they were entering Jerusalem, were magnificently received by 1902Cthe Church, and by the apostles, and by the elders: but when they wished to depart, they were begged not to go away, and, as we read afterwards concerning Paul, he was escorted on his way with tears. Do not the very rays of the eyes seem to pour a certain power into those who faithfully desire to see him?

6.

Sed quemadmodum justus laetificat cor innocentum, cum videtur, ita improbi justorum cognitione torquentur, quia vel tacitis sanctorum moribus arguuntur. Torquet castitas incontinentem, avaritiam liberalitas, impietatem fides. De hoc quoque parile vilis animantis sumamus exemplum. Nam sicut prodesse diximus mutum animal cum videtur, ita obesse percipimus lupum, si aliquem videndo praevenerit; vocem enim feruntur amittere, quos prius 1902Dlupus viderit. Basiliscus quoque (hic est noxius serpens) si quodcumque viderit prior animal, fertur occidere: et allegatur statim necari, qui potuerit a serpente hujusmodi praevideri. Ipse quoque serpens mori dicitur, si fuerit hominis praeventus aspectu. Ergo si tanta virtus vel in oculis serpentis, vel in oculis est hominis, ut si alterum alter prior viderit, possit occidere: non est virtus in oculis justi, qui repletus virtutis est gratia, cum praesertim tantum operetur fides; ut et illa quae fimbriam Domini tetigit, sanaretur: et ille cui intendit Dominus Jesus, statim 1903Aex ejus oculis gratiam sanitatis hauriret (Matth. IX, 22)?

6.

But just as a just man gladdens the heart of the innocent when he is seen, so the wicked are tormented by the knowledge of the just, because they are reproved even by the silent conduct of the saints. Chastity torments the incontinent, generosity avarice, faith impiety. Of this too let us take a like example from a cheap living creature. For as we have said that a dumb animal profits when it is seen, so we perceive that the wolf does harm, if he has got ahead of anyone in the seeing; for those whom the wolf 1902Dhas seen first are reported to lose their voice. The basilisk also (this is a harmful serpent), if it should see any animal first, is reported to kill it: and it is alleged that a man is slain at once who has been able to be seen beforehand by a serpent of this kind. The serpent itself also is said to die, if it has been forestalled by the sight of a man. Therefore, if there is such power either in the eyes of a serpent or in the eyes of a man, that if the one sees the other first he can kill him: is there no power in the eyes of a just man, who is filled with the grace of virtue — especially since faith works so much that even she who touched the Lord's fringe was healed, and he upon whom the Lord Jesus looked drew at once 1903Afrom his eyes the grace of health (Matth. IX, 22)?

7.

Sed qui videt justum, debet scire quid videat. Non illum videt in corpore, non in vestimento, non in patrimonio, non in vultu: sed intus videt. Non, inquam, illum videt, nisi ejus viderit mentem, nisi ejus sermonem intenderit, nisi sensum illius potuerit comprehendere, sapientiam de tractatu ejus potuerit assumere. Tunc igitur laetabitur cum ista perspexerit, cum ista cognoverit. Ita ergo et nos sicubi justum audimus, festinemus videre, sicut illa mulier quae audivit in domo pharisaei discumbere Dominum Jesum, et ingressa est, et ejus pedes perfudit unguento (Luc. VII, 37 et 38). Illius imitatores simus, quia Ecclesiae in illa muliere figuram esse quis dubitet? 1903BSicubi ergo justus sedeat, sicubi accumbat, festinemus videre eum. Pretiosum est videre virum justum, ut videas eum secundum imaginem Dei. Quod foris est, nihil prodest: quod intus est, sanat. Sane et in eo qui foris est, illum qui intus est, frequenter aspicimus: ut si viderimus pauperem, illum ad cujus similitudinem factus est, honoremus in paupere, de quo ait: Dedistis mihi manducare, quia quod uni eorum dedistis, mihi dedistis (Matth. XXV, 35 et 40).

7.

But he who sees a just man ought to know what he sees. He does not see him in the body, not in his clothing, not in his patrimony, not in his face: but he sees within. He does not, I say, see him, unless he has seen his mind, unless he has attended to his speech, unless he has been able to grasp his understanding, has been able to take up wisdom from his discourse. Then therefore will he rejoice, when he has looked upon these things, when he has come to know them. So then let us also, whenever we hear of a just man, hasten to see him, like that woman who heard that the Lord Jesus was reclining at table in the house of the Pharisee, and went in, and poured ointment over his feet (Luc. VII, 37 et 38). Let us be imitators of her, for who would doubt that in that woman there is a figure of the Church? 1903BWherever, then, a just man may sit, wherever he may recline at table, let us hasten to see him. It is a precious thing to see a just man, that thou mayest see him according to the image of God. What is outside profits nothing: what is within heals. Certainly in him who is without we often behold him who is within: so that if we see a poor man, let us honour in the poor man him to whose likeness he was made, of whom he says: You gave me to eat, because what you gave to one of these, you gave to me (Matth. XXV, 35 et 40).

8.

Capillamentum tuum ut grex tonsarum, quae revelatae sunt a monte Galaad. Ideo capillamentum, quia virtus omnium sensuum in capite est: Oculi enim sapientis in capite ejus (Eccl. II). Profunda igitur doctorum prudentia, quae potest ea quae obscura 1903Csunt revelare, et alta aperire sensuum.

8.

Thy hair is as a flock of shorn ones, which have been revealed from mount Galaad. [Vulg. Cant. IV, 1 Capilli tui sicut greges caprarum quae ascenderunt de monte Galaad] Therefore hair, because the power of all the senses is in the head: for The eyes of the wise man are in his head (Eccl. II). Deep, then, is the prudence of teachers, which can reveal the things that are obscure, 1903Cand open up the heights of the senses.

9.

(Vers. 2.) Dentes tui sicut grex tonsarum, quae ascenderunt a lavacro, quae omnes geminos creant, et infecunda non est in eis. Quod specie de capris dicitur, mystice de Ecclesiae grege sentiendum. Nec vilia tibi videantur ista animalia, vides quod in altis grex iste pascitur, audax in monte. Itaque ubi aliis praecipitia, ibi capris nullum periculum: ubi aliis periculum, ibi gregis illius alimentum, ibi cibus 1581 dulcior, ibi fructus electior. Speculantur a pastoribus suis, dumosa de rupe pendentes, ubi luporum incursus esse non possunt, ibi fecundae arbores fructum integrum subministrant. Cernere licet uberi lacte distentas, super teneram sobolem materna 1903Dpietate sollicitas. Ideo elegit eas Spiritus sanctus, quibus coitum venerabilis Ecclesiae compararet.

9.

(Vers. 2.) Thy teeth are as a flock of shorn ones, which have come up from the washing, all of which bring forth twins, and there is not a barren one among them. [Vulg. Cant. IV, 2 omnes gemellis fetibus, et sterilis non est inter eas] What is said in the literal sense of she-goats is to be understood mystically of the flock of the Church. And let these animals not seem cheap to thee; thou seest that this flock feeds on the heights, bold upon the mountain. And so where for others there are precipices, there for the she-goats is no danger: where for others is danger, there is the food of that flock, there the sweeter 1581 fare, there the choicer fruit. They are watched by their shepherds as they hang from the bushy rock, where the inroads of wolves cannot reach; there fruitful trees supply whole fruit. One may see them swollen with abundant milk, anxious with a mother's love over their tender offspring. 1903DTherefore the Holy Spirit chose them, to which he might compare the coming together of the venerable Church.

10.

Et ut mystice audias, capillamentum Verbi est altitudo, et eminentia quaedam justarum animasum; quoniam sensus sapientis in capite ejus. In altitudine enim cogitationis humanae certum est esse sapientiam. Et quemadmodum tondentur caprae, ut superflua deponant: ita etiam tonsarum animarum gregem, hoc est, multarum animarum virtutes habet sancta Ecclesia, in quo grege nihil possis insensibile reperire, nihil superfluum; quoniam fides sapientes 1904Afecit, spiritalis autem gratia ab omni superfluorum labe mundavit.

10.

And that thou mayest hear it mystically: the hair of the Word is the height, and a certain eminence, of just animasum; since the understanding of the wise man is in his head. For it is certain that wisdom lies in the height of human thought. And just as she-goats are shorn, that they may lay aside what is superfluous: so also holy Church has a flock of shorn souls, that is, the virtues of many souls, in which flock thou couldst find nothing without feeling, nothing superfluous; since faith has made them wise, 1904Aand spiritual grace has cleansed them from every stain of superfluous things.

11.

Merito igitur revelatae sunt animae justorum, et revelatae a monte Galaad, hoc est, a transmigratione testimonii, eo quod a Synagoga ad Ecclesiam testimonium coeleste migravit. In hoc itaque monte nascitur thymiama, resina, et caeteri odores quos negotiatores illi Ismaelitae, ut habes in libro primo Testamenti veteris, deferebant (Gen. XXXVII, 25). Itaque sicut caprae bonis refectae cibis, et solis calore vernantes, lavantur in flumine, et exsultantes mundae surgunt de flumine: ita animae justorum ascendunt ab spiritali lavacro.

11.

Rightly, therefore, have the souls of the just been revealed, and revealed from mount Galaad, that is, from the passing over of testimony, in that the heavenly testimony passed over from the Synagogue to the Church. And so on this mountain grow the incense-gum, the resin, and the other perfumes which those Ishmaelite traders were carrying, as thou hast it in the first book of the Old Testament (Gen. XXXVII, 25). And so, just as she-goats refreshed with good food and blooming in the warmth of the sun are washed in the river, and rise up out of the river exulting and clean: so the souls of the just come up from the spiritual washing.

12.

Istae sunt vere qui geminos creant, in quibus non est infecunditas aliqua virtutum, sterilitas ulla 1904Bmeritorum. Bene geminos creant, quia congeminant sensus suos; unde habes in Proverbiis scriptum: Et tu scribe haec tibi tripliciter, in consilium, et in cognitionem (Prov. XXII, 20). Triplicem praemisit scriptionem, et duo subdidit, consilium et cognitionem. Sed cognitio gemina est, una incorporalium, altera corporalium; unde etiam in posterioribus habes: Duo ubera tua sicut duo hinnuli capreae gemelli, qui pascuntur in liliis, donec aspiret dies, et inclinentur umbrae (Vers. 5).

12.

These are they who truly bring forth twins, in whom there is no barrenness of virtues, no sterility 1904Bof merits. Well do they bring forth twins, because they double their understandings; whence thou hast it written in Proverbs: And do thou write these things for thyself in threefold fashion, unto counsel and unto knowledge (Prov. XXII, 20). [Vulg. Prov. XXII, 20 Ecce descripsi eam tibi tripliciter, in cogitationibus et scientia] He set a threefold writing before, and added two things, counsel and knowledge. But knowledge is twofold: one of incorporeal things, the other of corporeal; whence also in what follows thou hast: Thy two breasts are as two twin fawns of a roe, which feed among the lilies, until the day break and the shadows retire (Vers. 5).

13.

Diximus de fecunditate, dicamus de dentibus. Nam et navigantes plerique, et properantes itinere terreno, ubi viderint speciosum aliquem locum, delectationis gratia demorantur, pascunt oculos animumque 1904Callevant. Nec mora ulla putatur commeandi, sed gratia. Ita et nobis pulcherrimos dentes justarum animarum considerare cordi est; docuit enim Scriptura pulcherrimos dentes esse justorum dicens secundum litteram quidem de patriarcha Juda, spiritualiter autem de Christo; Hilares oculi ejus a vino, et dentes sicut lac (Genes. XLIX, 12). In quo non utique carnis humanae officia, sed divinae gratiae munera praedicavit. Docet igitur exemplum, dentes non esse praetereundos, ubi de oculis disseruimus.

13.

We have spoken of fruitfulness; let us speak of the teeth. For very many who sail, and those hastening on a journey by land, when they have seen some beautiful place, linger there for the sake of the delight, they feed their eyes and lift up 1904Ctheir mind. Nor is any of this reckoned a delay in their travelling, but a favour. So it is our heart's desire also to consider the most beautiful teeth of just souls; for Scripture has taught that the teeth of the just are most beautiful, speaking indeed according to the letter of the patriarch Judah, but spiritually of Christ: His eyes are merry from wine, and his teeth are as milk (Genes. XLIX, 12). [Vulg. Gen. XLIX, 12 Pulchriores sunt oculi ejus vino, et dentes ejus lacte candidiores] In which it proclaimed not the services of human flesh, but the gifts of divine grace. The example teaches us, therefore, that the teeth are not to be passed over, where we have discoursed of the eyes.

14.

Qui sunt igitur justarum dentes animarum nisi qui informem ac durum accipientes cibum, vel frigidum plerumque, vel supra modum calentem nunc comminuunt, nunc fovent, nunc temperant, prout qualitas fuerit alimentorum? Dura comminuunt, 1904Dne asperitas litterae in veteri Testamento et saecularis intellectus rigor, nisi fuerit spiritali dente resolutus, vitalia ipsa interclusis librorum salutarium commeatibus, et gulam quamdam animae incuriosa edacitate suffocet. Par est igitur ut dividas primum, si solida tibi videtur esca quae sumitur, et distinguas eam; atque emollitam sine noxa aliqua animae in omnia ejus membra naturali divisione transfundas; ut vitalem succum omne ejus corpus epuletur, nihil cadaverosum, nihil mortuum ore tuo sumas, ne dicatur: Sepulcrum patens est guttur eorum (Psalm. XIII, 3). Sed vivum haurias verbum, 1905Aut in tuae mentis visceribus possit operari. Hi dentes super lac candidiores; quia dentes justorum.

14.

What then are the teeth of just souls, but those which, receiving food shapeless and hard, or often cold, or hot beyond measure, now break it small, now warm it, now temper it, according as the quality of the nourishment has been? They break the hard things small, 1904Dlest the harshness of the letter in the Old Testament and the rigidity of a worldly understanding, if it be not dissolved by the spiritual tooth, should choke the vital parts themselves — the passages of the books of salvation being shut off — and choke a certain gullet of the soul, by a careless voracity. It is fitting, therefore, that thou first divide it, if the food which is taken seems solid to thee, and that thou distinguish it; and that, having softened it, thou pour it without any harm to the soul into all its members by a natural division; so that its whole body may feast upon the vital juice, and that thou take nothing corpse-like, nothing dead, into thy mouth, lest it be said: Their throat is an open sepulchre (Psalm. XIII, 3). But mayest thou drink in the living word, 1905Athat it may be able to work in the inward parts of thy mind. These teeth are whiter than milk; because they are the teeth of the just.

15.

Denique cum omnes in Moyse baptizati in nube et in mari patres nostri fuerint, non otiose tamen scriptum est: Quia omnes eamdem escam spiritalem manducaverunt, et omnes eumdem potum spiritalem biberunt (I Cor. X, 3 et 4); ut istis sanctorum dentibus major quidam fulgor accederet, quos post transitum maris rubri, myrrhae fontis amaritudine per ligni gratiam temperata (Exodi XV, 25), cognoscemus esse mundatos: deinde duodecim fontium potu, postremo petrae spiritalem undam vomentis irriguo; Petra erat enim Christus (I Cor. X, 3 et 4). 1582 Ideo et manna manducaverunt (Num. XX, 11), ut toties abluti manducarent panem, ut scriptum 1905Best, angelorum (Psalm. LXXVII, 25). Nunc quoque in Evangelii mysteriis recognoscis baptizatos, licet toto corpore, postea tamen esca spiritali potuque mundari; ille enim est verus dentium fulgor, ubi bene consciae mentis resonat canora confessio. Ille mundus oculus, quem peccatorum gravium trabes nulla depresserit, quem purgamentorum levium festuca nulla turbaverit.

15.

In short, although all our fathers were baptized in Moses in the cloud and in the sea, it is nevertheless not written idly: That all ate the same spiritual food, and all drank the same spiritual drink (I Cor. X, 3 et 4); so that a certain greater brightness might come to these teeth of the saints, whom, after the passage of the Red Sea, we shall recognize to have been cleansed by the bitterness of the fountain of myrrh tempered through the grace of the wood (Exodi XV, 25); then by the drink of the twelve fountains, and last by the watering of the rock that poured out a spiritual wave; For the rock was Christ (I Cor. X, 3 et 4). 1582 Therefore they also ate manna (Num. XX, 11), that so often washed they might eat the bread, as it is written, 1905Bof angels (Psalm. LXXVII, 25). Now too in the mysteries of the Gospel thou recognizest that the baptized, though washed in the whole body, are nevertheless afterwards cleansed by spiritual food and drink; for that is the true brightness of the teeth, where the tuneful confession of a mind well aware of itself resounds. That eye is clean which no beam of grave sins has weighed down, which no mote of light defilements has troubled.

16.

Dentes tui sicut grex tonsarum, quae ascenderunt de lavacro, quae omnes geminos creant, et infecunda non est in eis. Non mediocris ista laudatio, primum apta comparatione tonsarum; capras enim et in altis pasci sine periculo novimus, et in praeruptis securas cibum sumere, deinde cum tondentur, deonerari superfluis. Harum gregi comparatur Ecclesia, multas in se habens animarum virtutes, quae per lavacrum 1905Csuperflua peccata deponant, quae mysticam fidem et moralem gratiam deferant Christo, quae crucem Domini Jesu loquantur. In his formosa est Ecclesia, unde ad eam Deus Verbum dicit: Ecce formosa es, proxima mea: ecce es formosa; oculi tui sicut columbae. Tota formosa es, proxima mea, et reprehensio non est in te; quia culpa demersa est. Ades huc a Libano, sponsa, ades huc a Libano, et pertransibis a principio fidei; eo quod renuntians mundo, transierit saeculum, pertransierit ad Christum.

16.

Thy teeth are as a flock of shorn ones, which have come up from the washing, all of which bring forth twins, and there is not a barren one among them. This is no ordinary praise: first, by the fitting comparison with shorn ones; for we know that she-goats both feed on the heights without danger, and take their food in safety upon sheer crags, and then, when they are shorn, are unburdened of what is superfluous. To their flock the Church is compared, having in herself many virtues of souls, which through the washing lay aside 1905Ctheir superfluous sins, which bring to Christ mystical faith and moral grace, which speak of the cross of the Lord Jesus. In these the Church is well-formed, whence God the Word says to her: Behold, thou art well-formed, my neighbor: behold, thou art well-formed; thine eyes are as doves. Thou art wholly well-formed, my neighbor, and there is no reproof in thee; [Vulg. Cant. IV, 1 and 7 amica mea … macula non est in te] because guilt has been drowned. Come hither from Lebanon, O bride, come hither from Lebanon, and thou shalt pass through from the beginning of faith; [Vulg. Cant. IV, 8 veni de Libano … coronaberis de capite Amana] in that, renouncing the world, she has passed beyond the age, and has passed over to Christ.

17.

(Vers. 3.) Sicut resticula coccinea labia tua. In cocco enim species ignis, et crucis Dominicae sanguis irrutilat. Coccinea labia Domini, quae passionem propriam loquebatur. Denique in Exodo coccum pro igne collocatum est (Exod. XXV, 4). Non enim ex 1905Dcocco mundus, sed ex quatuor constat elementis. Sed in cocco figura ignis expressa est, cujus vapor nisi coelum atque aerem, maria terrasque penetraret, omnia tamquam effetis viribus solverentur. Per resticulam igitur vinculum persuasionis agnoscimus, per coccum vel cupiditatis ardorem qui scintillat in animis audientium, vel indicium passionis.

17.

(Vers. 3.) Thy lips are as a scarlet cord. For in scarlet the appearance of fire, and the blood of the Lord's cross, glows red. The lips of the Lord are scarlet, which spoke his own passion. In short, in Exodus scarlet is set in the place of fire (Exod. XXV, 4). For the world does not consist 1905Dof scarlet, but of four elements. But in scarlet the figure of fire is expressed, and unless its heat penetrated heaven and the air, the seas and the lands, all things would be dissolved as though their powers were spent. By the cord, therefore, we recognize the bond of persuasion; by the scarlet, either the ardour of desire which sparkles in the minds of the hearers, or the token of the passion.

18.

(Vers. 4.) Sicut turris David cervix tua, quae aedificata est in Thalphioth, mille clypei pendent in ea, omnia jacula potentium. Erecta enim ad Deum cervix, et jugo Christi habilis, quae nulla in terras 1906Aillecebrarum inflexione curvetur; sicut Christi regalis est turris, cui jugum suum Nabuchodonosor non possit imponere. Condidit enim turrim illam David ille manu fortis, et supra murorum aedificavit excelsa; ut subsidio pariter et decori sit: subsidio, quia hostem praevidet et expellit: decori, quia non solum inter humilia, sed etiam inter excelsa supereminet. Ita tamen subsidio vel decori, si habeat in se verbi dogmata velut quaedam phalerarum monilia: habeat etiam jacula potentium prophetarum, quae adversus omnem extollentem se altitudinem, lacertis quibusdam fidei diriguntur.

18.

(Vers. 4.) Thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built in Thalphioth; a thousand shields hang upon it, all the darts of the mighty. For the neck is upright toward God, and fit for the yoke of Christ, and is bent by no bending down to the earth 1906Aof enticements; as the royal tower of Christ is, upon which Nebuchadnezzar cannot lay his yoke. For David, that man mighty of hand, founded that tower, and built it above the heights of the walls; that it might be both for defence and for adornment: for defence, because it foresees the enemy and drives him off: for adornment, because it stands out not only among lowly things, but even among lofty ones. Yet it is so for defence or for adornment only if it has in itself the teachings of the word as certain necklaces of trappings: if it has also the darts of the mighty prophets, which are aimed by certain arms of faith against every height that exalts itself.

19.

Noli igitur in pulverem mortis deducere animam tuam, cui etiam Dominus naturalem altitudinem tribuit et vigorem, quo se excitet et assurgat. 1906BUnde etiam de hac conjunctione animae et corporis dictum opportune accipitur, eo quod in naturali vitae nostrae mysterio, connexa anima corpori velut humi reptet, et pavimento adhaereat, partim propter hoc terrenum habitaculum, partim quia corpus istud de terra est. Et regionis igitur diversorium, et materia ipsa corporis nostri huic concurrit sententiae. Ideoque pulchre Apostolus de mortis hujus corpore liberari cupit; quia carcere quodam claudimur, et in quodam luxuriae gurgustio siti tenebris involvimur delictorum.

19.

Do not, therefore, bring down into the dust of death thy soul, to which the Lord has granted also a natural height and vigour, by which it may rouse itself and rise up. 1906BWhence also what is said of this conjunction of soul and body is opportunely taken, in that in the natural mystery of our life the soul, bound to the body, creeps as it were upon the ground and clings to the pavement, partly on account of this earthly dwelling, partly because this body is of earth. Both the lodging of the region, therefore, and the very matter of our body agree with this judgment. And therefore the Apostle beautifully desires to be freed from the body of this death; because we are shut in a certain prison, and, set in a certain hovel of wantonness, we are wrapped in the darkness of our offenses.

20.

Ergo secundum voluntatem Dei ambulemus, ut adhaerere dicamur Deo. Nam secundum corporis appetentiam qui vivit, caro est: qui secundum praecepta Dei, spiritus est. Non ergo fiat anima nostra caro, hoc est, ut dicamur caro; sicut et illi qui in 1906Cdiluvio perierunt, de quibus dictum est: Quia caro sunt (Genes. VI, 3). Sed magis caro nostra obediens animae gubernaculo, fiat anima, et hoc appellari mereatur nomine; sicut appellata est familia patriarchae Jacob, et generationis ejus sancta posteritas; sic enim scriptum est: Hi filii Balae quam dedit Laban Rachel filiae suae: peperit haec Jacob animas septem. Et ibidem: Omnes autem animae quae intraverunt cum Jacob in Aeyyptum, quae exierunt de femoribus ejus, praeter uxores filiorum, sexaginta sex (Genes. XLVI, 25 et 26). Et in principio Exodi legimus: Erant autem omnes animae de Jacob, quinque et septuaginta (Exod. I, 5). 1583

20.

Let us therefore walk according to the will of God, that we may be said to cleave to God. For he who lives according to the appetite of the body is flesh: he who lives according to the precepts of God is spirit. Let not our soul then become flesh, that is, so that we should be called flesh; like those also who perished in 1906Cthe flood, of whom it was said: That they are flesh (Genes. VI, 3). But rather let our flesh, obeying the governance of the soul, become soul, and deserve to be called by this name; as the household of the patriarch Jacob was called, and the holy posterity of his generation; for so it is written: These are the sons of Bala, whom Laban gave to Rachel his daughter: she bore these to Jacob, seven souls. And in the same place: And all the souls which entered with Jacob into Aeyyptum, which came out of his thighs, besides the wives of his sons, sixty-six (Genes. XLVI, 25 et 26). And at the beginning of Exodus we read: And all the souls from Jacob were seventy-five (Exod. I, 5). [Vulg. Exod. I, 5 septuaginta, seventy] 1583

21.

Ergo illi qui habitabant cum Joseph, et de Aegypto exierunt, animae sunt: qui autem 1906Dcum essent angeli Dei, hoc est vitae probitate angelorum imitantes gratiam ( Qui enim (Matth. XXII, 30) non ducunt uxores, et qui non nubunt, erunt sicut angeli Dei in coelo ); qui ergo cum angeli viderentur, capti sunt decore femineo, hi caro sunt, sicut Dominus Deus dixit: Non permanebit spiritus meus in hominibus in aeternum, propter quod caro sunt (Genes. VI, 3). Et merito exspertes libidinis, angelis comparantur, caro enim non sunt, quia non sunt in carne, sed in spiritu: quales erant illi qui doctorem Apostolum sequebantur, quibus ait: Vos autem non 1907Aestis in carne, sed in Spiritu (Rom. VIII, 9). Qui autem corporea feminarum capiuntur libidine, caro sunt: atque utinam caro tantum, et non etiam equi adhinnientes! Quia, inquit, in uxorem proximi sui adhinniebant (Jerem. V, 8).

21.

Therefore those who dwelt with Joseph and came out of Egypt are souls: but they who, 1906Dthough they were angels of God — that is, imitating by the uprightness of their life the grace of the angels ( For they who (Matth. XXII, 30) do not take wives, and they who do not marry, shall be as the angels of God in heaven ) [Vulg. Matth. XXII, 30 neque nubent neque nubentur, sed erunt sicut angeli Dei in caelo] — they, then, who, though they seemed to be angels, were captivated by womanly beauty, these are flesh, as the Lord God said: My spirit shall not remain in men for ever, because they are flesh (Genes. VI, 3). [Vulg. Gen. VI, 3 in homine … quia caro est, in the singular] And rightly are those who have no part in lust compared to angels, for they are not flesh, because they are not in the flesh, but in the spirit: such were those who followed the Apostle as their teacher, to whom he says: But you 1907Aare not in the flesh, but in the Spirit (Rom. VIII, 9). But those who are captivated by the bodily lust of women are flesh: and would that they were only flesh, and not neighing horses as well! For, he says, they neighed after the wife of his neighbor (Jerem. V, 8).

22.

(Vers. 7.) Tota es formosa, proxima mea, et reprehensio non est in te. Pulchritudinem vero quis potest majorem existimare decore ejus, quae amatur a rege, probatur a judice, dedicatur Domino, consecratur Deo. Semper sponsa, semper innupta; ut nec amor finem habeat, nec damnum pudor. Haec profecto vera pulchritudo est, cui nihil deest, quae sola meretur audire a Domino: Tota es formosa, proxima mea, et reprehensio non est in te.

22.

(Vers. 7.) Thou art wholly well-formed, my neighbor, and there is no reproof in thee. [Vulg. Cant. IV, 7 Tota pulchra es, amica mea, et macula non est in te] But who can reckon any beauty greater than the comeliness of her who is loved by the king, approved by the judge, dedicated to the Lord, consecrated to God. Always a bride, always unwed; so that neither may love have an end, nor modesty a loss. This assuredly is the true beauty, to which nothing is lacking, which alone deserves to hear from the Lord: Thou art wholly well-formed, my neighbor, and there is no reproof in thee.

23.

(Vers. 8.) Veni huc a Libano, veni huc a Libano: 1907Btransibis et pertransibis a principio fidei, a capite Sanir et Hermon, a latibulis leonum, a montibus pardorum. Quibus indiciis ostenditur perfecta et irreprehensibilis virginalis animae pulchritudo, altaribus consecrata divinis, inter occursus et latibula spiritalium bestiarum non inflexa mortalibus, sed intenta mysteriis Dei, meruisse dilectum, cujus ubera plena laetitiae; Vinum enim laetificat cor hominis.

23.

(Vers. 8.) Come hither from Lebanon, come hither from Lebanon: 1907Bthou shalt pass over and pass through from the beginning of faith, from the head of Sanir and Hermon, from the lairs of lions, from the mountains of leopards. By these tokens it is shown that the perfect and irreproachable beauty of the virgin soul, consecrated at the divine altars, not bent by mortal things amid the encounters and the lairs of spiritual beasts, but intent upon the mysteries of God, has deserved her beloved, whose breasts are full of gladness; for wine gladdens the heart of man.

24.

Et odor, inquit, vestimentorum tuorum, sicut odor Libani. Veni a Libano, sponsa, veni a Libano: transibis et pertransibis a principio fidei: transibis in saeculum certatura, pertransibis ad Christum triumphatura de saeculo. Audisti quia te a leonum et pardorum, id est, a spiritalium nequitiarum incursionibus 1907Cseparavit: audisti quia placet ei tuarum pulchritudo virtutum.

24.

And the odour, he says, of thy garments is as the odour of Lebanon. Come from Lebanon, O bride, come from Lebanon: thou shalt pass over and pass through from the beginning of faith: thou shalt pass over into the world to do battle, thou shalt pass through to Christ to triumph over the world. Thou hast heard that he has separated thee from the assaults of lions and leopards, that is, of spiritual wickednesses: 1907Cthou hast heard that the beauty of thy virtues pleases him.

25.

Ades huc a Libano, sponsa, ades huc a Libano: transibis, et pertransibis a principio fidei, a capite Sanir, et Hermoniim, a speluncis leonum, a montibus pardorum; hoc est, egredere de corpore, et totam te exne. Non potes enim mihi adesse, nisi ante peregrineris a corpore; quoniam qui in carne sunt, peregrinantur a Dei regno. Ades, inquit, ades. Bene repetivit, quia sive praesens, sive absens, adesse et placere Domino Deo tuo debes: adesto praesens, adesto absens, etsi adhuc in corpore es; mihi enim omnes praesentes sunt, quorum fides mecum est. Adest mihi qui exiit de saeculo: adest mihi qui me cogitat, me intuetur, de me sperat, cui ego portio sum: adest mihi qui 1907Dabfuerit sibi: ille mecum est, qui intra se non est; quoniam qui in carne est, non est in spiritu: ille mecum est, qui ex seipso egreditur: ille juxta me est qui extra se fuerit: ille mihi integer, qui propter me perdiderit animam suam; et ideo, Ades, ades, sponsa; transibis et pertransibis a principio fidei. Transit et pertransit caeteros, quae ad Christum pervenit: transit fidei merito, et operum claritate, quae lucet sicut Sanir et Hermon, hoc est, via lucernae: transit devictis tentationibus saeculi, et superatis 1908Anequitiis spiritalibus, legitimi petens coronam certaminis.

25.

Come hither from Lebanon, bride, come hither from Lebanon: thou shalt pass over, and thou shalt pass through, from the beginning of faith, from the head of Sanir, and Hermoniim, from the caves of lions, from the mountains of leopards; [Vulg. Veni de Libano… coronaberis de capite Amana, de vertice Sanir et Hermon, de cubilibus leonum, "come from Lebanon… thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana"] that is, go forth out of the body, and exne thyself wholly. For thou canst not be present to me unless thou first be a pilgrim away from the body; since they who are in the flesh are pilgrims away from the kingdom of God. Come hither, he says, come hither. Well did he repeat it, because whether present or absent thou oughtest to be present to and to please the Lord thy God: be present when present, be present when absent, even if thou art still in the body; for all are present to me whose faith is with me. He is present to me who has gone out from the world: he is present to me who thinks of me, who gazes on me, who hopes from me, to whom I am the portion: he is present to me who 1907Dhas been absent from himself: he is with me who is not within himself; since he who is in the flesh is not in the spirit: he is with me who goes forth out of his own self: he is beside me who has been outside himself: he is whole to me who for my sake has lost his own soul; and therefore, Come hither, come hither, bride; thou shalt pass over and pass through from the beginning of faith. She passes over and passes beyond the rest who has come through to Christ: she passes over by the merit of faith and by the brightness of works, she who shines like Sanir and Hermon, that is, the way of the lamp: she passes over when the temptations of the world are conquered and the spiritual 1908Awickednesses overcome, seeking the crown of a lawful contest.

26.

(Vers. 9.) Vulnerasti cor meum, soror mea, sponsa, vulnerasti cor meum in uno oculorum tuorum, et in uno crine colli tui. Oculi enim mentis isti sunt oculi, scilicet interioris hominis, non hi oculi qui ministerio funguntur obtutus. Est enim oculus et mentis et carnis, sed ille caecus est oculus qui non videt quae divina sunt, qui frustra mente carnis inflatus est. Est et alius oculus sensus Christi, quo Ecclesia videt Christum, sicut ipse dicit ad Sponsam: Corde nos cepisti ab uno oculorum tuorum. Merito uno oculo videtur Christus, quia non videtur oculo carnali, aut quia duos oculos habens Ecclesia, moralem et mysticum, fidei oculo plus videt Christum; 1908Bmysticus enim oculus acutior est, moralis dulcior.

26.

(Vers. 9.) Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, bride, thou hast wounded my heart with one of thine eyes, and with one hair of thy neck. For these eyes are the eyes of the mind, namely of the inner man, not these eyes which discharge the service of looking. For there is an eye both of the mind and of the flesh, but that eye is blind which does not see the things that are divine, which is puffed up in vain by the mind of the flesh. There is also another eye, the sense of Christ, by which the Church sees Christ, as he himself says to the Bride: Thou hast taken us in heart by one of thine eyes. [Vulg. Vulnerasti cor meum in uno oculorum tuorum, "thou hast wounded my heart with one of thine eyes"] Rightly is Christ seen with one eye, because he is not seen with the eye of the flesh, or because the Church, having two eyes, the moral and the mystical, sees Christ more with the eye of faith; 1908Bfor the mystical eye is the keener, the moral the sweeter.

27.

Et fortasse isti oculi sunt, quibus Paulus videbat aeterna, ubi coepit temporalia non videre. Denique qui Christum non videbat, priusquam oculos amitteret, vidit 1584 eum posteaquam amisit visum oculorum; vidit enim qui dixit: Quis es, Domine (Act. IX, 5)? Videbat utique Christum, quem etiam Dominum fatebatur. Et infra, Domine, inquit, quid me vis facere? Non ergo videbat eum, cujus exspectabat imperium? Quibus igitur oculis videre Paulus plus coepit, nisi iis quos nobis ipse monstravit, dicens: Orabo spiritu, orabo et mente (I Cor. XIV, 15)? Denique ut scias quia vidit orando: Factum est, inquit, revertente me in Hierusalem, cum orarem in 1908Ctemplo, pavorem habui, vidi illum dicentem mihi: Festina, exi citius de Hierusalem; non enim recipient testimonium tuum de me (Act. XXII, 17 et 18).

27.

And perhaps these are the eyes with which Paul saw eternal things, when he began not to see temporal things. In short, he who did not see Christ before he lost his eyes, saw 1584 him after he had lost the sight of his eyes; for he saw who said: Who art thou, Lord (Act. IX, 5)? Certainly he saw Christ, whom he also confessed as Lord. And below, Lord, he says, what wilt thou have me to do? Did he not then see him whose command he was awaiting? With what eyes therefore did Paul begin to see more, save with those which he himself showed us, saying: I will pray with the spirit, I will pray also with the mind (I Cor. XIV, 15)? In short, that thou mayest know that he saw while praying: It came to pass, he says, when I was returning to Jerusalem, while I was praying in the 1908Ctemple, I had a dread, I saw him saying to me: Make haste, go out more quickly from Jerusalem; for they will not receive thy testimony concerning me (Act. XXII, 17 et 18). [Vulg. fieri me in stupore… exi velociter ex Jerusalem: quoniam non recipient, "that I was in a trance… go out speedily from Jerusalem"]

28.

Isti ergo oculi in verbum Dei deficiunt, et dicunt: Quando consolaberis me? Istis oculis prophetae dicebantur videntes, quia per revelationem ea quae erant abscondita, mente cernebant. Sed quia oculus mentis et oculus carnis unus oculus sunt, et tunc homo consolatione fulcitur, cum caro et mens diversa non cupiunt, sed unum desiderant, et unum requirunt; ideo intenti ad eum qui ait: Ego et pater unum sumus (Joan. X, 30). Isti quoque oculi unum esse se confitentur; quia uno atque eodem desiderio officioque funguntur.

28.

These eyes therefore fail for the word of God, and say: When wilt thou comfort me? With these eyes the prophets were called seers, because through revelation they discerned with the mind the things that were hidden. But because the eye of the mind and the eye of the flesh are one eye, and man is then upheld by consolation when the flesh and the mind do not desire diverse things, but desire one thing and seek one thing; therefore they are intent upon him who says: I and the Father are one (Joan. X, 30). These eyes too confess that they are one; because they discharge one and the same desire and office.

29.

(Vers. 10.) Pulchriora sunt ubera tua vino, 1908Det odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata. Et infra: Odor vestimentorum tuorum sicut Libani. Vide quem nobis tribuas, virgo, processum! Primus enim odor tuus super omnia aromata, quae in Salvatoris missa sunt sepultura, et mortuos corporis motus membrorumque redolet obiisse delicias. Secundus odor tuus, sicut odor Libani, Dominici corporis integritatem, virgineae florem castitatis exhalat. Favum itaque mellis tua opera componant. Odor etiam vestimentorum est odor actuum nostrorum. 1909AAdmove igitur manus naribus tuis, et odorem actuum tuorum indefessa ac pervigili alacritate mentis explora. Mulcebit te odor dexterae tuae, et flagrantiam resurrectionis tua membra redolebunt, myrrham digiti tui sudabunt, hoc est, verae fidei gratiam spiritalia gesta flagrabunt. Capis ergo, virgo, ex interiore tuo corpore voluptatem, et ipsa tibi dulcis, ipsa tibi suavis es, ipsa tibi (quod peccantibus saepe contingit) non incipis displicere.

29.

(Vers. 10.) Thy breasts are more beautiful than wine, 1908Dand the odor of thine ointments above all spices. And below: The odor of thy garments is as of Lebanon. [Vulg. sicut odor thuris, "as the odor of frankincense"] See what advance thou grantest us, virgin! For thy first odor is above all the spices which were sent to the Saviour's burial, and it breathes out that the motions of the body are dead and that the delights of the members have perished. Thy second odor, as the odor of Lebanon, breathes forth the integrity of the Lord's body, the flower of virginal chastity. Let thy works therefore compose a honeycomb of honey. The odor of the garments also is the odor of our acts. 1909ABring thy hands therefore to thy nostrils, and with unwearied and ever-watchful eagerness of mind search out the odor of thine acts. The odor of thy right hand will soothe thee, and thy members will breathe out the fragrance of the resurrection, thy fingers will sweat myrrh, that is, thy spiritual deeds will breathe out the grace of true faith. Thou takest therefore, virgin, delight from thine inner body, and thou art sweet to thyself, thou art pleasant to thyself, and thou dost not begin to be displeasing to thyself (which often happens to those who sin).

30.

(Vers. 11.) Favus distillans, labia tua, sponsa. Favum itaque mellis tua opera componant; digna enim virginitas quae apibus comparetur, sic laboriosa, sic pudica, sic continens: rore pascitur apis, nescit concubitus, mella componit. Ros quoque virgini est sermo divinus; quia sicut ros Dei verba 1909Bdescendunt. Pudor virginis est intemerata natura, partus virginis est fetus labiorum, expers amaritudinis, fertilis suavitatis: in commune labor, communis est fructus. Quam te velim, filia, imitatricem esse hujus apiculae, cujus cibus flos est, ore soboles legitur, ore componitur. Hanc imitare tu, filia; verba tua nullum doli velamen obtendant, nullum habeant fraudis involucrum; ut et puritatem habeant, et plena gravitatis sint. Meritorum quoque tuorum tibi aeterna posteritas tuo ore pariatur, nec soli tibi, sed etiam pluribus congreges quid scis enim quando a te tua anima reposcatur; ne receptacula horreorum frumentis coacervata dimittens, nec vitae tuae usui futura nec meritis, rapiaris eo, quo thesaurum tuum ferre non possis. Dives igitur 1909Cesto, sed pauperi, ut naturae participes tuae, participes sint etiam facultatum.

30.

(Vers. 11.) A dropping honeycomb, thy lips, bride. Let thy works therefore compose a honeycomb of honey; for virginity is worthy to be compared to bees, so laborious, so chaste, so continent: the bee feeds on dew, knows no coupling, composes honey. Dew too, for the virgin, is the divine word; because the words of God come down like dew. 1909BThe virgin's modesty is an unviolated nature, the virgin's offspring is the fruit of the lips, without bitterness, fertile in sweetness: the labor is for the common good, the fruit is common. How I would wish thee, daughter, to be an imitator of this little bee, whose food is the flower, whose offspring is gathered with the mouth, composed with the mouth. Do thou imitate her, daughter; let thy words hold out no veil of guile, let them have no wrapping of fraud; that they may both have purity and be full of gravity. Let an eternal posterity of thy merits also be brought forth for thee by thy mouth, and gather not for thyself alone, but for many also for what dost thou know when thy soul shall be required of thee; lest, leaving behind the receptacles of thy granaries heaped up with grain, which will serve neither the use of thy life nor thy merits, thou be snatched away thither, whither thou canst not carry thy treasure. Be rich therefore, 1909Cbut for the poor man, that they who share thy nature may share thy means also.

31.

Favus distillans labia tua sponsa. Quis sit favus, doce nos, Salomon. Tu enim dixisti: Favus mellis sermones boni (Prov. XVI, 24). Et vere bonus favus, quem manducat Ecclesia, multorum prophetarum velut apum spirituali ubertate congesta mella redolentem. Hoc est mel de quo ait: Manducavi panem meum cum melle meo (Cant. V, 1). Stillant mel labia praedicantis, quando collisa duris casibus vel ruinis, lapsae animae membra refoventur. Favum distillant labia tua, sponsa, mel et lac sub lingua tua; os enim justi distillat sapientiam: de ore justi suavitatis et misericordiae mella procedunt: in ore justi nullus dolus, nulla fallacia, nulla est amaritudo 1909Dpeccati. Audit Ecclesia verba justi, audit plebs Dei verba sapientis, delectatur alloquii suavitate, moralis disputationis jucunditate mulcetur, dicens: Quam dulcia faucibus meis verba tua, super mel et favum ori meo (Psalm. CXVIII, 103)! Quia mel apum ad horam delectat, sed cito sapor ejus 1585 evanescit, et plerumque viscera vitiosa laeduntur: verborum autem moralium etsi pungunt mella, non laedunt.

31.

A dropping honeycomb, thy lips, bride. What the honeycomb is, teach us, Solomon. For thou hast said: Good words are a honeycomb of honey (Prov. XVI, 24). [Vulg. Favus mellis composita verba, "well-ordered words are a honeycomb of honey"] And truly a good honeycomb, which the Church eats, redolent of honey gathered by the spiritual richness of many prophets as of bees. This is the honey of which he says: I have eaten my bread with my honey (Cant. V, 1). [Vulg. comedi favum cum melle meo, "I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey"] The lips of the preacher drop honey, when the limbs of a fallen soul, bruised by hard mischances or downfalls, are revived. Thy lips drop a honeycomb, bride, honey and milk are under thy tongue; [Vulg. Favus distillans labia tua, "thy lips are a dropping honeycomb"] for the mouth of the just distils wisdom: from the mouth of the just proceed the honeys of sweetness and of mercy: in the mouth of the just there is no guile, no deceit, no bitterness 1909Dof sin. The Church hears the words of the just, the people of God hears the words of the wise man, is delighted by the sweetness of the address, is soothed by the pleasantness of moral discourse, saying: How sweet are thy words to my throat, above honey and the honeycomb to my mouth (Psalm. CXVIII, 103)! [Vulg. Quam dulcia faucibus meis eloquia tua, super mel ori meo, "how sweet are thy words to my throat, above honey to my mouth" — without et favum] For the honey of bees delights for an hour, but its savor quickly 1585 vanishes, and for the most part corrupt bowels are hurt: but the honeys of moral words, though they sting, do not hurt.

32.

Cognosce tamen cui illa credantur; scriptum est enim: In aurem insipientis nihil dicas; ne quando 1910Airrideat prudentes sermones tuos (Prov. XXIII, 9). Evomet enim et rejiciet sermonem tuum stultus, qui ejus sentire non potest suavitatem. Quomodo possunt verba Dei dulcia esse in faucibus tuis, in quibus est amaritudo nequitiae? Quomodo mel et lac sub lingua tua, cum dolum lingua tua componat; ut aliud pectore concipias, aliud forensi sermone praetendas, ut decipias incautum? Cum tibi Petrus apostolus dicat, ut deponas omnem malitiam, et fucum simulationis abjicias, dicens: Sicut dudum geniti infantes, rationabiles et sine dolo, lac concupiscite (I Pet. II, 2). Ostendens enim nobis quid sit mel et lac habere sub lingua, docet ut non maledicere maledicentibus, sed benedictionem referre noverimus: nesciamus odisse nisi viam iniquitatis, ut facere quod exsecramur, reluctanti 1910Bcaveamus affectu.

32.

Know however to whom those things are entrusted; for it is written: Say nothing into the ear of a fool; lest at any time 1910Ahe mock thy prudent words (Prov. XXIII, 9). [Vulg. In auribus insipientium ne loquaris, quia despicient doctrinam eloquii tui, "speak not in the ears of fools, for they will despise the instruction of thy speech"] For the fool will vomit out and cast back thy word, who cannot perceive its sweetness. How can the words of God be sweet in thy throat, in which is the bitterness of wickedness? How is there honey and milk under thy tongue, when thy tongue frames guile; so that thou conceivest one thing in thy breast, and puttest forward another in public speech, that thou mayest deceive the unwary? Since Peter the apostle says to thee that thou shouldest lay aside all malice, and cast off the paint of dissimulation, saying: As infants born a while ago, reasonable and without guile, desire ye milk (I Pet. II, 2). [Vulg. sicut modo geniti infantes, rationabile, sine dolo lac concupiscite, "as newborn infants desire the reasonable milk without guile"] For showing us what it is to have honey and milk under the tongue, he teaches that we should know not to curse those who curse, but to render blessing: let us not know how to hate save the way of iniquity, that with a resisting 1910Baffection we may beware of doing what we execrate.

33.

(Vers. 12.) Hortus conclusus soror mea sponsa, hortus conclusus, fons signatus. Emissiones tuae, etc. Eo quod in hortis ejusmodi impressam signaculis imaginem Dei, sinceri fontis unda resplendeat, nec volutabris spiritualium bestiarum sparsa coeno fluenta turbentur. Hinc ille murali septus spiritu pudor clauditur, ne pateat ad rapinam. Itaque sicut hortus furibus inaccessus, vitem redolet, flagrat oleam, rosam renidet; ut in vite religio, in olea pax, in rosa pudor sacratae virginitatis inolescat. Hic est odor quem Jacob patriarcha flagravit, quando meruit audire: Ecce odor filii mei, sicut odor agri pleni (Genes. XXVII, 27). Nam licet plenus omnibus fere fructibus patriarchae sancti fuerit ager, ille tamen fruges majori 1910Cvirtutis labore generavit, hic flores. Accingere itaque, virgo, et si vis hujusmodi tibi ut hortus aspiret, propheticis eum claude praeceptis: Pone custodiam ori tuo, et ostium circumstantiae labiis tuis (Psal. CXL, 3).

33.

(Vers. 12.) A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed. Thy shoots, etc. Because in gardens of this kind the image of God, impressed by the seals, shines back in the wave of the unmixed fountain, nor are the streams, bespattered with mire, disturbed by the wallowing-places of spiritual beasts. Hence that modesty, hedged about by a wall-like spirit, is shut in, lest it lie open to plunder. And so, like a garden inaccessible to thieves, it is redolent of the vine, it breathes the olive, it shines with the rose; that in the vine religion, in the olive peace, in the rose the modesty of consecrated virginity may grow. This is the odor which Jacob the patriarch breathed, when he deserved to hear: Behold the odor of my son, as the odor of a full field (Genes. XXVII, 27). For although the field of the holy patriarch was full of almost all fruits, yet he brought forth crops by a greater 1910Clabor of virtue, this one flowers. Gird thyself therefore, virgin, and if thou wishest such a garden to breathe upon thee, shut it with the prophetic precepts: Set a guard to thy mouth, and a door of enclosure to thy lips (Psal. CXL, 3). [Vulg. Pone, Domine, custodiam ori meo, et ostium circumstantiae labiis meis, "set, O Lord, a guard to my mouth"]

34.

Hortus conclusus soror mea sponsa, hortus conclusus, fons signatus; emissiones tuae paradisus malorum punicorum, cum fructu pomorum Cypri. Laudatur Sponsa eo quod hortus sit, habens in se odorem agri illius pleni, de quo dicit Isaac: Odor filii mei, tamquam odor agri pleni (Genes. XXVII, 27). Bona ergo anima flagrat odores justitiae. Et fortasse ager est patriarcha, hortus est anima inferioris alicujus, quasi agri portio: et Hortus conclusus, ne incursetur a bestiis: et Fons signatus, quae integritate signaculi, 1910Dperseverantiaque fidei propria peccata diluerit. Nam qui de Ecclesia acceperit; habet quod ad virginitatis gratiam referat; eo quod in paradiso delectationis posita sine labore fructus capiat spiritales, ut ei patriarcharum animae rurali quodam animae suae labore fructus suos conferant, quo ista capere possit perpetuam suavitatem: quae merito fons signatus dicitur, eo quod imago in illa Dei invisibilis exprimatur.

34.

A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed; thy shoots a paradise of pomegranates, with the fruit of the apples of Cyprus. [Vulg. cum pomorum fructibus, cypri cum nardo, "with the fruits of apples, cypress with spikenard"] The Bride is praised in that she is a garden, having in herself the odor of that full field, of which Isaac says: The odor of my son, as the odor of a full field (Genes. XXVII, 27). A good soul therefore breathes out the odors of justice. And perhaps the field is the patriarch, the garden is the soul of some lesser one, as it were a portion of the field: and a garden enclosed, lest it be assailed by beasts: and a fountain sealed, she who by the integrity of the seal 1910Dand by the perseverance of faith has washed away her own sins. For he who has received from the Church; has that which he may refer to the grace of virginity; in that, placed in the paradise of delight, she takes spiritual fruits without labor, so that the souls of the patriarchs confer their own fruits upon her by a certain rustic labor of their own soul, whereby she may be able to take perpetual sweetness: who is rightly called a fountain sealed, in that the image of the invisible God is expressed in her.

35.

Hortus conclusus soror mea sponsa, hortus conclusus, fons signatus. Omnes doctores congruunt 1911Ain paradiso, et lignum vitae radicatum et lignum scientiae, quo discernat bonum et malum: caeteraque ligna plena vigoris, plena vivificationis, spirantia et rationabilia. Ex quibus colligitur paradisum ipsum terrenum videri posse, non in solo aliquo, sed in nostro principali, quo animatur et vivificatur anima virtutibus et infusione Spiritus sancti. Denique Salomon in spiritu paradisum in homine esse evidenter declaravit. Et quia mysteria exprimit vel animae et Verbi, vel Christi et Ecclesiae; ideo ait de virgine anima vel Ecclesia, quam volebat virginem castam assignare Christo: Paradisus clausus, soror mea sponsa, paradisus clausus, fons signatus. Paradisus Graece, Latine hortus dicitur. Denique Susanna in paradiso erat, et sic legitur latine: et Adam in 1911Bparadiso erat, et sic legimus. Ergo non te moveat quod alii codices latini hortum habent, alii paradisum. Ibi ergo est casta uxor, ubi et virgo. Licet virgo excepta habeat claustra sua et signacula, sed utraque in paradiso; ut adversum aestus corporis et carnis flagrantiam refrigeretur virtutum umbraculis.

35.

A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed. All the teachers agree 1911Aupon paradise, and upon the tree of life rooted there and the tree of knowledge, by which one may discern good and evil: and the other trees full of vigor, full of quickening, breathing and rational. From which it is gathered that paradise itself can be seen as earthly, not in any soil, but in our ruling part, by which the soul is animated and quickened with virtues and with the infusion of the Holy Spirit. In short Solomon in the spirit plainly declared that paradise is in man. And because he expresses the mysteries either of the soul and the Word, or of Christ and the Church; therefore he says of the virgin soul or the Church, whom he wished to assign as a chaste virgin to Christ: A paradise enclosed, my sister, bride, a paradise enclosed, a fountain sealed. Paradise in Greek is in Latin called a garden. In short Susanna was in a paradise, and so it is read in Latin: and Adam was in 1911Bparadise, and so we read. Therefore let it not disturb thee that some Latin codices have hortum, others paradisum. There then is the chaste wife, where the virgin also is. Although the virgin, set apart, has her own enclosures and seals, yet both are in paradise; that against the heats of the body and the burning of the flesh she may be cooled by the shades of the virtues.

36.

Ergo paradisus in principali nostro est, silvescens plurimarum opinionum plantariis, in quo principaliter lignum vitae constituit Deus, id est, pietatis radicem. Ea est enim vitae nostrae substantia, si Domino et Deo nostro 1586 debitos cultus deferamus. Constituit etiam scientiae boni et mali seminarium; homo enim solus in caeteris animantibus terrenis, habet scientiam boni et mali. Alia quoque illic 1911Cplantaria diversa, quorum fructus virtutes sunt. Sed quia scientiae capax hominis affectus cognitus est a Deo, quod citius ad astutiam inclinaretur, quam ad summum prudentiae; neque eum judicem qui fines certos in anima nostra constituit, operis sui qualitas latere poterat: eliminare voluit astutiam de paradiso, quasi providus auctor nostrae salutis, et vitae studium et disciplinam pietatis infundere. Ideoque praecepit homini, ab omni ligno quod est in paradiso gustandum, de ligno autem scientiae boni et mali non esse gustandum. Sed quia creatura omnis passioni obnoxia est, ideo delectatio sicut serpentino illapsu humanis irrepsit affectibus.

36.

Therefore paradise is in our ruling part, growing wild with the nurseries of very many opinions, in which God chiefly set the tree of life, that is, the root of piety. For this is the substance of our life, if we render to the Lord our God 1586 the worship due to him. He set also a seed-plot of the knowledge of good and evil; for man alone among the other earthly living creatures has the knowledge of good and evil. There too are other 1911Cdivers nurseries, whose fruits are the virtues. But because the affection of man, capable of knowledge, was known to God, that it would sooner incline to cunning than to the height of prudence; nor could the quality of his own work lie hidden from him, the judge who set fixed bounds in our soul: he willed to cast out cunning from paradise, as the provident author of our salvation, and to pour in the pursuit of life and the discipline of piety. And therefore he commanded man that of every tree which is in paradise he should taste, but that of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil he should not taste. But because every creature is liable to passion, therefore delight crept into human affections as by a serpent's gliding.

37.

Itaque non immerito sanctus Moyses delectationem serpentis figuravit similitudine; prona est 1911Denim in ventrem sicut serpens, non pedibus incedens, aut ullis elevata cruribus: sed sinuoso flexu totius velut corporis sui lubrica. Ei terra cibus est sicut serpenti, quoniam escam nescit coelestem; corporalibus enim pascitur, atque in varias mutatur species cupiditatum, et tortuosis angulatur anfractibus. Venenum in dentibus habet, quibus unusquisque se eviscerat luxuriosus, helluo internecat, abliguritor perimit. Quantos ruperunt vina, dissolvit ebrietas, distendit cruditas! Nunc intelligo qua causa Dominus Deus insufflavit in faciem hominis; ibi enim sensus omnes, ibi sedes atque illecebra delectationis, in oculis, auribus, naribus, atque in ore; ut sensus nostros adversus delectationem fortiores 1912Afaceret. Haec ergo nobis sicut serpens astutiam infudit; non enim delectatio, sed labor et diuturna meditatio cum Dei gratia dat perfectam prudentiam. Tamen quia serpentis fraudibus involuta est humani generis haereditas, in eo astutiam serpentis sequamur, ut caput nostrum non projiciamus in pericula, sed prae caeteris integrum custodiamus; caput autem nostrum Christus est. Hoc maneat incolume, ut serpentis venena nobis non possint nocere: bona enim est sapientia cum haereditate, id est, cum fide; quoniam est haereditas credentium in Domino.

37.

And so not without reason did holy Moses figure delight under the likeness of a serpent; for it is prone 1911Dupon its belly like a serpent, not walking upon feet, nor raised upon any legs: but slippery, as it were, with the winding bend of its whole body. Earth is its food, as it is the serpent's, since it knows no heavenly meat; for it feeds upon bodily things, and is changed into various kinds of desires, and is bent into tortuous windings. It has venom in its teeth, with which every luxurious man disembowels himself, the glutton slays himself, the squanderer destroys himself. How many have wines burst, drunkenness undone, indigestion distended! Now I understand for what cause the Lord God breathed into the face of man; for there are all the senses, there the seat and the enticement of delight, in the eyes, the ears, the nostrils, and in the mouth; that he might make our senses stronger 1912Aagainst delight. This then poured cunning into us as the serpent did; for it is not delight, but labor and long meditation with the grace of God, that gives perfect prudence. Yet because the inheritance of the human race is wrapped in the deceits of the serpent, let us in this follow the serpent's cunning, that we cast not our head into dangers, but keep it whole before all else; and our head is Christ. Let this remain unharmed, that the serpent's venoms may not be able to hurt us: for wisdom is good with an inheritance, that is, with faith; since there is an inheritance of those who believe in the Lord.

38.

Quod si primus homo, qui cum Deo loquebatur in paradiso positus, labi tam facile potuit, ex terra creatus virgine, quae ad verbum Dei formata et creata recenti fuerat exortu, nondum ea parricidali 1912Bcaeterorumque concreta sanguine, flagitiis et dedecore polluta, nondum carne nostra damnata maledicto obnoxiae haereditatis: quanto facilius postea, lubrica ad peccandum via, majus advexit humano generi praecipitium, cum deterior tolerabiliori per vices generationis successerit! Etenim si magnetes lapis tantam naturae vim habet, ut ferrum ad se trahat, et transfundat se in illius speciem; ita ut plerisque experiri volentibus, cum plures annulos ferreos ad eum lapidem admoverint, omnes pari teneat modo: deinde si ei annulo cui adhaeserit lapis, alium admoveas annulum, et rursum per ordinem singulis substituas; licet in omnes penetret per ordinem ex illo lapide vis naturae, tamen priores nexu vehementiore constringit, posteriores remissiore: 1912Cquanto magis humani generis conditio atque natura, ex puriore statu in deteriorem lapsa est, quanto nequiorem attigit! Nam si per eas species imminuitur natura, quae non sunt culpae capaces: quanto magis per animos atque artus labe pollutos scelerum, virtus ejus hebetatur! Unde quia vincente malitia, abolita innocentia fuerat, non erat qui faceret bonitatem, non erat usque ad unum. Venit ergo Dominus, qui reformaret naturae gratiam, immo augeret; ut ubi superabundavit peccatum, superabundaret gratia.

38.

But if the first man, who spoke with God, set in paradise, could fall so easily, created from virgin earth, which had been formed and created at the word of God in a fresh springing forth, that earth not yet clotted with parricidal 1912Bblood and the blood of the rest, not yet polluted with crimes and disgrace, our flesh not yet condemned by the curse of a liable inheritance: how much more easily afterwards, the road to sinning being slippery, did it bring a greater downfall upon the human race, when the worse succeeded the more tolerable through the turns of generation! For if the magnet stone has such force of nature that it draws iron to itself, and pours itself over into its likeness; so that, when many who wished to make trial have brought several iron rings to that stone, it holds them all in like manner: then if to that ring to which the stone has clung thou bring another ring, and again set one under each in order; although the force of nature from that stone penetrates them all in order, yet it binds the earlier ones with a more vehement bond, the later with a slacker: 1912Chow much more has the condition and nature of the human race fallen from a purer state into a worse, the more wicked the state it has reached! For if nature is diminished through those kinds of things which are not capable of guilt: how much more is its strength blunted through minds and limbs polluted with the stain of crimes! Whence, because when malice prevailed innocence had been abolished, there was none that did goodness, there was not so much as one. The Lord therefore came, to reform the grace of nature, nay rather to increase it; that where sin had superabounded, grace might superabound.

39.

Ergo liquet quia auctor hominis Deus, et quod unus Deus, non multi dii, sed unus qui mundum operatus est, et unum, non multos mundos, ut dicunt philosophi. Primum ergo mundum creavit, 1912Ddeinde habitatorem mundi, cui totus mundus patria foret: nam si hodie quocumque accesserit sapiens, ubique civis est, ubique sua intelligit, nusquam se peregrinum, nusquam hospitem judicat: quanto magis ille primus homo, totius mundi erat incola, et ut Graece dicitur, cosmopolites, Dei recens 1587 confabulator, assiduus civis sanctorum! complantatus virtutibus, praepositus omnibus terrenis animantibus, marinis, volatilibus, totum mundum suam possessionem putabat, quem Dominus tuebatur ut opus suum, neque ut bonus parens atque auctor deserebat. Denique eousque creatum fovit, ut abdicatum redimeret, eliminatum reciperet, mortuum passione filii sui unigeniti resuscitaret. Est ergo hominis auctor 1913ADeus, et diligit opus suum operator bonus, nec derelinquit bonus pater, quem etiam sicut dives paterfamilias censu propriae haereditatis redemit.

39.

It is clear therefore that God is the author of man, and that there is one God, not many gods, but one who wrought the world, and one world, not many worlds, as the philosophers say. First then he created the world, 1912Dthen the inhabitant of the world, to whom the whole world should be a fatherland: for if today the wise man goes anywhere, everywhere he is a citizen, everywhere he understands what is his own, nowhere does he judge himself a stranger, nowhere a guest: how much more was that first man an inhabitant of the whole world, and, as it is said in Greek, cosmopolites, a fresh 1587 converser with God, an assiduous citizen among the saints! planted about with virtues, set over all living creatures of the land, of the sea, of the air, he counted the whole world his own possession, which the Lord watched over as his own work, nor did he, as a good parent and author, desert it. In short he so cherished the creature he had made, that he redeemed him when disowned, received him back when cast out, raised him up when dead by the passion of his only-begotten Son. God therefore is the author of man, 1913Aand as a good workman he loves his own work, nor does the good father forsake him whom, like a rich householder, he has redeemed at the cost of his own inheritance.

40.

Caveamus ergo ne hominem istum, id est νοῦν, mulier illa, id est, passio sensuum nostrorum delectatione et ipsa decepta atque illusa effeminet, et circumscriptum in leges suas et sententiam trahat. Fugiamus delectationem sicut serpentem, multas artes habet et maxime in homine. Nam alia animantia cibi aviditate capiuntur; homo quando plures sensus oculorum auriumque habet, tanto majora pericula. Itaque cave ne vigor mentis tuae, coitu quodam corporeae voluptatis inflexus emolliatur, atque in ejus omnes amplexus resolvatur, et fontem ejus aperiat, qui debet esse clausus et septus intentionis 1913Bstudio et consideratione rationis: Hortus enim clausus, fons signatus. Namque resoluto mentis sensu, se corporales delectationes effundunt perniciosae nimis, et in appetentiam plenam gravis periculi profluentes; quas si mentis vividae considerata mansisset custodia, refrenasset.

40.

Let us beware therefore lest that woman, that is, the passion of our senses, herself deceived and mocked by delight, make effeminate that man, that is, the νοῦν, and draw him, circumscribed, into her own laws and judgment. Let us flee delight as a serpent, it has many arts and chiefly in man. For other living creatures are caught by greediness for food; man, since he has more senses of the eyes and of the ears, has by so much the greater dangers. And so beware lest the vigor of thy mind, bent by a certain coupling with bodily pleasure, be softened, and be dissolved into all its embraces, and open its fountain, which ought to be closed and hedged about by earnestness of purpose 1913Band by the consideration of reason: For a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed. For when the sense of the mind is unstrung, bodily delights pour themselves forth, exceedingly destructive, and flowing on into an appetite full of grave danger; and if the considered guard of a living mind had remained, it would have reined them in.

41.

(Vers. 3, 4.) Emissiones, inquit, tuae paradisus malorum punicorum cum pomorum fructu, Cypri cum nardo, nardus et crocus, fistula et cinnamomum cum universis lignis Libani, mirrha et aloe cum omnibus primis unguentis. Laudantur munera animae quae missa sunt a Sponso, quibus dotata veniebat (Deo autem piae animae boni odores sunt), myrrha, aloe, crocus et alia, quibus spirat hortorum gratia, et peccatorum fetor aboletur. Itaque tanto secura praeconio, 1913Cconquiescere aquilonem gravem ventum petit, ne flores discutiat: spirare austrum, hoc est, velut hyemem praeterire, et mollioris flatus vernare temperiem. Invitat Sponsum in hortum suum, descendit sponsus, et fructuum ejus diversitate delectatus, laetatur quod invenerit fortiorem cibum, invenerit etiam dulciorem. Est enim velut panis quidam Verbi et mel, alius vehementior, alius suasorius sermo. Est et fides alia ferventior, ut vinum; alia lucidior, ut succus est lactis. Hunc cibum Christus epulatur in nobis, hoc poculum bibit, et ejus potus ebrietate nos provocat, ut ad meliora et optima ab inferioribus faciamus excessum.

41.

(Vers. 3, 4.) Thy shoots, he says, are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruit of apples, cypress with spikenard, spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Lebanon, myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments. The gifts of the soul are praised which were sent by the Bridegroom, with which she came dowered (and to God the good odors are those of a devout soul), myrrh, aloes, saffron and the rest, with which the grace of the gardens breathes, and the stench of sins is done away. And so, made secure by so great a proclamation, 1913Cshe asks that the north wind, that heavy wind, be still, lest it shake off the flowers: that the south wind blow, that is, that the winter as it were pass by, and that the mildness of a softer breath come as spring. She invites the Bridegroom into her garden, the bridegroom comes down, and delighted by the diversity of its fruits, he rejoices that he has found a stronger food, and has found a sweeter also. For there is as it were a certain bread of the Word and a honey, one discourse more vehement, another persuasive. There is also one faith more fervent, like wine; another clearer, as is the juice of milk. This food Christ feasts upon in us, this cup he drinks, and by the drunkenness of his draught he provokes us to make a passing over from lower things to better and best.

42.

Audiens haec anima hausit mysteriorum ebrietatem coelestium, et velut soporata a vino, et quasi in 1913Dexcessu vel stupore posita, dicit: Ego dormio, et cor meum vigilat (Cant. V, 2). Tum Verbi praesentis lumine percussa, excitatur a Verbo. Quartus autem hic processus est animae. Primum etenim charitatis impatiens, et Verbi moras non ferens, rogabat ut oscula mereretur, et meruit desideratum videre. Secundo introducta in cubiculum regis, cum mutua misceret alloquia, in umbra ejus requievit, et subito ei Verbum medio sermone discessit; quaerenti tamen non diu abfuit, sed saliens supra montes et transiliens super colles advenit. Nec multo post quasi 1914Acapreolus aut cervorum hinniculus dum affatur dilectam, exsilivit et reliquit. Tertio cum in cubili et noctibus, in civitate et foro, in plateis quaesitum non reperit, et aliquando orationibus suis gratia quaerentem revocavit, adeo etiam ut propius vocaretur a sponso. Quarto ipsa jam dormiens ab ipso excitatur, quamvis corde vigilaret; ut continuo vocem pulsantis audiret: sed moram passa, dum surgit (quia velocitatem Verbi non potuit comprehendere), dum aperit ostium, transivit Verbum, et ipsa exivit in Verbo ejus: et per vulnera requisitum, sed vulnera charitatis, vix tandem invenit, et tenuit, ut postea non amitteret. Ad summam haec compendioso sermone perstrinxi, nunc discutiamus singula.

42.

The soul hearing these things drank in the drunkenness of the heavenly mysteries, and as though lulled to sleep by wine, and set as it were in 1913Decstasy or stupor, says: I sleep, and my heart waketh (Cant. V, 2). Then, struck by the light of the Word present, she is roused by the Word. And this is the fourth advance of the soul. For first, impatient of love, and not bearing the Word's delays, she asked that she might merit kisses, and merited to see him whom she desired. Secondly, brought into the king's chamber, while she exchanged mutual speech, she rested in his shadow, and suddenly the Word departed from her in mid-discourse; yet he was not long absent from her as she sought him, but came leaping upon the mountains and skipping over the hills. And not long after, like 1914Aa roe or a young hart of the stags, while he speaks to his beloved, he leapt away and left her. Thirdly, when in her bed and by night, in the city and the market place, in the streets, she did not find him whom she sought, and at length grace by her prayers called back the one who was seeking, so far even that she was called nearer by the bridegroom. Fourthly, she herself, now sleeping, is roused by him, although she was waking in heart; so that at once she heard the voice of him that knocked: but having suffered delay, while she rises (because she could not grasp the swiftness of the Word), while she opens the door, the Word passed by, and she herself went out at his word: and having sought him through wounds, but the wounds of love, at last she scarcely found him, and held him, that she might not afterwards lose him. In sum I have touched upon these things in a compendious discourse, now let us examine them one by one.

43.

(Vers. 15.) Fons hortorum, puteus aquarum 1914Bviventium, quae fluunt impetu de Libano. Habes haec in Salomone, quia Proverbia ejus moralia, Ecclesiastes naturalis, in quo quasi vanitates istius despicit mundi: mystica sunt ejus Cantica 1588 Canticorum. Habes et in propheta: Seminate vobis ad justitiam, vindemiate ad fructum vitae, illuminate vobis lumen cognitionis (Ose. X, 12); hoc est enim lumen cognitionis, habere charitatis perfectionem. Ideoque dictum est: Ne timeas (Joan. XII, 15); charitas enim timorem excludit foras.

43.

(Vers. 15.) A fountain of gardens, a well of living 1914Bwaters, which flow with force from Lebanon. Thou hast these things in Solomon, since his Proverbs are moral, Ecclesiastes natural, in which he as it were despises the vanities of this world: mystical is his Song 1588 of Songs. Thou hast it also in the prophet: Sow for yourselves unto justice, gather the vintage unto the fruit of life, light for yourselves the light of knowledge (Ose. X, 12); [Vulg.
