Seminate vobis in justitia, et metite in ore misericordiae; innovate vobis novale, "sow for yourselves in justice, and reap in the mouth of mercy; break up your fallow ground"] for this is the light of knowledge, to have the perfection of charity. And therefore it is said: Fear not (Joan. XII, 15); [Vulg. Noli timere, filia Sion, "fear not, daughter of Sion"] for charity casteth fear out of doors.

44.

Ut cognoscamus autem quia Salomon ita hos interpretatus est tres puteos, quos fodit Jacob; ut ad moralem doctrinam et naturalem referret et mysticam, in singulis libris suis, quos de moralibus et naturalibus vel mysticis scripsit, posuit hos puteos. 1914CNam et in Proverbiis, cum declinandam speciem diceret saecularis illecebrae, ait: Bibe aquam de tuis vasis, et de puteorum tuorum fontibus, et superfluant tibi aquae de tuo fonte (Prov. V, 15). Et infra: Fons aquae tuae sit tibi proprius, et jucundare cum uxore quae ab adolescentia tua (Ibid., 18); eo quod adversus tentamenta mundi remedium nobis vera sapientia sit. Moralis quoque doctrina quae imaginem mundanae voluptatis meretriciis quibusdam illitam fucis irriguo suo diluat, et fluento sui fontis abstergat.

44.

But that we may know that Solomon so interpreted these three wells, which Jacob dug, as to refer them to moral and natural and mystical teaching, in each of his books, which he wrote concerning moral and natural or mystical things, he set these wells. 1914CFor in the Proverbs too, when he said that the appearance of worldly enticement must be turned aside, he says: Drink water out of thine own vessels, and out of the fountains of thine own wells, and let the waters overflow for thee out of thine own fountain (Prov. V, 15). [Vulg. Bibe aquam de cisterna tua, et fluenta putei tui, "drink water out of thy own cistern, and the streams of thy own well"] And below: Let the fountain of thy water be thine own, and rejoice with the wife who is from thy youth (Ibid., 18); [Vulg. Sit vena tua benedicta, et laetare cum muliere adolescentiae tuae, "let thy vein be blessed, and rejoice with the wife of thy youth"] in that true wisdom is a remedy for us against the temptations of the world. Moral teaching also, which may wash away with its own watering the image of worldly pleasure smeared with certain harlot's paints, and wipe it off with the stream of its own fountain.

45.

De naturalibus quoque habes in Ecclesiaste ipsum: Feci mihi piscinas aquarum ad irrigandum ex his saltum germinantem (Eccles. II, 6). Neque te moveat quod pro puteo piscinas posuit, quia et Moyses puteum latitudinis dixit; eo quod a sollicitudine omni 1914Det angustiis solvitur, qui mundum hunc pia mente transcenderit. Non immerito ergo piscinas habet Ecclesiastes, qui vidit nullam abundantiam esse sub sole: sed si quis abundare velit, in Christo abundet.

45.

Of natural things too thou hast the same in Ecclesiastes: I made myself pools of waters, to water from them a sprouting wood (Eccles. II, 6). [Vulg. Exstruxi mihi piscinas aquarum, ut irrigarem silvam lignorum germinantium, "I made myself pools of water, to water the wood of sprouting trees"] And let it not move thee that he put pools for a well, since Moses too spoke of a well of breadth; because he is loosed from all anxiety 1914Dand from straits who has passed beyond this world with a devout mind. Not without reason therefore has Ecclesiastes pools, who saw that there is no abundance under the sun: but if any wish to abound, let him abound in Christ.

46.

De mysticis et puteus superest nobis, quem etiam ipsum reperimus in Canticis Canticorum, dicente Scriptura: Fons hortorum, puteus aquae vivae, et impetu descendens a Libano. Etenim si mysteriorum altitudinem persequaris, puteus tibi videtur tamquam in profundo sita mystica esse sapientia: si vero haurire velis affluentiam charitatis, quae major et uberior 1915Aest, quam fides et spes, tunc tibi fons est. Exuberat enim charitas, ut et haurire eam cominus, et rigare ejus affluentia hortum tuum possis, spiritalibus fructibus redundantem. Et quia ipse puteus latitudinis est, qui charitatem habet; ideo dixit, quia ubi charitas est, ibi impetus magnus descendit a Libano.

46.

Of mystical things there remains for us also the well, which we find in the Song of Songs itself, Scripture saying: A fountain of gardens, a well of living water, and coming down with force from Lebanon. For if thou pursue the depth of the mysteries, it seems to thee a well, as though mystical wisdom were set in the deep: but if thou wouldst draw the affluence of charity, which is greater and richer 1915Athan faith and hope, then it is a fountain for thee. For charity overflows, so that thou canst both draw it close at hand, and water with its affluence thy garden, abounding in spiritual fruits. And because he himself is a well of breadth who has charity; therefore he said that where charity is, there a great force comes down from Lebanon.

47.

Ne quid autem moveat, quod et puteum, et fontem eumdem dixit; etiam Evangelium te instruat, in quo scriptum est: Quia venit Jesus in civitatem Samariae, quae dicitur Sichar, juxta praedium quod dedit Jacob Joseph filio suo (Joan. IV, 5 et 6). Erat autem ibi fons Jacob: Et fatigatus, inquit, sedebat sic super puteum. Unde ad mysticam doctrinam hunc referri puteum etiam ibi cognoscimus, quia ibi Samaritana illa, id est, custos (custos autem coelestium 1915Bpraeceptorum) hausit de puteo illo divina mysteria, cognoscens quia Deus spiritus est, et non in loco adoratur, sed in spiritu: et quia Messias venit, qui Christus est. Quibus auditis mulier illa quae speciem Ecclesiae gerit, Legis sacramenta cognovit et credidit.

47.

But lest anything move thee, that he called the same thing both a well and a fountain; let the Gospel also instruct thee, in which it is written: That Jesus came into a city of Samaria, which is called Sichar, near the estate which Jacob gave to Joseph his son (Joan. IV, 5 et 6). And the fountain of Jacob was there: And being wearied, he says, he sat thus upon the well. Whence we recognize there also that this well is referred to mystical teaching, because there that Samaritan woman, that is, a keeper (and a keeper of the heavenly 1915Bprecepts) drew from that well the divine mysteries, knowing that God is a spirit, and is not adored in a place, but in spirit: and that the Messias comes, who is Christ. Having heard these things, that woman, who bears the figure of the Church, knew the sacraments of the Law and believed.

48.

Fons hortorum, puteus aquarum viventium, et impetus descendens a Libano. Hos impetus Ecclesia deduxit a Libano, hoc impetu diluuntur peccata, hoc impetu puri fontis Spiritus sancti adfuit a Libano Sponsa, et a principio fidei transivit saeculum, et pertransivit ad regnum. Aliis fons est, aliis puteus pro captu nostro gratia spiritalis: aliis hortus clausus, fons signatus, aliis hortorum fons, qui in Ecclesiae dote numeratur: aliis impetus descendens a Libano, 1915Cet magnus impetus qui numquam deficit. Non enim deficiunt de petra ubera, neque nix a Libano, neque aqua quae fertur valido vento virgini Hierusalem.

48.

A fountain of gardens, a well of living waters, and a force coming down from Lebanon. These forces the Church has drawn down from Lebanon, by this force sins are washed away, by this force of the pure fountain of the Holy Spirit the Bride came from Lebanon, and from the beginning of faith she passed over the world, and passed through to the kingdom. To some spiritual grace is a fountain, to others a well, according to our capacity: to some a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed, to others a fountain of gardens, which is numbered in the Church's dowry: to others a force coming down from Lebanon, 1915Cand a great force which never fails. For the breasts fail not from the rock, nor the snow from Lebanon, nor the water which is borne by a strong wind to the virgin Jerusalem.

49.

Descendit impetus a Libano, quando collectis in unum apostolis et pluribus credentibus, factus est subito de coelo sonus, tamquam vi magna spiritus ferretur, et repleti sunt omnes Spiritu sancto diversitates donante linguarum (Act. II, 1, 3). Bonus impetus qui laedere nesciat, norit implere. Si quis igitur hunc impetum supervenientis e coelo gratiae vult mereri, descendat etiam ipse oculis in decursus aquarum. Qui hunc primum impetum fuderit, illum merebitur. Descendit oculis suis in hos ductus aquarum, quae lacrymis in Evangelio rigavit Domini pedes 1915D(Luc. VII, 38), et ideo fidei suae pretio emit animae suae et corpori sanitatem, jam non sanguinis proflua, sed gratiae spiritalis.

1589. 50. Descendit ergo David propheta, ideo de peccato gratiam retulit. Descendit in aquarum ductus, hoc est, replevit eos, et decurrentium aquarum lacrymis suis fluenta cumulavit, aut inanes et vacuos ductus solis replevit fletibus. Aut quod elocutionis moralis quosdam sensus ostendit, descendit in aquarum ductus, transivit eos. Et possemus dicere, transcendit 1916Aeos, et supergressus est: sed minuitur vis eloquii, quo vis major affluentiae descendentis, quam ascendentis exprimitur. Vide, rogo, quid de usu verba habeant, impetum suum sermo propheticus non amittat, licet usus ipse scriptorum sensui servire, majore decore consueverit.

49.

The force came down from Lebanon, when, the apostles and many believers being gathered into one, there was suddenly a sound from heaven, as though a spirit were borne with great violence, and they were all filled with the Holy Spirit, who granted diversities of tongues (Act. II, 1, 3). A good force, which knows not how to hurt, but knows how to fill. If anyone therefore wishes to merit this force of grace coming upon him from heaven, let him also himself go down with his eyes into the runnings of waters. He who has first poured out this force will merit that one. She went down with her eyes into these channels of waters, she who in the Gospel watered the Lord's feet with tears 1915D(Luc. VII, 38), and therefore by the price of her faith bought health for her soul and for her body, flowing now no longer with blood, but with spiritual grace.

1589. 50. David the prophet therefore went down, and therefore he brought back grace out of sin. He went down into the channels of waters, that is, he filled them, and heaped up the streams of the running waters with his own tears, or filled the empty and void channels with weeping alone. Or, because he shows certain senses of moral utterance, he went down into the channels of waters, he passed over them. And we might say, he transcended 1916Athem, and passed above them: but the force of the utterance is lessened, whereby a greater force of the affluence coming down than of that going up is expressed. See, I pray, what the words have from usage, that the prophetic discourse may not lose its own force, although usage itself has been accustomed, with greater fitness, to serve the sense of the writers.

51.

Puteus aquarum viventium; puteus enim si nihil haurias, inerti otio et degeneri situ facile corrumpitur: exercitatus autem nitescit ad speciem, dulcescit ad potum. Ita et acervus divitiarum, cumulo arenosus, speciosus est usu, otio autem inutilis habetur. Derivato igitur aliquid de puteo hoc: Ignem enim ardentem restinguit aqua, et eleemosyna resistit peccatis (Eccli. III, 33). Aqua autem stativa cito vermes facit: non stet thesaurus tuus, ne ignis tuus stet. 1916BStabit in te, nisi eum operibus tuae miserationis averteris.

51.

A well of living waters; for a well, if thou draw nothing from it, is easily corrupted by inert idleness and degenerate mould: but when it is used it grows bright to the sight, sweet to the drinking. So too a heap of riches, sandy in its pile, is comely in use, but in idleness is held useless. Draw off therefore something from this well: For water quencheth a burning fire, and alms resisteth sins (Eccli. III, 33). But standing water quickly breeds worms: let not thy treasure stand, lest thy fire stand. 1916BIt will stand in thee, unless thou turn it away by the works of thy mercy.

52.

(Vers. 16.) Exsurge, aquilo, et veni, auster, aspira hortum meum, et perfluunt aromata mea. Audisti, Christi sponsa, quia placet ei tuarum pulchritudo virtutum; audisti quia vestimentorum tuorum aromata, hoc est, bonum odorem integritatis omnibus odoribus praetulit. Audisti, quia hortus es clausus, suavium pomiferarum refertus frugibus. Pete igitur ut aspiret tibi Spiritus sanctus, aspiret supra cubile tuum, atque odorem piae mentis, et gratiae spiritalis accumulet. Excita Spiritum sanctum dicens: Exsurge, aquilo, et veni, auster; aspira hortum meum, et perfluant aromata mea. Descendat fraternus meus in hortum suum, et manducet fructum pomiferarum suarum. 1916CHortus Verbi animae vernantis affectus est, et in pomiferis virtutis est fructus. Venit ergo, et sive manduces, sive bibas, si Christum invocas, adest dicens: Venite, manducate panes meos, et bibite vinum meum (Prov. IX, 5).

52.

(Vers. 16.) Arise, north wind, and come, south wind, breathe upon my garden, and my spices flow forth. [Vulg. Surge, aquilo, et veni, auster; perfla hortum meum, et fluant aromata illius, "arise, north wind… and let the spices thereof flow"] Thou hast heard, bride of Christ, that the beauty of thy virtues pleases him; thou hast heard that he has preferred the spices of thy garments, that is, the good odor of integrity, above all odors. Thou hast heard that thou art a garden enclosed, filled with the produce of sweet fruit-trees. Ask therefore that the Holy Spirit may breathe upon thee, may breathe above thy bed, and may heap up the odor of a devout mind and of spiritual grace. Rouse the Holy Spirit, saying: Arise, north wind, and come, south wind; breathe upon my garden, and let my spices flow forth. Let my kinsman come down into his garden, and eat the fruit of his fruit-trees. 1916CThe garden of the Word is the affection of the soul in its spring, and in the fruit-trees is the fruit of virtue. He comes therefore, and whether thou eat or drink, if thou callest upon Christ, he is present, saying: Come, eat my loaves, and drink my wine (Prov. IX, 5). [Vulg. Venite, comedite panem meum, et bibite vinum quod miscui vobis, "come, eat my bread, and drink the wine which I have mingled for you"]

53.

Exsurge, inquit, aquilo, et veni, auster; aspira hortum meum, et perfluant aromata mea. Ex omnibus enim partibus mundi odor sacrae religionis adolevit, quo dilectae virginis membra fragrarunt. Unde et Ecclesia altitudinem servans mysteriorum coelestium, rejicit a se graviores venti procellas, et invitat vernantis gratiae suavitatem; et sciens quod ortus suus Christo displicere non possit, advocat Sponsum suum: Exsurge, inquit, aquilo, et veni, auster. Plerique haec ita accipiunt, quasi projiciatur aquilo, et invitetur 1916Dauster. Quod si ita accipiunt, exturbatur ab Ecclesia perfidiae glacialis asperitas, ne fiat fuga nostra hyeme vel sabbato, et invitatur australis verna temperies.

53.

Arise, he says, north wind, and come, south wind; breathe upon my garden, and let my spices flow forth. For from all parts of the world the odor of holy religion has grown, with which the limbs of the beloved virgin have been fragrant. Whence the Church also, keeping the loftiness of the heavenly mysteries, casts from herself the heavier storms of the wind, and invites the sweetness of springlike grace; and knowing that her garden cannot displease Christ, she calls her Bridegroom: Arise, he says, north wind, and come, south wind. Very many take these words thus, as though the north wind were cast out and the south wind invited. 1916DBut if they so take it, the icy harshness of unbelief is driven out from the Church, lest our flight be in winter or on the sabbath, and the southern springtime mildness is invited.

54.

Aut certe: Exsurge, aquilo, hoc est, surge, qui dormis, et exsurge a mortuis (Ephes. V, 14). Populus nationum qui diu ante dormisti, evigila aliquando, et illucescet tibi Christus. Postremo omnes invitantur ad Ecclesiam, et Synagogae populus et gentilium: sed prius Synagogae, quia priores ex Judaeis apostoli 1917Acrediderunt et per ipsos postea nationum populi congregati sunt. Vide ergo venientem solem nostrum ad austrum, postea gyrantem ad aquilonem. Hierusalem, Hierusalem: venit utique ad eam, quam etiam vocare dignatus est: sed haec Hierusalem terrena est, quae eccidit prophetas, hoc est, Synagoga Judaeorum: Quoties, inquit, volui congregare filios tuos, sicut gallina pullos suos, et noluisti? Ecce relinquetur domus vestra deserta (Matth. XXIII, 37 et 38). Gyravit igitur ad gentes: gyrando autem gyravit Spiritus Dei, et in gyros suos conversus est, ut fieret Deus omnia in omnibus.

54.

Or certainly: Arise, north wind, that is, rise, thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead (Ephes. V, 14). Thou people of the nations, who hast long slept before, awake at last, and Christ shall shine upon thee. Lastly all are invited to the Church, both the people of the Synagogue and that of the Gentiles: but the Synagogue's first, because the apostles from among the Jews believed first 1917Aand through them afterwards the peoples of the nations were gathered. See therefore our sun coming to the south, afterwards wheeling round to the north. Jerusalem, Jerusalem: he came indeed to her, whom he even deigned to call: but this Jerusalem is the earthly one, which slays eccidit the prophets, that is, the Synagogue of the Jews: How often, he says, would I have gathered thy children, as a hen her chickens, and thou wouldest not? Behold your house shall be left desolate (Matth. XXIII, 37 et 38). He wheeled therefore to the nations: and in wheeling the Spirit of God wheeled, and was turned into his own circles, that God might become all in all.

CAPUT QUINTUM.

CHAPTER FIVE.

1.

(Vers. 1.) Veniat dilectus meus in hortum meum, et comedat fructum pomorum suorum. Hinc hortum 1917Billum sibi Plato composuit, quem Jovis hortum alibi, alibi hortum mentis appellavit; Jovem enim et Deum et mentem dixit. In hunc introivisse animam, quam Venerem nuncupat, ut se abundantia et divitiis horti repleret, in quo repletus potu jaceret, potu qui nectar effunderet. Hoc igitur ex libro Canticorum composuit; eo quod anima Deo adhaerens in hortum mentis ingressa sit, in quo esset abundantia 1590 diversarum virtutum, floresque sermonum. Quis autem ignorat quod ex paradiso illo quem legimus in Genesi habere lignum vitae et lignum scientiae boni et mali (Gen. II, 8), et ligna caetera, abundantiam virtutum putaverit transferendam, et in hortum mentis esse plantandam?

1.

(Vers. 1.) Let my beloved come into my garden, and eat the fruit of his apples. [Vulg. Veniat dilectus meus in hortum suum, "let my beloved come into HIS garden"] Hence Plato framed for himself that garden 1917Bwhich he called in one place the garden of Jove, in another the garden of the mind; for he called Jove both God and mind. Into this, he says, the soul entered, which he names Venus, that she might fill herself with the abundance and riches of the garden, in which, filled with drink, she might lie, with a drink that poured out nectar. This therefore he framed out of the book of the Songs; in that the soul cleaving to God has entered into the garden of the mind, in which there was an abundance 1590 of divers virtues, and the flowers of discourses. And who does not know that out of that paradise, which we read in Genesis to have the tree of life and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil (Gen. II, 8), and the other trees, he thought an abundance of virtues was to be transferred, and to be planted in the garden of the mind?

2.

Quem in Canticis Canticorum hortum animae significat, vel ipsam animam; sic enim scriptum est: 1917CHortus clausus sponsa, soror mea, hortus clausus, fons signatus. Et infra ait anima: Exsurge, aquilo, et veni, auster; perfla hortum meum, et defluant unguenta tua, descendat frater meus in hortum suum (Cant. IV, 12 et 13). Quanto hoc pulchrius, quod anima ornata virtutum floribus, hortus sit; vel habeat in se paradisum germinantem, in quem hortum invitat Verbum Dei descendere; ut anima illa Verbi imbre coelestis, et ejus copiis irrigata fructificet! Verbum autem Dei pascitur animae virtutibus, quoties animam obedientem sibi et opimam invenerit: et carpit fructus ejus, et his delectatur. Cum autem descenderit in eam Dei Verbum, defluunt ex ea salubrium unguenta verborum, et diversarum 1917Dflagrant longe lateque redolentia gratiarum spiramina. Unde ait Sponsus (Sponsus autem animae Deus Verbum est, cui anima legitimo quodam connubii foedere copulatur):

2.

Which garden in the Song of Songs signifies the soul, or the soul herself; for so it is written: 1917CA garden enclosed, bride, my sister, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed. And below the soul says: Arise, north wind, and come, south wind; blow through my garden, and let thine ointments flow down, let my brother come down into his garden (Cant. IV, 12 et 13). How much more beautiful is this, that the soul adorned with the flowers of the virtues should be a garden; or should have in herself a sprouting paradise, into which garden she invites the Word of God to come down; that that soul, watered by the shower of the heavenly Word and by his abundance, may bear fruit! But the Word of God is fed with the virtues of the soul, as often as he has found the soul obedient to him and rich: and he plucks her fruits, and is delighted with them. And when the Word of God has come down into her, the ointments of healthful words flow down from her, and the breathings of divers 1917Dgraces glow, redolent far and wide. Whence the Bridegroom says (and the Bridegroom of the soul is God the Word, to whom the soul is joined by a certain lawful covenant of wedlock):

3.

(Vers. 2.) Ingressus sum in hortum meum, soror mea sponsa; vindemiavi myrrham meam cum unguentis meis, manducavi panem meum cum melle meo, bibi vinum meum cum lacte meo. Edite, proximi mei, et bibite et inebriamini, fratres mei. Ego dormio, et cor meum vigilat. Cognoscamus quos fructus vel cibos epuletur Deus, vel quibus delectetur. Delectatur eo, si quis 1918Amortificet peccatum suum, oblitteret culpam suam, sepeliat atque abolefaciat iniquitates suas; myrrha enim est sepultura mortuorum, mortua autem peccata sunt, quae vitae suavitatem habere non possunt. Perfunduntur autem divini sermonis unguentis, et fortiori cibo verbi velut pane, et suaviori sermone velut melle curantur quaedam vulnera delictorum. Sermones autem cibos esse docet et alibi Salomon, dicens: Favi mellis sermones boni (Prov. XVI, 24). In illo ergo horto sermones boni sunt, alius qui culpam coerceat, alius qui iniquitatem corripiat, alius qui mori faciat insolentiam, et velut sepeliat eam, quando correptus aliquis erroribus suis renuntiat.

3.

(Vers. 2.) I have entered into my garden, my sister, bride; I have gathered my myrrh with my ointments, I have eaten my bread with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk. Eat, my neighbors, and drink and be drunken, my brethren. I sleep, and my heart waketh. [Vulg. Veni in hortum meum… messui myrrham meam cum aromatibus meis; comedi favum cum melle meo… Comedite, amici… carissimi, "I came into my garden… I reaped my myrrh with my spices; I ate the honeycomb… eat, friends… dearest ones"] Let us learn what fruits or foods God feasts upon, or in what he is delighted. He is delighted in this, if any 1918Amortify his own sin, blot out his own fault, bury and abolish his iniquities; for myrrh is the burial of the dead, and dead are the sins which cannot have the sweetness of life. And certain wounds of offenses are bathed with the ointments of the divine word, and are healed with the stronger food of the word as with bread, and with sweeter discourse as with honey. And Solomon teaches elsewhere too that words are foods, saying: Good words are honeycombs of honey (Prov. XVI, 24). [Vulg. Favus mellis composita verba, "well-ordered words are a honeycomb of honey"] In that garden therefore there are good words, one that may restrain fault, another that may rebuke iniquity, another that may make insolence die, and as it were bury it, when one who has been rebuked renounces his errors.

4.

Est etiam sermo fortior, qui confirmat cor hominis 1918Bvalidioribus Scripturae coelestis alimentis. Comedi favum cum melle meo, bibi vinum meum cum lacte meo. Est enim sermo suasorius dulcis ut mel, et tamen peccatoris conscientiam in ipsa suavitate compungens. Est etiam ferventioris spiritus sermo, qui inebriat sicut vinum, et cor hominis laetificat. Est denique lacteus sermo, purus et candidus. Hos cibos dulcium utiliumque sermonum epulandos Sponsus proximis suis dicit: Edite, proximi mei, et bibite, et inebriamini, fratres mei. Proximi autem sunt qui eum sequuntur, et ejus nuptiis intersunt. Quo cibo et potu (bibit enim unusquisque (Prov. V, 15) aquam de suis vasis, et de puteorum suorum fontibus) repleta anima, atque inebriata, saeculo dormiebat, vigilabat Deo. Et ideo sicut posteriora docent, aperiri sibi Deus Verbum ejus januam postulabat, ut eam suo repleret ingressu. 1918C

4.

There is also a stronger word, which strengthens the heart of man 1918Bwith the more powerful nourishments of the heavenly Scripture. I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk. For there is a persuasive word, sweet as honey, and yet in its very sweetness pricking the conscience of the sinner. There is also a word of more fervent spirit, which makes drunken like wine, and gladdens the heart of man. There is finally a milky word, pure and white. These foods of sweet and useful words the Bridegroom bids his neighbors feast upon: Eat, my neighbors, and drink, and be drunken, my brethren. And the neighbors are those who follow him, and are present at his marriage. With which food and drink (for every man drinks (Prov. V, 15) water out of his own vessels, and out of the fountains of his own wells) the soul, filled and made drunken, was sleeping to the world, was waking to God. And therefore, as what follows teaches, God the Word demanded that her door be opened to him, that he might fill her with his entering. 1918C

5.

Hinc ergo epulatores illi Platonici, hinc nectar illud ex vino et melle prophetico, hinc somnus ille translatus est, hinc vita illa perpetua quam deos suos dixit epulari, quia Christus vita est. Ideoque talium sermonum seminibus, animae ejus repletus est venter, atque ipsa exivit in Verbo: quae autem exit anima a servitio isto, et elevat se a corpore, Verbum sequitur. Aliter, ut superius (Sup., cap. 4) aliquantulum repetamus: Hortus conclusus soror mea, sponsa, hortus conclusus, fons signatus. Quo significat signatum debere apud te manere mysterium, ne violetur operibus malae vitae, atque adulterio castitatis: ne divulgetur quibus non convenit, ne garrula loquacitate dispergatur in perfidos.

5.

Hence therefore those Platonic feasters, hence that nectar out of the prophetic wine and honey, hence that sleep was transferred, hence that perpetual life which he said his gods feast upon, because Christ is life. And therefore with the seeds of such words the belly of his soul was filled, and she herself went out at the Word: and the soul which goes out from that servitude, and lifts herself up from the body, follows the Word. Otherwise, that we may go back somewhat to what is above (Sup., cap. 4): A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed. By which he signifies that the mystery ought to remain sealed with thee, lest it be violated by the works of an evil life, and by the adultery of chastity: lest it be divulged to those to whom it is not fitting, lest it be scattered among the faithless by garrulous talkativeness.

6.

1918DBona debet ergo fidei tuae esse custodia, ut intemerata vitae ac silentii integritas perseveret. Unde et Ecclesia altitudinem servans mysteriorum coelestium, rejicit a se graviores venti procellas, et invitat vernantis gratiae suavitatem. Et sciens quod hortus suus Christo displicere non possit, advocat ipsum sponsum dicens: Exsurge, aquilo, et veni, auster: perfla hortum meum, et fluant unguenta mea (Cant. IV, 10): 1591 descendat frater meus in hortum suum, et edat fructum pomiferarum suarum (Cant. V, 1); bonas enim arbores et fructiferas habet, radicesque 1919Asuas quas tinxerat in sacri fontis irriguo, et in bonos fructus novae fecunditatis germine pullulaverint; ut non jam prophetica caedantur securi, sed evangelica ubertate fecundentur. Denique fertilitate earum Dominus delectatus respondet:

6.

1918DGood therefore ought the guard of thy faith to be, that the undefiled integrity of life and of silence may persevere. Whence the Church also, keeping the loftiness of the heavenly mysteries, casts from herself the heavier storms of the wind, and invites the sweetness of springlike grace. And knowing that her garden cannot displease Christ, she calls the bridegroom himself, saying: Arise, north wind, and come, south wind: blow through my garden, and let my ointments flow (Cant. IV, 10): 1591 let my brother come down into his garden, and eat the fruit of his fruit-trees (Cant. V, 1); for she has good and fruitful trees, and her roots 1919Awhich she had dipped in the watering of the sacred fountain, and they have sprouted into good fruits with the bud of a new fecundity; so that they may no longer be cut down by the prophetic axe, but be made fruitful with evangelical richness. In short the Lord, delighted by their fertility, answers:

7.

Ingressus sum in hortum meum, soror mea sponsa: vindemiavi myrrham cum unguentis meis, manducavi panem meum cum melle meo: bibi potum meum cum lacte meo. Quare panem et vinum dixerim, fideles intelligunt. Illud autem non dubium, quod nobis ipse manducat et bibit, sicut in nobis legisti, quia in carcere esse se dixit (Matth. XXV, 36). Unde et Ecclesia videns tantam gratiam, hortatur filios suos, hortatur proximos, ut ad sacramenta concurrant, dicens: Edite, proximi mei, et bibite, et inebriamini, fratres 1919Bmei. Quid edamus, quid bibamus, alibi tibi per prophetam Spiritus sanctus expressit, dicens: Gustate, et videte quoniam suavis est Dominus; beatus vir qui sperat in eo (Psalm. XXXIII, 9). In illo sacramento Christus est, quia corpus est Christi: non ergo corporalis esca, sed spiritalis est. Unde et Apostolus de typo ejus ait: Quia patres nostri escam spiritalem manducaverunt, et potum spiritalem biberunt (I Cor. X, 3). Corpus enim Dei corpus est spiritale, corpus Christi corpus est divini spiritus; quia spiritus est Christus, ut legimus: Spiritus ante faciem nostram Christus Dominus (Thren. IV, 20). Et in Petri epistola habemus: Christus pro nobis mortuus est (I Pet. II, 21). Denique cor nostrum esca ista confirmat, et potus iste laetificat cor hominis, ut propheta memoravit 1919C(Psal. CIII, 25).

7.

I have entered into my garden, my sister, bride: I have gathered myrrh with my ointments, I have eaten my bread with my honey: I have drunk my drink with my milk. Why I have said bread and wine, the faithful understand. But this is not doubtful, that he himself eats and drinks in us, as thou hast read in our case, that he said he was in prison (Matth. XXV, 36). Whence the Church also, seeing so great a grace, exhorts her sons, exhorts her neighbors, to run together to the sacraments, saying: Eat, my neighbors, and drink, and be drunken, my 1919Bbrethren. What we should eat, what we should drink, the Holy Spirit has expressed to thee elsewhere through the prophet, saying: Taste, and see that the Lord is sweet; blessed is the man that hopeth in him (Psalm. XXXIII, 9). In that sacrament is Christ, because it is the body of Christ: it is therefore not bodily food, but spiritual. Whence the Apostle also says concerning its type: That our fathers ate spiritual food, and drank spiritual drink (I Cor. X, 3). For the body of God is a spiritual body, the body of Christ is the body of the divine Spirit; because Christ is spirit, as we read: The Spirit before our face, Christ the Lord (Thren. IV, 20). [Vulg. Spiritus oris nostri, christus Dominus, "the breath of our mouth, Christ the Lord"] And in Peter's epistle we have: Christ died for us (I Pet. II, 21). [Vulg. Christus passus est pro nobis, "Christ suffered for us"] In short this food strengthens our heart, and this drink gladdens the heart of man, as the prophet recorded 1919C(Psal. CIII, 25).

8.

Hortus conclusus soror mea sponsa, hortus conclusus, fons signatus; ne facile aperiat os suum, nec vulgari resignet alloquio; neque enim de ipsis divinis decet, nisi verbo Dei interpellata respondeas. Quid tibi cum caeteris? Soli Christo loquere, soli fabulare Christo. Si enim ut mulieres taceant in Ecclesia scriptum est (I Cor. XIV, 34): quanto magis non decet patere virginis januam, non decet viduae patere fores! Cito insidiator pudoris obrepit, cito verbum excipit, quod revocare desideres. Si Evae clausa fuisset janua, nec Adam deceptus fuisset, nec respondisset interrogata serpenti. Introivit mors per fenestram, hoc est, per Evae ostium. Ingreditur mors per ostium tuum, si falsum loquaris, si turpiter, si 1919Dprocaciter, postremo si ubi non oportet, loquaris. Clausae sint igitur labiorum tuorum fores, et obseratum maneat vocis vestibulum, tunc fortasse reserandum, cum audieris Dei vocem, cum audieris Dei verbum. Tunc tibi myrrha sudabit, tunc tibi baptismatis aspirabit gratia, ut ab elementis mundi commoriare cum Christo, et cum Christo resurgas. Quid adhuc, inquit, velut viventes, de hoc mundo decernitis? Ne tetigeritis, ne attaminaveritis, ne gustaveritis, quae sunt ad corruptionem ipso usu (Coloss. II, 20 et seq.).

8.

A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed; let her not easily open her mouth, nor unseal it with common speech; for it is not fitting that thou answer even concerning divine things themselves, unless questioned by the word of God. What hast thou to do with the rest? Speak to Christ alone, converse with Christ alone. For if it is written that women should keep silence in the Church (I Cor. XIV, 34): how much more is it unfitting that a virgin's door lie open, unfitting that a widow's doors lie open! Quickly the plotter against modesty creeps in, quickly he catches up a word which thou wouldst wish to call back. If Eve's door had been closed, neither would Adam have been deceived, nor would she, being questioned, have answered the serpent. Death entered through the window, that is, through Eve's door. Death enters through thy door, if thou speak falsehood, if basely, if 1919Dshamelessly, lastly if thou speak where it is not fitting. Let the doors of thy lips therefore be closed, and let the vestibule of thy voice remain barred, then perhaps to be unbarred, when thou shalt have heard the voice of God, when thou shalt have heard the word of God. Then myrrh shall sweat for thee, then the grace of baptism shall breathe upon thee, that thou mayest die together with Christ from the elements of the world, and rise again with Christ. Why yet, he says, as though living, do ye decree concerning this world? Touch not, handle not, taste not, the things which are unto corruption by the very use (Coloss. II, 20 et seq.). [Vulg. Ne tetigeritis, neque gustaveritis, neque contrectaveritis: quae sunt omnia in interitum ipso usu, "touch not, taste not, handle not: which are all unto destruction by the very use"]

9.

Descendat frater meus in hortum suum, ut comedat 1920Afructum pomiferarum suarum. Quae sunt istae pomiferae? Lignum aridum factus es in Adam, sed nunc per gratiam Christi pomifera arbor pullulasti. Libenter accepit Dominus Jesus, et dignatione coelesti respondit Ecclesiae suae: Descendi, inquit, in hortum meum, vindemiavi myrrham cum unguentis meis, manducavi panem meum cum melle meo, et bibi vinum meum cum lacte meo.

9.

Let my brother come down into his garden, that he may eat 1920Athe fruit of his fruit-trees. What are these fruit-trees? Thou wast made a dry tree in Adam, but now through the grace of Christ thou hast sprouted as a fruit-bearing tree. Gladly did the Lord Jesus receive it, and with heavenly condescension answered his Church: I have come down, he says, into my garden, I have gathered myrrh with my ointments, I have eaten my bread with my honey, and I have drunk my wine with my milk.

10.

Edite, inquit, fratres mei, et inebriamini: Vindemiavi myrrham cum unguentis meis (Psalm. LXXIX, 9). Quae est ista vindemia? Cognoscite vineam, et agnoscetis vindemiam. Vineam, inquit, ex Aegypto transtulisti, hoc est, populum Dei. Vos estis vinea, vos estis vindemia: quasi vinea plantati, quasi vindemia fructum dedistis. Vindemiavi myrrham cum 1920Bunguentis meis, hoc est, in odorem quem accepistis.

10.

Eat, he says, my brethren, and be drunken: I have gathered myrrh with my ointments (Psalm. LXXIX, 9). What is this vintage? Know the vineyard, and ye shall recognize the vintage. A vineyard, he says, thou hast brought out of Egypt, that is, the people of God. Ye are the vineyard, ye are the vintage: as a vineyard ye were planted, as a vintage ye have given fruit. I have gathered myrrh with 1920Bmy ointments, that is, unto the odor which ye have received.

11.

Manducavi panem meum cum melle meo. Vides quod in hoc pane nulla sit amaritudo, sed omnis suavitas sit. Bibi vinum meum cum lacte meo, vides hujusmodi esse laetitiam, quae nullius peccati sordibus polluatur? Quotiescumque enim bibis, remissionem accipis peccatorum, et inebriaris spiritu. Unde et Apostolus ait: Nolite inebriari vino . . . . . sed implemini Spiritu sancto (Ephes. V, 18). Vino enim qui inebriatur, vacillat et titubat: spiritu qui inebriatur, radicatus in Christo est, et ideo praeclara ebrietas, quae sobrietatem mentis operatur.

1592. 12. Edite, inquit, amici mei, et bibite, et inebriamini. Bona ebrietas quae ad meliora atque jucunda 1920Cfacit quemdam mentis excessum, ut immemor sollicitudinum animus noster, vino jucunditatis hilaretur. Bona mensae spiritalis ebrietas; denique poculum inebrians quam praeclarum (Psalm. XXII, 5)! Et alibi habes: Rivos ejus inebria, multiplica generationes ejus (Psalm. LXIV, 11); eo quod ebrietas terrae cum infusa fuerit imbre coelesti, suscitare semina, fructus multiplicare consueverit. Itaque ubi Verbum quod sicut pluvia descendit de coelo, venas terrae nostrae, id est, animae ac mentis inebriavit praedicatione divina, excitantur virtutum studia diversarum, fructusque fidei, et castae devotionis adolescit, meritoque ei dicitur: Visitasti terram, et inebriasti eam (Psalm. LXIV, 10). Susceptione etenim corporis visitavit, ut sanaret infirmos: inebriavit gratia spiritali, ut anxios 1920Djucunditate mulceret.

11.

I have eaten my bread with my honey. Thou seest that in this bread there is no bitterness, but that all is sweetness. I have drunk my wine with my milk, dost thou see that such is the gladness, which is polluted by the filth of no sin? For as often as thou drinkest, thou receivest remission of sins, and art made drunken with the spirit. Whence the Apostle also says: Be not drunk with wine . . . . . but be filled with the Holy Spirit (Ephes. V, 18). For he who is made drunken with wine staggers and totters: he who is made drunken with the spirit is rooted in Christ, and therefore that drunkenness is excellent which works soberness of mind.

1592. 12. Eat, he says, my friends, and drink, and be drunken. A good drunkenness, which makes a certain ecstasy of the mind toward better and pleasant 1920Cthings, so that our mind, forgetful of its anxieties, may be gladdened with the wine of pleasantness. Good is the drunkenness of the spiritual table; in short, how excellent is the inebriating cup (Psalm. XXII, 5)! [Vulg. calix meus inebrians quam praeclarus est, "how excellent is my inebriating chalice"] And elsewhere thou hast: Make drunk her streams, multiply her generations (Psalm. LXIV, 11); [Vulg. multiplica genimina ejus, "multiply her fruits"] in that the drunkenness of the earth, when it has been poured in by a heavenly shower, is wont to raise up seeds and to multiply fruits. And so, when the Word, which came down from heaven like rain, has made drunken with divine preaching the veins of our earth, that is, of the soul and of the mind, the pursuits of divers virtues are aroused, and the fruit of faith and of chaste devotion grows up, and rightly is it said to him: Thou hast visited the earth, and hast made it drunken (Psalm. LXIV, 10). For by the assumption of a body he visited, that he might heal the sick: he made drunken with spiritual grace, that he might soothe the anxious 1920Dwith pleasantness.

13.

Ego dormio, et cor meum vigilat. Est sanctorum somnus etiam operarius, secundum quod scriptum est: Ego dormio, et cor meum vigilat. Et secundum quod Jacob sanctus divina dormiens videbat mysteria, quae vigilans non viderat; de coelo ad terras aerem praevium sanctis, respicientem Dominum, et pollicentem terrae ejus possessionem. Itaque brevi somno dormiens, impetravit quod magno labore postea acquisivit, haereditas enim ejus est somnus sanctorum, feriatus ab omnibus corporis voluptatibus. 1921Aab omni animi perturbatione, tranquillitatem menti invehens, placiditatem animae; ut tamquam soluta nexu corporis se ablevet, et Christo adhaereat. Hic est somnus vitae sanctorum.

13.

I sleep, and my heart waketh. There is also a working sleep of the saints, according to what is written: I sleep, and my heart waketh. And according as holy Jacob, sleeping, saw the divine mysteries which waking he had not seen; from heaven to the lands the air going before the saints, the Lord looking back, and promising the possession of that land. And so, sleeping in a short sleep, he obtained what afterwards he acquired with great labor, for his inheritance is the sleep of the saints, keeping holiday from all pleasures of the body. 1921Afrom every disturbance of the mind, bringing tranquillity to the mind, placidity to the soul; that, as though loosed from the bond of the body, it may lift itself up and cleave to Christ. This is the sleep of the life of the saints.

14.

Vox dilecti mei pulsantis, aperi mihi, soror mea. Et si dormias, et si modo devotionem animae tuae noverit Christus, venit, et pulsat ejus januam, et dicit: Aperi mihi, soror mea. Bene soror, quia nuptiae spiritales sunt Verbi atque animae; neque enim animae norunt conjugiorum foedera, et usus copulae corporalis, sed sunt sicut angeli Dei in coelo. Aperi, inquit, mihi, sed extraneis claude. Claude saeculo, claude mundo. Neque ipsa foras ad illa materialia prodeas, neque tuum relinquens lumen, alienum requiras; quia materiale lumen tenebrosam infundit 1921Bcaliginem, ut non videatur lumen verae gloriae. Aperi ergo mihi, noli coarctari, sed dilata te, et adimplebo te. Et quia decurso orbe terrarum, plus molestiarum et offensionis reperi, nec facile habui ubi requiescerem; ideo tu aperi, ut in te filius hominis reclinet caput, cui non est requies nisi super humilem et mansuetum.

14.

The voice of my beloved knocking, open to me, my sister. Even if thou sleep, if only Christ has known the devotion of thy soul, he comes, and knocks at her door, and says: Open to me, my sister. Well sister, because the marriage of the Word and of the soul is spiritual; for souls know not the covenants of wedlock, and the practice of bodily union, but are as the angels of God in heaven. Open, he says, to me, but close to strangers. Close to the age, close to the world. Neither go thou forth outside to those material things, nor, leaving thine own light, seek another's; because material light pours in a dark 1921Bgloom, so that the light of true glory is not seen. Open therefore to me, be not straitened, but enlarge thyself, and I will fill thee. And because, having traversed the world, I have found more of troubles and of offense, and have not easily had where I might rest; therefore do thou open, that in thee the son of man may lay his head, for whom there is no rest save upon the humble and the meek.

15.

Aperi mihi, soror mea, surge, proxima mea, columba mea, perfecta mea. Charitate proxima, columba simplicitate, virtute perfecta. Quia caput meum plenum est rore; sicut enim ros coeli nocturnas amovet siccitates, ita ros Domini nostri Jesu Christi nocturnis et saecularibus tenebris aeternae vitae stillavit humorem. Hoc est caput quod aestu mundi nescivit arescere, unde ait: Quia si in humido haec faciunt, 1921Cin arido quid facient (Luc. XXIII, 31)? Hoc igitur caput rorat aliis, sibi abundat. Et bene abundat caput Christi; quia caput tuum Christus est, qui plenus est semper, nec liberalitatibus hauritur suis, aut diutina deficit largitate. In hoc caput ferrum non ascendit, quod est instrumentum belli, insigne discordiae. Nunc specta, et vide ros ille qualis sit, scilicet non vulgaris humoris.

15.

Open to me, my sister; arise, my neighbor, my dove, my perfect one. [Vulg. has amica mea and immaculata mea, "my friend… my spotless one," and prints no surge] Neighbor by charity, a dove by simplicity, perfect by virtue. For my head is full of dew; for as the dew of heaven removes the drynesses of the night, so the dew of our Lord Jesus Christ has distilled the moisture of eternal life upon the nightly and worldly darkness. This is the head which knew not how to grow parched by the heat of the world, whence he says: For if they do these things in the moist, 1921Cwhat shall they do in the dry (Luc. XXIII, 31)? [Vulg. in viridi ligno… quid fiet, "in the green wood… what shall be done"] This head therefore bedews others, and for itself abounds. And well does the head of Christ abound; because thy head is Christ, who is always full, and is not drained dry by his own liberalities, nor fails through long-continued largess. Upon this head no iron comes up, which is the instrument of war, the badge of discord. Now look, and see of what sort that dew is, namely, not of common moisture.

16.

Cincinni autem mei guttarum noctis. Noli, proxima mea, accipere cincinnos corporalium capillorum: non illa ornamenta, sed crimina sunt: lenocinia formae, non praecepta virtutis. Alios cincinnos Nazaraeus habet, in quibus ferrum non ascendit, quos nemo praecidit: qui non calamistris compositi aut arte digesti, sed multiplici crispantes virtutum nitentium 1921Dgratia refulserunt. Disce in historia quales Nazaraeus cincinnos habeat, quos quamdiu Samson habuit intactos, numquam vinci potuit. Cincinnos perdidit, et meritum virtutis amisit (Jud. XVI, 17).

16.

But my locks are of the drops of the night. [Vulg. cincinni mei guttis noctium, ablative and with "nights" plural] Do not, my neighbor, take these locks to be locks of bodily hairs: those are not ornaments, but crimes; the pandering of beauty, not the precepts of virtue. The Nazirite has other locks, upon which no iron comes up, which no one cuts away: locks not arranged with the curling-iron or ordered by art, but which shone forth curling with the manifold grace of gleaming virtues. 1921DLearn in the history what manner of locks the Nazirite has, which, so long as Samson kept them untouched, he could never be overcome. He lost his locks, and lost the merit of his virtue (Jud. XVI, 17).

17.

(Vers. 3.) Audita igitur voce Verbi, quam exueras tunicam nocte, ne quaesieris quemadmodum induas eam; ostenditur enim, et frequenter offertur per nequitiam spiritalem quemadmodum, inquam, induas eam, obliviscaris et nescias; quasi jam Dominus assistat, exsors corporalium vinculorum turbata 1922Aconsurgas, internam orationibus praepares mentem, dum surgis; ut ex humilibus ad superna contendas, et studeas fores tui cordis aperire. Dum 1593 manus tuas tendis ad Christum, fidelem tua gesta spirabunt odorem.

17.

(Vers. 3.) Therefore, when the voice of the Word has been heard, seek not how thou mayest put on the tunic which thou hadst put off by night; for it is shown, and frequently offered through spiritual wickedness, how — how, I say, thou mayest put it on: forget it and know it not; as though the Lord were already standing by, do thou, free of bodily bonds, rise up troubled, 1922Aand prepare the inward mind by prayers while thou risest; that from lowly things thou mayest strive toward the things above, and be zealous to open the doors of thy heart. While 1593 thou stretchest out thy hands to Christ, thy deeds shall breathe a faithful odor.

18.

Aperi mihi, soror mea. Nonne cum aliquem de primariis viris desideras convenire, primo ad ejus domum appropinquas: postea quaeris informari atque instrui, ut cognoscas mentem patris familias: deinde ut ejus domum ingrediaris, imploras, ne quis te abjiciat et excludat. Pulsa ergo et tu regiam illam coelestem, pulsa non manu corporis, sed quadam orationis tuae dextera. Non sola manus corporis pulsat, pulsat et vox; scriptum est enim: Vox fratris mei pulsat ad januam. Pulsamus et digito. Denique 1922Bet Thomas digito meruit januam resurrectionis aperire: et tibi dicit Jesus: Infer digitum tuum huc, et mitte in manus meas et latus meum, et noli esse incredulus, sed fidelis (Joan. XX, 27). Pulsa ergo digito, si non potes tota manu, pulsa januam. Christus est janua qui ait: Per me si quis introierit, salvabitur (Joan. X, 9).

18.

Open to me, my sister. When thou desirest to meet one of the foremost men, dost thou not first draw near to his house: afterwards thou seekest to be informed and instructed, that thou mayest learn the mind of the master of the house: then, that thou mayest enter his house, thou entreatest that no one cast thee off and shut thee out. Knock thou therefore also at that heavenly palace; knock not with the hand of the body, but with a certain right hand of thy prayer. Not the hand of the body alone knocks; the voice knocks also, for it is written: The voice of my brother knocks at the door. [Vulg. Cant. 5:2 Vox dilecti mei pulsantis, "the voice of my beloved knocking"] We knock also with the finger. In short, 1922BThomas too deserved with his finger to open the door of the resurrection: and Jesus says to thee: Put thy finger hither, and put it into my hands and my side, and be not unbelieving, but faithful (Joan. XX, 27). [Vulg. et vide manus meas, et affer manum tuam, et mitte in latus meum, "and see my hands, and bring thy hand, and put it into my side"] Knock therefore with thy finger, if thou canst not with the whole hand; knock at the door. Christ is the door, who says: By me if any man shall enter in, he shall be saved (Joan. X, 9).

19.

Cum hanc igitur januam pulsaveris, vide quomodo ingrediaris; ne forte etiam ingressus, extra conspectum regis sis. Multi ingrediuntur palatia, et non statim regem istum terrae vident, sed frequenter observant, ut aliquando videre mereantur. Nec praesumunt videndi copiam, sed jussu repraesentantur, et precem fundunt; ut cum benevolentia suscipiantur, caventes primos sui sermonis ingressus; 1922Cne quid titubet, ne quid offendat: quanto magis rogandus est Deus, ut ostium misericordiae suae nostra ingrediatur oratio!

19.

When therefore thou hast knocked at this door, see how thou enterest; lest perchance, even having entered, thou be outside the sight of the king. Many enter palaces, and do not straightway see that king of the earth, but often keep watch, that at some time they may deserve to see him. Nor do they presume upon the opportunity of seeing him, but are presented at his bidding, and pour out a petition; that they may be received with good will, being careful of the first approaches of their speech; 1922Cthat nothing stumble, that nothing give offense: how much more must God be entreated, that our prayer may enter the door of his mercy!

20.

Vox fratris mei pulsat ad januam. Januam quoque tuam disce, o sponsa Christi, temporibus obserare nocturnis; ne facile quisquam patentem reperiat. Sponsus ipse vult clausam esse, cum pulsat. Janua nostra os nostrum est, Christo propemodum soli debet aperiri, nec aperiatur nisi ante pulsarit Dei Verbum. Vox fratris mei pulsat ad januam. Audi pulsantem, audi introire cupientem.

20.

The voice of my brother knocks at the door. Learn thou also, O bride of Christ, to bar thy door in the times of night; lest anyone easily find it lying open. The Bridegroom himself wills it to be shut when he knocks. Our door is our mouth; it ought to be opened to Christ almost alone, nor let it be opened unless the Word of God has first knocked. The voice of my brother knocks at the door. Hear him knocking, hear him desiring to come in.

21.

(Vers. 2.) Aperi mihi, soror mea, sponsa, columba mea, perfecta mea; quia caput meum plenum est rore, et crines mei guttis noctis. Considera quando maxime pulsat januam tuam Deus Verbum, cum repletum 1922Dest caput ejus rore nocturno. In tribulatione enim et tentationibus positos visitare dignatur; ne quis forte succumbat victus aerumnis. Repletur ergo caput ejus rore vel guttis, quando corpus ejus laborat. Tunc ergo vigilandum, ne cum venerit Sponsus, recedat exclusus. Si enim dormias, et cor tuum non vigilet, discedit antequam pulset: si cor tuum vigilet, pulsat, et aperiri sibi januam poscit.

21.

(Vers. 2.) Open to me, my sister, my bride, my dove, my perfect one; for my head is full of dew, and my hair of the drops of the night. Consider when God the Word most of all knocks at thy door: when 1922Dhis head has been filled with the nightly dew. For he deigns to visit those set in tribulation and temptations; lest anyone perchance succumb, overcome by hardships. His head therefore is filled with dew or with drops, when his body labors. Then therefore must we keep watch, lest, when the Bridegroom has come, he depart shut out. For if thou sleep, and thy heart do not keep watch, he departs before he knocks: if thy heart keep watch, he knocks, and demands that the door be opened to him.

22.

Habemus ergo animae nostrae januam, habemus et portas de quibus dictum est: Tollite portas 1923Aprincipes vestras, et elevamini portae aeternales, et introibit Rex gloriae (Psal. XXIII, 7). Est ergo et coelum in his in quibus sunt portae aeternales. Si has fidei tuae portas velis attollere, intrabit ad te Rex gloriae, triumphum portans propriae passionis. Habet enim justitia portas, nam etiam de his legimus scriptum, dicente Domino Jesu per prophetam suum: Aperite mihi portas justitiae (Psal. CXVII, 19). Et infra ait propheta David: Lauda, Hierusalem, Dominum, lauda Deum tuum, Sion; quoniam confortavit seras portarum tuarum (Psal. CXLVII, 12).

22.

We have therefore a door of our soul; we have also gates, of which it is said: Lift up your gates, ye princes, 1923Aand be ye lifted up, ye everlasting gates, and the King of glory shall enter in (Psal. XXIII, 7). There is therefore also a heaven in those in whom are the everlasting gates. If thou wilt raise up these gates of thy faith, the King of glory shall enter in to thee, bearing the triumph of his own passion. For justice has gates, for of these too we read it written, the Lord Jesus saying through his prophet: Open to me the gates of justice (Psal. CXVII, 19). And below the prophet David says: Praise the Lord, O Jerusalem; praise thy God, O Sion; for he hath strengthened the bolts of thy gates (Psal. CXLVII, 12).

23.

Est ergo anima quae habet januam, est quae habet portas. Ad hanc januam venit Christus, et pulsat: pulsat et portas. Aperi ergo illi, vult introire, vult Sponsam invenire vigilantem. Noli ergo 1923Bbono amatori facere moras, cito recedit: et tu somno corporis tui videris exclusisse pulsantem. Excludis enim, cum desidiosus es, cum piger, cum somnolentus. His repagulis Christus excluditur: si castus et si sobrius sis, cave ne negligens sis. Majorem Christo facit injuriam, qui advenientem repellit.

23.

There is therefore a soul which has a door, there is one which has gates. To this door Christ comes, and knocks: he knocks also at the gates. Open therefore to him; he wills to enter, he wills to find the Bride keeping watch. Do not therefore 1923Bmake delays for a good lover: he quickly withdraws; and thou by the sleep of thy body shalt be seen to have shut out him who knocks. For thou dost shut him out when thou art slothful, when lazy, when drowsy. By these bars Christ is shut out: if thou be chaste and sober, beware lest thou be negligent. He does Christ the greater injury who repels him at his coming.

24.

Aperi mihi, soror mea, proxima mea: pulsat ad januam etiam quando tu dormis: si tamen vel excitatus evigiles, vel vocatus januam tui pectoris aperias, introibit. Quod si fugias lectionem propheticam, si domi non legas, in Ecclesia audire nolis, nonne sicut ille qui averso connivet obtutu, ne videat quod possit videre, claudit oculos ne aspiciat, cui potestas videndi est: aut sicut in furore plerique injecere manus oculis suis; ita et tu primo averteris 1923Cconniventi magis dissimulatione, quam refragatione praerupta. Nam cum ad Ecclesiam venis et te Christianum 1594 esse asseris, sanus videris, aperis oculos quibus possis videre: sed dum audire dissimulas quae leguntur, claudis ne videas tibi, et si aliis videre videaris. Injicis etiam quasdam perfidiae et intemperantiae manus oculis animae tuae, et caecitatem infers cordi tuo (quod est gravius) voluntariam; ut videns non videas, audiens non audias.

24.

Open to me, my sister, my neighbor: he knocks at the door even when thou sleepest: if only, being roused, thou awake, or, being called, thou open the door of thy breast, he will enter in. But if thou flee the prophetic reading, if thou read not at home, if thou be unwilling to hear in the Church — art thou not like him who winks with averted gaze, that he may not see what he could see, and shuts his eyes lest he look, though the power of seeing is his: or as many in a rage have cast their hands upon their own eyes; so thou too at first turnest away 1923Crather by a winking dissimulation than by an abrupt refusal. For when thou comest to the Church and assertest that thou art 1594 a Christian, thou seemest whole, thou openest the eyes with which thou couldst see: but while thou dissemblest to hear the things that are read, thou closest them lest thou see for thyself, even if to others thou seem to see. Thou castest also certain hands of faithlessness and intemperance upon the eyes of thy soul, and bringest upon thy heart a blindness (which is the graver thing) that is voluntary; so that seeing thou seest not, hearing thou hearest not.

25.

Putas te solum esse cum fornicaris, et non recordaris, quia oculi Domini vident orbem terrarum? Non audis dicentem: Ecce venit hora ut dispergamini unusquisque in sua, et me solum relinquatis: sed non sum solus; quia Pater mecum est (Joan. XVI, 32). Adest ergo Pater, adest et Filius Dei, adsunt 1923Dministri, adsunt Cherubim et Seraphim, qui dicunt. Sanctus, sanctus, sanctus, plena est terra majestatis tuae (Esai. VI). Plenus est enim mundus virtutum, quia plenus est nequitiarum: plenus est orbis terrarum remediis, quia plenus est laqueis.

25.

Dost thou think that thou art alone when thou committest fornication, and dost not remember that the eyes of the Lord behold the round earth? Dost thou not hear him who says: Behold, the hour comes that ye be scattered every man to his own, and leave me alone: but I am not alone, for the Father is with me (Joan. XVI, 32). The Father therefore is present, the Son of God also is present, the ministers are present, 1923Dthe Cherubim and Seraphim are present, who say. Holy, holy, holy, the earth is full of thy majesty (Esai. VI). [Vulg. plena est omnis terra gloria ejus, "the whole earth is full of his glory"] For the world is full of virtues, because it is full of wickednesses: the round earth is full of remedies, because it is full of snares.

26.

(Vers. 3.) Exui tunicam meam, quomodo induam illam? Etenim in hac saeculi nocte prius tibi corporalis vitae amictus est exuendus; exuit enim se carnem Dominus, ut pro te de dominationibus et potestatibus mundi hujus triumpharet. Quomodo induam illam? Vide, anima Deo devota, quid dicat. 1924ASic se actus corporis, et terrenos exuit mores, ut nesciat quomodo etiamsi velit, rursus induere possit. Quomodo induam illam? Hoc est qua verecundia, quo pudore, qua postremo memoria? Consuetudo enim boni, usum veteris pravitatis amisit.

26.

(Vers. 3.) I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? [Vulg. Expoliavi me tunica mea: quomodo induar illa?, passive, "how shall I be clothed with it?"] For indeed in this night of the world the garment of bodily life must first be put off by thee; for the Lord put off flesh from himself, that for thee he might triumph over the dominations and powers of this world. How shall I put it on? See, O soul devoted to God, what she says. 1924ASo has she put off the acts of the body and earthly manners, that she knows not how, even if she should wish it, she could put them on again. How shall I put it on? That is: with what shame, with what modesty, with what memory at the last? For the habit of good has lost the use of the old depravity.

27.

Audiens anima, Aperi mihi, caput roris plenum habens, hoc est, tentationibus saecularibus, subito turbata, et quasi surrectura quae surgere jubebatur, ait cum aloem redoleret et myrrham insigne sepulturae: Exui tunicam meam, quomodo induam illam: lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo illos? Veretur enim ne iterum in tentationes resurgat, ne iterum in culpam redeat atque peccatum, et egressus suos virtutumque profectus terrenis incipiat vestigiis inquinare. 1924B

27.

The soul, hearing Open to me, having a head full of dew — that is, of worldly temptations — suddenly troubled, and as one about to rise who was bidden to rise, says, while she gave off the scent of aloes and myrrh, the badge of burial: I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on: I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? For she fears lest she rise again into temptations, lest she return again into fault and sin, and begin to defile her goings-out and the advances of her virtues with earthly footprints. 1924B

28.

Certe hoc modo etiam perfectionem virtutis suae indicat, quae tantam Christi meruerit charitatem; ut ad eam veniat, et pulset januam ejus, et veniat cum Patre, et coenet cum eadem anima, et ipsa cum eo, sicut in Apocalypsi dixit Joannes apostolus (Apoc. III, 20). Etenim cum in superioribus audisset: Ades huc a Libano; et cum adverteret in carne se Christo adesse non posse, sed tunc adesse si adesset in spiritu, conformans se ad ejus voluntatem, ut esset et ipsa conformis imagini Christi; jam non sentit carnis exuvias, jam quasi spiritus exuens se corporis conjunctione, jam quasi oblita, et quae si velit, copulae illius meminisse non possit, ait: Exui tunicam meam, quomodo induam eam? Exui enim tunicam illam pelliceam, quam acceperunt Adam et Eva post 1924Cculpam, tunicam corruptelae, tunicam passionum; Quomodo induam illam? Non requirit ut induat: sed ita significat abjectam, ut jam indumento sibi esse non posset.

28.

Assuredly in this way also she declares the perfection of her virtue, in that she has deserved so great a charity of Christ; that he comes to her, and knocks at her door, and comes with the Father, and sups with that same soul, and she with him, as John the apostle said in the Apocalypse (Apoc. III, 20). For indeed, when she had heard in the passages above: Be here from Lebanon; [Vulg. Veni de Libano, sponsa, veni de Libano, "Come from Lebanon, my bride, come from Lebanon"] and when she perceived that she could not be present to Christ in the flesh, but was then present if she were present in the spirit, conforming herself to his will, that she too might be conformed to the image of Christ; now she does not feel the spoils of the flesh, now, as it were a spirit stripping itself of the conjunction of the body, now as one forgetful, and one who, if she wished, could not remember that coupling, she says: I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? For I have put off that garment of skins which Adam and Eve received after 1924Ctheir fault, the tunic of corruption, the tunic of the passions; How shall I put it on? She does not ask to put it on: but so signifies it cast away, that now it could not be a garment for her.

29.

(Vers. 3.) Lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo illos? Hoc est, vestigia mea lavi, dum egrederer, ac me elevarem de corporis contubernio, de illa connexione et familiaritate carnalis amplexus: quomodo inquinabo ea, ut in corporis claustrum, et illum tenebrosum passionum ejus carcerem revertar? Sic passiones sepelimus, si crucem hujus corporis non resolvamus; si chirographum peccati quod deletum est, in cruce Christi non rescribamus, si veteris hominis amictum quem exuimus, non induamus. Scriptum est enim in Canticis: Exui tunicam 1924Dmeam, quomodo induam eam? lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo eos? Mortificata igitur sunt corporis membra, cur ejus pullulant vitia? Ideo non praevaluit lex, quia carnem non mortificavit: ideo quasi umbra praeteriit, quia non decoloravit: ideoque obumbravit nos a sole justitiae, quia coacervavit crimina.

29.

(Vers. 3.) I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? That is: I have washed my footsteps, while I was going out, and lifting myself from the fellowship of the body, from that connection and familiarity of the carnal embrace: how shall I defile them, so as to return into the enclosure of the body and that dark prison of its passions? So do we bury the passions, if we do not loose the cross of this body; if we do not write again, upon the cross of Christ, the bond of sin which has been blotted out; if we do not put on the garment of the old man which we have put off. For it is written in the Canticles: I have put off my tunic, 1924Dhow shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? The members of the body therefore have been mortified: why do its vices sprout up? Therefore the law did not prevail, because it did not mortify the flesh: therefore it passed by like a shadow, because it did not discolor: and therefore it overshadowed us from the sun of justice, because it heaped up crimes.

30.

Aliquando nudam esse, virtutis indicium est, quia scriptum est: Exui tunicam meam, quomodo induam eam? lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo eos? Quam igitur tunicam exuerit, et non invenerit quemadmodum 1925Ainduat eam, de Scripturis si possumus, revelemus. Est enim quaedam tunica corporalis, sunt cupiditatum quaedam intexta velamina; et ideo interdum melius est nudum esse corpore, quam corde velatum. Unde etiam Paulus nos admonet, 1595 exspoliari multo melius esse quam supervestiri, dicens alibi: Exspoliantes vos veterem hominem cum actibus suis, induite novum qui renovatur in agnitionem, secundum imaginem ejus qui creavit eum. Ergo quae exspoliata est, et pedes lotos habet, nescit quemadmodum inquinari non possit; obliviscitur enim per gratiam, quod hauserat per naturam.

30.

Sometimes to be naked is a sign of virtue, because it is written: I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? What tunic therefore she has put off, and how she has not found in what manner 1925Ato put it on, let us disclose from the Scriptures if we can. For there is a certain bodily tunic, there are certain woven veils of desires; and therefore it is sometimes better to be naked in body than veiled in heart. Whence Paul too admonishes us that it is 1595 much better to be stripped than to be clothed over, saying elsewhere: Stripping yourselves of the old man with his acts, put on the new, who is renewed unto knowledge, according to the image of him who created him. [Vulg. et induentes novum, a participle, "and putting on the new"] Therefore she who has been stripped, and has her feet washed, knows not how she may not be defiled; for by grace she forgets what she had drawn in by nature.

31.

Lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo illos? Didicisti in Evangelio, quod pedes lavare fidei mysterium, humilitatis insigne sit, juxta quod scriptum 1925Best: Si ego lavi pedes vestros Dominus et magister, quanto magis vos debetis alter alterius pedes lavare (Joan. XIII, 14)! Sed hoc ad humilitatem pertinet. Quantum vero ad mysterium, pedes debet lavare suos, qui vult partem habere cum Christo: Si enim non lavero, inquit, tibi pedes, non habebis partem mecum (Ibid. 8). Cum hoc Petro dicitur, quid de nobis censetur? Qui lavit igitur pedes, non necesse habet iterum lavare; et ideo caveat ne inquinet eos.

31.

I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? Thou hast learned in the Gospel that to wash the feet is a mystery of faith, a badge of humility, according to what is written: 1925BIf I, the Lord and master, have washed your feet, how much more ought you to wash one another's feet (Joan. XIII, 14)! [Vulg. et vos debetis alter alterius lavare pedes, "you also ought to wash one another's feet"] But this pertains to humility. As to the mystery, however, he ought to wash his feet who wishes to have part with Christ: For if I shall not wash, he says, thy feet, thou shalt not have part with me (Ibid. 8). [Vulg. Si non lavero te, "If I wash thee not"] When this is said to Peter, what is judged of us? He therefore who has washed his feet has no need to wash again; and therefore let him beware lest he defile them.

32.

Et bene sancta Ecclesia dicit: Lavi pedes meos; non dicit, quomodo iterum lavabo eos, sed quomodo iterum inquinabo eos? Quasi oblita maculae veteris, oblita contagii, admonet quomodo in ministerio corporali spirituale debeamus actuum nostrorum diluere vestigium. Itaque pedes cum semel 1925Claveris aeterni fontis irriguo, et mysterii mundaveris sacramento, cave iterum corporeae cupiditatis illuviem, ne lutulenti actus terrenis sordibus inquinentur. Isti sunt pedes quos David lavit in spiritu, qui te docet quemadmodum eos inquinare non possis, dicens: Stantes erant pedes nostri, in atriis tuis, Hierusalem (Psal. CXXI, 2). Utique hic non corporis, sed animi intellige pedes; quomodo enim terrenus homo in coelo haberet corporis pedes? Hierusalem, sicut Paulus te docuit, in coelo est: et idem te docuit quemadmodum in coelo stare possis, cum dicit: Nostra autem conversatio in coelis est (Philip. III, 20). Conversatio morum, conversatio factorum, conversatio fidei.

32.

And well does holy Church say: I have washed my feet; she does not say, how shall I wash them again, but how shall I defile them again? As one forgetful of the old stain, forgetful of the contagion, she admonishes how in a bodily ministry we ought to wash away the spiritual trace of our acts. And so, when once thou hast washed thy feet 1925Cwith the watering of the eternal fountain, and hast cleansed them by the sacrament of the mystery, beware again of the filth of bodily desire, lest thy miry acts be defiled with earthly squalor. These are the feet which David washed in the spirit, who teaches thee how thou canst not defile them, saying: Our feet were standing in thy courts, O Jerusalem (Psal. CXXI, 2). Assuredly understand here the feet not of the body but of the mind; for how should an earthly man have the feet of the body in heaven? Jerusalem, as Paul has taught thee, is in heaven: and the same has taught thee how thou canst stand in heaven, when he says: But our conversation is in heaven (Philip. III, 20). The conversation of manners, the conversation of deeds, the conversation of faith.

33.

Feci judicium et justitiam, non tradas me 1925Dcalumniantibus me. Ubi justitia, ibi misericordia. Misericordia a peccato liberat. Quomodo ergo peccatoribus trador? Simile est illud in Canticis: Exui tunicam meam, quomodo induam eam? Lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo eos? Exui tunicam peccatoris, velamenque terreni; cur quasi peccator atque terrenus adjudicor? Lavi pedes meos, ne qua peccati sors posset adhaerere vestigio; cur das in me delinquentibus potestatem?

33.

I have done judgment and justice, deliver me not 1925Dto them that slander me. Where justice is, there is mercy. Mercy frees from sin. How therefore am I delivered to sinners? Like to this is that in the Canticles: I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? I have put off the tunic of the sinner, and the veil of the earthly man; why am I adjudged as though a sinner and an earthly man? I have washed my feet, that no lot of sin might cleave to my footstep; why dost thou give power over me to them that offend?

34.

Hieremias in suis Threnis ait: Defecit iniquitas 1926Atua, filia Sion, non adjiciet te expellere adhuc: visitavit iniquitates tuas, filia Edom, revelavit super peccata tua (Thren. IV, 21). Advertis quoniam iniquitas non potuit sine Dei visitatione deficere, nec plena esse correctio, nisi per gratiam Domini Salvatoris? Quomodo autem deficiat iniquitas, audi dicentem Ecclesiam: Exui tunicam meam, quomodo induam eam? Lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo eos? Veteris igitur hominis vestimentum vitiis erroris intextum, in lavacri regeneratione depositum, nescit quomodo possit induere; studio enim correctionis inoleverat oblivio peccatorum. Tanta vis consummatae emendationis est, ut in quamdam pueritiae redeat spiritalis aetatem, quae vias erroris ignoret; crimen etiamsi velit, non possit admittere; quia desueverit 1926Busus nosse peccandi.

34.

Jeremiah in his Lamentations says: Thy iniquity hath failed, 1926AO daughter of Sion; he shall no more add to drive thee out: he hath visited thine iniquities, O daughter of Edom, he hath revealed upon thy sins (Thren. IV, 21). [Vulg. Completa est iniquitas tua… discooperuit peccata tua, "Thy iniquity is accomplished… he hath uncovered thy sins"] Dost thou observe that iniquity could not fail without God's visitation, nor the correction be full, save through the grace of the Lord and Savior? But how iniquity fails, hear the Church saying: I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? The garment of the old man, therefore, woven through with the vices of error, laid down in the regeneration of the laver, she knows not how she could put on; for by the zeal of correction a forgetfulness of sins had grown up in her. So great is the force of a completed amendment, that she returns to a certain age of spiritual childhood, which knows not the ways of error; even if she wished, she could not admit a crime, because use has grown unaccustomed 1926Bto knowing how to sin.

35.

(Vers. 4.) Frater meus misit manum suam de prospectu, et venter meus conturbatus est super eum. Exsurrexi ego aperire fratri meo. Manus meae distillaverunt myrrham, digiti mei myrrha pleni. Primum ergo tamquam a prospectu mittit manum suam, quando Deus ex operibus aestimatur, unde ait: Si mihi non creditis, operibus credite (Joan. X, 38). Deinde augetur amor, et intimis visceribus conceptus inolescit. Inde intelligibili utero, in quo est receptaculum Verbi, seminibus ejus infusis, plenitudinem ejus corporaliter inhabitantem haurire anima nostra desiderat; non enim turbatur uterus feminarum, nisi quae fuerint alvo graves. Surgit igitur anima ut aperiat Dei Verbo, sed dum se expandit atque 1926Caperit, ut opera mundi istius Verbi receptione mortificet, sicut ille qui ait: Mortificationem Domini Jesu in corpore nostro circumferimus (II Cor. IV, 19). Ergo dum aperit, transit Sponsus; vult enim semper quaeri, frequentius inveniri. Et si clausam invenerit januam, pulsat; et si per moram 1596 fuerit exclusus, recedit; sed cito redit, ei iterum pulsat, ut vel postea Sponsam suam inveniat praeparatam.

35.

(Vers. 4.) My brother put his hand in from the lookout, and my belly was troubled over him. I myself rose up to open to my brother. My hands dropped myrrh, my fingers were full of myrrh. [Vulg. Dilectus meus misit manum suam per foramen, et venter meus intremuit ad tactum ejus, "My beloved put his hand through the opening, and my belly trembled at his touch"] First therefore he puts in his hand as though from a lookout, when God is estimated from his works, whence he says: If ye believe not me, believe the works (Joan. X, 38). [Vulg. et si mihi non vultis credere, "and if you will not believe me"] Then love is increased, and, conceived in the innermost bowels, grows up. Thereupon, his seeds having been poured into the intelligible womb in which is the receptacle of the Word, our soul desires to drink in his fullness dwelling bodily; for the womb of women is not troubled, save the wombs of those who have been heavy in the belly. The soul therefore rises to open to the Word of God, but while she spreads herself out and 1926Copens, that she may mortify the works of this world by the reception of the Word, like him who says: We carry about the mortification of the Lord Jesus in our body (II Cor. IV, 19). Therefore, while she opens, the Bridegroom passes by; for he wills always to be sought, and more frequently to be found. And if he shall have found the door shut, he knocks; and if through delay 1596 he has been shut out, he withdraws; but quickly he returns, to him he knocks again, that at least afterwards he may find his Bride prepared.

36.

Potest quidem et sic accipi, Frater meus transivit: sicut legimus quod penetravit dilectae intima medullarum, quemadmodum ad Mariam dictum est: Et tuam ipsius animam pertransibit gladius, ut revelentur multorum cordium cogitationes (Luc. II, 35). Denique addidit Sponsa, animam suam exisse in verbo ejus, quod fit quando anima peregrinatur a 1926Dcorpore, et Deo praesens est. Audiens haec Sponsus misit operationem suam Verbum, tamquam per cavernam, non adhuc facie ad faciem: misit tamquam manum, Et venter, inquit, meus conturbatus est super eum. Quisquis sic vivit, potest dicere:

36.

It can indeed be taken thus also, My brother passed by: as we read that he penetrated the inmost marrow of the beloved, in the manner in which it was said to Mary: And a sword shall pierce through thy own soul, that the thoughts of many hearts may be revealed (Luc. II, 35). [Vulg. ex multis cordibus, "out of many hearts"] In short, the Bride added that her soul had gone out at his word, which happens when the soul is a pilgrim from 1926Dthe body and is present to God. Hearing these things the Bridegroom sent his operation, the Word, as through a cave, not yet face to face: he sent it as it were a hand, And my belly, she says, was troubled over him. Whosoever so lives can say:

37.

Frater meus misit manum suam per prospectum, et venter meus conturbatus est ad illum: surrexi aperire fratri meo. Bonum est ut ad adventum Domini interiora turbentur. Si ad angeli adventum Maria turbata est, quanto magis ad Christi nos turbamur 1927Aadventum! Influentibus quippe divinis, corporeus peregrinatur affectus, et usus ille exterioris hominis exolescit. Et tu turbare, tu festina. Festinantes illi agnum manducare praecipiuntur. Surge, aperi, ad januam Christus est, vestibula domus tuae pulsat: si aperueris, introibit, et introibit cum Patre. Nec solum cum ingressus fuerit, mercedem relinquit: sed etiam priusquam ingrediatur, mercedem praemittit. Adhuc anima turbatur, adhuc parietes domus suae palpat, adhuc ostium quaerit ubi Christus est, adhuc solvit vinculum carnis et corporis claustrum, adhuc Christus foris pulsat.

37.

My brother put his hand through the lookout, and my belly was troubled at him: I rose to open to my brother. It is good that at the coming of the Lord the inward parts be troubled. If Mary was troubled at the coming of the angel, how much more are we troubled at the coming 1927Aof Christ! For when the things of God flow in, bodily affection goes into exile, and that habit of the outward man grows out of use. Do thou also be troubled, do thou make haste. Those men are bidden to eat the lamb in haste. Rise, open: Christ is at the door, he knocks at the porches of thy house: if thou shalt open, he will enter in, and will enter in with the Father. Nor only when he has entered does he leave a reward: but even before he enters he sends the reward on ahead.
