Still the soul is troubled, still she gropes along the walls of her house, still she seeks the door where Christ is, still she looses the bond of the flesh and the enclosure of the body, still Christ knocks outside.

38.

Quae est fenestra nostra, nisi per quam opera Christi videmus, oculus scilicet animi et mentis obtutus? Et ideo, virgo, per fenestram tuam 1927BChristus introeat, Christus manum suam mittat: per fenestram tibi Verbi veniat, non corporis amor. Si igitur per fenestram tuam manum mittit Dei Verbum, vide quemadmodum fenestras tuas praepares, vide quemadmodum ab omni pulvere delictorum detergere debeas. Nihil tetrum habeant, nihil adulterinum fenestrae virginis. Procul stibium et caeteras affectati nugas decoris, procul adulterini amoris illecebras. Similis aurium clausura est, quibus non suspendenda onera, non figenda vulnera, sed unus ornatus est, audire quod prosit.

38.

What is our window, but that through which we see the works of Christ, namely the eye of the soul and the gaze of the mind? And therefore, O virgin, let Christ come in through thy window, 1927Blet Christ put in his hand: let the love of the Word come to thee through the window, not the love of the body. If therefore the Word of God puts in his hand through thy window, see how thou preparest thy windows, see how thou oughtest to wipe them clean from every dust of offenses. Let the windows of a virgin have nothing foul, nothing adulterate. Far off be the antimony and the other trifles of an affected comeliness, far off the enticements of an adulterate love. Like to this is the closing of the ears, on which no burdens are to be hung, no wounds to be fixed, but there is one adornment: to hear what profits.

39.

(Vers. 6.) Aperui, inquit, fratri meo; frater meus transivit. Quomodo transivit, id est, mentis interna penetravit, sicut dictum est Mariae: Et tuam ipsius animam pertransibit gladius. Vivum enim Dei 1927Cverbum, sicut gladius acutus, penetrans corporalium repagula cogitationum, cordis interna rimatur. In cubili ergo tuo et nocturno in tempore constituta, semper meditare Christum, et ejus adventum omnibus sperato momentis. Si tardare tibi videtur, exsurge. Tardare videtur, cum diu dormis: tardare videtur, cum ab oratione vacas: tardare videtur, cum vocem non excitas psalmis. Primitias vigiliarum tuarum Christo dicato, primitias actuum tuorum Christo immola.

39.

(Vers. 6.) I opened, she says, to my brother; my brother passed by. How he passed by: that is, he penetrated the inward parts of the mind, as it was said to Mary: And a sword shall pierce through thy own soul. For the living Word of God, 1927Clike a sharp sword, penetrating the bars of bodily thoughts, searches out the inward parts of the heart. Set therefore in thy chamber and in the time of night, meditate always upon Christ, and hope for his coming at every moment. If he seems to thee to delay, rise up. He seems to delay when thou sleepest long: he seems to delay when thou art idle from prayer: he seems to delay when thou dost not lift up thy voice in psalms. Dedicate to Christ the firstfruits of thy watchings, offer to Christ the firstfruits of thy acts.

40.

(Vers. 5.) Surrexi ego aperire fratri meo; manus meae distillaverunt myrrham, digiti mei myrrha pleni sunt super manus clausurae. Quid hoc significet, consideremus. Primum operibus suis videtur, ut dixi, Deus verbum tamquam per cavernam, non plene 1927Datque perfecte: deinde augetur amor et conceptus adolescit, atque ex seminibus ejus quae quodam utero intelligibili susceperit anima, totam plenitudinem divinitatis ejus habitantem in eo corporaliter, ut legimus, videre desiderat. Surrexit ut propius illud Dei Verbum videret, et in hoc ipso processus ejus significatur, quod surrexit per vigorem atque virtutem; praesentia enim Verbi hausit animae virtutem, sicut praesentia Mariae cum utero gravis esset, erudivit Joannem in utero constitutum: adeo ut exsiliret ex utero et exsultaret, Domini praesentiam recognoscens. 1928A

40.

(Vers. 5.) I myself rose up to open to my brother; my hands dropped myrrh, my fingers are full of myrrh upon the hands of the fastening. [Vulg. digiti mei pleni myrrha probatissima, "my fingers full of the choicest myrrh"] Let us consider what this signifies. First, as I have said, God the Word is seen in his own works as through a cave, not fully 1927Dand perfectly: then love is increased and the conception grows, and out of his seeds, which the soul has received in a certain intelligible womb, she desires to see the whole fullness of his divinity dwelling in him bodily, as we read. She rose that she might see that Word of God more closely, and in this very thing her advance is signified, that she rose by vigor and strength; for the presence of the Word drew forth the strength of the soul, as the presence of Mary, when she was heavy with the womb, instructed John set within the womb: so far that he leapt out of the womb and exulted, recognizing the presence of the Lord. 1928A

41.

Surrexit aperire, et opera ejus et facta mortificata sunt mundo. Talis enim debet esse anima, quae Verbum est receptura, ut moriatur mundo, et consepeliatur in Christo; sic enim Christus invenitur, et tale sibi quaerit hospitium. Deinde ipsa ministeria operationum, id est, manus et digiti, quibus comprehenditur Christus, mortificantur: quos digitos velut eminentia factorum nostrorum opera 1597 possumus aestimare. Itaque velut e complexu suo cum jam extenderet intelligibiles manus suas et digitos, ut comprehenderet Verbum, transisse sibi illud, non tamen adhuc pertransisse anima pia dicit. Et hunc processum habet, quando transit et pertransit animam Verbum Dei, quia scriptum est: Et tuam ipsius animam pertransibit gladius, ut revelentur multorum 1928Bcordium cogitationes (Luc. II, 35). Hinc adhuc transitur, non pertransitur; ut in Maria in posterioribus forte pertransitur, ubi tamquam signaculum ponitur in medio ejus Dominus Jesus.

41.

She rose to open, and her works and deeds were mortified to the world. For such ought the soul to be which is to receive the Word: that she die to the world, and be buried together in Christ; for thus is Christ found, and such a lodging does he seek for himself. Then the very ministries of her operations, that is, the hands and the fingers by which Christ is grasped, are mortified: which fingers we may reckon as the eminent works 1597 of our deeds. And so, as it were out of her own embrace, when she was already stretching out her intelligible hands and fingers to grasp the Word, the devout soul says that it passed by her, yet not that it had as yet passed through. And this advance it has, when the Word of God passes by and passes through the soul, because it is written: And a sword shall pierce through thy own soul, that the thoughts of many 1928Bhearts may be revealed (Luc. II, 35). Hence there is as yet a passing by, not a passing through; as in Mary, in the later things, there is perhaps a passing through, where the Lord Jesus is set as a seal in the midst of her.

42.

Denique statim alius profectus Verbi pertranseuntis, quia exivit anima in Verbo ejus; hoc est, Verbum ejus secuta exivit de corpore, elevans se de ejus habitaculo, et peregrinam se faciens ei, ut adesset Deo, et esset civis sanctorum. Simul enim et carnis domestici et Dei esse non possumus. Ergo in hoc loco exire significatur, ut dixi, anima, quando abducit se a corporis voluptatibus. Denique scriptum est: Exi de Babylone, fugiens a Chaldaeis (Esai. XLVIII, 20). Non utique ut regiones Babylonis Hebraeus fugiat, sed mores, ut prophetico admonetur 1928Calloquio: siquidem sint qui in Babylone sunt Hebraei, et de Babylone exisse moribus doceant. Nam et illi de quibus dicit propheta, quod sedebant super flumina Babylonis (Ps. CXXXVI, 1), sedebant quidem in regione Babylonis, sed in ejus non erant vitiis et confusione. Nam quomodo erant in illa confusione vitiorum, qui flebant gerentes poenitentiam, quod de arca devotionis ac fidei, et virtutis paternae meritis excidissent? Quae autem in verbo exit anima, Verbum requirit; unde et ista dum quaereret, incidit in custodes qui circumeunt civitatem.

(Vers. 7.) Invenerunt me custodes qui circumeunt civitatem, percusserunt me, et vulneraverunt me, et tulerunt pallium meum a me custodes murorum. Bene quidem quasi sponsa veniebat cum pallio, quo obnuberet 1928Dcaput suum, cum sponsus occurreret; sicut Rebecca, quae cognito quod Isaac sibi veniret obvius, descendit de camelo, et pallio se operuit: ita et haec anima, nuptialis vestis praemittebat insignia, ne forte rejiceretur quasi vestem non habens nuptialem; vel ut caput velaret propter angelos. Sed illi percusserunt eam, ut amplius probaretur; exercentur enim animae tentationibus. Tulerunt ei pallium, quaerentes si verum decorem nudae virtutis afferret, vel quia sine integumento quis in illam coelestem civitatem debeat introire, nulla deferens secum operimenta 1929Afucorum. Sunt etiam qui requirant, ne qua anima exuvias secum vehat carnalis illecebrae, et concupiscentiam corporalem. Nudatur pallio, cum ejus conscientia manifestatur. Sed est quae bene nudatur, cui licet imitari dicentem: Venit enim princeps hujus mundi, et in me invenit nihil (Joan. XIV, 30); quia certe in illo solo nihil invenit, qui peccatum non fecit.

42.

In short, straightway there is another advance of the Word passing through, in that the soul went out at his Word; that is, following his Word she went out of the body, lifting herself out of its habitation, and making herself a pilgrim to it, that she might be present to God, and be a fellow-citizen of the saints. For we cannot at once be of the household both of the flesh and of God. Therefore in this place the soul is signified to go out, as I have said, when she withdraws herself from the pleasures of the body. In short, it is written: Go out of Babylon, fleeing from the Chaldeans (Esai. XLVIII, 20). [Vulg. Egredimini de Babylone, fugite a Chaldaeis, both plural imperatives] Not indeed that the Hebrew should flee the regions of Babylon, but its manners, as he is admonished by the prophetic 1928Caddress: since indeed there are those who are Hebrews in Babylon, and teach by their manners that they have gone out of Babylon. For those too of whom the prophet says that they sat upon the rivers of Babylon (Ps. CXXXVI, 1) sat indeed in the region of Babylon, but were not in its vices and confusion. For how were they in that confusion of vices, who wept, doing penance, because they had fallen away from the ark of devotion and faith, and from the merits of their fathers' virtue? But the soul which goes out at the word seeks the Word; whence she also, while she was seeking, fell in with the guards who go about the city.

(Vers. 7.) The guards who go about the city found me, they struck me, and wounded me, and the guards of the walls took away my cloak from me. Well indeed did she come as a bride with a cloak, with which she might veil 1928Dher head when the bridegroom should meet her; as Rebecca, who, having learned that Isaac was coming to meet her, came down from the camel and covered herself with a cloak: so this soul too sent before her the badges of the nuptial garment, lest perchance she be cast out as not having a wedding garment; or that she might veil her head because of the angels. But they struck her, that she might be the more tested; for souls are exercised by temptations. They took her cloak from her, seeking whether she brought the true beauty of naked virtue, or because one ought to enter that heavenly city without a covering, bringing with him no coverings 1929Aof paint. There are also those who search out whether any soul carries with her the spoils of carnal enticement and bodily concupiscence. She is stripped of her cloak when her conscience is made manifest. But there is one who is well stripped, to whom it is permitted to imitate him who says: For the prince of this world comes, and in me he finds nothing (Joan. XIV, 30); [Vulg. et in me non habet quidquam, "and in me he hath not anything"] for assuredly he found nothing in him alone who did no sin.

44.

Beata et illa est in qua non gravia aut multa invenit, sed invenit in ea amictum fidei et sapientiae disciplinam. Itaque sine dispendio sui (quia et si volet quis, non potest auferre veram sapientiam) etsi adversarius obstrepat; ubi tamen vera innoxiae conversationis elucet integritas, transivit custodes, et filiabus illius coelestis civitatis admixta, Verbum 1929Brequirit; et quaerendo ejus in se excitat charitatem, et ubi Verbum quaerat, agnoscit. Non injuria tantummodo est non invenisse quem quaeras, sed etiam plerumque ubi non oportet, quaesisse vulnus est: quaesisse in domibus virorum qui sibi falso nomen doctorum assumunt, quaesisse procacius quam verecundius. Ergo caveamus exemplo illius, ne nos custodes inveniant qui circumeunt civitatem.

44.

Blessed also is she in whom he finds nothing grievous or manifold, but finds in her the garment of faith and the discipline of wisdom. And so without loss to herself (because even if one should wish it, he cannot take away true wisdom), though the adversary clamor; yet where the true integrity of a harmless conversation shines out, she has passed the guards, and, mingled with the daughters of that heavenly city, seeks the Word; 1929Band by seeking she stirs up his charity within herself, and learns where she may seek the Word. It is not only an injury not to have found him whom thou seekest, but very often to have sought where one ought not is itself a wound: to have sought in the houses of men who falsely take to themselves the name of teachers, to have sought more boldly than modestly. Therefore let us beware, by her example, lest the guards who go about the city find us.

45.

Invenerunt, inquit, me custodes qui circumeunt civitatem: percusserunt et vulneraverunt me, et tulerunt pallium meum a me custodes murorum. Non in se, filiae, sed in nobis vulneratur Ecclesia. Caveamus igitur ne lapsus noster, vulnus Ecclesiae fiat, ne quis a nobis pallium auferat, hoc est, amictum prudentiae, insigne patientiae, quo mollioris ambitio 1929Cvestis exuitur: Qui enim mollioribus vestibus induuntur, in domibus regum sunt (Matth. XI, 8). Nobis autem pallium dedit Christus, quo apostolos suos et corpus suum ipse vestivit, quod denique jubet te dare, si quis a te tunicam petit, ut dimittas ei et pallium (Matth. V, 40); id est, insigne philosophiae tuae tradas, et quasi amictu prudentiae 1598 tuae eum qui ante nudus fuerat, circumvestias.

45.

They found me, she says, the guards who go about the city: they struck and wounded me, and the guards of the walls took away my cloak from me. Not in herself, O daughters, but in us is the Church wounded. Let us therefore beware lest our fall become the Church's wound, lest anyone take from us the cloak, that is, the garment of prudence, the badge of patience, by which the ambition of a softer 1929Cgarment is put off: for They who are clothed in softer garments are in the houses of kings (Matth. XI, 8). [Vulg. qui mollibus vestiuntur, "they who are clothed in soft things"] But to us Christ has given the cloak with which he himself clothed his apostles and his own body, which in short he bids thee give, if any man asks thy tunic, that thou leave him the cloak also (Matth. V, 40); that is, that thou hand over the badge of thy philosophy, and clothe him about, as with the garment of thy prudence, 1598 who before was naked.

46.

Ibi igitur, filiae, Christum quaeramus, ubi quaerit Ecclesia, in montibus boni odoris, qui excelsorum sublimitate factorum, suavem vitae odorem pro meritorum verticibus exhalant. Ea quae Christum requirit, non debet esse vulgaris, non debet esse in foro, non in plateis, voce querula, gressu lubrica, facilis aditu, vilis aspectu. Terrenum tibi Apostolus consortium negat, et ultra naturae propemodum terminos, 1929Dalis ad coelum docet spiritalibus evolandum. Quae sursum sunt, inquit, sapite, non quae super terram (Colos. III, 2). Sed quia hoc impossibile erat in hoc corporis velut claustro reclusis, et quia defunctis corporibus, anima fertur ad superiora revolare, alligata dum vivimus, quadam nostrae lege naturae, ideo addidit: Mortui enim estis, et vita vestra abscondita est cum Christo in Deo (Ibid., 3). Si abscondita est cum Christo in Deo, non appareat mundo, Christus enim mortuus est mundo, vivit Deo. Vide nunc quemadmodum desiderari amet Christus, confabulationes 1930Anon amet. Aperuit virgo illa suas fores Dei Verbo: sed transivit, inquit, et exivit anima mea in verbo ejus (Vers. 6). Exivit a mundo, exivit a saeculo, remansit in Christo. Quaesivi, inquit, eum et non inveni eum. Amat enim Christus diu requiri.

46.

There therefore, O daughters, let us seek Christ where the Church seeks him, on the mountains of good odor, which by the sublimity of their lofty deeds breathe out the sweet odor of life according to the summits of their merits. She who seeks Christ ought not to be common, ought not to be in the market or in the streets, querulous of voice, slippery of step, easy of approach, cheap of aspect. The Apostle denies thee earthly fellowship, and teaches that beyond the bounds, almost, of nature 1929Dwe must fly up to heaven on spiritual wings. Mind the things that are above, he says, not the things that are upon the earth (Colos. III, 2). But because this was impossible for those shut up in this enclosure, as it were, of the body, and because when bodies are dead the soul is said to fly back to the things above, being bound by a certain law of our nature while we live, therefore he added: For you are dead, and your life is hidden with Christ in God (Ibid., 3). If it is hidden with Christ in God, let it not appear to the world, for Christ is dead to the world, and lives to God. See now how Christ loves to be desired, and loves not 1930Aidle talk. That virgin opened her doors to the Word of God: but he passed by, she says, and my soul went out at his word (Vers. 6). [Vulg. Anima mea liquefacta est, ut locutus est, "My soul melted when he spoke"] She went out from the world, she went out from the age, she remained in Christ. I sought him, she says, and I found him not. For Christ loves to be sought long.

47.

Invenerunt illam custodes murorum. Fortasse sunt et alii custodes, quos magis intelligere debeamus. Est enim civitas quae portas murorum clausas non habet, de qua dictum est: Et portae ejus non claudentur per diem (Apoc. XXI, 25); nox enim non erit amplius in ea, afferent gloriam et honorem gentes in illam. Illa est ergo civitas Hierusalem quae in coelo est, intra quam jam quasi perfecta et immaculata servaris; non enim intrat in eam omne commune. Communis castitas non est, communis pudicitia 1930Bnon est, quae in libro vitae scribitur.

47.

The guards of the walls found her. Perhaps there are also other guards, whom we ought rather to understand. For there is a city which does not have the gates of its walls shut, of which it is said: And the gates thereof shall not be shut by day (Apoc. XXI, 25); for night shall be no more in it, the nations shall bring glory and honor into it. That therefore is the city Jerusalem which is in heaven, within which thou art now kept as one perfect and unspotted; for no common thing enters into it. Chastity which is written in the book of life is not common, modesty 1930Bis not common.

48.

Si igitur invenimus civitatem, ingrediamur in eam, videamus lumen ejus, videamus muros, videamus fundamenta muralia, videamus etiam custodes murorum. Sed quomodo ingrediamur in eam? In hac civitate vita est, et una est via quae ducit ad vitam. Via ergo Christus est, ergo Christum sequamur. Sed ipsa in coelo est civitas; quomodo igitur ascendamus ad coelum? Docet Evangelista, qui dicit: Et eduxit me in spiritu in montem magnum et altum, et ostendit mihi civitatem sanctam Hierusalem descendentem de coelo (Apoc. 21, 10). Ascendamus ergo spiritu, quia caro ascendere ad eam non potest. Ascendamus nos interim in coelum, ut ad nos postea de coelo illa descendat, in qua lumen est simile 1930Clapidis pretiosissimi, ut lapidis jaspidis et crystalli habet murum magnum et altum.

48.

If therefore we have found the city, let us enter into it, let us see its light, let us see the walls, let us see the foundations of the walls, let us see also the guards of the walls. But how shall we enter into it? In this city is life, and there is one way which leads to life. The way therefore is Christ; let us therefore follow Christ. But that city is itself in heaven; how then shall we ascend to heaven? The Evangelist teaches, who says: And he led me forth in the spirit into a mountain great and high, and showed me the holy city Jerusalem coming down from heaven (Apoc. 21, 10). [Vulg. sustulit me in spiritu, "he took me up in the spirit"] Let us therefore ascend in the spirit, because the flesh cannot ascend to it. Let us ascend meanwhile into heaven, that afterwards it may come down from heaven to us: in which the light is like 1930Ca most precious stone, and as of the jasper stone and of crystal it has a wall great and high.

49.

Didicisti lumen, didicisti murum; disce portas, disce custodes. Habet, inquit, portas duodecim, et in portis angelos duodecim, in quibus duodecim tribuum filiorum Israel superscripta sunt nomina (Ibid., 12). In portis patriarcharum nomina continentur, in muro apostolorum; fundamenta enim civitatis, apostoli, et lapis angularis est Christus, in quo omnis structura consurgit. Deus extra, Deus intus, Deus ubique est: Habet enim, inquit, civitas majestatem Dei (Ibid., 11). Ergo et vos, sanctae virgines, et quicumque justi estis, et immaculatam animae geritis castitatem, cives sanctorum estis et domestici Dei. Sed tunc nobilitatem istam patriae possidebitis, si Christum intra civitatis istius septa quaeratis, ingressi 1930Dper fidem actusque pretiosos, patriarcharum clarificati lumine, fundati super apostolos, versantes inter angelos.

49.

Thou hast learned of the light, thou hast learned of the wall; learn of the gates, learn of the guards. It has, he says, twelve gates, and in the gates twelve angels, upon which the names of the twelve tribes of the children of Israel are written above (Ibid., 12). In the gates the names of the patriarchs are contained, in the wall those of the apostles; for the foundations of the city are the apostles, and the corner-stone is Christ, in whom the whole structure rises up. God is without, God is within, God is everywhere: For the city has, he says, the majesty of God (Ibid., 11). [Vulg. habentem claritatem Dei, "having the brightness of God"] Therefore you also, holy virgins, and whosoever of you are just, and bear the unspotted chastity of the soul, are fellow-citizens of the saints and of the household of God. But then shall you possess that nobility of your fatherland, if you seek Christ within the enclosures of that city, having entered 1930Dby faith and by precious acts, made bright by the light of the patriarchs, founded upon the apostles, dwelling among the angels.

50.

Quomodo igitur isti custodes angeli sunt, qui castae animae pallium tollunt? Aliud pallium virginum est, aliud adolescentulae circumforaneae. Illa quae in foro Christum requirit, pallium etiam quod habebat, amisit. Prudentia enim non in foro, non in plateis, sed in Ecclesia possidetur. Et fortasse ut cum ipsis quoque veniamus in gratiam, atque omnium misericordem Deum esse doceamus; quia et 1931Ailli Christum aliquando reperiunt, si tamen jugiter quaerant, pallium hoc amictus est corporis. Ergo qui quaesivit Christum in cubili, si tamen sic quaesivit, sicut ille qui dixit: Sic memor fui tui super stratum meum (Ps. LXII, 7); si quaesivit in noctibus, juxta quod scriptum est: In noctibus extollite manus vestras in sancta (Ps. CXXXII, 2): si quaesivit in civitate, in foro et in plateis, in civitate Dei nostri: in foro fortasse, 1599 in quo judex ille divini juris sedet, in plateis unde collecti sunt qui ad coenam Dominicam convenerunt; potest custodientibus civitatem Dei angelis, dum diu quaerit, occurrere. Quin etiam ex coelesti custodum natura, coelestem intelligere possumus civitatem, coeleste forum justitiae sempiternae, non viles plateas, sed fortasse in quibus 1931Bsuperfluere fons ille consuevit, de quo scriptum est: Superfluant tibi aquae de tuo fonte, et in plateis tuis diffundantur tuae aquae (Prov. V, 16). Qui sic ergo Christum requirit, ad angelos pervenit.

50.

How therefore are those guards angels, who take away the cloak of a chaste soul? The cloak of virgins is one thing, that of a girl who haunts the streets another. She who seeks Christ in the market has lost even the cloak she had. For prudence is possessed not in the market, not in the streets, but in the Church. And perhaps, that we may come into favor with them also, and may teach that God is merciful to all — since they too 1931Aat some time find Christ, if only they seek continually — this cloak is the garment of the body. Therefore he who has sought Christ in his chamber, if only he has so sought as he who said: So have I been mindful of thee upon my bed (Ps. LXII, 7); [Vulg. Si memor fui tui, "If I have been mindful of thee"] if he has sought him in the nights, according to what is written: In the nights lift up your hands to the holy places (Ps. CXXXII, 2): if he has sought him in the city, in the market and in the streets, in the city of our God: in the market perhaps 1599 in which that judge of divine right sits, in the streets from which were gathered those who came together to the Lord's supper — he can, while he seeks long, meet with the angels who keep the city of God. Nay more, from the heavenly nature of the guards we can understand the city to be heavenly, and the market a heavenly market of everlasting justice, and the streets not cheap streets, but perhaps those in which 1931Bthat fountain was wont to overflow, of which it is written: Let thy waters overflow from thy fountain, and let thy waters be poured out in thy streets (Prov. V, 16). [Vulg. Deriventur fontes tui foras, et in plateis aquas tuas divide, "Let thy fountains be conveyed abroad, and divide thy waters in the streets"] He therefore who so seeks Christ comes to the angels.

51.

Sed si bonis ad angelos meritis pervenitur: cur qui pervenerit, vulneratur? Est et gladius bonus, cujus gladii bonum vulnus. Vulnerat Dei verbum, sed non ulcerat. Est vulnus boni amoris, sunt vulnera charitatis; ideoque dixit: Vulneratae charitatis ego sum (Cant. II, 5). Quae perfecta est, vulnerata charitate est. Bona sunt igitur Verbi vulnera, bona sunt amantis vulnera: Utiliora enim vulnera amici, quam voluntaria oscula inimici (Prov. XXVII, 6). Vulneratae charitatis Rebecca est, quae relictis parentibus, migravit ad sponsum. Vulneratae charitatis 1931CRachel, quae zelavit sororem, amavit maritum; sorori enim quod abundaret filiis, ipsa sterilis adhuc invidebat, quia typum gerebat Ecclesiae, cui dicitur: Laetare sterilis quae non paris, erumpe et clama quae non parturis (Esai. 54, 1).

51.

But if it is by good merits that one comes to the angels, why is he who has come wounded? There is also a good sword, and the wound of that sword is good. The Word of God wounds, but does not ulcerate. There is a wound of good love, there are wounds of charity; and therefore she said: I am of wounded charity (Cant. II, 5). [Vulg. quia amore langueo, "for I languish with love"] She who is perfect is wounded with charity. Good therefore are the wounds of the Word, good are the wounds of the lover: For more useful are the wounds of a friend than the willing kisses of an enemy (Prov. XXVII, 6). [Vulg. Meliora sunt vulnera diligentis quam fraudulenta oscula odientis, "Better are the wounds of one that loves than the deceitful kisses of one that hates"] Of wounded charity is Rebecca, who, leaving her parents, journeyed to her bridegroom. Of wounded charity 1931Cis Rachel, who was jealous of her sister and loved her husband; for she, still barren, envied her sister in that she abounded in sons, because she bore the type of the Church, to whom it is said: Rejoice, thou barren that bearest not, break forth and cry, thou that travailest not (Esai. 54, 1). [Vulg. Lauda, sterilis, quae non paris; decanta laudem, et hinni, quae non pariebas, "Praise, O thou barren that bearest not; sing forth praise and cry out, thou that didst not bear"]

52.

Invenerunt ergo custodes et vulneraverunt eam, et tulerunt ei pallium, hoc est, actus corporalis involucrum sustulerunt, ut nuda mentis simplicitas quaereret Christum; quia nemo potest amictu vestitus philosophiae, in habitu scilicet sapientiae saecularis Christum videre. Et bene tollitur ei amictus philosophiae, ne quis eam per philosophiam depraedetur. Bene tollitur ei pallium, quae ad Christum propinquat, ut Deum visura, mundo corde gradiatur: Beati enim mundo corde, quia 1931Dipsi Deum videbunt (Matth. V, 8). Denique ubi mundavit cor, Verbum reperit, Deum vidit. Quaere ergo illum, virgo, immo quaeramus omnes; anima enim sexum non habet. Sed ideo fortasse femineum nomen accepit, quod eam violentior aestus corporis agit: ipsa autem impetum carnis et amoris sui molli quadam et blanda ratione demulcet. Ergo orationibus et obsecrationibus Deum invitare debemus, ut quasi bonus auster afflare dignetur. Verbique nobis auram coelestis aspiret: quae fructiferas arbores non turbare vento gravi, sed leni spiramine flatuque molli consuevit agitare. 1932A

52.

The guards therefore found her and wounded her, and took her cloak from her: that is, they carried off the wrapping of bodily action, that the naked simplicity of the mind might seek Christ; because no one clothed with the garment of philosophy, that is, in the habit of worldly wisdom, can see Christ. And well is the garment of philosophy taken from her, lest anyone despoil her through philosophy. Well is the cloak taken from her who draws near to Christ, that, being about to see God, she may walk with a clean heart: for Blessed are the clean of heart, for 1931Dthey shall see God (Matth. V, 8). In short, when she had cleansed her heart, she found the Word, she saw God. Seek him therefore, O virgin — nay rather, let us all seek him; for the soul has no sex. But perhaps it received a feminine name for this reason, that a more violent heat of the body drives it: while it soothes the onset of the flesh and of its own love by a certain soft and gentle reasoning. Therefore by prayers and supplications we ought to invite God, that he may deign to breathe upon us like a good south wind. And let him breathe upon us the breeze of the heavenly Word: which is wont to stir the fruit-bearing trees not with a heavy wind, but with a light breathing and a soft blast. 1932A

53.

Tulerunt mihi pallium meum. Bonus amictus aeternitas vitae, hoc pallium Sponsae voluerunt auferre custodes murorum, quo primus homo fuerat exutus; sed anima Deo devota quaesitum diu tenendo, et non dimittendo dilectum, pretioso charitatis divinae se vestivit involucro. Beati ergo qui tali pallio vestiuntur, et amictum ejusmodi legis observatione meruerunt; quia non sunt legis obliti, sed quae erant legis operati sunt.

53.

They took away my cloak from me. A good garment is the eternity of life; this cloak of the Bride the guards of the walls wished to take away, of which the first man had been stripped; but the soul devoted to God, by holding fast the long-sought and not letting go her beloved, clothed herself with the precious wrapping of divine charity. Blessed therefore are they who are clothed with such a cloak, and have deserved a garment of this kind by the observance of the law; because they have not been forgetful of the law, but have done the things that were of the law.

54.

(Vers. 8.) Adjuro vos, filiae Hierusalem, si inveneritis dilectum meum, ut annuntietis ei, quia amore langueo. Quis est autem hic animae languor? Morale historiae veteris facit nos meminisse debere, quae est de sancto Moyse qui rogavit Deum ut se demonstraret ei, et facie ad faciem eum videret. Noverat utique 1932Bsanctus vates Domini, quod invisibilem Deum facie ad faciem videre non posset. Sed devotio sancta mensuram supergreditur, et putavit Deo etiam hoc esse possibile, ut corporeis oculis faceret id quod est incorporeum comprehendi. Non reprehensibilis hic error, sed etiam grata cupiditas atque inexplebilis, quod Dominum suum velut manu tenere, et obtutu oculorum videre cupiebat. Sciebat enim ad imaginem et similitudinem Dei factum esse hominem; quando enim electus a Domino Deo ad populum liberandum, repletus est spiritu sapientiae, viderat angelum illum, et faciem ejus in gloria, denique fulgente ejus lumine pavefactus est, et vidit rubum uri et non exuri. Visu coepit fulgorem, quem miratus accessit; cupiditate et gratia provocatus, ut diligentius 1932Cfulgorem illum in rubo consideraret. Itaque si tantum accepit ardorem, tantam subiit cupiditatem, cum angelum vidisset in flamma ignis de rubo, ut licet pavore apprehensus considerare non auderet, considerare tamen vellet: quanto magis vultum Domini corporaliter videre desiderabat, dicens intra se: Vultum illum plenum lucis, 1600 plenum gratiae, plenum virtutis, plenum divinitatis, quem esse dicam? Non possum plus dicere vel sentire de Deo; cum enim consummaverit homo, tunc incipit; et cum finierit, tunc aporiabitur, ut scriptum est, quia incomprehensibilis est Dei aeterna majestas (Eccles. XVIII, 6).

54.

(Vers. 8.) I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you announce to him that I languish with love. [Vulg. ut nuntietis ei, "that you tell him"] But what is this languor of the soul? The moral sense makes it needful that we remember the old history, which is of holy Moses, who asked God to show himself to him, and that he might see him face to face. Assuredly 1932Bthe holy seer of the Lord knew that he could not see the invisible God face to face. But holy devotion oversteps measure, and he thought that even this was possible to God, that he should cause that which is incorporeal to be comprehended by bodily eyes. This error is not blameworthy, but rather a pleasing and insatiable desire, in that he longed to hold his Lord as it were with the hand, and to see him with the gaze of the eyes. For he knew that man had been made to the image and likeness of God; for when, chosen by the Lord God to free the people, he was filled with the spirit of wisdom, he had seen that angel, and his face in glory; in short, he was made afraid by his shining light, and saw the bush burning and not burnt up. By sight he took in the brightness, and marvelling at it he drew near; provoked by desire and by grace, that he might more diligently 1932Clook upon that brightness in the bush. And so, if he received so great an ardor and underwent so great a desire when he had seen the angel in the flame of fire out of the bush, that, though seized with dread he dared not look, yet he wished to look: how much more did he desire to see the countenance of the Lord bodily, saying within himself: That countenance full of light, 1600 full of grace, full of virtue, full of divinity — what shall I say it is? I can say or feel no more of God; for when a man has finished, then he begins; and when he has made an end, then shall he be at a loss, as it is written, because the eternal majesty of God is incomprehensible (Eccles. XVIII, 6).

55.

Irritus quidem erat postulationis effectus, sed affectus probabilis servi; quia ultra naturam suam devotione progressus de Domini angelis aestimabat, 1932Dquantum de Domini vultu plus desideraret, quo cupiditatem suam gestiret explere. Noverat in ipso homine aliam post mortem futuram gloriam, aliam claritatem: Sicut enim stella a stella differt in claritate, sic et resurrectio mortuorum; quae et si seminatur in corruptione, surget tamen in incorruptione, surget in gloria, surget in virtute, surget corpus spiritale (I Cor. XV, 41-44). Qui ergo haec scire poterat, merito praesumebat ut vultum Domini videre cuperet, cum esset obita corporis morte visurus. Talem certe etiam formam ceperat, talem Dominus jam probabat, ut non distaret ab angelis coelestis minister et exsecutor oraculi. Et ideo 1933Acum sciret, Quia angeli pusillorum quotidie vident faciem Patris qui in coelis est (Matth. XVIII, 10), putavit quod jam eam videre posset et deberet, tamquam oblitus corporis, et carne deposita; sicut ille qui in corpore adhuc positus, cum raperetur in paradisum, ultra in corpore, sive extra corpus raptus esset ad tertium coelum se nescire dixit (II Cor. XII, 3). Sancti enim etiam cum gerunt corpus, non sunt tamen in carne, sed in spiritu; quoniam qui in cupiditate et conversatione carnali sunt, Deo placere non possunt. Sancti autem conversatione in carne non sunt, sed in coelo.

55.

Vain indeed was the effect of his petition, but the affection of the servant was worthy of approval; because, having advanced by devotion beyond his own nature, he estimated from the Lord's angels 1932Dhow much more he might desire of the Lord's countenance, whereby he longed to fill up his desire. He knew that in man himself there would be after death one glory and another brightness: For as star differs from star in brightness, so also is the resurrection of the dead; which, even if it is sown in corruption, yet shall rise in incorruption, shall rise in glory, shall rise in power, shall rise a spiritual body (I Cor. XV, 41-44). He therefore who could know these things rightly presumed to desire to see the countenance of the Lord, since he would see it when the death of the body had been undergone. Such a form assuredly had he taken on, and such did the Lord already approve, that the heavenly minister and executor of the oracle stood no distance from the angels. And therefore, 1933Asince he knew That the angels of the little ones daily see the face of the Father who is in heaven (Matth. XVIII, 10), [Vulg. angeli eorum in caelis semper vident faciem Patris mei, "their angels in heaven always see the face of my Father"] he thought that he could and ought already to see it, as one forgetful of the body and having laid the flesh aside; like him who, being still set in the body, when he was caught up into paradise, said that he knew no further whether he had been caught up to the third heaven in the body or outside the body (II Cor. XII, 3). For the saints, even when they bear a body, are nevertheless not in the flesh but in the spirit; since they who are in carnal desire and conversation cannot please God. But the saints are not in the flesh by their conversation, but in heaven.

56.

Ex his ergo Sponsae languentis comprehendamus affectum. Videre Dei vultum quasi innocens, velut quodam vultu conscientiae desiderabat, aperire 1933Bei faciem mentis internae, et cognosci plenius gestiebat. Amor impatiens, diu noctuque meretricias fores pulsans, si diutius potiendi desideria differuntur, ipsa deficit exspectatione, dum sperat: in quo utique non finis amoris, sed incrementum est. Et quidquid est desiderabile, si non contingat desideranti, deficit in illud, et quasi ipsam deponit animam qui desiderat; si tamen spes proprior assurgat, dat vires spes proxima; si autem absentia sit dilecti, eo ipso quo absentem desiderat, quae concupivit, animae suae patitur defectionem. Itaque quanto longius est illud quod desideratur, tanto magis deficit qui desiderat. Id ergo deficere est, in id unumquemque totis studiis migrare, quod diligat; illud cogitat, illi adhaeret, illud personat, quod receperit diligendum, in id 1933Cquadam animae defectione transfunditur. Ut si mater filii exspectet praesentiam, quemadmodum exspectabat Tobiae uxor filium peregrinantem; deficiens a desiderio, et in angustiis constituta, et tamquam resoluta videbatur (Tob. X, 3 et seq.). Quid enim aliud nisi defectum quemdam ejus verba significant? Sed quo magis lassatur affectus, eo amplius amor crescit; et quo diutius abest qui desiderat, eo exspectantis desideria majore quadam vi amoris ignescunt. Caro deficit, sed cupiditas alitur et augetur.

56.

From these things therefore let us comprehend the affection of the languishing Bride. She desired to see the countenance of God as one innocent, as it were with a certain countenance of conscience, and she longed to open 1933Bto him the face of her inward mind, and to be more fully known. Impatient love, knocking day and night at harlots' doors, if the desires of possessing are put off longer, itself faints in the waiting while it hopes: in which assuredly there is not an end of love, but an increase. And whatever is desirable, if it does not fall to him who desires it, he faints toward it, and he who desires lays down, as it were, his very soul; yet if a nearer hope rises up, the hope close at hand gives strength; but if there be an absence of the beloved, by that very thing whereby she desires him absent, she who has longed suffers a fainting of her own soul. And so, the further off that is which is desired, the more does he faint who desires it. This therefore is to faint: that every man passes over with all his zeal into that which he loves; he thinks it, he cleaves to it, he sounds it forth, that which he has received to be loved; into it he is poured over by a certain 1933Cfainting of the soul. As if a mother should await the presence of a son, in the way that the wife of Tobias awaited her travelling son; fainting from desire, and set in straits, she seemed as one undone (Tob. X, 3 et seq.). For what else do her words signify but a certain fainting? But the more the affection is wearied, the more abundantly does love grow; and the longer he who desires is absent, the more do the desires of her who waits take fire with a certain greater force of love. The flesh faints, but the desire is nourished and increased.

57.

Itaque sancta anima nescit aliud desiderare quam Sponsum, qui est Christus Jesus: illum concupiscit, illum desiderat, in illum totis intendit viribus, illum gremio mentis fovet, illi se aperit et effundit, et hoc solum veretur, ne illum possit amittere. 1933DItaque quanto majore desiderio excitata fuerit anima, cupiens adhaerere Deo salutari suo, tanto magis deficit. Ergo ista defectio imminutionem quidem fragilitatis, sed assumptionem virtutis operatur. Concupiscebat, desiderabat, deficiebat, toto dissolvebatur affectu, ut totus illius fieret quem desiderabat, sicut etiam David ipse dicit: Effundo in conspectu ejus orationem meam, et tribulationem meam ante ipsum pronuntio, in deficiendo ex me spiritum 1934Ameum (Ps. CXLI, 3 et 4). Defecit enim ejus spiritus; immo ab eo defecit spiritus, qui seipsum negat, ut adhaereat Christo. Itaque quasi amoris impatiens, circumcursabat, et quaerebat ubique Dei Verbum, quia vulnerata, quia nuda; et quaerebat adminiculum alicujus a quo Christus rogaretur ut veniret. Vide aestuantem, vide desiderantem: Adjuro, inquit, vos, filiae Hierusalem, etc.; deinde filiabus coelestis illius civitatis admixta, Verbum requirit, et quaerendo, et speciem ejus contemplando, ejus in se excitat charitatem. 1601

57.

And so the holy soul knows not how to desire anything other than the Bridegroom, who is Christ Jesus: him she longs for, him she desires, toward him she strains with all her powers, him she cherishes in the bosom of her mind, to him she opens and pours out herself, and this alone she fears, that she might lose him. 1933DAnd so, the greater the desire with which the soul has been roused, longing to cleave to God her Savior, so much the more does she faint. Therefore this fainting works indeed a diminution of frailty, but a taking-on of strength. She longed, she desired, she fainted, she was dissolved in her whole affection, that she might become wholly his whom she desired, as David himself also says: I pour out my prayer in his sight, and I declare my tribulation before him, in the failing of my spirit 1934Afrom me (Ps. CXLI, 3 et 4). For his spirit failed; nay rather, the spirit failed from him who denies himself, that he may cleave to Christ. And so, as one impatient of love, she ran about, and sought everywhere the Word of God, because she was wounded, because she was naked; and she sought the support of someone by whom Christ might be asked to come. See her burning, see her desiring: I adjure you, she says, O daughters of Jerusalem, etc.; then, mingled with the daughters of that heavenly city, she seeks the Word, and by seeking and by contemplating his beauty, she stirs up his charity within herself. 1601

58.

(Vers. 10.) Frater, inquit, meus candidus et rubeus, electus ex millibus. Decet enim, virgo, ut plene noveris quem diligis, atque omne in eo et ingenitae divinitatis et assumptae mysterium 1934Bincorporationis agnoscas. Candidus ergo est merito, quia patris splendor: rubeus, quia partus est virginis. Color in eo fulget et rutilat utriusque naturae. Memento tamen antiquiora in eo divinitatis insignia, quam corporis sacramenta; quia non coepit a Virgine, sed qui erat venit in Virginem.

58.

(Vers. 10.) My brother, she says, is white and red, chosen out of thousands. [Vulg. Dilectus meus candidus et rubicundus, "My beloved is white and ruddy"] For it is fitting, O virgin, that thou shouldst fully know whom thou lovest, and shouldst acknowledge in him every mystery both of his unbegotten divinity and of the incorporation 1934Bhe assumed. He is therefore white by right, because he is the splendor of the Father: red, because he is the offspring of a virgin. The color of each nature shines and glows red in him. Yet remember that the badges of divinity in him are more ancient than the mysteries of the body; because he did not begin from the Virgin, but he who was came into the Virgin.

59.

Frater meus candidus et rubeus. Candidus claritate aeterna, rubeus specie coloris humani, quem sacramento incarnationis assumpsit. Meritoque et ipsum quod est rubeum, bene olet; quia caro Christi sine peccato est, quam perfidi contrectantes, manus suas inquinaverunt: sancti venerantes, pietatis odore flagrarunt.

59.

My brother is white and red. White with eternal brightness, red with the appearance of human color, which he took on by the mystery of the incarnation. And rightly does that very thing which is red smell sweet; because the flesh of Christ is without sin, which the faithless, handling it, defiled their own hands: while the saints, venerating it, burned with the odor of piety.

60.

Electus ex millibus. Decem millia, inquit Jacob, Ephraem et millia Manasse; id est, et Judaeorum 1934Cet gentium dominetur, et ex utroque populo acquirat sibi Ecclesiae plenitudinem. Ideoque dexteram suam super Ephraem posuit sanctus Jacob; eo quod legis, dicente Ecclesia in Canticis canticorum: Frater meus candidus et rubeus, electus ex decem millibus. Denique etiam David juvenculae Mariae auctorem ex cujus successione Christus est natus per Virginis partum, in decem millibus praedicabant: Saul autem in millibus, cum reverentiae gratiam circa regem magis praeponere debuissent.

60.

Chosen out of thousands. Ten thousands, says Jacob, of Ephraim and thousands of Manasseh; that is, let him have dominion over both the Jews 1934Cand the Gentiles, and out of both peoples acquire for himself the fullness of the Church. And therefore holy Jacob laid his right hand upon Ephraim; because thou readest, the Church saying in the Song of Songs: My brother is white and red, chosen out of ten thousands. [Vet. Lat. — Migne prints this same lemma as ex millibus, "out of thousands," at §§58–59 above, and ex decem millibus here; the plate is inconsistent with itself and is not harmonized] In short, they proclaimed even David — the author of the young Mary, from whose succession Christ was born by the Virgin's childbearing — in ten thousands: but Saul in thousands, though they ought rather to have set the favor of reverence toward the king first.

61.

(Vers. 11.) Caput ejus aurum Cephas. Quod significat stabilem eminentemque sapientiam. Corpus Christi Ecclesia est, hujus caput ut aurum, quia sapientia pretiosa sanctorum est, hoc est, viri justi atque prudentes. 1934D

61.

(Vers. 11.) His head is gold Cephas. [Vulg. aurum optimum, "the finest gold"] Which signifies a steadfast and eminent wisdom. The body of Christ is the Church, and the head of this is as gold, because the wisdom of the saints is precious, that is, men just and prudent. 1934D

62.

Crines ejus ut abietes, nigrae sicut corax. De hoc alibi dixit, Capillamentum tuum, ut grex tonsarum. Ideo capillamentum, quia virtus omnium sensuum in capite est: Oculi enim sapientis in capite ejus (Eccle. II, 14). Profunda igitur doctorum prudentia, quae potest ea quae obscura sunt revelare, et alta aperire sensuum. Et hujusmodi disputatores crines Ecclesiae sunt, sicut pulli corvorum quibus Dominus escam dat: sicut dedit sancto Jacob, et eum 1935Apavit a juventute sua. Hos alios ac profundos ubertate doctrinae pascit Dominus coelestibus sacramentis.

62.

His hairs are as fir trees, black as the raven. [Vulg. comae ejus sicut elatae palmarum, nigrae quasi corvus, "his locks as the branches of palm trees, black as a crow"] Of this he said elsewhere, Thy head of hair is as a flock of shorn ones. Therefore a head of hair, because the power of all the senses is in the head: for The eyes of the wise man are in his head (Eccle. II, 14). The deep prudence, therefore, of the teachers, which can reveal the things that are obscure and open the heights of the senses. And disputants of this kind are the hairs of the Church, like the young of the ravens to whom the Lord gives food: as he gave to holy Jacob, and fed him 1935Afrom his youth. These others, and deep ones, the Lord feeds with heavenly mysteries in the richness of doctrine.

63.

(Vers. 12.) Oculi quoque ejus sicut columbae. Oculi sunt viri videlicet spiritualibus ornati sensibus, qui ad videnda mysteria sunt acuti, et parati ad penetranda Scripturae secreta divinae, rationali lacte fulgentes, in quibus non sit aliqua doli maculosa confusio, sed simplicis affectus immaculata sinceritas. Ideo in aquarum abundantia lotas has columbas in lacte memoravit, dicens:

63.

(Vers. 12.) His eyes also are as doves. The eyes are men, that is to say, adorned with spiritual senses, who are keen for the seeing of mysteries, and ready for penetrating the secrets of divine Scripture, shining with rational milk, in whom there is no spotted confusion of guile, but the unspotted sincerity of a simple affection. Therefore he made mention of these doves as washed in milk amid an abundance of waters, saying:

64.

Oculi ejus sicut columbae super abundantiam aquarum, lotae in lacte, sedentis super plenitudinem, Baptizat in lacte Dominus, id est, in sinceritate. Et isti sunt qui vere baptizantur in lacte, qui sine dolo credunt, et puram fidem deferunt, ut immaculatam 1935Binduant gratiam. Ideo candida Sponsa ascendit ad Christum, quia in lacte baptizata est; ideo mirantur eam virtutes, dicentes: Quae est haec quae ascendit dealbata (Cant. VIII, 7)? Ante paululum dicebat, Nigra sum (Cant. I, ): nunc dealbata cernitur, et ascendit ad coelum, et innixa Dei Verbo, alta jam penetrat.

64.

His eyes are as doves upon the abundance of waters, washed in milk, of one sitting upon fullness, [Vulg. super rivulos aquarum, quae lacte sunt lotae, et resident juxta fluenta plenissima, "upon the little brooks of waters, which are washed with milk, and sit beside the plentiful streams"] The Lord baptizes in milk, that is, in sincerity. And these are they who are truly baptized in milk, who believe without guile, and bring a pure faith, that they may put on an unspotted 1935Bgrace. Therefore the Bride ascends white to Christ, because she has been baptized in milk; therefore the powers marvel at her, saying: Who is this that comes up made white (Cant. VIII, 7)? [Vulg. Cant. 8:5 Quae est ista quae ascendit de deserto, deliciis affluens, "Who is this that comes up from the desert, flowing with delights"] A little while before she was saying, I am black (Cant. I, ): now she is seen made white, and ascends to heaven, and, leaning upon the Word of God, now penetrates the heights.

65.

Nec immerito illic aquarum abundantia, ubi Christus, unde mens humana repleri cupiat. Has sitit aquas cervus, quas cum biberit, sitire non possit. Has aquas sitivit Propheta cum dixit: Sitivit in te anima mea (Psalm. XLI, 3). Sedet ergo Christus super abundantiam aquarum, et super plenitudinem, et ideo qui baptizat in aqua dicit: Et nos omnes de plenitudine ejus accepimus (Joan I, 16). Unde et oculus suppliciorum non alienus est ab Ecclesia; quia 1935Cetsi baptizabat Joannes in Enon, baptizabat juxta Salim, ubi erat aquarum abundantia, et duodecim fontes, et septuaginta palmarum arbores. Hos fontes habet Ecclesia, hoc est, in veteri Testamento duodecim patriarchas, in novo duodecim apostolos; ideoque dictum est: In Ecclesiis benedicite Dominum Deum de fontibus Israel (Psalm. LXVII, 27). His fontibus ante perfunditur, quicumque mysteria sacrosancta consequitur; isti enim fontes ex aeterno fonte manantes, toto orbe fluxerunt. Ubi isti fontes, ibi ascensio animarum. Denique Salim interpretati sunt, ipsum ascendentem; ille enim vere ascendit, qui propria peccata deponit. Hoc igitur 1602 verbo purificatoriae sanctificationis usus exprimitur.

65.

And not undeservedly is there an abundance of waters there where Christ is, from whom the human mind desires to be filled. These are the waters the stag thirsts for, which, when he has drunk them, he cannot thirst. These waters the Prophet thirsted for when he said: My soul hath thirsted for thee (Psalm. XLI, 3). [Vulg. Ps 41:3 Sitivit anima mea ad Deum fontem vivum, "My soul hath thirsted after God, the living fountain"; the printed wording is that of Ps 62:2] Christ therefore sits upon the abundance of waters, and upon the fullness, and therefore he who baptizes in water says: And we all have received of his fullness (Joan I, 16). Whence the eye of punishments also is not alien to the Church; because 1935Ceven if John was baptizing in Aenon, he was baptizing near Salim, where there was an abundance of waters, and twelve fountains, and seventy palm trees. These fountains the Church has, that is, in the Old Testament the twelve patriarchs, in the New the twelve apostles; and therefore it was said: In the churches bless ye the Lord God from the fountains of Israel (Psalm. LXVII, 27). With these fountains is he first washed over, whosoever attains the most sacred mysteries; for these fountains, flowing out from the eternal fountain, have streamed over the whole world. Where these fountains are, there is the ascent of souls. In short, they have interpreted Salim as "the very one ascending"; for he truly ascends who lays down his own sins. By this word, therefore, 1602 the use of a purifying sanctification is expressed.

66.

(Vers. 13.) Genae ejus sicut phialae aromatis, 1935Dgignentes unguentaria. Jam genas et caetera intelligimus animae esse virtutes, doctorumque diversitates: qui vel spiritalem menti alimoniam dispensatione sedula subministrent, vel praedicatione crucis Dominicae sicut Verbi quadam linea alligent audientem, vel modestia verecundi et juventutis flore gratissimi. Etsi a tactu pudore revocentur, redoleat tamen in his odor Christi: et sicut in genis quasi de capite sacerdotali descendit unguentum, sic et in his eluceat pulchritudo doctrinae.

66.

(Vers. 13.) His cheeks are as phials of spice, 1935Dbringing forth perfumeries. [Vulg. Genae illius sicut areolae aromatum, consitae a pigmentariis, "his cheeks are as beds of spices set by the perfumers"] Now we understand the cheeks and the rest to be the virtues of the soul, and the diversities of teachers: who either administer spiritual nourishment to the mind by a diligent stewardship, or bind the hearer, by the preaching of the Lord's cross, as it were with a certain line of the Word, or are most pleasing by the modesty of a bashful nature and by the bloom of youth. And even if they are called back from touch by shame, yet let the odor of Christ be fragrant in them: and as upon the cheeks the ointment comes down as it were from the priestly head, so also let the beauty of doctrine shine out in them.

67.

Labia ejus distillantia myrrham plenam. Per 1936Amyrrham enim passionis unguentum et resurrectionis gratia declaratur, quae redivivum vitae odorem suscitatis mortuorum visceribus infundit. Clausae sint igitur labiorum fores tuorum, et obseratum maneat vocis vestibulum, tunc fortasse reserandum, cum audieris Verbum Dei. Tunc tibi myrrha sudabit, tunc tibi baptismatis aspirabit gratia; ut ab elementis mundi commoriare, et cum Christo resurgas.

67.

His lips distilling full myrrh. [Vulg. Labia ejus lilia distillantia myrrham primam, "his lips are lilies distilling the first myrrh"] For by 1936Amyrrh is declared the ointment of the passion and the grace of the resurrection, which pours the revived odor of life into the raised-up inward parts of the dead. Let the doors of thy lips therefore be shut, and let the porch of thy voice remain barred, then perhaps to be unbolted when thou hast heard the Word of God. Then shall myrrh sweat forth for thee, then shall the grace of baptism breathe upon thee; that thou mayest die together with the elements of the world, and rise again with Christ.

68.

Labia ejus distillantia myrrham plenam. Nos autem otiosos nos putamus, si verbo tantummodo studere videamur: et pluris aestimamus eos qui operantur, quam eos qui studium cognoscendae veritatis exercent; dicunt enim plerique: Ecce homo et opera ejus: quasi qui verbo studeat, non operatur; cum 1936Bmagis opus istud quam caetera sint. Si enim opus justitia est, si opus temperantia, si opus fortitudo: utique opus est etiam sapientia; istae enim quatuor principales virtutes habentur. Nam si operatur Christus secundum quod justitia est, utique operatur secundum quod Verbum est. Et operabatur cum esset in principio apud Patrem. Denique per ipsum omnia facta sunt, ut scias operatorem omnium esse, et opus nostrum esse Christum Jesum. Etenim secundum quod Verbum est, Verbum inquirentibus, grande opus Verbum est. Unde cum Martha festinaret circa ministerium, Maria autem Verbum Domini audiret, ea quae audiebat ei quae ministrabat meruit anteferri; dicenti enim Marthae: Domine, non est tibi curae, quod soror mea reliquit me solam ministrare? Dic ergo illi 1936Cut me adjuvet; respondens dixit Dominus: Martha, Martha, Maria bonam partem elegit, quae non auferetur illi (Luc. VII, 40-42). Ita Verbum cognoscere majus opus esse quam ministrare, divinae auctoritate sententiae definitur.

68.

His lips distilling full myrrh. But we think ourselves idle if we seem to devote ourselves to the word only: and we reckon of more account those who work than those who exercise the pursuit of knowing the truth; for very many say: Behold the man and his works — as though he who devotes himself to the word does not work; whereas 1936Bthis is a work rather than the others. For if justice is a work, if temperance is a work, if fortitude is a work, then assuredly wisdom also is a work; for these four are held to be the principal virtues. For if Christ works according to that which is justice, assuredly he works according to that which is the Word. And he was working when he was in the beginning with the Father. In short, through him all things were made, that thou mayest know that he is the worker of all things, and that our work is Christ Jesus. For indeed, according to that which he is the Word, the Word to those who inquire, the Word is a great work. Whence, when Martha was hastening about the service, but Mary was hearing the word of the Lord, she who was hearing deserved to be set before her who was serving; for when Martha said: Lord, hast thou no care that my sister hath left me to serve alone? Bid her therefore 1936Cthat she help me, the Lord answered and said: Martha, Martha, Mary hath chosen a good part, which shall not be taken away from her (Luc. VII, 40-42). [Vulg. Luke 10:42 Maria optimam partem elegit, quae non auferetur ab ea, "Mary hath chosen the best part"] Thus it is determined by the authority of a divine sentence that to know the Word is a greater work than to serve.

69.

Sed fortasse dicat aliquis dictum esse ab Apostolo: Non in sermone esse regnum Dei, sed in virtute (I Cor. IV, 20). Scriptum non nego, sed in quo sermone, cognosco; nempe quem inflatus effuderit, qui audientibus sermo prodesse non possit, qui sine ostensione sit spiritus atque virtutis. Hunc sermonem Paulus non dignatur cognoscere; vult enim magis virtutem sermonis commendare. Denique volebat talem suum esse Apostolus sermonem, qui in infirmitate veniebat, ut alios faceret fortiores: in timore 1936Det tremore, ut timentes nihil metuerent, nisi Dominum Jesum; trementes, pacem ejus tranquillitatemque servarent. Audi ergo qualem Apostolus sermonem habebat: Et sermo, inquit, meus, et praedicatio mea non in persuasione humanae sapientiae verborum, sed in ostensione spiritus et virtutis (II Cor. II, 4); quia fides non in forensi sermone sapientiae, sed in ostensione spiritus, sed in Dei virtute firmatur.

69.

But perhaps someone may say that it has been said by the Apostle: That the kingdom of God is not in speech, but in power (I Cor. IV, 20). I do not deny that it is written, but I recognize in what speech; namely, that which a puffed-up man has poured out, a speech which cannot profit the hearers, which is without the showing of the Spirit and of power. This speech Paul does not deign to recognize; for he wishes rather to commend the power of speech. In short, the Apostle wished his own speech to be such as came in weakness, that it might make others stronger: in fear 1936Dand trembling, that those who feared might dread nothing but the Lord Jesus; that, trembling, they might keep his peace and tranquillity. Hear then what manner of speech the Apostle had: And my speech, he says, and my preaching were not in the persuasion of the words of human wisdom, but in the showing of the Spirit and of power (II Cor. II, 4); [Vulg. 1 Cor 2:4 non in persuasibilibus humanae sapientiae verbis, "not in the persuasive words of human wisdom"] because faith is established not in the forensic speech of wisdom, but in the showing of the Spirit, but in the power of God.

70.

Ergo in sermone sanctorum virtus est, in sermone autem forensi isto et philosophico vanitas mundi. Virtutem autem esse in sermone sanctorum, 1937Aetiam iste propheta te edoceat, qui ait: Dominus dabit verbum evangelizantibus virtute multa (Psal. LXVII, 12); hoc est, ut multa possint virtute Evangelium praedicare. Probatum est ergo quod in Evangelii praedicatione sit virtus, praedicatio autem Evangelii sermo sanctorum est; ita dubium non residet quin in sermone sancto sit virtus. Et haec myrrha distillans ex labiis Sponsi, scilicet per quam vitia mortificantur.

70.

Therefore in the speech of the saints there is power, but in that forensic and philosophical speech the vanity of the world. And that there is power in the speech of the saints, 1937Alet this prophet also teach thee, who says: The Lord will give the word to them that preach the gospel with much power (Psal. LXVII, 12); that is, that they may be able to preach the Gospel with much power. It is proved therefore that in the preaching of the Gospel there is power, and the preaching of the Gospel is the speech of the saints; so there remains no doubt that in holy speech there is power. And this is the myrrh distilling from the lips of the Bridegroom, namely that by which vices are mortified.

71.

(Vers. 14.) Manus illius tornatiles, aureae, plenae hyacinthis. Vide quae manus fecerint hominem, et quem hominem fecerint! Illum utique Quem secundum Christum induimus, exspoliantes veterem hominem cum actibus suis, et induentes novum qui renovatur ad agnitionem secundum imaginem ejus qui creavit eum: ubi non est servus, et liber, sed omnia et 1937Bin omnibus Christus (Colloss., III, 9-11). Christum ergo induimus, sicut et alibi dictum est: Christum induistis (Galat. III, 27). Accepimus Spiritum sanctum, qui non solum nostra peccata dimittit; 1603 sed etiam nos facit sacerdotes suos aliis peccata dimittere. Ideo ait Propheta: Tu finxisti me et posuisti super me manum tuam (Psal. CXXXVIII, 5): finxit per lutum, posuit manum per gratiam spiritalem; licet plerique hunc psalmum ex persona dictum Salvatoris accipiant. Audi quia manus Domini etiam spiritus dicitur, clamat Job: Spiritus divinus qui fecit me (Job. XXXIII, 4). Istae sunt ergo manus quae hominem paraverunt, Christus et Spiritus.

71.

(Vers. 14.) His hands are turned on the lathe, golden, full of hyacinths. See what hands made man, and what a man they made! Him assuredly Whom we have put on according to Christ, despoiling the old man with his acts, and putting on the new who is renewed unto the knowledge according to the image of him who created him: where there is no bondman and free, but all things and 1937BChrist in all (Colloss., III, 9-11). We have put on Christ therefore, as it is said also elsewhere: Ye have put on Christ (Galat. III, 27). We have received the Holy Spirit, who not only forgives our sins; 1603 but even makes us his priests, to forgive sins to others. Therefore the Prophet says: Thou hast fashioned me and hast laid thy hand upon me (Psal. CXXXVIII, 5): [Vulg. Tu formasti me, "Thou hast formed me"] he fashioned by the clay, he laid on his hand by spiritual grace; although very many take this psalm to be spoken in the person of the Savior. Hear that the hand of the Lord is also called Spirit: Job cries out, The divine Spirit who made me (Job. XXXIII, 4). [Vulg. Spiritus Dei fecit me, "The Spirit of God made me"] These therefore are the hands which prepared man, Christ and the Spirit.

72.

Ergo Dominus Jesus et corporis auctor est nostri, qui primum fecit hominem ad imaginem; 1937Cet postea de luto finxit, et voluit salvare quod fecerat, salvare quod plasmaverat, ut totum hominem salvum faceret. Istae sunt manus Dei tornatiles, id est, perfectae: aureae propter sapientiam qui est Christus, plenae hyacinthis propter Spiritum sanctum, et plenitudinem charismatum ejus.

72.

Therefore the Lord Jesus is also the author of our body, who first made man unto the image; 1937Cand afterwards fashioned him from clay, and willed to save what he had made, to save what he had molded, that he might make the whole man safe. These are the hands of God turned on the lathe, that is, perfect: golden on account of the wisdom which is Christ, full of hyacinths on account of the Holy Spirit, and the fullness of his graces.

73.

(Vers. 14.) Venter ejus eburneus, distinctus saphiris. Apostolus, Et si, inquit, crucifixus est ex infirmitate; ecce venter, id est, carnis infirmitas: Vivit tamen ex virtute Dei (II Cor. XIII, 4); ecce distinctio saphiri. Saphirus enim eo quod coeli sereni speciem gerat, quae sunt divinitatis opera significat. Onir autem propter castitatem et nitorem et perpetuitatem, carnem illam significat Salvatoris, de qua idem Apostolus dicit: Quia etsi Christum secundum 1937Dcarnem novimus, sed nunc jam non novimus (II Cor. V, 16).

73.

(Vers. 14.) His belly is of ivory, set apart with sapphires. The Apostle says, And if he was crucified out of weakness — behold the belly, that is, the weakness of the flesh — yet he lives out of the power of God (II Cor. XIII, 4); behold the distinction of the sapphire. For the sapphire, in that it bears the appearance of a serene sky, signifies the works which are of the divinity. But Onir, on account of chastity and brightness and perpetuity, signifies that flesh of the Savior, of which the same Apostle says: That although we have known Christ according to 1937Dthe flesh, yet now we know him no more (II Cor. V, 16).

74.

(Vers. 15.) Crura illius columnae marmoreae, fundatae super bases aureas. Significant columnas esse Ecclesiae Apostolos, qui fundati sunt in timore sancto. Nam sicut Petrus, Jacobus, Joannes et Barnabas columnae esse videbantur Ecclesiae, sic et quicumque vicerit hoc saeculum, fit columna Dei, quam confirmat qui dicit: Ego confirmavi columnas ejus (Psal. LXXIV, 4). Ita et basis aurea timor plenus disciplinae, 1938Aquia initium sapientiae est. In timore ergo sapientium apostolica praedicatio tamquam super basim auream columna firmatur Christi. Igitur eloquio et apostolico sermoni tribunal est timor justi, et basis aurea plena prudentiae: simulacrum autem bonum tamquam effigies veritatis, sermo sanctorum.

74.

(Vers. 15.) His legs are pillars of marble, founded upon golden bases. They signify that the Apostles are the pillars of the Church, who were founded in holy fear. For as Peter, James, John and Barnabas seemed to be pillars of the Church, so also whosoever has overcome this world becomes a pillar of God, whom he strengthens who says: I have strengthened its pillars (Psal. LXXIV, 4). So too the golden base is fear full of discipline, 1938Abecause it is the beginning of wisdom. In the fear therefore of the wise, apostolic preaching is made firm as a pillar of Christ upon a golden base. Accordingly, to speech and to the apostolic word the tribunal is the fear of the just man, and the golden base full of prudence: but the good image, as a likeness of the truth, is the speech of the saints.

75.

Et vide tamquam basim auream esse sanctorum timorem. Lege Esaiam: vide quantis subjecerit timorem, ut faceret irreprehensibilem et bonum timorem: Spiritus, inquit, sapientiae et intellectus, spiritus consilii atque virtutis, spiritus cognitionis atque pietatis, spiritus timoris Domini (Esai. XI, 2); quantis timorem subjecit, ut haberet quod sequi possit! Informatur per sapientiam, instruitur per intellectum, consilio dirigitur, virtute firmatur, cognitione regitur, 1938Bpietate decoratur. Tolle timori Domini ista, et est irrationabilis et insipiens timor, unus ex illis: Foris pugnae, intus timores (II Cor. VII, 5); quibus afflictus esset et Paulus, nisi habuisset Dominum consolantem.

75.

And see that the fear of the saints is as it were a golden base. Read Isaiah: see how many things he set fear under, that he might make fear blameless and good: The Spirit, he says, of wisdom and understanding, the Spirit of counsel and of power, the Spirit of knowledge and of piety, the Spirit of the fear of the Lord (Esai. XI, 2); [Vulg. spiritus consilii et fortitudinis, spiritus scientiae et pietatis, "the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, the spirit of science and of godliness"] how many things did he set fear under, that it might have what it could follow! It is formed by wisdom, it is instructed by understanding, it is directed by counsel, it is made firm by power, it is ruled by knowledge, 1938Bit is adorned by piety. Take these things away from the fear of the Lord, and it is an irrational and unwise fear, one of those: Without, fightings; within, fears (II Cor. VII, 5); with which Paul too would have been afflicted, had he not had the Lord consoling him.

76.

(Vers. 15.) Species ejus ut Libani, electus ut cedri. In altis habitat, et humilia respicit, quia species ejus sicut cedrus Libani, quae comam nubibus, radicem terris inserit; principium enim ejus e coelo, posteriora ejus in terris fructus coelo proximos ediderunt. Genus hoc arboris numquam amittit viriditatem suam, hyeme juxta ac aestate comam pascit, nec diverso colore mutatur: solam hanc arborem ventus suo numquam honore despoliat: sola numquam veteri exuitur amictu, aut novo flore vestitur. Sic apostolica quoque gratia nescit defectum, et 1938Cperpetua venustate sui floret. Anima ergo corrumpi nescit, quae florentibus membris viget, semper justitiae caeterarumque virtutum culmina patienti magnanimitate sustentat; et ideo non defluit neque deficit, quia nihil in ea ruinosum est ac remissum, nihil mobile, nihil lubricum, nihil quod vitio sermonis ex ea possit effundi.

76.

(Vers. 15.) His appearance is as of Lebanon, choice as the cedars. He dwells in the heights, and looks upon lowly things, because his appearance is like the cedar of Lebanon, which sets its foliage among the clouds and its root in the earth; for its beginning is from heaven, and its hinder parts in the earth have brought forth fruits nearest to heaven. This kind of tree never loses its greenness, in winter as in summer it feeds its foliage, nor is it changed with a different color: this tree alone the wind never despoils of its honor: this alone is never stripped of its old covering, nor clothed with a new bloom. So apostolic grace too knows no failing, and 1938Cblossoms with a perpetual comeliness of its own. The soul therefore knows not how to be corrupted, which is vigorous with flourishing members, and always upholds with patient greatness of heart the summits of justice and of the other virtues; and therefore it neither flows away nor fails, because in it there is nothing ruinous or slack, nothing shifting, nothing slippery, nothing which could be poured out of it by a fault of speech.

77.

(Vers. 16.) Guttur illius suavissimum, et totus desiderium. Judicia Domini vera, justificata in semetipsa, desiderabilia super aurum et lapidem pretiosum multum (Psal. XVIII, 10 et 11). Quod severum in aliis, in Christo dulce est, in Christo suave est; quia ipse suavis est: Denique gustate et videte quoniam suavis est Dominus (Psal. XXIII, 9). Dulcia judicia confitenti, quia ipse dicit: Ego sum, ego sum, qui deleo 1938Diniquitates tuas, et memor non ero: tu autem memor esto, et judicemur. Dic iniquitates tuas, ut justificeris (Esai. XLIII, 25 et 26). Dulcia judicia agenti poenitentiam, quia ipse dixit: Gaudium erit super uno peccatore poenitentiam agente, quam in nonaginta et novem justis, qui non indigent poenitentia (Luc. XV, 7). Si dulcia sunt igitur judicia Domini, suavitatis 1604 fructus percipere elaboremus. Vis scire quam dulcia judicia Domini? Qui credit in ipso, non judicatur (Joan. III, 18). Dulcia ergo judicia credentibus: qui autem 1939Anon credunt, non Christi judicio damnati sunt, qui venit non ut judicet mundum, sed ut salvet et redimat; sed impietatis suae subiere judicium, qui in remissionem peccatorum credere noluerunt. Non enim possunt ad ejus pertinere beneficium, quem cognoscere refutarunt. Ergo qui non crediderunt in eum, judicio ejus videntur indigni. Judicium enim ejus quid sit, agnosce, dicente ipso: Hoc autem judicium est, qua lux venit in hunc mundum (Ibid. 19). Dulce ergo, quod lux est, dulce judicium, quod praecedit misericordia; sic enim scriptum est, Misericordiam et judicium cantabo tibi, Domine (Psal. C, 1). Dulce etiam guttur et suavissimum, unde haec misericordia praecedit et hoc judicium.

77.

(Vers. 16.) His throat is most sweet, and he is wholly desire. The judgments of the Lord are true, justified in themselves, to be desired above gold and much precious stone (Psal. XVIII, 10 et 11). [Vulg. Cant 5:16 et totus desiderabilis, "and he is wholly desirable"] What is severe in others, in Christ is sweet, in Christ is pleasant; because he himself is pleasant: In short, taste and see that the Lord is sweet (Psal. XXIII, 9). Sweet are the judgments to him who confesses, because he himself says: I am he, I am he, who blot out 1938Dthine iniquities, and I will not be mindful of them: but be thou mindful, and let us be judged. Tell thine iniquities, that thou mayest be justified (Esai. XLIII, 25 et 26). [Vulg. Ego sum, ego sum ipse qui deleo iniquitates tuas propter me, et peccatorum tuorum non recordabor. Reduc me in memoriam, et judicemur simul: narra si quid habes ut justificeris] Sweet are the judgments to him who does penance, because he himself said: There shall be joy over one sinner doing penance, than over ninety and nine just who need not penance (Luc. XV, 7). If therefore the judgments of the Lord are sweet, let us labor to receive the fruit of his 1604 sweetness. Wilt thou know how sweet the judgments of the Lord are? He who believes in him is not judged (Joan. III, 18). Sweet therefore are the judgments to them that believe: but they who 1939Abelieve not have been condemned not by the judgment of Christ, who came not to judge the world but to save and redeem it; rather they have undergone the judgment of their own impiety, who would not believe unto the remission of sins. For they cannot belong to the benefit of him whom they have refused to acknowledge. Therefore they who have not believed in him are seen to be unworthy of his judgment. For learn what his judgment is, since he himself says: And this is the judgment, whereby the light came into this world (Ibid. 19). Sweet therefore is that which is light, sweet is the judgment which mercy goes before; for so it is written, Mercy and judgment will I sing to thee, O Lord (Psal. C, 1). Sweet also and most pleasant is the throat, whence this mercy goes before and this judgment.

78.

(Vers. 16.) Et totus desiderabilis. Speciosus forma 1939Bprae filiis hominum (Psal. XLIV, 3). Homo quippe erat secundum carnem, sed ultra hominem, secundum divinam operationem. Denique cum leprosum tangeret, homo videbatur: sed ultra hominem, cum emundaret. Et cum Lazarum fleret mortuum, quasi homo flebat: sed supra hominem erat, cum mortuum vinctis jubet pedibus exire. Homo videbatur, cum penderet in cruce: sed supra hominem cum reseratis tumulis, mortuos resuscitaret. Nec sibi adblandiatur virus Apollinaris, quod ita legitur: Et specie inventus ut homo (Philip. II, 7); non enim negatus est homo Jesus, cum alibi ipse Paulus de eo dicat: Mediator Dei et hominum homo Christus Jesus (I Tim. II, 5); sed confirmatus est. Id enim est usus et moris Scripturae sic significare, sicut et in Evangelio legimus: 1939CEt vidimus gloriam ejus, gloriam quasi unigeniti a Patre (Joan. I). Sicut ergo quasi unigenitus dicitur, et non abnuitur unigenitus Dei Filius; ita et homo dicitur, et perfectio fuisse in eo hominis non negatur.

78.

(Vers. 16.) And he is wholly desirable. Shapely in form 1939Babove the sons of men (Psal. XLIV, 3). He was indeed man according to the flesh, but beyond man, according to his divine working. In short, when he touched the leper, he was seen to be man: but beyond man, when he cleansed him. And when he wept for Lazarus dead, he wept as man: but he was above man, when he bade the dead man come forth with his feet bound. He was seen to be man, when he hung upon the cross: but above man, when, the tombs being unbarred, he raised the dead. Nor let the venom of Apollinaris flatter itself, because it is read thus: And in appearance found as a man (Philip. II, 7); [Vulg. et habitu inventus ut homo, "and in habit found as a man"] for Jesus was not denied to be man, since elsewhere Paul himself says of him: The mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus (I Tim. II, 5); but he was confirmed. For it is the usage and custom of Scripture so to signify, as we read also in the Gospel: 1939CAnd we saw his glory, the glory as of the only-begotten of the Father (Joan. I). As therefore he is called "as the only-begotten," and yet the only-begotten Son of God is not denied; so also he is called man, and the perfection of man in him is not denied.

79.

Cum esset igitur in forma servi, humiliatus usque ad mortem, erat tamen in Dei gloria. Quid ei igitur obfuit servitus? Servus autem factus legitur, quia legitur factus ex Virgine, et creatus in carne; omnis enim creatura servit, dicente propheta: Quia universa serviunt tibi. Haec est Christi gloria, quia servitutem suscepit in corpore suo, ut libertatem omnibus redderet: peccata nostra portavit, ut mundi peccatum tolleret: servus, peccatum, maledictum factus est, ut tu peccati servus esse desisteres, te 1939Ddivinae sententiae maledicto absolveret.
