Ille ergo maledictum suscepit tuum: Maledictus enim omnis qui pendet in ligno: ille maledictum in cruce factus est, ut tu benedictus esses in Dei regno: ille dehonestatus et depretiatus est, nec aliquid aestimatus: ille dicebat, In vacuum laboravi; per quem meruit Paulus dicere, Non in vacuum laboravi (Philip. II, 16); ut boni operis fructum, et evangelicae praedicationis gloriam suis conferret servulis, per quam universi sarcina laboris absolverentur.

79.

Since therefore he was in the form of a servant, humbled even unto death, he was nevertheless in the glory of God. What then did servitude harm him? But he is read to have been made a servant, because he is read to have been made of a Virgin, and created in the flesh; for every creature serves, as the prophet says: For all things serve thee. This is the glory of Christ, that he took servitude upon himself in his own body, that he might restore liberty to all: he bore our sins, that he might take away the sin of the world: he was made a servant, sin, a curse, that thou mightest cease to be the servant of sin, and that he might absolve thee 1939Dfrom the curse of the divine sentence. He therefore took upon himself thy curse: for Cursed is every one that hangeth upon a tree: he was made a curse upon the cross, that thou mightest be blessed in the kingdom of God: he was dishonored and cheapened, and accounted as nothing: he said, I have labored in vain; through whom Paul deserved to say, I have not labored in vain (Philip. II, 16); that he might confer upon his little servants the fruit of good work, and the glory of evangelical preaching, whereby all men might be released from the burden of labor.

80.

(Vers. 17.) Talis est dilectus meus, et amicus meus, filiae Hierusalem. Sic Ecclesia vel anima 1940Asancta filiabus coelestis illius civitatis admixta Verbum requirit, et quaerendo illius in se excitat charitatem: illis vero requirentibus et dicentibus: Quo abiit dilectus tuus, o pulcherrima mulierum? Quo declinavit dilectus tuus, ut quaeramus eum tecum?

80.

(Vers. 17.) Such is my beloved, and my friend, O daughters of Jerusalem. So the Church, or the 1940Aholy soul, mingled with the daughters of that heavenly city, seeks the Word, and by seeking stirs up his charity within herself: while they indeed ask and say: Whither is thy beloved gone, O most beautiful of women? Whither has thy beloved turned aside, that we may seek him with thee?

CAPUT SEXTUM.

CAPUT SEXTUM.

1.

(Vers. 1.) Dilectus, inquit, meus descendit in hortum suum ad areolam aromatis, ut pascatur in hortis, et lilia colligat. Ubi Verbum quaerat, agnoscit: quod inter orationes sanctorum moretur, et quod ipsis inhaereat, novit, et quod Ecclesiam suam vel animas justorum suorum inter lilia pascat, intelligit. Hoc mysterium tibi in Evangelio demonstravit, cum in sabbato duceret per seminata discipulos. 1940BMoyses per desertum duxit populum Judaeorum, Christus per seminata ducit, Christus per lilia ducit; quia per passionem ejus desertum floret ut lilium. Sequamur ergo, ut in die sabbati illius magni, in quo requies magna est, fructus colligamus. Nec verearis ne Pharisaei accusent seminata colligentes: etsi illi accusant, Christus excusat, et similes facit quas vult animas se sequentes David illius, qui supra Legem panem propositionis manducabat, novae gratiae sacramenta prophetica jam tum mente concipiens.

1.

(Vers. 1.) My beloved, she says, is gone down into his garden, to the little bed of spice, that he may feed in the gardens, and gather lilies. She recognizes where she should seek the Word: she knows that he tarries among the prayers of the saints, and that he cleaves to them, and she understands that he feeds his Church, or the souls of his just ones, among the lilies. This mystery he showed thee in the Gospel, when on the sabbath he led his disciples through the sown fields. 1940BMoses led the people of the Jews through the desert, Christ leads through the sown fields, Christ leads through the lilies; because through his passion the desert flowers as a lily. Let us follow then, that on the day of that great sabbath, in which is the great rest, we may gather fruits. And fear not that the Pharisees may accuse those who gather from the sown fields: even if they accuse, Christ excuses, and makes the souls which he wills, following him, like to that David who ate the loaves of proposition above the Law, already then conceiving in mind the prophetic sacraments of the new grace.

2.

(Vers. 2.) Ego dilecto meo, et dilectus meus mihi, qui pascitur inter 1605 lilia. Facilis vox et communis videtur, sed paucorum est. Satis rarus est enim qui potest dicere: Ego dilecto meo, et dilectus meus mihi. Quid est dicere: Tuus sum? ille enim dicit qui adhaeret 1940CDeo totis sensibus, qui aliud cogitare non novit, ille hac voce utitur, qui potest dicere Domino: Ostende nobis Patrem, et sufficit nobis (Joan. XIV, 8). Numquid hac voce utitur avidus pecuniae, honoris, potestatis? Multis non est satis Deum scire, et quidem pluribus. Tanti populi, tantae nationes, tanti divites paupertatem putant Domino servire; et qui supra omnes est, illis exiguus et angustus est: illis non est satis Dei Filius, in quo sunt omnia. Denique ille dives in Evangelio cui dictum est: Si vis perfectus esse, vende omnia quae habes, et da pauperibus (Matth. XIX, 21); Deum sibi non sufficere judicavit. Denique et contristatus est, quasi quod pluris esset, relinquere juberetur, quod minus esset, eligere. Ille ergo dicit: Tuus sum, qui potest dicere: Ecce nos reliquimus 1940Domnia, et secuti sumus te (Ibid., 27).

2.

(Vers. 2.) I am my beloved's, and my beloved is mine, who feeds among 1605 the lilies. The utterance seems easy and common, but it belongs to few. For rare enough is he who can say: I am my beloved's, and my beloved is mine. What is it to say, I am thine? For he says it who cleaves to 1940CGod with all his senses, who knows not how to think of anything else; he uses this utterance who can say to the Lord: Show us the Father, and it sufficeth us (Joan. XIV, 8). Does the man greedy of money, of honor, of power use this utterance? For many it is not enough to know God — indeed for very many. So many peoples, so many nations, so many rich men think it poverty to serve the Lord; and he who is above all is to them a small and narrow thing: for them the Son of God, in whom are all things, is not enough. In short, that rich man in the Gospel to whom it was said: If thou wilt be perfect, sell all that thou hast, and give to the poor (Matth. XIX, 21), [Vulg. vade, vende quae habes, "go, sell what thou hast" — without omnia] judged that God was not sufficient for him. In short, he was also made sorrowful, as though he were bidden to leave what was of more worth, and to choose what was of less. He therefore says, I am thine, who can say: Behold, we have left 1940Dall things, and have followed thee (Ibid., 27).

3.

Apostolorum vox ista est, nec omnium tamen apostolorum; nam et Judas apostolus fuit, et in convivio Christi inter apostolos recumbebat. Dicebat et ipse: Tuus sum; sed voce, non corde. Venit et introivit in eum Satanas, et coepit dicere: Non est tuus, Jesus, sed meus est. Denique ea quae mea sunt, cogitat, quae mea sunt in pectore suo volvit: tecum epulatur, et mecum pascitur: a te panem accepit, a me pecuniam: tecum bibit, et mihi tuum sanguinem vendit: tuus est apostolus, et meus mercenarius. Non potest dicere saecularis: Tuus sum; plures 1941Aenim dominos habet. Venit libido, et dicit: Meus es; quia ea quae sum corporis, concupiscis: in illius adolescentulae amore te mihi vendidisti: in illius concubitu meretricis pretium pro te annumeravi. Venit avaritia, et dicit: Argentum et aurum quod habes, servitutis tuae pretium est: possessio quam tenes, juris tui emptio, venditio libertatis tuae est. Venit luxuria, et dicit: Meus es; unius diei convivium, pretium tuae vitae est: ille sumptus epularum, tui capitis licitatio, tui est summa contractus. Et quod pejus est, caro emptus es pretio, vilior cibo es tuo: pretiosior est unius diei mensa tua, quam totius temporis vita: inter calices te redemi, inter epulas acquisivi. Venit ambitio, et dicit tibi: Plane meus es. Nescis quod ideo imperare aliis te feci, ut mihi ipse 1941Bservires? Nescis quod ideo potestatem in te contuli, ut meae te subjicerem potestati? An ignoras ipsi Domino salvatori dictum esse ab hujus mundi principe, cum ei ostendisset omnia regna mundi: Haec omnia tibi dabo, si procidens adoraveris me (Matth. IV, 9)? Ante ergo ipse subjicitur, qui alios vult habere subjectos. Veniunt omnia vitia, et singula dicunt: Meus es. Quem tanti competunt, quam vile mancipium est! Quomodo ergo tu qui hujusmodi es, potes Christo dicere: Tuus sum? Respondebit tibi ille: Non quicumque dicit mihi, Domine, Domine, intrabit in regnum coelorum (Matth. VII, 21): non quicumque dicit mihi: Tuus sum, meus est.

3.

This is the voice of the apostles, and yet not of all the apostles; for Judas too was an apostle, and reclined among the apostles at the banquet of Christ. He too was saying, I am thine; but with the voice, not with the heart. Satan came and entered into him, and began to say: He is not thine, Jesus, but he is mine. In short, he thinks of the things which are mine, he turns over in his breast the things which are mine: he banquets with thee, and feeds with me: from thee he received bread, from me money: with thee he drinks, and to me he sells thy blood: he is thine as apostle, and mine as hireling. The worldly man cannot say, I am thine; for he has 1941Amany masters. Lust comes and says: Thou art mine; because thou lustest after the things which I am, the things of the body: in the love of that young girl thou hast sold thyself to me: in the embrace of that harlot I have counted out the price for thee. Avarice comes and says: The silver and gold which thou hast is the price of thy servitude: the possession thou holdest is the purchase of thy right, the sale of thy liberty. Luxury comes and says: Thou art mine; the banquet of a single day is the price of thy life: that outlay of feasts is the bidding for thy head, it is the sum of thy contract. And what is worse, thou hast been bought at a dear price, thou art cheaper than thy food: thy table for one day is more precious than the life of thy whole time: among cups I have redeemed thee, among feasts I have acquired thee. Ambition comes and says to thee: Plainly thou art mine. Knowest thou not that I therefore made thee command others, that thou thyself 1941Bmightest serve me? Knowest thou not that I therefore conferred power upon thee, that I might subject thee to my power? Or art thou ignorant that it was said to the Lord Savior himself by the prince of this world, when he had shown him all the kingdoms of the world: All these will I give thee, if falling down thou wilt adore me (Matth. IV, 9)? Before all, then, he himself is made subject who wishes to have others subject. All the vices come, and each says: Thou art mine. He whom so many claim, how cheap a chattel is he! How therefore canst thou, who art of this sort, say to Christ: I am thine? He will answer thee: Not whosoever saith to me, Lord, Lord, shall enter into the kingdom of heaven (Matth. VII, 21): [Vulg. Non omnis qui dicit mihi, "Not every one that saith to me"] not whosoever says to me, I am thine, is mine.

4.

Meus plane es, si vocem non redarguat conscientia, si sermonem tuum animus aut opera non refutent. 1941CEgo non nego meum esse eum qui ipse se non neget, aut certe si pro me seipsum sibi abneget. Nolo habere servulum plurimis dominis servientem. Nam quomodo meus est, si mihi dicat verbo: Tuus sum; et operibus neget, et factis se diabolo adjudicet et obstringat? Non est meus quem libido succendit, quia mea est castitas: non est meus quem cura spoliandi minores exagitat, quia mea largitio est: non est meus, quem aura mobilitatis inquietat, quia tranquillitas mea est. Non est meus vini crapula temulentus, illuvie ambitionis impurus, gloriae saecularis cupidus, ebrius in periculum, qui non potest sobriae moderationis servare vestigium. Pax sum ego, litigare non novi: ut quid mihi eum de quo veniat diabolus et dicat. Meus est; nam mihi sua colla curvavit, 1941Dmea in illo plura reperio: nomen sibi tuum vindicat, et meum munus. Non est ergo Christi, nisi qui est alienus a crimine: non est Christi, nisi qui potest semper se Christi servulum demonstrare.

4.

Plainly thou art mine, if conscience does not refute the voice, if thy mind or thy works do not disprove thy speech. 1941CI do not deny him to be mine who does not deny himself, or at any rate if for my sake he denies his very self to himself. I will not have a little servant who serves very many masters. For how is he mine, if he says to me in word: I am thine; and denies it by his works, and by his deeds adjudges and binds himself to the devil? He is not mine whom lust inflames, because chastity is mine: he is not mine whom the care of despoiling lesser men drives about, because bounty is mine: he is not mine whom the breeze of fickleness disquiets, because tranquillity is mine. He is not mine who is drunken with the surfeit of wine, unclean with the filth of ambition, greedy of worldly glory, drunk unto peril, who cannot keep the track of sober moderation. I am peace, I know not how to quarrel: to what end is he mine, concerning whom the devil may come and say, He is mine; for to me he has bowed his neck, 1941DI find more of my own in him: he claims thy name for himself, and my gift. He is not Christ's, therefore, save he who is a stranger to crime: he is not Christ's, save he who can always show himself Christ's little servant.

5.

Ego dilecto meo, et dilectus meus mihi, qui pascitur inter lilia. Videtur inter dilecta dilecti dicere: Secretum meum mihi, secretum meum mihi. Quaedam enim in exstasi amoris sui occulta fidei intellexerat mysteria, quae nec ipsis filiabus Hierusalem propalanda censebat. Mysterium 1606 regis bonum est abscondere; peccat enim Deo, qui commissa 1942Asibi mysteria putaverit indignis esse vulganda. Periculum itaque est non solum falsa dicere, sed etiam vera, si quis ea insinuet quibus non oportet. Quod vitium quadripartitum est, vel adulationis, vel avaritiae, vel jactantiae, vel loquacitatis incautae; quia dum adulari vult aliquis, ei cui loquitur, effundit mysterium: nonnulli etiam studio lucri, mercedem proditionis sequuntur, ut tegenda silentio vendant loquendo: alii ut plura noscere videantur, et scientiam suam jactitent, aperiunt quod celare deberent: plerique dum sine judicio loquuntur, verbum emittunt quod revocare non possunt. Ideo vir laudatur serius, et non otiosus. Erat homo abscondens sermones suos, ut apostolus Paulus, qui novit quid loquatur singulis, et quando quid loqui debeat. 1942B

5.

I am my beloved's, and my beloved is mine, who feeds among the lilies. She seems to say, among the beloved things of the beloved: My secret to me, my secret to me. For she had understood, in the ecstasy of her love, certain hidden mysteries of the faith, which she judged were not to be published even to the daughters of Jerusalem themselves. It is good to hide the mystery 1606 of the king; for he sins against God who thinks that the mysteries committed 1942Ato him are to be divulged to the unworthy. [Vulg. Tob 12:7 Sacramentum regis abscondere bonum est, "It is good to hide the sacrament of the king"] And so it is a danger not only to speak false things, but even true ones, if a man insinuates them to those to whom he ought not. Which fault is of four parts, either of flattery, or of avarice, or of boasting, or of incautious talkativeness; because while a man wishes to flatter the one to whom he speaks, he pours out the mystery: some too, from zeal for gain, pursue the wage of betrayal, so as to sell by speaking what ought to be covered with silence: others, that they may seem to know more, and may vaunt their own knowledge, open what they ought to conceal: very many, while they speak without judgment, let go a word which they cannot call back. Therefore a serious man is praised, and not an idle one. There was a man hiding his own words, as the apostle Paul, who knew what he should speak to individuals, and when he ought to speak anything. 1942B

6.

(Vers. 3.) Beneplacita es, soror mea, speciosa sicut Hierusalem, admiratio sicut ordinata. Veritas amoris ex sui perpetuitate probatur. Laudatur itaque Sponsa a Sponso, quod tam bene et constanter requisierit eum. Ideoque jam non solum soror dicitur, sed etiam beneplacita nominatur, quasi ei complacita; quia complacuit Patri: et, speciosa sicut Hierusalem, admiratio sicut ordinata; quod civitatis aeternae universa habeat mysteria: et admirationi sit omnibus videntibus eam; quia plena ut aequitas, atque perfecta est: et fulgorem de Verbi lumine mutuata, dum id semper intendit, sit etiam terribilis ordine quodam ad summum provecta virtutum.

6.

(Vers. 3.) Thou art well-pleasing, my sister, shapely as Jerusalem, a wonder as one set in order. [Vulg. Pulchra es, amica mea; suavis, et decora sicut Jerusalem; terribilis ut castrorum acies ordinata, "Thou art beautiful, my love; sweet and comely as Jerusalem; terrible as an army set in array"] The truth of love is proved by its perpetuity. And so the Bride is praised by the Bridegroom, because she has sought him so well and so constantly. And therefore she is now not only called sister, but is even named well-pleasing, as being pleasing to him; because she has pleased the Father: and, shapely as Jerusalem, a wonder as one set in order; because she has all the mysteries of the eternal city: and is a wonder to all who see her; because she is full as equity is, and perfect: and, having borrowed her brightness from the light of the Word, while she ever strains toward it, she is even terrible, being advanced by a certain order to the summit of the virtues.

7.

Pulchra es, inquit, amica mea, suavis et decora, etc. Et vera pulchra, quae intendit in illum de quo 1942Cdicitur: Speciosus forma prae filiis hominum (Psal. XLIV, 3). Unde et nos intendamus quantum possumus animum in illo, et in illo simus. Illud animo teneamus, quod est pulchrum, decorum, bonum; ut fiat illuminatione ejus et fulgore speciosa anima nostra, et mens dilucida. Nam si oculi nostri cum aliqua obducuntur caligine, pascuntur agrorum viriditate, et specie nemoris vel collis umbrosi omnem inaequalitatem aegrescentis obtutus repellunt, et quadam salubri specie pupillae ipsae atque orbes colorari videntur: quanto magis hic mentis oculus, cum illud summum intuetur bonum, et in eo versatur, atque eo pascitur, splendescit atque enitet. Pulchra ut Hierusalem. Non caduci itaque ista est corporis pulchritudo, vel morbo peritura vel senio, sed nullis obnoxia 1942Dcasibus, opinioni bonorum numquam moritura meritorum; quoniam non humanis jam, sed coelestibus, quorum vitam vivit in terris, digna est comparari. Ideoque Sponsus Sponsae jam quasi perfectae, dicit:

7.

Thou art beautiful, he says, my love, sweet and comely, etc. And truly beautiful is she who strains toward him of whom 1942Cit is said: Shapely in form above the sons of men (Psal. XLIV, 3). Whence let us too strain our mind toward him as much as we can, and be in him. Let us hold in mind that which is beautiful, comely, good; that by his illumination and brightness our soul may become shapely, and our mind clear. For if our eyes, when they are overspread with any dimness, are fed with the greenness of the fields, and by the appearance of a grove or a shady hill drive off all the unevenness of a sickening gaze, and the very pupils and orbs seem to be colored with a certain healthful appearance: how much more does this eye of the mind, when it beholds that highest good, and is engaged in it, and is fed by it, grow bright and shine forth. Beautiful as Jerusalem. [Vulg. decora sicut Jerusalem, "comely as Jerusalem"] This therefore is not the beauty of a perishable body, to perish either by disease or by old age, but a beauty subject to no 1942Dmischances, never to die in the esteem of good deserts; since it is worthy to be compared now not with human things, but with heavenly, whose life she lives on earth. And therefore the Bridegroom says to the Bride, as to one already perfect:

8.

(Vers. 4.) Averte oculos tuos a me, quia me avolare fecerunt. Noli contra me intendere devotione nimia ac fide. Possibilitatem naturae et conditionis propriae supergressa es, quia lucem inaccessibilem e regione intueri est grave. Averte, inquit, oculos tuos a me: eo quod plenitudinem divinitatis ejus, et splendorem veri luminis sustinere non possit. 1943A

8.

(Vers. 4.) Turn away thine eyes from me, because they have made me fly away. Strain not against me with too great devotion and faith. Thou hast passed beyond the possibility of thy nature and of thine own condition, because to gaze straight upon the inaccessible light is a heavy thing. Turn away, he says, thine eyes from me: for this reason, that she cannot bear the fullness of his divinity, and the splendor of the true light. 1943A

9.

Possumus tamen et sic accipere: Averte oculos tuos a me; etsi tu perfecta es, aliae mihi adhuc redimendae sunt animae, aliae fulciendae. Elevas enim me videndo: ego autem ideo descendi, ut omnes elevem. Etsi surrexi, et habeo Patris sedem, tamen orphanos non relinquam vos, paterno quasi praesidio destitutos, sed praesentia mea vos confirmabo. Habes in Evangelio hoc scriptum: Ecce ego vobiscum sum usque ad consummationem mundi (Matth. XXVIII, 20). Averte ergo oculos a me, quia me elevas. Quanto enim quis ad Dominum intendit, tanto amplius Dominum elevat, et ipse elevatur. Unde et ille ait: Exaltabo te, Domine, quoniam suscepisti me (Psalm. XXIX, 1). Sanctus enim exaltat Dominum, peccator humiliat. Vult ergo avertere illam oculos, ne eam 1943Bconsiderans, quod jam ad superiora sequi possit, elevetur et caeteras animas derelinquat. Ideo et in Evangelio non omnibus discipulis, sed perfectioribus suam gloriam demonstravit (Matth. XVII, 1). Constitue nunc aliquem doctorem, qui rem obscuram aperire velit audientibus, quemadmodum etsi ipse potens in sermone sit et scientia; condescendat tamen ad eorum inscitiam, qui non intelligunt, et simplici et planiore atque usitato sermone utatur, ut possit intelligi. Si quis igitur inter audientes vivacior sensu sit, qui facile sequi possit, elevat eum atque excutit. Hunc videns doctor revocat, ut patiatur magis doctorem humilioribus 1607 et planioribus immorari; quo et caeteri sequi possint.

9.

Yet we can also take it thus: Turn away thine eyes from me; even if thou art perfect, there are other souls still to be redeemed by me, others to be propped up. For thou liftest me up by seeing me: but I came down for this, that I might lift up all. Even if I have risen, and have the seat of the Father, yet I will not leave you orphans, destitute as of a father's protection, but by my presence I will strengthen you. Thou hast this written in the Gospel: Behold, I am with you even to the consummation of the world (Matth. XXVIII, 20). Turn away then thine eyes from me, because thou liftest me up. For the more a man strains toward the Lord, so much the more does he lift up the Lord, and is himself lifted up. Whence he too says: I will exalt thee, O Lord, because thou hast upheld me (Psalm. XXIX, 1). For the holy man exalts the Lord, the sinner humbles him. He wishes therefore that she turn away her eyes, lest, 1943Bbeholding her, because she can now follow to higher things, he be lifted up and leave the other souls behind. Therefore in the Gospel also he showed his glory not to all the disciples, but to the more perfect (Matth. XVII, 1). Set before thyself now some teacher who wishes to open an obscure matter to his hearers: how, even if he himself be powerful in speech and knowledge, he yet condescends to the ignorance of those who do not understand, and uses a simple and plainer and more familiar speech, that he may be able to be understood. If therefore there be among the hearers one quicker in sense, who can easily follow, he lifts him up and rouses him. Seeing this, the teacher calls him back, that he may rather allow the teacher to dwell upon lowlier 1607 and plainer things; whereby the rest also may be able to follow.

10.

(Vers. 5-7.) Laudatur etiam Sponsa quod fidelis, 1943Cquod verbo potens, quod fructibus fecunda diversis, quod una sit columba, habens spiritus unitatem, in qua sit pax, quae facit utraque unum: et quae non sit composita ex diversis elementis discretae compugnantisque naturae. Quid enim tam diversum, quam ignis et aqua, aer et terra, ex quibus corporis nostri creatura consistit? Anima autem benedicta omnis simplex, quae imitatur dicentem: Ut omnes unum sint, sicut et tu, Pater, in me, et ego in te, et ipsi in nobis unum sint (Joan. XVII, 21); haec enim est consummatio atque perfectio, unde et addidit: Ut sint unum, sicut et nos unum sumus: et ego in his, et tu in me, ut sint consummati in unum (Ibid., 23). Haec est ergo una columba atque perfecta, quae simplex atque spiritualis, neque hujus turbatur corporis passionibus, in quo 1943Dforis pugnae, intus timores sunt. Denique hoc verbo unitatis concordiam et pacem significari Scriptura nos docet, dicens: Multitudo autem credentium habebat cor unum et animam unam, et non erat separatio in eis ulla (Act. IV, 32).

10.

(Vers. 5-7.) The Bride is praised also because she is faithful, 1943Cbecause she is powerful in word, because she is fruitful with diverse fruits, because she is one dove, having the unity of the Spirit, in which is peace, which makes both one: and because she is not composed of the diverse elements of a distinct and warring nature. For what is so diverse as fire and water, air and earth, out of which the creature of our body consists? But every blessed soul is simple, which imitates him who says: That all may be one, as thou, Father, in me, and I in thee, and that they themselves may be one in us (Joan. XVII, 21); for this is the consummation and the perfection, whence he added also: That they may be one, as we also are one: and I in these, and thou in me, that they may be consummated in one (Ibid., 23). This therefore is the one and perfect dove, which, being simple and spiritual, is not disturbed by the passions of this body, in which 1943Dthere are fightings without, fears within. In short, Scripture teaches us that by this word of unity concord and peace are signified, saying: And the multitude of them that believed had one heart and one soul, and there was no separation at all among them (Act. IV, 32). [Vulg. Multitudinis autem credentium erat cor unum et anima una, without the final clause]

11.

Nec otiose fecunditatis laudatur anima, non solum quod sit fecunda virtutibus, sed etiam quod nihil habeat in se mali; illud enim decorum atque speciosum est, in quo nihil sit mali. Decorum enim quod bonum; quod autem indecorum, hoc malum. Speciosa fecunditas est bonorum operum, contraria 1944Aergo speciositati sterilitas; quoniam qui privatur specie vel decore, in eo est omne malum: quod autem malum, hoc sterile et infecundum. Cujus rei quod evidentius indicium, quam terra est? Terra enim quae bona est, fertilis atque fecunda: quae autem mala, ea jejuna atque sterilis; quae vero fertilis, haec et decora. Quid enim pleno agro pulchrius, cum seges fluctuat, cum poma irrutilant, vel cum uvarum serta dependent, aut baccis onusta olea curvescit, vel viridanti gramine montium vertices, vallium humilia vestiuntur?

11.

Nor is the soul praised idly for its fruitfulness, not only because it is fruitful in virtues, but also because it has nothing of evil in itself; for that is comely and shapely in which there is nothing of evil. For that is comely which is good; but that which is uncomely, this is evil. Shapely is the fruitfulness of good works, contrary 1944Atherefore to shapeliness is barrenness; since in him who is deprived of shapeliness or comeliness there is every evil: but that which is evil, this is barren and unfruitful. And of this matter what plainer sign is there than the earth? For the earth which is good is fertile and fruitful: but that which is bad is meager and barren; but that which is fertile, this is also comely. For what is more beautiful than a full field, when the crop waves, when the apples redden, or when the garlands of grapes hang down, or the olive, laden with berries, bends over, or when the tops of the mountains and the low places of the valleys are clothed with green herbage?

12.

Omnium enim bonorum auctor est Deus: et quae sunt ejus, profecto omnia bona sunt, et nusquam illic malum; et si in illo mens nostra maneat, malum nescit. Anima igitur quae in Deo non manet, ipsa 1944Bsibi auctor malorum est, itaque peccat: Anima autem quae peccat, ipsa morietur; aureis enim vinculis soluta virtutum, prona fertur praecipitio, et labitur ad inferiora. Anima autem beata est, quam nulla adversa corporis praelia expugnant. Haec anima sicut passer laqueo contrito, evolat. Escae enim malorum sunt voluptates corporeae: his quisquis intendit, laqueo innectit animam suam. Qui autem ab escis ejus temperat, et de tenebris ejus exit, ejus anima fulget ut diluculum, de qua dicitur:

12.

For God is the author of all good things: and the things which are his are assuredly all good, and there is no evil there; and if our mind abide in him, it knows no evil. The soul therefore which does not abide in God is 1944Bto itself the author of evils, and so it sins: but the soul that sinneth, the same shall die; for being loosed from the golden chains of the virtues, it is borne headlong down the precipice, and slips to lower things. But blessed is the soul which no adverse battles of the body storm. This soul, like a sparrow when the snare is broken, flies away. For the baits of evils are the pleasures of the body: whosoever strains after these entangles his own soul in the snare. But he who abstains from its baits, and comes out of its darkness, his soul shines as the dawn, of which it is said:

13.

(Vers. 9.) Quaenam est haec prospiciens tamquam diluculum, pulchra sicut luna? Prospicit enim tamquam de domo libera, nec dicit: Tenebrae cooperiunt me, et parietes circumdant me, et quis scit si videt Altissimus (Eccli. XXIII, 26)? Sed ipsa magis lucem 1944Cexpetit tamquam in superioribus domus suae, id est, corporis sui: et supra mundum posita, divina intuetur et ad aeterna se elevat, ut Deo adsit; jam lumen suorum operum sicut luna orbem suum toto orbe circumferens.

13.

(Vers. 9.) Who is this that looks forth as the dawn, beautiful as the moon? [Vulg. Quae est ista quae progreditur quasi aurora consurgens, pulchra ut luna, "Who is she that cometh forth as the morning rising, fair as the moon"] For she looks forth as from a free house, nor does she say: Darkness covers me, and walls surround me, and who knows whether the Most High sees (Eccli. XXIII, 26)? [Vulg. Tenebrae circumdant me, et parietes cooperiunt me, et nemo circumspicit me: quem vereor?, with the verbs the other way about and a different close] But she rather seeks the light 1944Cas in the upper parts of her house, that is, of her body: and, set above the world, she gazes upon divine things and lifts herself to eternal things, that she may stand near to God; now carrying the light of her works round her own orb, as the moon does, over the whole world.

14.

(Vers. 10.) In hortum nucis descendi, videre in nativitate torrentis, et inspicere si floruisset vinea, et germinassent mala punica. Invitatur denique Dei Verbum in hortum nucis, in quo fructus propheticae lectionis, et sacerdotalis est gratia, quae amara in tentationibus, dura in laboribus, in virtutibus interioribus fructuosa est. Unde etiam virga Aaron nucea floruit, non jam natura sua, sed virtute secreta. Descendat ergo in hortum, ut vindemiet fidem, capiat odores, pabulum coeleste reperiat, suavitatem 1944Dnostri mellis epuletur.

14.

(Vers. 10.) I went down into the garden of the nut tree, to see in the birth of the torrent, and to look whether the vine had flowered, and the pomegranates had budded. [Vulg. Descendi in hortum nucum, ut viderem poma convallium, "I went down into the garden of nuts, to see the fruits of the valleys"] The Word of God is invited, in short, into the garden of the nut tree, in which is the fruit of prophetic reading, and the priestly grace, which is bitter in temptations, hard in labors, fruitful in the inward virtues. Whence Aaron's rod of nut-wood also flowered, not now by its own nature, but by a hidden power. Let him come down therefore into the garden, that he may gather the vintage of faith, take in the odors, find the heavenly fodder, feast upon the sweetness 1944Dof our honey.

15.

Dum enim laudatur a Sponso, verecunde refugiens coram laudari; deinde sponsi amore revocata, ait: In hortum nucis descendi videre in nativitate torrentis. Ubi enim est Ecclesia, nisi ubi virga et gratia floret sacerdotalis? Ibi est frequenter, ut in amaritudinibus et in tentationibus probetur. Per nucem amaritudines intelligimus, per torrentem tentationes, sed tamen tolerabiles, quia scriptum est: Torrentem pertranavit anima nostra (Psalm. CXXIII, 5). Itaque descendit in locum amaritudinis, ubi floret 1945Avitis et malorum granatorum specie diversus et multiplex fructus, qui velut uno tegmine totius corporis fide et charitate munitur. 1608

15.

For while she is praised by the Bridegroom, modestly shrinking from being praised to her face; then, called back by the love of the Bridegroom, she says: I went down into the garden of the nut tree to see in the birth of the torrent. For where is the Church, save where the priestly rod and grace flowers? There she is frequently, that she may be tried in bitternesses and in temptations. By the nut we understand bitternesses, by the torrent temptations, yet tolerable ones, because it is written: Our soul hath swum through the torrent (Psalm. CXXIII, 5). [Vulg. torrentem pertransivit anima nostra, "our soul hath passed through the torrent"] And so she went down into the place of bitterness, where the vine flowers, 1945Aand the fruit various and manifold in the appearance of pomegranates, which as it were under one covering of the whole body is fortified by faith and charity. 1608

16.

In illa ergo amaritudine non cognovit se anima, Corruptibile enim corpus gravat animam, et terrenum habitaculum cito inclinatur (Sap. IX). Se autem cognoscere semper debet. Sed tentatus est Petrus, et non se cognovit et Petrus; nam si cognovisset, non negasset auctorem. Sed cognovit eum Christus. Denique cognovit, quia et respexit; cognoscit enim Dominus qui sunt ejus: et tamquam suum misericordiae suae frenis ut bonus rector, revocavit a lapsu: rector ergo noster Christus est. Unde et sequitur:

16.

In that bitterness, therefore, the soul did not know itself, For the corruptible body weighs down the soul, and the earthly dwelling is quickly bowed down (Sap. IX). [Vulg. Corpus enim quod corrumpitur aggravat animam, et terrena inhabitatio deprimit sensum multa cogitantem] But it ought always to know itself. Yet Peter was tempted, and Peter too did not know himself; for if he had known himself, he would not have denied his author. But Christ knew him. In short, he knew him, because he also looked back at him; for the Lord knows who are his: and, as a good ruler, by the reins of his mercy he called back his own from the fall: our ruler therefore is Christ. Whence there follows also:

17.

(Vers. 11.) Posuit me currus Aminadab: eo quod anima conjungatur corpori, et velut quidam 1945Bequorum frementium currus, rectorem sui quaerat aurigam. Aminadab enim pater Naason fuit, sicut in Numeris legimus (Num. I, 7), qui erat princeps populi Juda, cujus figura refertur ad Christum, qui verus populi princeps animam justi velut currum agitator ascendens, Verbi habenis gubernat, ne violentorum equorum furore in praeceps et abrupta rapiatur. Sunt enim ejus velut quatuor equi quatuor affectiones, ira, cupiditas, voluptas, timor: quibus furentibus cum coeperit agi, nequaquam seipsam cognoscit. Corruptibile enim corpus animam gravat, et tamquam irrationabilium animalium currus invitam rapit, volventibus curis velut quodam impetu proruentem, donec memoratae corporis passiones Verbi virtute mitescant. 1945C

17.

(Vers. 11.) The chariot of Aminadab has set me: [Vulg. Nescivi: anima mea conturbavit me propter quadrigas Aminadab, "I knew not: my soul troubled me because of the four-horse chariots of Aminadab"] in that the soul is joined to the body, and, like a certain 1945Bchariot of raging horses, seeks a charioteer to rule it. For Aminadab was the father of Naason, as we read in Numbers (Num. I, 7), who was prince of the people of Juda, whose figure is referred to Christ, who, the true prince of the people, mounting the soul of the just man as a chariot, guides it as driver with the reins of the Word, lest by the fury of violent horses it be swept headlong and over precipices. For his four horses, as it were, are the four affections: anger, desire, pleasure, fear; and when it has begun to be driven by these in their raging, it in no way knows itself. For the corruptible body weighs down the soul, and, like a chariot of irrational animals, sweeps it away unwilling, rushing forward as by a certain onset while cares roll on, until the aforesaid passions of the body grow mild by the power of the Word. 1945C

18.

Haec Verbi tamquam boni agitatoris est providentia, ne illi animae quae in se non est morti obnoxia, corpus mortale conjunctum, agitationem sui faciat esse difficilem. Primo igitur hos veloces corporis motus domitet, et nexu rationis infrenet: deinde caveat ne dispari motu se velut equi implicent; ut bonum aut improbus decoloret, aut tardus impediat, aut turbidus inquietet. Fremit enim equus malitiae, seseque jactando currum laedit, gravat jugalem. Hunc bonus auriga demulcet, et in campum veritatis emittit, fraudis declinat anfractus. Tutus ad superiora cursus est, periculosus ad inferiora descensus. Inde quasi bene emeriti qui portaverunt jugum Verbi, usque ad Domini praesepe ducuntur, in quo non fenum est esca, sed panis qui descendit de coelo. Hujus 1945Dcurrus rotae sunt, de quibus dixit Propheta: Et spiritus vitae erat in rotis (Ezech. I, 20); eo quod currus animae teres atque rotundus sine ulla offensione volvatur.

18.

This is the foresight of the Word as of a good driver: that the mortal body joined to that soul which is not in itself subject to death may not make the driving of it difficult. First therefore let him tame these swift motions of the body, and bridle them with the bond of reason: then let him beware lest, by unequal motion, they entangle themselves like horses; so that either the wicked one discolors the good, or the slow one hinders it, or the turbulent one disquiets it. For the horse of malice snorts, and by tossing itself injures the chariot, and weighs down its yoke-fellow. This the good charioteer soothes, and sends forth into the field of truth, and turns aside from the windings of deceit. Safe is the course to higher things, perilous the descent to lower. Thence, as though well discharged from service, they who have borne the yoke of the Word are led even to the Lord's manger, in which the food is not hay, but the bread which came down from heaven. Of this 1945Dchariot there are wheels, of which the Prophet said: And the spirit of life was in the wheels (Ezech. I, 20); in that the chariot of the soul, smooth and round, rolls on without any stumbling.

19.

Posuit me currus Aminadab. Anima ergo currus, quae bonum rectorem sustinet. Si currus est anima, habet equos vel bonos vel malos. Boni equi virtutes animae: mali equi, passiones corporis sunt. Bonus ergo rector malos equos restringit et revocat, bonos incitat. Boni equi sunt quatuor, prudentia, temperantia, fortitudo, justitia: mali equi iracundia, concupiscentia, timor, iniquitas. Interdum ipsi equi inter se dissident, et aut iracundia protendit, aut 1946Atimor: et se invicem impediunt, et cursum retardant. At vero boni equi volant, et a terris ad superiora se subrigunt, animamque elevant; maxime si jugum habeant suave et onus leve dicentis: Tollite jugum meum super vos; jugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve (Matth. XI, 29).

19.

The chariot of Aminadab has set me. The soul therefore is a chariot, which bears a good ruler. If the soul is a chariot, it has horses either good or bad. The good horses are the virtues of the soul: the bad horses are the passions of the body. The good ruler therefore restrains and calls back the bad horses, and urges on the good. The good horses are four: prudence, temperance, fortitude, justice: the bad horses are wrathfulness, concupiscence, fear, iniquity. Sometimes the horses themselves disagree among themselves, and either wrathfulness stretches forward, or 1946Afear: and they hinder one another, and slow the course. But indeed the good horses fly, and raise themselves from the earth to higher things, and lift up the soul; especially if they have the sweet yoke and light burden of him who says: Take my yoke upon you; for my yoke is sweet, and my burden light (Matth. XI, 29).

20.

Ipse rector est, qui novit equos proprios gubernare, ut aequalis omnium cursus sit: velociores prudentia tardat, justitia admonet flagello proprio segniores; temperantia mansuetiores, fortitudo duriores reddit. Novit copulare discordes, ne forte currum suum dissipent. Itaque licet intelligibili spectaculo videre unamquamque animam cum summo certamine ad coelum rapi, festinantes equos qui priores praeveniant ad bravium Christi, quorum prius imponatur palma cervicibus. 1946BIsti sunt equi subjecti fidei jugo, astricti vinculo charitatis, justitiae frenis, retinaculis sobrietatis.

20.

He himself is the ruler, who knows how to govern his own horses, so that the course of all may be even: he slows the swifter ones by prudence, justice admonishes the more sluggish with its own lash; temperance makes them gentler, fortitude makes them harder. He knows how to couple the discordant, lest perchance they shatter his chariot. And so it is possible to see, by an intelligible spectacle, every soul caught up to heaven with the highest striving, the horses hastening to come first to the prize of Christ, upon whose necks the palm may first be laid. 1946BThese are the horses subjected to the yoke of faith, bound fast by the chain of charity, by the reins of justice, by the halters of sobriety.

21.

Pulchre ergo ait: Posuit me currus Aminadab, hoc est, pater populi: ipse autem qui pater populi, idem pater Naason, id est, serpentum. Jam tu recole quis sicut serpens in cruce pependerit pro salute universorum, et intelligas illam animam esse pacificam, cui Pater Deus praesul sit, Christus agitator; quia scriptum est et hoc nomen in nostris: Pater, pater, agitator Israel (IV Reg. XI, 12). Hic ergo agitator dicit:

21.

Beautifully therefore she says: The chariot of Aminadab has set me, that is, the father of the people: but he himself who is the father of the people is likewise the father of Naason, that is, of serpents. Now do thou call to mind who hung upon the cross like a serpent for the salvation of all men, and thou wilt understand that soul to be peaceable whose president is God the Father, and whose driver is Christ; because this name too is written in our books: Father, father, driver of Israel (IV Reg. XI, 12). [Vulg. 4 Kings 2:12 Pater mi, pater mi, currus Israel et auriga ejus, "My father, my father, the chariot of Israel and its charioteer"] This driver therefore says:

22.

(Vers. 12.) Convertere, Sunamitis, convertere. Bene et quasi agitator dicit, et quasi ad currum dicit: Convertere, Sunamitis, hoc est, pacifica; quae enim pacifica est anima, cito se convertit et corrigit, etiamsi ante peccavit: et magis eam 1609 Christus ascendit, 1946Cet regere dignatur, cui dicitur: Ascende in equos tuos, et equitatus tuus sanitas. Et alibi: Misisti equos tuos Tharsis (Habac. III, 8). Hi sunt equi Christi. Ascendit ergo equos suos Christus: ascendit Verbum Dei animas pias. Cum fuerit anima nostra pacifica, ut dicatur ei: Convertere; convertere Sunamitis, quod pacificam significat: tunc accipiet in se sicut signaculum Christum, hoc est, imaginem Dei, ut sit ad imaginem; quoniam Qualis coelestis, tales et coelestes, et oportet nos portare imaginem coelestis (I Cor. XV, 48), id est, pacem. Atque ut sciamus verum esse, habes in eisdem Canticis, in ultimo jam perfectae animae dici, quod et mihi dicat Dominus Jesus: Pone me ut signaculum in cor tuum, ut signaculum in brachium tuum (Cant. VIII, 6); ut luceat pax in corde meo, et in operibus 1946Dmeis Christus, formetur in me sapientia, et justitia, et redemptio.

22.

(Vers. 12.) Turn, O Sunamite, turn. [Vulg. Revertere, revertere, Sulamitis, "Return, return, O Sulamitess"] Well does he say it as a driver, and as one speaking to a chariot: Turn, O Sunamite, that is, O peaceable one; for the soul which is peaceable turns and corrects itself quickly, even if it has sinned before: and the more does 1609 Christ mount it, 1946Cand deign to rule it — he to whom it is said: Mount upon thy horses, and thy riding is health. And elsewhere: Thou hast sent thy horses of Tharsis (Habac. III, 8). [Vulg. Hab 3:8 qui ascendes super equos tuos, et quadrigae tuae salvatio, "who wilt mount upon thy horses, and thy four-horse chariots are salvation"] These are the horses of Christ. Christ therefore mounts his own horses: the Word of God mounts holy souls. When our soul has become peaceable, so that it is said to it: Turn; turn, O Sunamite, which signifies "peaceable," then it will receive into itself Christ as a seal, that is, the image of God, that it may be unto the image; since As is the heavenly, such are they also that are heavenly, and we must bear the image of the heavenly (I Cor. XV, 48), that is, peace. And that we may know it to be true, thou hast in the same Song of Songs, at the very end, this said to the soul now made perfect, which may the Lord Jesus say to me also: Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm (Cant. VIII, 6); that peace may shine in my heart, and Christ in my works, 1946Dthat wisdom, and justice, and redemption may be formed in me.

CAPUT SEPTIMUM.

CAPUT SEPTIMUM.

1.

(Vers. 1.) Quam speciosi facti sunt gressus tui in calceamentis, filia Aminadab! Moduli femorum tuorum similes torquibus, opere artificis. Non est dubium gressus hic quoque Ecclesiae, vel animae profectus significari. Quam speciosi sunt pedes evangelizantium pacem, evangelizantium bona (Esai. LII, 7)! Utique speciosos dicit evangelicae praedicationis et disputationis progressus, ut alibi dicitur: Transgredere flumina (Esai. XLVII, 2), hoc est, fluenta et lubrica istius mundi transcurre stabili 1947Amentis incessu. Quod de animae incessu dici David evidenter ostendit, afferens quod torrentem iniquitatum anima sua transiret (Psal. CCXIII, 5).

1.

(Vers. 1.) How shapely are thy steps made in sandals, O daughter of Aminadab! The measures of thy thighs are like collars, the work of a craftsman. [Vulg. Quam pulchri sunt gressus tui in calceamentis, filia principis! Juncturae femorum tuorum sicut monilia quae fabricata sunt manu artificis, "How beautiful are thy steps in shoes, O prince's daughter! the joints of thy thighs are like jewels"] There is no doubt that here too the steps of the Church, or the advance of the soul, are signified. How shapely are the feet of them that preach the gospel of peace, of them that preach good things (Esai. LII, 7)! [Vulg. Isa 52:7 Quam pulchri super montes pedes annuntiantis et praedicantis pacem, "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that announceth and preacheth peace"] Assuredly he calls shapely the advances of evangelical preaching and disputation, as it is said elsewhere: Pass over the rivers (Esai. XLVII, 2), that is, run across the streams and slippery places of this world with a steadfast 1947Agait of the mind. That this is said of the going of the soul, David plainly shows, when he brings forward that his soul had passed over the torrent of iniquities (Psal. CCXIII, 5).

2.

Sed quid sibi vult quod ait, in calceamentis speciosos esse gressus Ecclesiae? Legimus dictum ad Moysen: Solve calceamentum pedum tuorum (Exodi III, 5): quo videtur admonitus, ne corporalibus vinculis teneretur astrictus. Ergo speciosam in Canticis significat animae pulchritudinem, quae carne tanquam calceamento utitur, et in ipso calceamento impedimentum non patitur, sed incessus decore praecellit. Calceat ergo se anima ecclesiasticam gratiam, ut cursum vitae hujus et transitum cum decore praetereat. Quod fit si calceamentum suum non inquinet luto corporali, non in vitiorum mergat voragine: si castiget 1947Bcarnem suam, ne moretur ad cursum, et arvinae pinguis pondere praegravetur. Bonum calceamentum animae pudicitia est, bonus gressus est in vestigio castitatis. Sapientia autem amictus est animae, unde scriptum est: Honora eam, et amplectetur te (Prov. IV, 8). Utamur ergo corpore tanquam calceamento ad inferioris opera virtutis: ad ministerium, non praeceptum: ad obsequium, non delectationem: ad obedientiam, non dissensionem; et in via sapientiae vestigium collocemus, ne gressus nostros vis torrentis aliqua concludat.

2.

But what does he mean by saying that the steps of the Church are shapely in sandals? We read it said to Moses: Loose the sandal of thy feet (Exodi III, 5): whereby he seems to be admonished not to be held bound by bodily chains. Therefore in the Song of Songs he signifies the shapely loveliness of the soul, which uses the flesh as a sandal, and in that very sandal suffers no hindrance, but excels in the comeliness of its going. Let the soul therefore shoe itself with ecclesiastical grace, that it may pass over the course and passage of this life with comeliness. Which comes about if it does not befoul its own sandal with bodily mud, does not plunge it into the abyss of vices: if it chastise 1947Bits own flesh, lest it delay in the course, and be weighed down with the burden of fat grease. The good sandal of the soul is modesty, the good step is in the track of chastity. But wisdom is the mantle of the soul, whence it is written: Honor her, and she will embrace thee (Prov. IV, 8). [Vulg. Arripe illam, et exaltabit te; glorificaberis ab ea cum eam fueris amplexatus, "Take hold of her, and she shall exalt thee"] Let us use the body therefore as a sandal for the works of a lower virtue: for ministry, not for command: for compliance, not for delight: for obedience, not for dissension; and let us set our footstep in the way of wisdom, lest some force of a torrent shut in our steps.

3.

Ideo ad Moysen dictum est: Solve calceamentum (Exodi III, 5). Dictum est et ad Jesum Nave (Josue V, 16). De Christo autem dictum non est, sed magis scriptum est, dicente Baptista: Post me venit vir, 1947Ccujus non sum dignus calceamenta portare (Matth. III, 11); quia illi bene admonentur, ut solvant calceamentum suum, qui sine peccato esse non poterant. Hic autem non solum calceamentum non solvit, sed etiam calceamenta aliorum absolvit; quia non solum corpus suum a peccatis immune servavit, sed etiam omnium dedit indulgentiam peccatorum.

3.

Therefore it was said to Moses: Loose thy sandal (Exodi III, 5). It was said to Jesus son of Nave also (Josue V, 16). But of Christ it was not said, but rather it is written, the Baptist saying: After me there comes a man, 1947Cwhose sandals I am not worthy to bear (Matth. III, 11); [Vulg. Matt 3:11 qui autem post me venturus est, fortior me est, "he that shall come after me is mightier than I"; post me venit vir is the wording of John 1:30] because they are rightly admonished to loose their sandal who could not be without sin. But he not only did not loose his sandal, but even unlooses the sandals of others; because he not only kept his own body immune from sins, but also gave pardon of the sins of all.

4.

Ergo Ecclesia ad imitationem Christi speciosa est, et in calceamentis omni abluta delicto. Et fortasse quando sapientiam inter perfectos loquitur, speciosa est in superioribus membris: quando autem etiam inferioris gradus aut doctrinae homines Verbum sequuntur, fidei seriem non obliviscuntur, sacerdotis praecepta custodiunt, speciosa est in calceamentis. Plerumque clerus erravit, sacerdos mutavit sententiam, 1947Ddivites cum saeculi istius terreno rege senserunt, populus fidem propriam reservavit.

4.

Therefore the Church, in imitation of Christ, is shapely, and in its sandals washed from every fault. And perhaps when it speaks wisdom among the perfect, it is shapely in its upper members: but when men even of a lower rank or of lower teaching follow the Word, do not forget the order of faith, keep the precepts of the priest, it is shapely in its sandals. Very often the clergy has erred, the priest has changed his sentence, 1947Dthe rich have sided with the earthly king of this world, and the people has kept its own faith.

5.

Unde etiam de Domino Jesu bene possumus dicere, quia et in his quae corporalia sunt, speciosos gressus Verbum habeat, cum de moralibus disputatur. Et apostoli ideo fortasse nudis mittuntur pedibus, ne obumbretur eorum disputatio, sed eluceat. Itaque Ecclesia filia Aminadab, hoc est, voluntarii vel beneplaciti; quia voluntarius eam vel beneplacitus congregavit, et in calceamentis speciosa est. Meritoque additum est:

1948A1610 6. Moduli femorum tuorum similes torquibus, opere manuum artificis; ut posteritatis Ecclesiae ornamenta canerentur. Per femur enim insigne generationis agnoscimus, juxta illud: Accingere gladium tuum super femur, potentissime (Psal. XLIV, 4): quo significatur quod Filius Dei cum semetipsum exinanisset, Verbi accinctus divinitatem, et generationem calceatus humanam, prodiret ex Virgine omnibus daturus salutem. Moduli autem dicuntur ornamenta pretiosa, quae suspendi matronarum cervicibus solent. Tantus ergo processus Ecclesiae significatur, ut ornamentis pretiosissimis comparatus sit, et torquibus triumphantium; haec enim ornamenta sunt bellatorum. Unde et Symmachus Epitrachelia dixit, hoc est, quae sint circa collum. Sive ergo generatio Christi 1948Bex Virgine, sive Ecclesiae propagatio, specie quidem tamquam manu artificis torquibus adornatis, verae autem virtutis insignis spiritualibus cervices fidelium adornavit. Denique tota ista descriptio membrorum Ecclesiae plena decoris et laudis est. Nam et:

5.

Whence of the Lord Jesus also we can rightly say that even in those things which are bodily the Word has shapely steps, when there is disputing about moral matters. And the apostles are perhaps for this reason sent with bare feet, lest their disputation be overshadowed, but rather shine out. And so the Church is the daughter of Aminadab, that is, of the willing or the well-pleasing one; because a willing or well-pleasing one gathered her, and she is shapely in her sandals. And deservedly it is added:

1948A1610 6. The measures of thy thighs are like collars, the work of the hands of a craftsman; that the ornaments of the Church's posterity might be sung. For by the thigh we recognize the badge of generation, according to that word: Gird thy sword upon thy thigh, O most mighty (Psal. XLIV, 4): whereby it is signified that the Son of God, when he had emptied himself, girt with the divinity of the Word, and shod with human generation, should come forth from the Virgin to give salvation to all. But "measures" are the precious ornaments which are wont to be hung upon the necks of matrons. So great therefore is the advance of the Church that is signified, that it is compared to the most precious ornaments, and to the collars of those who triumph; for these are the ornaments of warriors. Whence Symmachus also said Epitrachelia, that is, the things which are about the neck. Whether therefore the generation of Christ 1948Bfrom the Virgin, or the propagation of the Church, in appearance indeed as by the hand of a craftsman with adorned collars, but with the spiritual badges of true virtue, has adorned the necks of the faithful. In short, this whole description of the members of the Church is full of comeliness and of praise. For also:

7.

(Vers. 2.) Umbilicus ejus tamquam crater tornatilis praedicatur, mixto non deficiens; eo quod in omni doctrina tornatus, plenitudine cognitionis, et potu non deficit spiritali. Et venter ejus non solum acervo tritici, id est, cibis fortioribus coelestis mysterii saginetur, verum etiam tamquam liliis quibusdam moralium suavitate repleatur. Unde et ipsa tamquam bene merita regina Christi sanguine coronatur, sicut scriptum est: Et ornatus capitis sui sicut purpura. Sanguis Christi purpura est, qui inficit 1948Csanctorum animas, non solum colore resplendens, sed etiam potestate; quia reges facit, et meliores reges quibus regnum dat aeternum.

7.

(Vers. 2.) Her navel is as a turned goblet, it is proclaimed, not lacking in the mixture; in that, being turned upon the lathe in every doctrine, in the fullness of knowledge, it does not lack the spiritual draught either. And her belly is not only fattened with the heap of wheat, that is, with the stronger foods of the heavenly mystery, but is also filled as with certain lilies with the sweetness of moral things. Whence she too, as one well deserving, is crowned as a queen with the blood of Christ, as it is written: And the adornment of her head is as purple. [Vulg. Cant 7:5 comae capitis tui sicut purpura regis, "the hairs of thy head as the purple of the king"] The blood of Christ is the purple, which dyes 1948Cthe souls of the saints, resplendent not only in color, but also in power; because it makes them kings, and better kings, to whom it gives an eternal kingdom.

8.

Umbilicus tuus ut crater tornatilis, numquam indigens poculis. Umbilicus animae ac venter subtilis est illius quae ascendit ad Christum; ideoque laudatur voce Sponsi dicentis: Umbilicus tuus ut crater tornatilis, non deficiens mixto; venter tuus acervus tritici muniti inter lilia. Est enim in omni doctrina tornatus, et potus spiritalis non deficiens plenitudine, et coelestium secretorum cognitione. Venter quoque animae mysticus, sicut umbilicus, quo ventre non solum fortem cibum sumit quo corda firmantur, sed etiam suave ac florulentum quo delectantur.

8.

Thy navel is as a turned goblet, never wanting cups. The navel of the soul and the belly of her who ascends to Christ is fine; and therefore she is praised by the voice of the Bridegroom saying: Thy navel is as a turned goblet, not lacking in the mixture; thy belly a heap of fortified wheat among the lilies. [Vulg. Cant 7:2 numquam indigens poculis… venter tuus sicut acervus tritici vallatus liliis; the first form Migne prints in this same paragraph agrees with the Vulgate, the second does not] For it is turned upon the lathe in every doctrine, and the spiritual draught is not lacking in fullness, and in the knowledge of heavenly secrets. The belly of the soul also is mystical, as the navel is; by which belly it takes not only the strong food by which hearts are made firm, but also the sweet and flowery food by which they are delighted.

9.

(Vers. 4.) Oculi tui stagna in Hesebon, in portis 1948Dfiliae multorum, Nares tuae sicut turris Libani. Stagna in Hesebon quid est, nisi in cogitationibus rationabilibus abundantia, quae est in portis Ecclesiae, cui merito defertur multitudo doctrinae? Filia enim multorum, plurimarum doctrinarum posteritas est et effectus, cujus nares odor est sacrificiorum, floribus omnibus praestans.

9.

(Vers. 4.) Thine eyes are pools in Hesebon, in the gates 1948Dof the daughter of many, Thy nostrils are as the tower of Lebanon. [Vulg. Cant 7:4 Oculi tui sicut piscinae in Hesebon quae sunt in porta filiae multitudinis. Nasus tuus sicut turris Libani, "Thy eyes like the fishpools in Hesebon which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude. Thy nose is as the tower of Libanus"] What are the pools in Hesebon, save an abundance in reasonable thoughts, which is in the gates of the Church, to which the multitude of doctrine is deservedly brought? For the daughter of many is the posterity and the effect of very many doctrines, whose nostrils are the odor of sacrifices, surpassing all flowers.

10.

Nasus tuus sicut turris Libani: praecelsa supra modum, et ideo prospicit faciem Damasci, gentilem scilicet populum odorans, et fidem ejus cujus gratia delictorum suorum detergeret fetorem. Facies 1949Aigitur Damasci fides est gentium, nullo obumbrata amictu, nullo adoperta vestitu, nuda ac libera, coelo magis intenta quam terris. Hanc speculantur et prospiciunt nares Ecclesiae, quod suave est in ea et odorum aspirationis et gratiae colligentes.

10.

Thy nose is as the tower of Lebanon: exceedingly lofty beyond measure, and therefore it looks forth upon the face of Damascus, smelling out, that is, the gentile people, and its faith, by whose grace it might wipe away the stench of its own faults. The face 1949Atherefore of Damascus is the faith of the Gentiles, overshadowed by no covering, hidden by no clothing, naked and free, more intent upon heaven than upon earth. This the nostrils of the Church watch and look forth upon, gathering what is sweet in it both of the breathings of odors and of grace.

11.

Et bene, Nares ejus sicut turris Libani; quia in sacrificiis odor suavis Ecclesiae est, in quibus est boni odoris hostia remissio peccatorum. Sume igitur has nares, o homo, ut a graveolentibus florulenta secernas, et tunc vivificabit te Dominus. Cum enim adverterit quid de ipso petis, ut avertat oculos mentis tuae a vanitate, cooperatur animae tuae, ut si specie aliqua capitur, aut duritia aut infirmitate non flectitur, flectat eam jugo Verbi, et habenis suis regat, ut voluntate Dei abducatur a vitiis, et vitae 1949Bodorem capiat aeternae.

11.

And well is it said, Her nostrils are as the tower of Lebanon; [Vulg. Cant. 7:4 nasus tuus sicut turris Libani, "thy nose is as the tower of Lebanon"] because in the sacrifices there is the sweet odor of the Church, in which sacrifices the remission of sins is a victim of good odor. Take therefore these nostrils, O man, that thou mayest sunder the flowery things from the rank-smelling, and then the Lord will quicken thee. For when he shall have marked what thou askest of him, that he may turn the eyes of thy mind away from vanity, he works together with thy soul, so that, if it is caught by some appearance, or is not bent either by hardness or by weakness, he may bend it with the yoke of the Word, and govern it with his own reins, that by the will of God it may be drawn away from vices, and may take the odor of everlasting 1949Blife.

12.

(Vers. 8.) Ascendam in palmam, et apprehendam sublimitatem ejus. Unde cognosce quia et istam ascendit, et perduxit eam ad locum palmae, quando et dicit ad eam: Quam pulchra et suavis facta es, charitas, in deliciis tuis! Statura tua similis facta est palmae (Cant. VII, 6). Et ipsa ait: Dixi, ascendam ad palmam. Sed etiam ipsa charitas palma est, ipsa est enim plenitudo victoriae; plenitudo enim Legis charitas est. Curramus ergo ut comprehendamus, curramus ut vincamus: qui vicit, ascendit in palmam, et manducat fructus ejus: qui vicit, jam non currit, sed sedet, sicut 1611 scriptum est: Qui vicerit, dabo ei sedere mecum in sede mea, sicut et ego vici, et sedi cum Patre meo in sede ipsius (Apoc. III, 21). Hinc 1949Cphilosophi currilia illa animarum in suis libris expressere certamina, nec tamen ad palmam pervenire potuerunt; quoniam suavitatem Verbi et altitudinem, illorum animae nescierunt. Ascendam, inquit, in palmam vincendo saeculum; ut teneam Verbi altitudinem, quam cognovit haec anima, in qua erat Verbi conversio; sic enim ait:

12.

(Vers. 8.) I will go up into the palm tree, and I will lay hold of its height. [Vulg. Dixi: Ascendam in palmam, et apprehendam fructus ejus, "I said: I will go up into the palm tree, and will take hold of the fruit thereof"] Whence know thou that he has gone up even to this soul, and has led her through to the place of the palm, when he also says to her: How beautiful and sweet art thou become, O charity, in thy delights! Thy stature is become like to a palm tree (Cant. VII, 6). [Vulg. Cant. 7:6–7 Quam pulchra es, et quam decora, carissima, in deliciis… Statura tua assimilata est palmae, "How beautiful art thou, and how comely, my dearest, in delights"] And she herself says: I said, I will go up to the palm tree. But charity itself also is a palm, for it is itself the fulness of victory; for the fulness of the Law is charity. Let us run therefore that we may lay hold, let us run that we may conquer: he who has conquered goes up into the palm, and eats the fruits thereof; he who has conquered runs no longer, but sits, as 1611 it is written: He that shall overcome, I will give to him to sit with me in my seat, as I also have overcome, and have sat with my Father in his seat (Apoc. III, 21). Hence 1949Cthe philosophers set forth in their books those race-course contests of souls, nor yet were they able to come through to the palm; because their souls knew not the sweetness and the height of the Word. I will go up, she says, into the palm by conquering the world; that I may hold the height of the Word, which this soul knew, in whom was the turning of the Word; for thus she says:

13.

(Vers. 10.) Ego fratri meo, et super me conversio ejus. Hunc sensum tertio repetivit diverse in Canticis canticorum. In principio ait: Frater meus mihi, et ego ei: qui pascitur in liliis usque dum aspiret dies, et inclinentur umbrae (Cant. II, 16). Deinde ait: Ego fratri meo, et frater meus mihi, qui pascitur inter lilia (Cant. VI, 2). In fine ait: Ego fratri meo, et super me conversio ejus. Primum ad institutionem 1949Danimae; ideo et praemisit: Frater meus mihi; illo enim demonstrante se etiam adhaerendi Deo sumpsit affectum. Quod sequitur secundum profectum: tertium secundum perfectionem? In primo quasi in institutione adhuc umbras videt anima, necdum Verbi appropinquantis revelatione commotas; et ideo adhuc ei dies Evangelii non refulgebat: in secundo sine umbrarum confusione odores pios carpit: in tertio jam perfecta requiem in se Verbo ministrat; ut convertatur super eam, et caput suum reclinet atque requiescat; et invitat ad agrum suum dicens: 1950A

13.

(Vers. 10.) I am my brother's, and his turning is upon me. [Vulg. Ego dilecto meo, et ad me conversio ejus, "I to my beloved, and his turning is toward me"] This sense she has repeated a third time, and diversely, in the Song of Songs. At the beginning she says: My brother is mine, and I am his: who feeds among the lilies until the day breathe, and the shadows incline (Cant. II, 16). [Vulg. Cant. 2:16–17 Dilectus meus mihi, et ego illi, qui pascitur inter lilia, donec aspiret dies, "My beloved to me, and I to him, who feedeth among the lilies, till the day break"] Then she says: I am my brother's, and my brother is mine, who feeds among the lilies (Cant. VI, 2). At the end she says: I am my brother's, and his turning is upon me. The first belongs to the instruction 1949Dof the soul; and therefore she also set first: My brother is mine; for by his showing himself she took also the affection of cleaving to God. That which follows is according to progress: the third according to perfection? In the first, as in instruction, the soul still sees shadows, not yet stirred by the revelation of the Word drawing near; and therefore the day of the Gospel did not yet shine upon her: in the second, without the confusion of shadows, she gathers holy odors: in the third, being now perfect, she ministers rest to the Word within herself; that he may be turned upon her, and lay back his head and rest; and she invites him to her field, saying: 1950A

14.

(Vers. 11.) Veni, frater meus, exeamus in agrum, requiescamus in castellis. Supra ad hortum invitabat; hic ad agrum habentem non solum florum gratiam, sed etiam triticum et hordeum, hoc est, solidarum firmamentum virtutum, ut fructus ejus aspiciat. Requiescamus, inquit, in castellis, ad quae Adam cum de paradiso ejectus esset, fuerat relegatus: in his requiescebat, sed terram operabatur.

14.

(Vers. 11.) Come, my brother, let us go out into the field, let us rest in the strongholds. [Vulg. Veni, dilecte mi, egrediamur in agrum, commoremur in villis, "Come, my beloved, let us go forth into the field, let us abide in the villages"] Above she was inviting him to the garden; here to a field having not only the grace of flowers, but also wheat and barley, that is, the firm foundation of solid virtues, that he may look upon its fruit. Let us rest, she says, in the strongholds, to which Adam, when he had been cast out of paradise, had been banished: in these he rested, but he tilled the earth.

15.

Qua autem ratione exire eum in agrum velit, intellectus manifestus est; ut quasi bonus pastor pascat gregem suum, lassos allevet, revocet errantes. Nam et si ista anima nova et vetera servavit, tamen sunt adhuc velut agni, qui lactis succo nutriendi sunt. Ergo quasi perfecta, non pro se, sed 1950Bpro aliis intervenit; ut de sinu patris exeat, ut procedat foras tamquam sponsus de thalamo suo; egrediens currat viam, ut infirmos lucretur; non in illo secreto patris solio, et in illa luce immoretur, quo invalidi equi conscendere non queunt; sed ut assumatur et inducatur in domum Sponsae atque secretum. Sit foris sibi, ut nobis intus: sit medium nostrum, etsi non videatur a nobis. Unde ait: Quis dabit te, frater, lactantem ubera matris meae? Inveniens te foris, osculabor te (Cant. VIII, 1). Bona anima, quae foris est, ut Verbum intus sit: illa extra corpus, ut Verbum habitet in nobis.

15.

But for what reason she would have him go out into the field, the understanding of it is plain: that as a good shepherd he may feed his flock, may lift up the weary, may call back the wandering. For although this soul has kept both new things and old, yet there are still, as it were, lambs who must be nourished with the juice of milk. Therefore, as one perfect, she intercedes not for herself, but 1950Bfor others; that he may go out from the bosom of the Father, that he may proceed forth as a bridegroom out of his chamber; that going out he may run his way, that he may win the weak; that he may not linger in that hidden throne of the Father, and in that light, whereto weak horses are not able to climb; but that he may be taken up and brought into the house and the secret place of the Bride. Let him be without for himself, that he may be within for us: let him be in our midst, even though he be not seen by us. Whence she says: Who shall give thee to me, my brother, sucking the breasts of my mother? Finding thee without, I will kiss thee (Cant. VIII, 1). [Vulg. Quis mihi det te fratrem meum sugentem ubera matris meae, ut inveniam te foris, et deosculer te, "Who shall give thee to me for my brother, sucking the breasts of my mother, that I may find thee without, and kiss thee"] Good is the soul which is without, that the Word may be within: she outside the body, that the Word may dwell in us.

16.

Assumam te, inquit, et inducam te (Ibid., 2). Recte assumitur, et inducitur Verbum Dei; quia pulsat animam, ut aperiatur sibi ostium: et nisi 1950Capertum sibi ostium invenerit, non intrat. Si quis autem aperuerit januam, intrat et coenat. Sic Sponsa Verbum assumit, ut assumendo doceatur. Unde non immerito ascendit ad hanc, ad superiores mansiones, et semper processum accipit virtutis.

16.

I will take thee up, she says, and I will bring thee in (Ibid., 2). [Vulg. Cant. 8:2 Apprehendam te, et ducam in domum matris meae, "I will take hold of thee, and bring thee into my mother's house"] Rightly is the Word of God taken up and brought in; because he knocks at the soul, that the door may be opened to him: and unless 1950Che shall have found the door opened to him, he does not enter. But if any man shall have opened the gate, he enters and sups. Thus the Bride takes up the Word, that by taking him up she may be taught. Whence not undeservedly he goes up to her, to the higher dwellings, and she ever receives an advance of virtue.

17.

Veni, frater meus, exeamus in agrum, requiescamus in castellis, diluculo surgamus in vineis, videamus si floruit vitis. Multi agri fructus habent; sed ille melior est, qui et fructibus redundat et floribus. Est ergo Ecclesiae ager diversis fecundus copiis. Hic cernas germina virginitatis flore vernantia; illic tamquam in campis silvae, viduitatem gravitate pollentem; alibi tamquam uberem frugem Ecclesiae, segetem conjugii replentem mundi horrea, ac veluti maritatae vineae fetibus torcularia Domini Jesu redundantia, 1950Din quibus fidelis conjugii fructus exuberat.

17.

Come, my brother, let us go out into the field, let us rest in the strongholds, let us rise at daybreak in the vineyards, let us see whether the vine has flowered. [Vulg. Cant. 7:12 Mane surgamus ad vineas; videamus si floruit vinea, "Let us get up early to the vineyards; let us see if the vineyard flourish"] Many fields have fruits; but that one is better which abounds both with fruits and with flowers. The field of the Church, then, is fruitful with diverse plenties. Here thou mayest discern the buds of virginity blooming with flower; there, as woods in the plains, widowhood strong in gravity; elsewhere, as the rich produce of the Church, the harvest of wedlock filling the barns of the world, and, as it were with the offspring of a wedded vine, the winepresses of the Lord Jesus overflowing, 1950Din which the fruit of faithful wedlock abounds.

18.

Sponsa desiderii sui copiam adepta, loquitur ad Sponsum: Veni, frater meus, exeamus in agrum, requiescamus in castellis, diluculo surgamus ad vineas. Advertis quemadmodum Sponsa Verbum invitet Dei, ut veniat in terras et tollat peccata mundi. Ager hic erat ante desertus, sentibus nostrorum squalidus delictorum, horridus spinis. Castellum erat in quo religatus Adam perpetuo suae posteritatis haeredes stringebat exsilio. Illo ergo ducit Ecclesia Christum, 1951Aut Adam liberet. Deinde exsulibus absolutis, coepit mundi istius ager idoneos habere cultores: et qui erat ante jejunus, factus est vitis aeternae 1612 plantatione fecundus. Nec sic tamen spiritalibus laeta palmitibus evagatur, sed ad has vineas vocat Christum, ubi psallentes sint et orantes, ubi noctibus ac diebus fructus innoxius perseveret.

18.

The Bride, having obtained the plenty of her desire, speaks to the Bridegroom: Come, my brother, let us go out into the field, let us rest in the strongholds, let us rise at daybreak to the vineyards. Thou seest how the Bride invites the Word of God, that he may come into the lands and take away the sins of the world. This field was before a desert, squalid with the briars of our offences, bristling with thorns. There was a stronghold in which Adam, bound fast, held the heirs of his posterity in perpetual exile. Thither, then, the Church leads Christ, 1951Athat he may set Adam free. Then, the exiles being loosed, the field of this world began to have fit tillers: and that which was before hungry was made fruitful by the planting 1612 of the eternal vine. Nor yet does she so range abroad, glad with spiritual shoots, but she calls Christ to these vineyards, where there are those who sing psalms and those who pray, where by nights and by days a harmless fruit endures.

19.

(Vers. 12.) Ibi, inquit, dabo ubera mea, ibi mandragorae dederunt odorem. Plerique discernunt quemdam inter mandragoras sexum; ut et mares et feminas putent esse, sed feminas gravis odoris. Significat ergo gentes, quae ante fetebant, cum essent infirmiores evirata quadam imbecillitate perfidiae, boni odoris fructus ferre coepisse, postquam in adventum Domini crediderunt. Legimus etiam quod 1951Bmandragoras acceperit sancta Rachel a sorore sua Lia, ut concederet ei illa nocte dormire cum sancto Jacob. Mandragoras Ruben primogenitus attulerat matri suae Liae, quae lippientibus oculis, Synagogae figuram accepit; quia Christi gratiam videre non potuit, hebetato debilis mentis obtutu. Quo declaratur quod fructus quos ante Synagoga a primogenito Dei Filio susceperat, Ecclesiae concessit. Sed quia illius noctis petito concubitu concepit et genuit Lia suae posteritatis haeredem, implevit mysterium; quoniam credentibus apostolis, reliquiae Judaeorum per electionem gratiae salvae factae sunt, unde ait Ecclesia:

19.

(Vers. 12.) There, she says, will I give my breasts, there the mandrakes have given their odor. [Vulg. Cant. 7:12–13 ibi dabo tibi ubera mea. Mandragorae dederunt odorem, "there will I give thee my breasts. The mandrakes have given a smell"] Very many distinguish a certain sex among mandrakes, so that they think there are both males and females, but that the females are of heavy odor. It signifies therefore that the nations, which before stank, since they were the weaker by a certain unmanned feebleness of unbelief, began to bear fruits of good odor after they believed unto the coming of the Lord. We read also that 1951Bholy Rachel received mandrakes from her sister Lia, that she might grant her to sleep that night with holy Jacob. Ruben the firstborn had brought the mandrakes to his mother Lia, who, with bleared eyes, took on the figure of the Synagogue; because she could not see the grace of Christ, the gaze of her feeble mind being dulled. By which it is declared that the fruits which the Synagogue had before received from the firstborn Son of God, she yielded up to the Church. But because, the lying-together of that night having been sought, Lia conceived and bore an heir of her posterity, she fulfilled the mystery; since, when the apostles believed, the remnant of the Jews were made safe through the election of grace, whence the Church says:

20.

(Vers. 13.) Nova et vetera, frater meus, servavi tibi. Quis dabit te fratrem lactantem ubera matris meae? Inveniens te foris, osculabor te, et quidem non 1951Cspernent me. Assumam te et inducam in domum matris meae, et in secretum ejus quae concepit me, ibi docebis me. Habens ergo informationem novarum et veterum Scripturarum, nec despicabilem se sentiens, non solum orationibus in secreto cordis Verbum tenet, verum etiam psallentis chori vocibus, velut quibusdam eum gratiae osculis osculatur.

20.

(Vers. 13.) New things and old, my brother, have I kept for thee. Who shall give thee to me for a brother, sucking the breasts of my mother? Finding thee without, I will kiss thee, and indeed they shall not 1951Cdespise me. I will take thee up and bring thee into my mother's house, and into the secret place of her that conceived me: there thou shalt teach me. [Vulg. Cant. 7:13–8:2 omnia poma nova et vetera, dilecte mi, servavi tibi… et jam me nemo despiciat. Apprehendam te, et ducam in domum matris meae: ibi me docebis, "all fruits new and old, my beloved, I have kept for thee… and now no man may despise me. I will take hold of thee, and bring thee into my mother's house: there thou shalt teach me"] Having therefore the shaping of the new and the old Scriptures, and not feeling herself contemptible, she holds the Word not only by prayers in the secret of the heart, but also with the voices of a psalm-singing choir she kisses him, as with certain kisses of grace.

21.

Nova et vetera, frater meus, servavi tibi; hoc est dicere, teneo omnia mandata novi et veteris Testamenti. Sola hoc dicere Ecclesia potest: non dicit alia congregatio, non dicit Synagoga, nec secundum litteram nova tenens; nec secundum spiritum vetera. Non dicit haeresis Manichaea, Vetera servavi tibi, quae prophetas non suscipit. Merito dealbata dicitur, quae utriusque fulget gratia Testamenti. 1951D

21.

New things and old, my brother, have I kept for thee; this is to say, I hold all the commandments of the New and the Old Testament.
