The Church alone can say this: no other congregation says it, the Synagogue does not say it, holding neither the new things according to the letter, nor the old things according to the spirit. The Manichaean heresy does not say, The old things have I kept for thee, seeing that it does not receive the prophets. Deservedly is she called made white, who shines with the grace of either Testament. 1951D

22.

Adjuravi vos, filiae Hierusalem, quod suscitetis et resuscitetis dilectionem usquequo voluerit. Adhuc quaerit suscitantem, et suscitari eam sibi poscit a filiabus Hierusalem, quarum gratia, hoc est, animarum fidelium Sponsum in amorem sui uberiorem desiderat provocari. Dicunt itaque virtutes illae, quae portas custodiunt coeli.

22.

I have adjured you, O daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir up and stir up again love, until such time as it shall will. [Vulg. Cant. 8:4 Adjuro vos, filiae Jerusalem, ne suscitetis, neque evigilare faciatis dilectam, donec ipsa velit, "I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, till she please"] Still she seeks one that stirs up, and demands that it be stirred up for her by the daughters of Jerusalem, for whose sake, that is, for the sake of faithful souls, she desires that the Bridegroom be provoked to a more abundant love of her. And so those powers speak, which guard the gates of heaven.

CAPUT OCTAVUM.

CHAPTER THE EIGHTH.

1.

(Vers. 5.) Quae est quae haec ascendit candida, innitens super fratrem suum? Quomodo ea quae posuit 1952Acaput suum super laevam sapientiae, ut aperiret manus pauperi ad subveniendum inopi; partitis aut derelictis sibi, non invasis atque direptis divitiis uteretur, quae gloriae cupida bonis etiam operibus acquisivit, non inani saecularis jactantia ambivit dignitatis, hoc est enim caput suum, et quoddam sensuum principale supra manum sapientiae constituere. Ea, inquam, anima meritis ascendit albentibus, ex isto vitae hujus ut plerique habent deserto, ad illum florentem semper locum jucunditatis aeternae.

1.

(Vers. 5.) Who is she who this one comes up white, leaning upon her brother? [Vulg. Cant. 8:5 Quae est ista quae ascendit de deserto, deliciis affluens, innixa super dilectum suum?, "Who is this that cometh up from the desert, flowing with delights, leaning upon her beloved?"] In what manner she who has set 1952Aher head upon the left hand of wisdom, that she might open her hand to the poor for the relieving of the needy; that she might use riches divided or left to her, not riches seized and plundered — riches which, desirous of glory, she acquired even by good works, and did not court by the empty boasting of worldly dignity: for this it is to set one's own head, and a certain ruling part of the senses, above the hand of wisdom. That soul, I say, ascends by whitening merits, out of the desert of this life, as very many hold it to be, unto that ever-flowering place of eternal gladness.

2.

Istae sunt virtutes, quae et in Esaiae libro dicunt: Quis est iste qui advenit de Edom, rubor vestimentorum ex Bosor, sic speciosus in stola (Esai. LXIII, 1)? Istae, inquam, sunt quae mirantur ex isto confragoso scopulosoque deserto aliquam ascendere animam 1952Bposse, sine magnorum labe vitiorum; et ideo gratulantur repertam, quae vestimenta innocentiae naturalis non polluerit atramento insipientiae saecularis, sed magis sapientiae spiritalis et gratiae candore mundarit. Admirantur filiae Hierusalem, sanctae animae patriarcharum et prophetarum, veterumque justorum, vel coelestes potentiae dicentes: Quae est haec quae ascendit dealbata innitens super fratrem suum? Hoc est, talibus solemnitatibus fulget Ecclesia, et quae ante per diem fusca erat, jam splendet in noctibus, et relucet. Ipse quoque Dominus tanto munere psallentium delectatus ait:

2.

These are the powers which also in the book of Isaiah say: Who is this that comes from Edom, the redness of garments out of Bosor, so shapely in his robe (Esai. LXIII, 1)? [Vulg. Quis est iste, qui venit de Edom, tinctis vestibus de Bosra? iste formosus in stola sua, "Who is this that cometh from Edom, with dyed garments from Bosra? this well-formed one in his robe"] These, I say, are they that marvel that out of this broken and rocky desert any soul is able to ascend 1952Bwithout the ruin of great vices; and therefore they give thanks that she has been found, who has not defiled the garments of natural innocence with the ink of worldly folly, but rather has cleansed them with the whiteness of spiritual wisdom and of grace. The daughters of Jerusalem marvel, the holy souls of the patriarchs and of the prophets and of the just of old, or the heavenly powers, saying: Who is this that comes up made white, leaning upon her brother? That is, with such solemnities the Church shines, and she who before was dark through the day now is splendid in the nights, and gives back her light. The Lord himself also, delighted with so great an offering of them that sing psalms, says:

3.

(Vers. 6.) Pone me ut signaculum super cor tuum, ut sigillum super brachium tuum; quia valida est ut mors charitas, durus 1613 sicut inferi, zelus tuus. 1952CAlae ejus, alae ignis, et flammae. Aqua multa excludere non poterit charitatem, et flumina non obruent eam. Quia vidit tantam devotionem Ecclesia, idoneos putat esse jam populos, qui in corde nostro et in brachio signaculum ejus portare possimus. Ipsum enim Pater signavit Deus, et qui testimonium ejus accepit, signavit quia Deus verax est; et ideo operantes cibum permanentem in vitam aeternam, signati sunt ad imaginem et similitudinem Christi, qui est imago invisibilis Dei. Sicut ergo Deus verax est; et tu signa in tuo sensu et opere veritatem; ut os tuum non loquatur mendacium, manus tuae non operentur opera hominum, quae sunt fallacis istius mundi: sed illa quae Dei sunt, ut pauperibus largiantur, debiles sublevent, mortuos honorent tumulis sepulturae. His operibus 1952Dcharitas quaeritur, ut nemo possit a Christo vel periculo mortis avelli; unde ille ait: Quis nos separabit a Christo? Tribulatio, an angustia, an persecutio (Rom. VIII, 35)? Et infra: Confido enim quia neque mors, neque vita, neque angeli (Ibid.).

3.

(Vers. 6.) Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a signet upon thy arm; because charity is strong as death, hard 1613 as hell is thy zeal. 1952CIts wings are wings of fire, and of flames. Much water shall not be able to shut out charity, and the floods shall not overwhelm it. [Vulg. Cant. 8:6–7 quia fortis est ut mors dilectio, dura sicut infernus aemulatio; lampades ejus lampades ignis atque flammarum. Aquae multae non potuerunt exstinguere caritatem, nec flumina obruent illam, "because love is strong as death, jealousy hard as hell; the lamps thereof are lamps of fire and of flames. Many waters could not quench charity, neither shall the floods drown it"] Because the Church has seen so great a devotion, she thinks the peoples now fit, that we may be able to bear his seal in our heart and on our arm. For him the Father, God, has sealed, and he who has received his testimony has set his seal that God is true; and therefore they that work the food which endures unto life everlasting are sealed unto the image and likeness of Christ, who is the image of the invisible God. As therefore God is true, do thou also seal truth in thy thought and in thy work; that thy mouth speak not a lie, that thy hands work not the works of men, which are of this deceitful world, but those things which are of God: that they may give bountifully to the poor, may raise up the weak, may honor the dead with the mounds of burial. By these works 1952Dcharity is sought, that no one may be able to be torn away from Christ even by the peril of death; whence he says: Who shall separate us from Christ? Tribulation, or distress, or persecution (Rom. VIII, 35)? [Vulg. Quis ergo nos separabit a caritate Christi?, "Who then shall separate us from the charity of Christ?"] And below: For I am confident that neither death, nor life, nor angels (Ibid.). [Vulg. Rom. 8:38 Certus sum enim, "For I am sure"]

4.

Ea specie etiam zelus durus est charitatis, et alae ejus alae ignis. Habet alas sicut columba, sunt enim pennae columbae deargentatae, quibus evolat qui diligit, dicens: Ecce elongavi fugiens, et mansi in solitudine (Psal. LIV, 8). Sed alae charitatis alae sunt ignis, quibus dilectionis inflammat ardorem. 1953AHoc vapore fecit Dominus ferventes Angelos suos spiritus, et ministros suos ignem urentem, sed non exurentem. Legimus et alam templi, supra quam diabolus tentans, fecit ascendere Salvatorem, quae erat in templi culmine (Matth. IV, 3). Sunt ergo fastigia pietatis, sunt culmina charitatis, quae vaporem gratiae pectoribus humanis adolere consueverunt; ut multa aqua exstinguere atque excludere non queat charitatem, et flumina eam saecularium tempestatum nulla concludant. Diximus quia charitas excludit timorem, non diximus quod excludit omnem perturbationem; etenim qui Deum diligit, profunda est ei confirmatae mentis tranquillitas.

4.

In that same fashion also the zeal of charity is hard, and its wings are wings of fire. It has wings as a dove, for the feathers of the dove are silvered, wherewith he who loves flies forth, saying: Behold, I have gone far off in flight, and I abode in the wilderness (Psal. LIV, 8). But the wings of charity are wings of fire, wherewith it kindles the ardor of love. 1953AWith this heat the Lord made his angels fervent spirits, and his ministers a fire that burns, but does not burn up. We read also of a wing of the temple, upon which the devil, tempting, made the Savior to go up, which was on the pinnacle of the temple (Matth. IV, 3). There are therefore heights of piety, there are pinnacles of charity, which have been wont to kindle the vapor of grace in human breasts; so that much water is not able to quench nor to shut out charity, and no floods of worldly tempests shut it in. We have said that charity shuts out fear; we have not said that it shuts out every disturbance; for indeed to him who loves God there is a deep tranquillity of a mind confirmed.

5.

(Vers. 7.) Aquae, inquit, multae non poterunt 1953Bexcludere charitatem, et flumina non inundabunt ipsam. Multa aqua diversarum est passionum, et flumina saecularium cupiditatum corporalibus motibus incitata, quae tamen murum charitatis subvertere non possunt. Ideoque charitate fundatus dicit: Torrentem pertransivit anima nostra (Psal. CXXIII, 5). Numquid aqua maris excludere Moysis potuit charitatem? Et ut secundum litteram tibi psalmorum series suffragetur, nempe diligens Deum, tutum sibi credidit iter esse per maria: qui autem non dilexerunt Deum, hi demersi fluctibus, dignum sacrilegii sui exitum pertulerunt.

5.

(Vers. 7.) Many waters, he says, shall not be able 1953Bto shut out charity, and the floods shall not overflow it. Much water is of diverse passions, and the floods of worldly desires stirred up by the motions of the body, which nevertheless cannot overthrow the wall of charity. And therefore one grounded in charity says: Our soul has passed through the torrent (Psal. CXXIII, 5). Could the water of the sea shut out the charity of Moses? And that the series of the psalms may support thee according to the letter: assuredly he who loved God believed the way through the seas to be safe for him; but they who loved not God, being sunk in the waves, endured an end worthy of their sacrilege.

6.

(Vers. 5.) Quae est haec quae ascendit, innitens super fratrem suum? Superius dixerunt, Quaenam est haec prospiciens tanquam diluculum, speciosa 1953Csicut luna, electa ut sol? Hic plus quod adjiceretur, inventum est, eo quod Verbo Dei innixa ascenderat; perfectiores enim supra Christum recumbunt, sicut et Joannes in Christi pectore recumbebat. Ita ergo et haec vel incumbebat in Christum, vel supra ipsum se reclinabat: aut certe quoniam de nuptiis loquimur, jam quasi tradita in Christi dextera, in thalamum ducebatur Sponso. Et quia jam copula charitatis est, blanditur ei sponsus, et dicit:

6.

(Vers. 5.) Who is this that comes up, leaning upon her brother? Above they said, Who then is this that looks forth as the daybreak, shapely 1953Cas the moon, choice as the sun? [Vulg. Cant. 6:9 Quae est ista quae progreditur quasi aurora consurgens, pulchra ut luna, electa ut sol?, "Who is she that cometh forth as the morning rising, beautiful as the moon, bright as the sun?"] Here something more was found to be added, in that she had ascended leaning upon the Word of God; for the more perfect recline upon Christ, as John also was reclining on the breast of Christ. So therefore this one also either was leaning upon Christ, or was laying herself back upon him: or assuredly, since we are speaking of a marriage, being now as it were delivered over into the right hand of Christ, she was being led into the chamber for the Bridegroom. And because there is now a bond of charity, the bridegroom caresses her, and says:

7.

Sub arbore malo elevavi te, illic parturivit te mater tua, illic parturivit te quae peperit te. Bona anima quae requiescit sub arbore fructuosa, et maxime boni odoris. Nam si Nathanael bonus, in quo dolus nullus erat, sub arbore fici visus est; utique bona anima, quae sub arbore mali elevata est a 1953DSponso suo. Plus est enim elevari, quam videri: plus etiam a sponso elevari. Nam etsi Nathanael videbatur sub arbore, tamen anima ejus sponsa non erat; quia occulte veniebat ad Christum, quia verebatur Judaeos. Non erat speciosa sicut luna, electa ut sol, quae erat in umbra; quia quae sponsa per diem nubit, publice confitetur. Ideo ista sub arbore mali, illa sub ficu; quia haec confessionis suae odorem diffundebat longius, illa habebat puritatis innocentiae suavitatem, flagrantiam spiritus non habebat.

7.

Under the apple tree I raised thee up; there thy mother travailed with thee, there she travailed with thee that bore thee. [Vulg. Cant. 8:5 Sub arbore malo suscitavi te; ibi corrupta est mater tua, ibi violata est genitrix tua, "Under the apple tree I raised thee up: there thy mother was corrupted, there she was defloured that bore thee"] Good is the soul which rests under a fruitful tree, and one especially of good odor. For if Nathanael was good, in whom there was no guile, and was seen under a fig tree, surely good is the soul which under the apple tree was raised up by 1953Dher Bridegroom. For it is more to be raised up than to be seen: and more still to be raised up by the bridegroom. For although Nathanael was seen under the tree, yet his soul was not a bride; because he came secretly to Christ, because he feared the Jews. She was not shapely as the moon, choice as the sun, who was in the shadow; because a bride who weds by day confesses publicly. Therefore this one is under the apple tree, that one under the fig; because this one spread abroad more widely the odor of her confession, while that one had the sweetness of the purity of innocence, and had not the burning of the spirit.

8.

Illic, inquit, parturivit te mater tua, illic parturivit te quae peperit te; ibi enim nascimur, ibi renascimur. 1954AParturiuntur autem in quibus imago Christi formatur, unde 1614 et Paulus ait: Filioli quos ego parturio, donec formetur Christus in vobis (Galat. IV, 19). Parturivit enim qui in utero accipit spiritum salutis, et aliis infundit. Unde quoniam jam formatus in hac erat Christus, ait:

8.

There, he says, thy mother travailed with thee, there she travailed with thee that bore thee; for there we are born, there we are born again. 1954ABut those are travailed with in whom the image of Christ is formed, whence 1614 Paul also says: Little children, whom I travail with, until Christ be formed in you (Galat. IV, 19). [Vulg. Filioli mei, quos iterum parturio, "My little children, of whom I am in labour again"] For he has travailed who receives in the womb the spirit of salvation, and pours it into others. Whence, since Christ was now formed in her, he says:

9.

(Vers. 6.) Pone me ut signaculum in cor tuum, ut sigillum in brachium tuum. Signaculum Christi in fronte est, signaculum in corde: in fronte, ut semper confiteamur: in corde, ut semper diligamus: signaculum in brachio, ut semper operemur. Luceat ergo imago ejus in confessione nostra, luceat in lectione, luceat in operibus et factis; ut si fieri potest, tota ejus species exprimatur in nobis. Ipse sit caput nostrum, quia caput viri Christus: ipse 1954Boculus noster, ut per illum videamus Patrem: ipse vox nostra, per quam loquamur ad Patrem: ipse dextera, per quam Deo Patri sacrificium nostrum offeramus. Ipse quoque est signaculum nostrum, quod est charitatis et perfectionis insigne; quia diligens pater signavit Filium, sicut legimus: Quem Pater signavit Deus (Joan. VI, 27). Charitas itaque nostra Christus. Bona charitas, quando obtulit se morti pro dilectis: bona charitas quae peccatum remisit.

9.

(Vers. 6.) Set me as a seal in thy heart, as a signet on thy arm. The seal of Christ is on the forehead, the seal is in the heart: on the forehead, that we may always confess; in the heart, that we may always love; the seal on the arm, that we may always work. Let his image therefore shine in our confession, let it shine in our reading, let it shine in our works and deeds; that, if it can be brought to pass, his whole appearance may be expressed in us. Let him be our head, because the head of the man is Christ; let him be 1954Bour eye, that through him we may see the Father; let him be our voice, whereby we may speak to the Father; let him be the right hand, whereby we may offer our sacrifice to God the Father. He also is our seal, which is the badge of charity and of perfection; because the Father, loving, sealed the Son, as we read: Whom the Father, God, hath sealed (Joan. VI, 27). [Vulg. Hunc enim Pater signavit Deus, "For him hath God the Father sealed"] And so Christ is our charity. Good is the charity, when he offered himself to death for those he loved: good is the charity which forgave sin.

10.

Et ideo anima nostra induat charitatem, et charitatem ejusmodi quae sit valida ut mors; quia sicut mors finis peccatorum est, ita et charitas; quoniam qui diligit Dominum, peccare desinit: Charitas enim non cogitat malum, neque gaudet super 1954Ciniquitate, sed omnia sustinet (I Cor. XIII, 5). Nam qui quae sua sunt non quaerit, quomodo aliena quaeret? Est et mors illa valida per lavacrum, per quod peccatum omne sepelitur, et culpa dimittitur. Talis erat charitas quam deferebat Evangelica illa mulier, de qua Dominus ait: Remissa sunt peccata ejus multa, quoniam dilexit multum (Luc. VII, 47). Est et illa mors sanctorum martyrum valida, quae culpam abolet superiorem; et ideo valida, cujus non impar est charitas, quae adaequatur martyrum passioni, ut auferat meritum delictorum.

10.

And therefore let our soul put on charity, and charity of such a kind as is strong as death; because as death is the end of sins, so also is charity; since he who loves the Lord ceases to sin: for charity thinks no evil, neither rejoices over 1954Ciniquity, but bears all things (I Cor. XIII, 5). For he who seeks not the things that are his own, how shall he seek the things of another? There is also that strong death through the laver, by which every sin is buried and the fault is remitted. Such was the charity which that woman of the Gospel bore, of whom the Lord says: Her many sins are forgiven, because she has loved much (Luc. VII, 47). [Vulg. Remittuntur ei peccata multa, "Many sins are forgiven her"] There is also that strong death of the holy martyrs, which does away former guilt; and therefore is it strong, and charity is not unequal to it, charity which is made equal to the passion of the martyrs, that it may take away the desert of offences.

11.

Zelus quoque ut inferi, quoniam qui Dei zelum habet, pro Christo nec suis parcit. Itaque et mortem habet charitas, et zelum habet charitas, et alas ignis habet charitas. Denique Christus diligens 1954DMoysen, in igne ei apparuit. Et Hieremias habens in se donum divinae charitatis, dicebat: Et erat ignis inflammans in ossibus meis, et dissolutus sum undique, et ferre non possum (Jerem. XX, 9). Bona igitur charitas alas habens ignis ardentis, quae volitat per pectora et corda sanctorum, et exurit quidquid materiale et terrenum est: quidquid vero sincerum est, probat; et quod contigerit, suo igne meliorat.

11.

Zeal also is as hell, since he who has the zeal of God spares not even his own for Christ. And so charity has death, and charity has zeal, and charity has wings of fire. In fine, Christ, loving 1954DMoses, appeared to him in fire. And Jeremiah, having in himself the gift of divine charity, said: And there was a fire flaming in my bones, and I am undone on every side, and I cannot bear it (Jerem. XX, 9). [Vulg. et factus est in corde meo quasi ignis exaestuans, claususque in ossibus meis: et defeci, ferre non sustinens, "and it became in my heart as a burning fire, shut up in my bones: and I fainted, not able to bear it"] Good therefore is charity, having the wings of burning fire, which flits through the breasts and the hearts of the saints, and burns up whatever is material and earthly; but whatever is unmixed it proves, and what it has touched it makes better with its own fire.

12.

Hunc ignem in terram misit Dominus Jesus, et refulsit fides, accensa est devotio, illuminata est charitas, justitia resplenduit. Hoc igne inflammavit corda apostolorum suorum, sicut testatur Cleophas 1955Adicens: Nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis, cum aperiret Scripturas (Luc. XXIV, 32)? Alae igitur ignis in flamma, Scripturae sunt divinae. Denique aperiebat Scripturas, et ignis exibat, atque audientium corda penetrabat. Et verae alae ignis, quia: Eloquia Domini eloquia casta, argentum igne examinatum (Psalm. XI, 7). Cum Paulus quoque assumeretur a Christo, vidit circumfulsisse super se lucem, et super eos qui simul erant, cecidit a pavore, et probatior resurrexit. Denique apostolus factus est, qui presecutor advenerat. Spiritus quoque sanctus descendit, et replevit totam domum in qua erant plurimi sedentes, et visae sunt dispertitae linguae tanquam ignis. Bonae alae, alae charitatis, vere alae quae volitabant per ora apostolorum: et alae ignis, quia purgatum 1955Bsermonem loquebantur.

12.

This fire the Lord Jesus sent upon the earth, and faith shone out, devotion was kindled, charity was lit up, justice shone again. With this fire he inflamed the hearts of his apostles, as Cleophas testifies, 1955Asaying: Was not our heart burning within us, when he opened the Scriptures (Luc. XXIV, 32)? The wings of fire in the flame, therefore, are the divine Scriptures. In fine, he was opening the Scriptures, and fire was going forth, and was piercing the hearts of them that heard. And they are true wings of fire, because: The words of the Lord are chaste words, silver tried by the fire (Psalm. XI, 7). When Paul also was taken up by Christ, he saw a light shine round about upon him, and upon those who were with him; he fell from terror, and rose up the more approved. In fine he was made an apostle, who had come as a presecutor. The Holy Spirit also came down, and filled the whole house in which very many were sitting, and there appeared parted tongues as it were of fire. Good wings, wings of charity, truly wings which flitted through the mouths of the apostles: and wings of fire, because they spoke a purified 1955Bspeech.

13.

His alis evolavit Enoch, raptus ad coelum; his alis evolavit Elias, curru igneo et equis igneis ad superna translatus. His alis Dominus Deus per columnam ignis ducebat patrum plebem. Has alas habuit Seraphim, quando sumpsit carbones ignis de altari, et tetigit os prophetae, et iniquitates ejus abstulit, et peccata purgavit. Harum alarum igne purgati sunt filii Levi, et baptizabantur populi nationum, sicut testificatur Joannes, dicens de Domino Jesu: Ipse vos baptizabit in spiritu et igne (Matth. III, 11). Merito uri renes suos volebat et cor suum David, quia alas igneas charitatis sciebat non esse metuendas. Merito Hebraei pueri in fornace ardenti non sentiebant ignis incendia, quia charitatis 1955Ceos flamma refrigerabat. Et ut plenius cognoscamus 1615 quia alas habet perfecta charitas, audisti dudum dicentem: Quoties volui congregare filios tuos, sicut gallina congregat pullos suos sub alas suas (Matth. XXIII, 27).

13.

With these wings Enoch flew forth, caught up to heaven; with these wings Elias flew forth, carried over to the things above by a fiery chariot and by fiery horses. With these wings the Lord God led the people of the fathers by a pillar of fire. These wings the Seraphim had, when it took coals of fire from the altar, and touched the mouth of the prophet, and took away his iniquities, and purged his sins. By the fire of these wings the sons of Levi were purged, and the peoples of the nations were baptized, as John testifies, saying of the Lord Jesus: He shall baptize you in the spirit and in fire (Matth. III, 11). [Vulg. in Spiritu Sancto et igni, "in the Holy Spirit and fire"] Deservedly did David wish his reins and his heart to be burned, because he knew that the fiery wings of charity are not to be feared. Deservedly did the Hebrew children in the burning furnace not feel the burnings of the fire, because the flame of charity 1955Ccooled them. And that we may know more fully 1615 that perfect charity has wings, thou hast heard him saying a while ago: How often would I have gathered thy children together, as a hen gathers her chickens under her wings (Matth. XXIII, 27).

14.

Sumamus igitur has alas, quae sicut flammae ad superiora dirigunt. Exuat unusquisque animam suam involucris sordidioribus, et quasi aurum igne approbet detersam luto. Sic enim purgatur anima, velut aurum optimum. Pulchritudo autem animae sincera virtus et decor, verior cognitio supernorum; ut videat illud bonum ex quo pendent omnia, ipsum autem ex nullo. Eo igitur vivit, atque intellectum accipit. Vitae enim fons est summum illud bonum, cujus in nobis accenditur desiderium, cui appropinquare 1955Det misceri voluptas est: quod ei qui non videt, desiderio est: et ei qui videt, vita est; ideoque universa alia despicit, hoc uno mulcetur, et delectatur. Hoc est quod subministrat universam substantiam: ipsum autem manens in semetipso, dat aliis; nihil autem in se ex aliis suscipit, de quo Propheta ait: Dixi Domino: Deus meus es tu; quoniam bonorum meorum non eges (Psal. XV, 2).

14.

Let us take therefore these wings, which as flames direct us to the things above. Let every man strip his soul of its filthier wrappings, and, as gold by fire, let him approve it wiped clean of the mire. For thus the soul is purged, as the best gold. But the beauty of the soul is unmixed virtue and comeliness, a truer knowledge of the things above; that it may see that good upon which all things hang, while itself hangs upon none. By it, therefore, it lives, and receives understanding. For that highest good is the fountain of life, the desire of which is kindled within us, to draw near 1955Dto which and to be mingled with it is a delight: which to him who does not see it is a matter of desire, and to him who sees it is life; and therefore he despises all other things, and by this one alone is soothed and delighted. This is that which supplies all substance; but itself, remaining in itself, gives to others, and takes nothing into itself from others; of which the Prophet says: I said to the Lord: Thou art my God, for thou hast no need of my goods (Psal. XV, 2).

15.

Alae ejus, alae ignis. Omnes oportet per ignem probari, quicumque ad paradisum redire desiderant; non enim otiose scriptum est quod, ejectis 1956AAdam et Eva de paradisi sede, posuit Deus in exitu paradisi gladium igneum versatilem. Omnes oportet transire per flammas, sive ille Joannes evangelista sit, quem ita dilexit Dominus, ut de eo diceret ad Petrum: Si eum volo manere, quid ad te? Tu me sequere (Joan. XXI, 22). De morte ejus aliqui dubitaverunt, de transitu per ignem dubitare non possumus; quia in paradiso est, nec separatur a Christo. Sive ille sit Petrus, qui claves accepit regni coelorum, qui supra mare ambulavit, oportet dicat: Transivimus per ignem et aquam, et eduxisti nos in refrigerium (Psalm. LXV, 12). Sed Joanni cito versabitur gladius igneus; quia non invenitur in eo iniquitas, quem dilexit aequitas. Si quid in eo vitii humani fuit, charitas divina decoxit: Alae enim ejus 1956Bsicut alae ignis. Qui hic habuerit charitatis ignem, illic ignem gladii timere non poterit. Ipsi Petro qui toties mortem suam pro Christo obtulit, dicit: Transi, recumbe. Sed ille dicit: Igne nos examinavit, sicut ipse examinavit argentum.

15.

Its wings are wings of fire. All must be proved by fire, whosoever desire to return to paradise; for not idly is it written that, when 1956AAdam and Eve had been cast out of the seat of paradise, God set at the outgoing of paradise a fiery sword that turned about. All must pass through the flames, whether it be John the evangelist, whom the Lord so loved that he said of him to Peter: If I will that he remain, what is that to thee? Follow thou me (Joan. XXI, 22). [Vulg. Sic eum volo manere donec veniam, quid ad te?, "So I will have him to remain till I come, what is it to thee?"] Some have doubted of his death; of his passing through fire we cannot doubt, because he is in paradise, nor is he separated from Christ. Whether it be Peter, who received the keys of the kingdom of heaven, who walked upon the sea, he must say: We have passed through fire and water, and thou hast brought us out into a refreshment (Psalm. LXV, 12). But for John the fiery sword will quickly turn aside, because there is not found in him iniquity, whom equity loved. If there was in him anything of human vice, divine charity boiled it away: For its wings 1956Bare as wings of fire. He who here shall have had the fire of charity, there will not be able to fear the fire of the sword. To Peter himself, who so often offered his own death for Christ, he says: Pass over, recline. But he says: He has tried us by fire, as he himself has tried silver.

16.

Alae ejus, alae ignis. Ergo quia volandi nobis data est copia, excitet unusquisque in se gratiam Dei, ac posteriora obliviscens, priora appetens, ad destinata contendat. Procul a militiae honoribus, procul ab aestibus mundi; ne quod fabulae ferunt, aestu solis cera resoluta lcareos volatus penna relabente destituat. Nam licet gravitas dictorum obsit, poetico tamen sale declarare voluerunt prudentium maturitati tutos esse volatus per coelum; juvenilem vero levitatem obnoxiam cupiditatibus mundi, refluentibus 1956Cpennis, et per oblivia veritatis meritorum compage resoluta, majore pernicie in terram relabi.

16.

Its wings are wings of fire. Since therefore the power of flying has been given to us, let every man stir up in himself the grace of God, and, forgetting the things behind, seeking after the things before, let him press on toward what is appointed. Far from the honors of the soldiery, far from the heats of the world; lest, as the fables relate, the wax loosed by the heat of the sun, the feather slipping back, fail the lcareos flights. For although the weightiness of their sayings be against it, yet with poetic salt they meant to declare that flights through heaven are safe for the maturity of the prudent; but that youthful levity, liable to the desires of the world, its feathers flowing back, 1956Cand the framework of its merits loosened through the forgettings of truth, slips back to the earth with the greater ruin.

17.

Haud facilis volatus omnibus, difficilis etiam discordantibus in terris animalibus vitae cursus humanae est. At si tibi actus nostri ordo conveniat, videbit et in nobis propheta rotam illam unam super terram, conjunctam animalibus quatuor. Videbit ergo rursus Ezechiel, videt enim adhuc et viget et vigebit (Ezech. I, 15 et 16): videbit, inquam, rotam in medio rotae, super terram sine offensione labentem. Rota enim super terram, vita est corporis ad animae aptata virtutem, et ad evangelicum cohaerenti cursu formata praeceptum. Rota autem in medio rotae, velut vita intra vitam, quod sanctorum sibi 1956Dvita non dissonet; sed qualis fuerit superioris aetatis, talis sit et sequentis: vel hoc in hac vita corporis vitae volvatur usus aeternae. Cum ista congruerint, tunc divina vox resultet, tunc super similitudinem throni similitudo sicut species hominis apparebit. Hic homo Verbum est, quia Verbum caro factum est (Joan. I, 14): hic homo nostrorum est agitator animalium, nostrorum rector est morum, qui pro nostrorum ratione meritorum, aut currum plerumque, aut montem ascendit, aut navim.

17.

Flight is by no means easy for all; the course of human life is difficult also for the living creatures that are at discord upon the earth. But if the order of our action agree with thee, the prophet will see even in us that one wheel upon the earth, joined to the four living creatures. Ezechiel therefore will see again — for he still sees, and is in vigor, and will be in vigor (Ezech. I, 15 et 16) — he will see, I say, a wheel in the midst of a wheel, gliding upon the earth without stumbling. For the wheel upon the earth is the life of the body, fitted to the virtue of the soul, and formed with a coherent course to the precept of the Gospel. But the wheel in the midst of the wheel is as it were a life within a life, in that the life of the saints 1956Dis not out of tune with itself; but such as it was in the earlier age, such let it be also in that which follows: or that in this life of the body the practice of eternal life be turned round. When these things shall have agreed together, then let the divine voice sound back, then above the likeness of the throne a likeness as the appearance of a man will appear. This man is the Word, because the Word was made flesh (Joan. I, 14): this man is the driver of our living creatures, the ruler of our manners, who, according to the measure of our merits, mounts now for the most part a chariot, now a mountain, now a ship.

18.

Pone me ut signaculum in cor tuum, ut sigillum 1957Ain brachium tuum, quae nova et vetera servasti mihi. Signaculum meum es, ad imaginem meam es et similitudinem. Fulget in te imago justitiae, imago sapientiae, imago virtutis. Et quia imago Dei in corde tuo est, sit et in operibus tuis: sit effigies Evangelii in tuis factis, ut in tuis moribus mea praecepta custodias. Effigies Evangelii erit in te, 1616 si percutienti maxillam, alteram praebeas, si diligas inimicum tuum, si crucem tuam tollas, et me sequaris. Ideo crucem ego pro te portavi; ne tu propter me portare dubitares. Audierunt hoc filiae Hierusalem, quod jam Dominus Deus Ecclesiam sibi copulabat: et quia considerantes magnitudinem Verbi, impares se tantis nuptiis aestimabant; ne forte tantae copulae pondus sustinere non possent, excusant dicentes: 1957B

18.

Set me as a seal in thy heart, as a signet 1957Aon thy arm, thou who hast kept new things and old for me. Thou art my seal, thou art unto my image and likeness. In thee shines the image of justice, the image of wisdom, the image of virtue. And because the image of God is in thy heart, let it be also in thy works: let the likeness of the Gospel be in thy deeds, that in thy manners thou mayest keep my precepts. The likeness of the Gospel will be in thee, 1616 if to him that strikes thy cheek thou offer the other, if thou love thine enemy, if thou take up thy cross and follow me. Therefore have I carried the cross for thee, lest thou shouldst hesitate to carry it for my sake. The daughters of Jerusalem heard this, that the Lord God was now joining the Church to himself: and because, considering the greatness of the Word, they esteemed themselves unequal to so great a marriage, lest perchance they might not be able to bear the weight of so great a union, they excuse themselves, saying: 1957B

19.

(Vers. 8.) Soror nostra parvula, et ubera non habet. Sic enim qui volunt differre nuptias, excusare consuerunt, ut praetendant immaturae aetatis infirmitatem, et astruant quod ubera non habeant, quae nubilis significant tempus aetatis. Hoc solet symbolum commune omnibus virginibus esse nupturis; ut cum ubera coeperint eminere, tunc conjunctioni habiles judicentur. Turbatae igitur, quod studio dilectionis urgeat nuptias Sponsus, dicunt:

19.

(Vers. 8.) Our sister is a little one, and she has no breasts. [Vulg. Soror nostra parva, "Our sister is little" — without the diminutive] For thus they who wish to put off a marriage are wont to excuse it: that they allege the weakness of unripe age, and assert that she has no breasts, which signify the time of marriageable age. This token is wont to be common to all virgins about to wed: that when the breasts have begun to stand out, then they are judged fit for union. Troubled therefore, because in the zeal of love the Bridegroom urges on the marriage, they say:

20.

Quid faciemus sorori nostrae, in die qua loquetur in ea, vel ut Symmachus, qua loquetur ei? Hoc est, in sponsalium celebritate solet fieri collocutio, et confirmatio nuptiarum. Quid ergo faciemus, dicunt turbatae, quia urget conjunctio spiritalis? A tantis nuptiis excusare non possunt; nemo enim 1957Cest qui copulam vel animae et spiritus, vel Christi et Ecclesiae non beatam putet. Sed quia plenitudo Verbi vel Spiritus sancti vibrat et fulget, et nihil est quod illis possit aequari; ideo differre desiderant, ut illa dilatione vel anima, vel Ecclesia possit esse perfectior. Dicunt ergo:

20.

What shall we do to our sister, in the day in which he shall speak in her, or, as Symmachus has it, in which he shall speak to her? [Vulg. Cant. 8:8 Quid faciemus sorori nostrae in die quando alloquenda est?, "What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to?"] That is: in the solemnity of a betrothal a conference is wont to take place, and a confirmation of the marriage. What then shall we do, they say, being troubled, because the spiritual union presses upon us? They cannot excuse themselves from so great a marriage; for there is no one 1957Cwho does not think the union either of the soul and the spirit, or of Christ and the Church, a blessed thing. But because the fulness of the Word and of the Holy Spirit quivers and flashes, and there is nothing that can be made equal to them, therefore they desire to defer it, that by that postponement either the soul or the Church may be able to be more perfect. They say therefore:

21.

(Vers. 9.) Si murus est, aedificemus super eum receptacula argentea: et si janua est, sculpamus super eam tabulas cedrinas. Murus est anima sancti. Habet et Ecclesia muros suos, quae jam perfectior dicit: Ego civitas munita (Esai. XXVII, 3). Hic est murus, qui habet duodecim portas apostolicas, per quas populo nationum patet ingressus in Ecclesiam. Sed murus quamvis ambitum totius urbis includat, tunc 1957Dtamen est munitior, cum receptacula habuerit praeparata, in quibus propugnatores urbis tutum spectandi ac tuendi possint habere subsidium. Sed quia rationalis haec civitas est, et omnis spes ejus in Dei Verbo est, non ferrea, sed argentea ei propugnacula requiruntur, eloquiis coelestibus magis quam corporeis voluptatibus hostiles impetus repulsare consueta.

21.

(Vers. 9.) If she is a wall, let us build upon her chambers of silver: and if she is a gate, let us carve upon her boards of cedar. [Vulg. Si murus est, aedificemus super eum propugnacula argentea; si ostium est, compingamus illud tabulis cedrinis, "If she be a wall, let us build upon it bulwarks of silver: if she be a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar"] The soul of a holy man is a wall. The Church too has her walls, who, being now more perfect, says: I am a fortified city (Esai. XXVII, 3). [Vulg. Isa. 27:3 Ego Dominus qui servo eam, "I am the Lord that keep it"; the reading here follows the Greek] This is the wall which has twelve apostolic gates, through which entrance into the Church lies open to the people of the nations. But a wall, although it enclose the circuit of the whole city, is then 1957Dthe better fortified when it has had chambers made ready, in which the defenders of the city may have a safe resource for watching and for guarding. But because this city is rational, and all its hope is in the Word of God, not iron but silver bulwarks are required for it, accustomed to beat back the assaults of the enemy by heavenly sayings rather than by bodily pleasures.

22.

Eo fulta praesidio, eo splendore fulgens, habilior Christi copulae judicatur. Et quia janua Christus est, qui ait: Per me si quis introierit, salvabitur (Joan. 1958AX, 9); et ecclesia janua nuncupatur, quia per ipsam patet populis aditus ad salutem. Sed ne haereticorum corrumpatur tineis aut vermibus, dicunt filiae Hierusalem, vel angeli, vel animae justorum: Aedificemus super eam tabulas cedrinas, hoc est, fidei sublimis bonum odorem. Est enim suavis hujus materiae odor, quam non vermis, non tinea corrumpat. Ideo hujus materiae usus eligitur tectorum fastigiis elevandis formandisque litterarum elementis, quibus aetas puerilis ad studium liberalis eruditionis imbuitur. Est ergo materia ista sublimis ad gratiam, levis ad onus, suavis ad odorem, utilis ad instrumentum scientiae, habilis ad ministerium cognitionis aeternae. Sed quemadmodum Sponsam suam diligens Christus urgebat ad copulae spiritalis solemnitatem; ita et Ecclesia Verbi decore jam capta, festinabat 1958Bad nuptias. Ideoque morarum et dilationis impatiens, quas filiae Hierusalem innectere gestiebant, dicit:

22.

Propped by that defence, shining with that splendor, she is judged the fitter for the union with Christ. And because Christ is the gate, who says: If any man enter in by me, he shall be saved (Joan. 1958AX, 9); the church too is called a gate, because through her the approach to salvation lies open to the peoples. But lest she be corrupted by the moths or worms of the heretics, the daughters of Jerusalem, or the angels, or the souls of the just, say: Let us build upon her boards of cedar, that is, the good odor of a sublime faith. For the odor of this timber is sweet, and neither worm nor moth corrupts it. Therefore the use of this timber is chosen for raising the peaks of roofs, and for shaping the elements of letters, with which the age of boyhood is steeped in the study of liberal learning. This timber, then, is sublime for grace, light for burden, sweet for odor, useful as an instrument of knowledge, apt for the ministry of eternal knowledge. But even as Christ, loving his Bride, was urging her toward the solemnity of the spiritual union, so also the Church, now taken with the comeliness of the Word, was hastening 1958Bto the marriage. And so, impatient of the delays and of the postponement which the daughters of Jerusalem were eager to weave in, she says:

23.

(Vers. 10.) Ego murus, et ubera mea turres. Hoc est, nolite dubitare utrum murus sim; illae enim dixerant: Si murus est; ego, inquit, murus sum, et non parva ubera habeo, sed ut turres ubera mea sunt. Quomodo dicitis, quia non habeo ubera? Sensus habeo ut turres sapientiae, in quibus est abundantia, sicut scriptum est: Et abundantia in turribus tuis (Psalm. CXXI, 7). His uberibus, id est, sensibus, habilem se tantis nuptiis aestimabat. Sed filiae Hierusalem adhuc non poterant aestimare, quia sensuum ejus abundantiam non videbant. Et addidit: 1958C

23.

(Vers. 10.) I am a wall, and my breasts are towers. [Vulg. Ego murus, et ubera mea sicut turris, "I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower"] That is: do not doubt whether I am a wall; for they had said: If she is a wall; I, she says, am a wall, and I have not small breasts, but my breasts are as towers. How do you say that I have no breasts? I have senses as towers of wisdom, in which there is abundance, as it is written: And abundance in thy towers (Psalm. CXXI, 7). By these breasts, that is, by her senses, she esteemed herself fit for so great a marriage. But the daughters of Jerusalem could not yet so esteem her, because they did not see the abundance of her senses. And she added: 1958C

24.

(Vers. 11.) Ego eram in oculis ejus tanquam inveniens pacem; hoc est, deliberatis de meis sensibus, cum pacem Dei invenerim, quae exsuperat omnem mentem, et custodit corda et sensus in Christo Jesu. Talis, inquit, eram in oculis sponsi, qualis quae habet pacem; scriptum est enim: Qui recte quaerunt pacem, gaudebunt. 1617

24.

(Vers. 11.) I was in his eyes as one finding peace; [Vulg. Cant. 8:10 ex quo facta sum coram eo, quasi pacem reperiens, "since I am become in his presence as one finding peace"] that is: my senses being deliberated upon, since I have found the peace of God, which surpasses all understanding, and keeps hearts and senses in Christ Jesus. Such, she says, was I in the eyes of the bridegroom, as one who has peace; for it is written: They who rightly seek peace shall rejoice. 1617

25.

(Vers 12.) Ego, inquit, civitas munita, ego civitas obsessa (Esai. XXVII, 3). His munitur muris, his obsessa defenditur. Et vere murus anima, quae praetenditur in castris. Unde et ipsa ait in Canticis: Ego murus, et ubera mea sicut turres (Vers. 10). Bonus murus quem pinxit Dominus, sicut ipse ait: In manibus meis pinxi muros tuos, et in conspectu meo es semper (Esai. XLIX, 16). Bona anima quae habet 1958Dspeculatorem Deum, et in manibus ejus est; sicut anima prophetica, quae in manus Dei commendatur ut spiritus (Psal. XXX, 6), et quae in conspectu Dei est: Oculi enim Domini super justos; sicut et ipse ait: Ego eram in oculis ejus tanquam inveniens pacem. Bonas turres habet, quae habet et de intelligibilibus verbum, et de moralibus disciplinam. Festinantibus igitur dilecto atque dilecta, celebrata conjunctio spiritalis est, mutuo expetita consensu. Ideoque tamquam nuptiale canens carmen, exsultavit Spiritus in propheta dicens: 1959A

25.

(Vers 12.) I, she says, am a fortified city, I am a besieged city (Esai. XXVII, 3). By these walls she is fortified, by these, when besieged, she is defended. And truly the soul is a wall, which is stretched out before the camp. Whence she herself also says in the Song: I am a wall, and my breasts are as towers (Vers. 10). A good wall is that which the Lord has painted, as he himself says: In my hands I have painted thy walls, and thou art always in my sight (Esai. XLIX, 16). [Vulg. Ecce in manibus meis descripsi te; muri tui coram oculis meis semper, "Behold, I have graven thee in my hands: thy walls are always before my eyes"] Good is the soul which has 1958DGod as its watchman, and is in his hands; as the prophetic soul, which is commended into the hands of God as a spirit (Psal. XXX, 6), and which is in the sight of God: For the eyes of the Lord are upon the just; as he himself also says: I was in his eyes as one finding peace. She has good towers, who has both a word concerning things intelligible and a discipline concerning things moral. The beloved and the beloved one therefore hastening, the spiritual union was celebrated, sought by mutual consent. And so, singing as it were a marriage song, the Spirit exulted in the prophet, saying: 1959A

26.

(Vers. 11.) Vinea facta est Salomoni in Beelamon, dedit vineam suam iis qui servant. Clamat ergo Spiritus, Plantata est congregatio populorum, sed vitis aeternae radice fundata, et spiritalia sub jugum Verbi corde mansueto corda subjecit. Plantata autem est in multitudine nationum. Hoc enim intelligendum Beelamon, Symmachus, Aquila, aliaeque translationes graeco sermone docuerunt. Repudiata est vetus copula quae fructum afferre non poterat, data est vinea novis fidelibusque cultoribus, qui non solum facere fructum possent, sed etiam custodire. Una igitur ovis erravit, sed revocata totius spatia orbis implevit. Unam ovem error abduxerat, sed multitudinem populorum Domini gratia congregavit. Erravit homo, sed in Ecclesia jam murus est 1959Bet murus validus. Erravit Adam, murus est David, qui mandata Dei oblitus non est. Custodita igitur et vallata haec vinea munimine spiritali, mille fructus dat Christo, ducentos autem fructus custodibus; ideoque ait Ecclesia:

26.

(Vers. 11.) A vineyard was made for Solomon in Beelamon; he gave his vineyard to those who keep it. [Vulg. Vinea fuit pacifico in ea quae habet populos: tradidit eam custodibus, "The peaceable had a vineyard in that which hath people: he let out the same to keepers"] The Spirit therefore cries out: A congregation of peoples has been planted, but founded upon the root of the eternal vine; and it has subjected spiritual hearts, with a meek heart, under the yoke of the Word. And it was planted in a multitude of nations. For that Beelamon is to be understood thus, Symmachus, Aquila, and the other translations have taught in the Greek tongue. The old union, which could not bring forth fruit, was repudiated; the vineyard was given to new and faithful tillers, who could not only make fruit, but also keep it. One sheep therefore went astray, but being called back it filled the spaces of the whole world. Error had led away one sheep, but the grace of the Lord gathered together a multitude of peoples. Man went astray, but in the Church he is now a wall, 1959Band a strong wall. Adam went astray; David is a wall, who did not forget the commandments of God. This vineyard, therefore, being guarded and walled about with a spiritual fortification, gives a thousand fruits to Christ, but two hundred fruits to the keepers; and so the Church says:

27.

(Vers. 12.) Vitis mea in conspectu meo mille Salomoni, et ducenti servantibus fructum. Perfectio et plenitudo Christi est portio parvulorum. Habes hoc mysterium in Genesi, ubi quinque partes Benjamin fratri juniori tribuit Joseph, singulas reliquis fratribus (Gen. XLIII, 34). Domino igitur quinque sensuum portio et praerogativa defertur, quam illi utique tribuit ipse quem diligit, sicut dilexit et Paulum, cui dedit ad gentes evocandas sapientiae principatum. Facessat hic sacris virginibus metus, quibus tanta 1959Cpraesidia tribuit primum Ecclesia, quae tenerae prolis sollicita successu, ipsa quasi murus abundantibus in modum turrium excrescit uberibus: donec, soluto obsidionis hostilis incursu, pacem validae juventuti maternae praesidio virtutis acquirat. Unde et Propheta ait: Fiat pax in virtute tua, et abundantia in turribus tuis (Psalm. CXXI, 7).

27.

(Vers. 12.) My vine is in my sight: a thousand for Solomon, and two hundred for those that keep the fruit. [Vulg. Vinea mea coram me est. Mille tui pacifici, et ducenti his qui custodiunt fructus ejus, "My vineyard is before me. A thousand are for thee, the peaceable, and two hundred for them that keep the fruit thereof"] The perfection and fulness of Christ is the portion of little ones. Thou hast this mystery in Genesis, where Joseph assigned five portions to Benjamin the younger brother, and single portions to the remaining brethren (Gen. XLIII, 34). To the Lord therefore is offered the portion and the prerogative of the five senses, which he himself whom he loves assuredly assigns to him, as he loved Paul also, to whom he gave the chief place of wisdom for the calling forth of the nations. Let fear depart here from the holy virgins, to whom the Church first assigns so great 1959Cdefences, she who, anxious for the increase of her tender offspring, herself as a wall grows up with breasts abounding after the manner of towers: until, the assault of the hostile siege being broken up, she may win peace for a strong youth by the defence of a mother's strength. Whence the Prophet also says: Let peace be in thy strength, and abundance in thy towers (Psalm. CXXI, 7).

28.

(Vers. 13.) Qui sedes in hortis, amici intendentes voci tuae, vocem tuam insinua mihi. Ideoque haec anima habens gratiam suorum uberum, ingreditur in hortos, et inveniens illic Sponsum sedentem et disputantem cum amicis, ait: Qui sedes in hortis, vocem tuam insinua mihi: mihi, inquit, non amicis.

28.

(Vers. 13.) Thou that sittest in the gardens, the friends listening to thy voice, make thy voice known to me. [Vulg. Quae habitas in hortis, amici auscultant; fac me audire vocem tuam, "Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the friends hearken; make me hear thy voice" — the Vulgate's addressee is feminine, Migne's masculine] And so this soul, having the grace of her own breasts, enters into the gardens, and finding there the Bridegroom sitting and disputing with his friends, says: Thou that sittest in the gardens, make thy voice known to me: to me, she says, not to the friends.

29.

Qui sedes in hortis, amici intendentes voci tuae, vocem tuam insinua mihi. Delectabatur enim 1959Dquod in hortis Christus sedebat, et in hortis positi amici intendebant voci ejus: sed quia amici illi de coelestibus erant, Archangeli vel Dominationes et Throni (homines enim expulsi de paradiso fuerant propter inobedientiam coelestium mandatorum, atque ideo adhuc Ecclesia vocem ejus audire non poterat, quam cupiebat audire), ideo ait: Vocem tuam insinua mihi. Unde et nos si volumus eum in nobis sedere, simus horti clausi atque muniti, feramus virtutum flores, gratiae suavitatem, ut disputantem 1960Acum angelis Dominum Jesum audire possimus. Sed quia futurum erat, ut cum ad plenitudinem Ecclesia pervenisset, persecutionibus variis tentaretur; ideo cum Verbi gratia delectaretur, subito cernit insidias persecutorum: et quae plus Sponso quam sibi timeret, aut quia a persecutoribus Christus magis appetitur in nobis, ideo ait:

29.

Thou that sittest in the gardens, the friends listening to thy voice, make thy voice known to me. For she was delighted 1959Dthat Christ was sitting in the gardens, and that the friends set in the gardens were listening to his voice: but because those friends were of the heavenly ones, Archangels or Dominations and Thrones (for men had been driven out of paradise on account of disobedience to the heavenly commandments, and therefore the Church could not yet hear his voice, which she desired to hear), therefore she says: Make thy voice known to me. Whence we also, if we wish him to sit within us, let us be gardens enclosed and fortified, let us bear the flowers of the virtues, the sweetness of grace, that we may be able to hear the Lord Jesus disputing 1960Awith the angels. But because it was to come to pass that, when the Church had come through to fulness, she would be tried by various persecutions, therefore, while she was being delighted with the grace of the Word, suddenly she discerns the ambushes of the persecutors: and since she feared more for the Bridegroom than for herself, or because Christ is the more sought after by persecutors in us, therefore she says:

30.

(Vers. 14.) Fuge, frater meus, et similis esto tu capreolae aut hinnulo cervorum, super montes aromatum. Propter infirmos fugit, qui tentamenta graviora ferre non possent. Ideo scriptum est, ut de civitatibus ad civitates fugiamus: et si nos in hac civitate fuerint persecuti, fugiamus in aliam. Propter infirmos igitur, ut diximus, fugiat persequentes: aut fugiat ab infimis, et transeat ad montes aromatum, 1960Bqui pro martyrio odorem possit beatae resurrectionis 1618 afferre. Montes aromatum sancti sunt. Ad eos confugit Christus; quia Fundamenta ejus in montibus sanctis (Psal. LXXXVI, 1). Ad eos igitur confugit, qui sunt ejus stabilia fundamenta. In nobis fugit, in illis fida statione subsistit. Mons igitur aromatum Paulus est, qui potest dicere: Bonus odor Christi sumus Deo (II Cor. II, 15). Mons aromatum David, cujus orationis odor ascendebat ad Dominum, et ideo dicebat: Dirigatur oratio mea sicut incensum in conspectu tuo (Psal. CXL, 2).

30.

(Vers. 14.) Flee, my brother, and be thou like to a little roe or to the fawn of the harts, upon the mountains of spices. [Vulg. Cant. 8:14 Fuge, dilecte mi, et assimilare capreae hinnuloque cervorum super montes aromatum, "Flee away, O my beloved, and be like to the roe and to the young hart upon the mountains of spices"] He flees on account of the weak, who could not bear the heavier trials. Therefore it is written that we should flee from cities to cities: and if they shall have persecuted us in this city, let us flee into another. On account of the weak, therefore, as we have said, let him flee those who pursue: or let him flee from the lowest things, and pass over to the mountains of spices, 1960Bhe who for martyrdom may be able to bring the odor 1618 of the blessed resurrection. The mountains of spices are the saints. To them Christ flees for refuge; because His foundations are in the holy mountains (Psal. LXXXVI, 1). To them therefore he flees for refuge, who are his stable foundations. In us he flees; in them he abides in a faithful station. A mountain of spices, therefore, is Paul, who can say: We are the good odor of Christ unto God (II Cor. II, 15). A mountain of spices is David, the odor of whose prayer went up to the Lord, and therefore he said: Let my prayer be directed as incense in thy sight (Psal. CXL, 2).

31.

Symmachus tamen et Aquila interpretati sunt, quod Christus dicit ad Ecclesiam: Quae sedes in hortis, hoc est, jam in hortis sedes, superno digna es paradiso; et ideo Vocem tuam insinua mihi, cui amici intendunt: 1960Cego quoque eam audire desidero. Coepit in hortis esse Ecclesia, postquam in hortis captus est Christus.

31.

Symmachus, however, and Aquila have rendered it as that Christ says to the Church: Thou that sittest in the gardens, that is, thou already sittest in the gardens, thou art worthy of the paradise above; and therefore Make thy voice known to me, to which the friends are listening: 1960CI too desire to hear it. The Church began to be in the gardens, after Christ was taken in the gardens.

32.

Fuge, frater meus, et similis efficere cervo aut hinnulo cervi, in montes aromatum. Serpentinis enim invisus colubris, et effugitans canum, atque humi reptantibus infestus serpentibus, nescit habitare nisi in sublimitate virtutum, nescit commorari nisi talibus Ecclesiae filiabus, quae possunt dicere: Christi enim bonus odor sumus Deo (II Cor. II, 15). Sed quibusdam odor mortis in mortem, in iis qui pereunt: quibusdam odor vitae in vitam, in iis utique qui odorem resurrectionis Dominicae vivida fide sperant. Isti sunt aromatum montes, qui Domini corpus Jesu acceperunt, et in linteis cum aromatibus 1960Dligaverunt. Quicumque enim crediderunt, quia Jesus mortuus est, et sepultus est, et resurrexit, hi excelsum verae cacumen fidei virtutum fastigiis extulerunt. Ubi ergo Christus quaeritur, in pectore prudentis scilicet sacerdotis?

32.

Flee, my brother, and be made like to a hart or to the fawn of a hart, unto the mountains of spices. For, hated by the serpent snakes, and fleeing of dogs, and an enemy to the serpents that creep upon the ground, he knows not how to dwell save in the sublimity of virtues, knows not how to abide save with such daughters of the Church as can say: For we are the good odor of Christ unto God (II Cor. II, 15). But to some it is the odor of death unto death, in them that perish; to others the odor of life unto life, in those assuredly who with a living faith hope for the odor of the Lord's resurrection. These are the mountains of spices, who received the body of the Lord Jesus, and bound it in linen cloths with spices. 1960DFor whosoever have believed that Jesus died, and was buried, and rose again, these have lifted up the lofty summit of true faith with the peaks of the virtues. Where then is Christ sought, namely in the breast of a prudent priest?

33.

Fuge, frater meus. Hortatur ut fugiat Sponsus, quia jam sequi poterit ipsa terrena fugientem. Dicit ut similis sit damulae quae evadit de retibus; vult enim et ipsa fugere, et evolare supra mundum. Fugiamus ergo in patriam verissimam. Illic patria 1961Anobis, et pater a quo creati sumus, ubi est Hierusalem civitas quae est mater omnium. Sed quae est fuga? Non utique pedum, qui sunt corporis; isti enim quocumque currunt, in terra currunt, et de solo ad solum transeunt. Nec navibus fugiamus aut curribus, aut equis qui obligantur et cadunt; sed fugiamus animo et oculis, aut pedibus interioribus assuescamus oculos nostros videre quae dilucida et clara sunt, spectare vultum continentiae et temperantiae, omnesque virtutes; in quibus nihil scabrum, nihil obscurum et tortuosum sit: et seipsum spectet quis et conscientiam suam, illum oculum mundet; ne quid sordidum habeat. Quod enim videtur, non debet dissonare ab eo qui videt; quoniam conformes nos Deus imaginis voluit esse Filii sui. 1961B

33.

Flee, my brother. She exhorts the Bridegroom to flee, because now she herself, earthly as she is, will be able to follow him as he flees. She says that he should be like a little doe which escapes out of the nets; for she herself also wishes to flee, and to fly out above the world. Let us flee therefore into the truest fatherland. There is our fatherland, 1961Aand the Father by whom we were created, where is Jerusalem the city which is the mother of all. But what is that flight? Assuredly not of the feet, which are of the body; for these, wherever they run, run upon the earth, and pass from soil to soil. Nor let us flee by ships or by chariots, or by horses which are bound fast and fall; but let us flee with the mind and with the eyes, or with the inward feet let us accustom our eyes to see the things that are bright and clear, to gaze upon the countenance of continence and of temperance, and upon all the virtues, in which there is nothing rough, nothing dark and crooked: and let a man look upon himself and upon his own conscience, and cleanse that eye, that it may have nothing filthy. For that which is seen ought not to be out of tune with him who sees; since God willed us to be conformed to the image of his Son. 1961B

34.

Cognitum igitur nobis est illud bonum, nec longe est ab unoquoque nostrum: In ipso enim vivimus, adsumus, et movemur; ipsius enim et genus sumus (Act. XVII, 28), ut Apostolus gentiles voluit monere. Ipsum est enim bonum quod quaerimus, et solum bonum; nemo enim bonus, nisi solus Deus. Hic est oculus, qui magnum illum et verum decorem intuetur. Solem nisi sanus et valens oculus non aspicit, nec bonum potest videre nisi anima 1962Abona: fiat ergo bonus, qui vult videre Dominum, et quod est bonum. Hujus boni similes simus, et secundum id operemur quae bona sunt. Hoc est bonum quod supra omnem operationem est, supra omnem mentem atque intellectum, ipsum est quod semper manet, dans vivere et esse omnibus; quia fons est omnium vitae Christus, de quo propheta ait: In umbra ejus vivemus. Nunc enim vita nostra abscondita in Christo est: cum autem apparuerit Christus vita nostra, et nos apparebimus cum ipso in gloria.

34.

That good, therefore, is known to us, nor is it far from every one of us: For in him we live, and are present, and are moved; for we are also his offspring (Act. XVII, 28), [Vulg. In ipso enim vivimus, et movemur, et sumus, "For in him we live, and move, and are"] as the Apostle wished to admonish the Gentiles. For it is itself the good which we seek, and the only good; for no one is good, but God alone. This is the eye which beholds that great and true comeliness. None but a sound and strong eye looks upon the sun, nor can any but a good soul 1962Asee the good: let him therefore become good who wishes to see the Lord, and that which is good. Let us be like to this good, and according to it let us work the things that are good. This is the good which is above all working, above all mind and understanding; it is that which always remains, giving to all to live and to be; because Christ is the fountain of the life of all, of whom the prophet says: In his shadow we shall live. For now our life is hidden in Christ: but when Christ our life shall have appeared, then we also shall appear with him in glory. [Vulg. Col. 3:3–4 vita vestra est abscondita cum Christo in Deo. Cum Christus apparuerit, vita vestra, tunc et vos apparebitis cum ipso in gloria, "your life is hid with Christ in God"]

35.

Ergo non timeamus mortem; quoniam requies est corporis, animae autem vel libertas vel absolutio. Nec vereamur eum qui potest carnem interficere, animam autem non potest; quia non 1962Btimemus eum qui potest vestimentum auferre, non timemus eum qui potest nostra furari, nos autem non potest. Nos igitur animae simus, si volumus esse Hebraei de iis qui sunt socii Jacob, id est, imitatores ejus. Nos animae simus, nostra autem membra vestimenta sunt: servanda sunt quidem vestimenta ne scindantur, ne inveterascant: sed illo magis qui his utitur, servare se debet et custodire.

35.

Therefore let us not fear death; since it is the rest of the body, but of the soul either liberty or release. Nor let us dread him who can kill the flesh, but cannot kill the soul; because we do not 1962Bfear him who can take away a garment, we do not fear him who can steal what is ours, but cannot steal us. Let us therefore be souls, if we wish to be Hebrews of those who are the companions of Jacob, that is, his imitators. Let us be souls; but our members are garments: garments indeed are to be kept, that they be not torn, that they grow not old: but far more ought he who uses them to keep and guard himself.

On this text

Cruces for this work — where the plate is defective or the reading uncertain →

The Latin is Migne's printing of Commentarius in Cantica canticorum (PL 15, coll. 1851–1962B), by Ambrose of Milan, from the Corpus Corporum transcription. Parenthetical references in small type stand on Migne's page — editorial identifications inherited from the edition he reprinted or supplied by his shop, not necessarily the author's own; the author's own citations run in the prose itself. Every gilt column mark is an address: migne.app/pl/15/1851 resolves to the first.

Du bon, à bon marché — carried to its limit: the good, free.
